Abductor digiti minimi

Basic Science · 1 / 108

1Basics

Anatomy — foot ADM (Orthobullets)

Abductor digiti minimi (foot)
Origin Calcaneal tuberosity
Insertion Base of 5th toe
Action Abducts small toe
Innervation Lateral plantar nerve
Arterial supply Lateral plantar artery

Blood supply — hand ADM

Hand ADMhypothenar · abducts 5th digit · deep branch of ulnar nerve (C8, T1)

Main blood supply✓ ulnar artery (deep branch) · 1 major + 2 constant minor pedicles · major pedicle leaves ulnar artery just distal to pisiform

Flap useADM can be used as a local flap in crushed hands

X vs Y

Artery Supplies
Ulnar artery ✓ Hypothenar muscles (ADM, FDM)
Radial artery Thenar muscles
Posterior interosseous artery Superficial + deep extensor compartments of forearm
Anterior interosseous artery Forearm flexor compartment
Palmar metacarpal artery Palmar interossei
Hand ADM Foot ADM
Nerve Deep branch ulnar Lateral plantar
Artery Ulnar Lateral plantar

How it's asked

"Main blood supply to abductor digiti minimi (hand)?"ulnar artery (✗ PIA, ✗ AIA, ✗ palmar metacarpal, ✗ radial)

"Nerve to abductor digiti minimi of the foot?"lateral plantar nerve

Adductor longus

Basic Science · 2 / 108

1Basics

Anatomy

Adductor longus
Origin Anterior body of pubis, just lateral to symphysis
Insertion Middle third of linea aspera · between adductor magnus/brevis (medial) and vastus medialis origin (lateral)
Action Adducts + flexes thigh · helps laterally rotate hip
Innervation Anterior division of obturator nerve (L2, L3, L4)
Arterial supply Obturator artery + medial circumflex femoral artery

Cross-sectional anatomy — proximal thigh

Adductor longuslies just deep to the superficial femoral artery

Adductor brevisbetween longus and magnus · deep to the deep femoral vessels

Adductor magnuslargest, most posterior adductor

Sartoriussuperficial · covers the superficial femoral vessels

Gracilismedial + superficial

4Diagnosis & Classification

L3 radiculopathy vs femoral neuropathy

Key testhip adduction (obturator nerve) · weak in L2/L3 radiculopathy, spared in femoral neuropathy (L2–4)

Femoral-innervated (✗ distinguishing)quadriceps · iliacus · sartorius · psoas

X vs Y

Muscle Nerve Distinguishes L3 root from femoral nerve?
Adductor longus Obturator (ant. division) ✓
Quadriceps Femoral ✗
Iliacus Femoral ✗
Sartorius Femoral ✗
Psoas Femoral (listed as such in the OB key) ✗

How it's asked

"L3 radiculopathy best differentiated from femoral neuropathy by testing?"adductor longus (obturator) (✗ quadriceps, ✗ iliacus, ✗ sartorius, ✗ psoas)

"Muscle just deep to the superficial femoral artery on proximal-thigh cross-section?"adductor longus (✗ magnus — large, posterior · ✗ brevis — deep to profunda · ✗ sartorius — covers SFA · ✗ gracilis — medial)

Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS)

Basic Science · 3 / 108

1Basics

Pathology

DefinitionLou Gehrig's disease · progressive motor neuron disorder · degeneration of anterior horn cells · commonest degenerative disease of the motor neuron system

LMN componentloss of motor neurons in anterior horns + motor cranial nerve nuclei

UMN componentloss of myelinated fibres in corticospinal + corticobulbar tracts

Hallmarkabnormal motor conduction, normal sensory conduction

Genes & lab markers

Familial~5% · some map to superoxide dismutase gene on chromosome 21 · cause otherwise unknown

Labsno lab test confirms ALS · SPEP/immunoelectrophoresis only exclude differentials

3Presentation

Symptomspainless weakness of one limb → spreads · fasciculations · cramps · dysarthria/dysphagia · ↓ head control · breathing difficulty · urinary frequency/incontinence (late) · sensation normal

Neck ptosisneck drop from neck-extensor weakness

UMN signs LMN signs
Spasticity Atrophy
Hyperreflexia Weakness
(+) Hoffmann Fasciculations
(+) Babinski Clumsiness
Spastic dysarthria ↓ reflexes, ↓ tone

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria

ClinicalUMN + LMN signs in the same limb · no pain, no sensory symptoms · ± cranial-nerve findings → highly indicative

Period of observationdocuments progressive loss of UMN + LMN function

Often misdiagnosedcervical myelopathy / radiculopathy

Electrodiagnostics — definitive test

Definitive diagnosis✓ electrodiagnostic studies (NCS + EMG) · also exclude peripheral mimics

EMG/NCS finding in ALS
Motor ↓ CMAP amplitude widespread · slowed motor CV
Denervation Fibrillations + positive sharp waves + ↓ recruitment in ≥3 extremities
Spontaneous Fibrillation + fasciculation potentials
Reinnervation Present (denervation + reinnervation)
Sensory Normal SNAPs

Not definitive✗ genetic testing (only some familial types) · ✗ MRI brain/cord (rules out structural mimics) · ✗ muscle biopsy (not required) · ✗ SPEP

5Management

No curesupportive · maintain function + comfort

Riluzoleblocks tetrodotoxin-sensitive Na⁺ channels in damaged neurons · delays ventilator dependence · prolongs life 2–3 months

X vs Y

ALS Peripheral compressive neuropathy (CTS/cubital)
UMN signs (Hoffmann, Babinski, hyperreflexia) Present Absent
Sensory symptoms None Present
SNAPs Normal Abnormal
Distribution Spreads, multiple limbs Single nerve territory

How it's asked

"Most useful study for definitive diagnosis of ALS?"electrodiagnostic studies (✗ genetic testing, ✗ MRI, ✗ muscle biopsy, ✗ SPEP)

"Hallmark EMG/NCS pattern?"abnormal motor, normal sensory conduction · denervation + reinnervation

"Hand weakness + wasting + hyperreflexia/Hoffmann, normal sensation?"ALS (✗ carpal/cubital tunnel)

"Riluzole mechanism?"blocks tetrodotoxin-sensitive sodium channels · +2–3 months survival

Anterior interosseous nerve

Basic Science · 4 / 108

1Basics

Innervated muscles

AIN musclesFPL · FDP index + long · pronator quadratus

Motor onlyno cutaneous sensation

Gantzer muscle (TO)

Gantzer muscle✓ accessory head of FPL (or of FDP) · anatomical variant in up to 70% (TO)

Origindeep surface of FDS, coronoid process or medial epicondyle (TO)

InsertionFPL or FDP (TO)

Innervationexclusively AIN (TO)

Traps✗ accessory popliteus · ✗ accessory biceps femoris · ✗ accessory rectus femoris (TO)

3Presentation

Supracondylar humerus fracture (extension type)AIN = commonest nerve injury in children

Muscle affected✓ pronator quadratus (most common electrophysiological abnormality) · also FPL, FDP index/long

Often misdiagnosedas tendon injury (only 3 of 13 correct initially in Seror's series)

Book test (TO, FNB 2024)

"Book test assesses?"answer key not provided by source · options: adductor pollicis, opponens pollicis, FPL, dorsal interossei · module files it under FPL (AIN muscle) — read the options

X vs Y

Muscle Nerve Lost in AIN palsy?
Pronator quadratus AIN ✓
FPL AIN ✓
FDP index/long AIN ✓
FDP ring/small Ulnar ✗
FCR Median (main trunk) ✗
FCU Ulnar ✗
EDC PIN ✗

How it's asked

"Extension supracondylar fracture, 4-yr-old, nerve deficit — muscle most likely affected?"pronator quadratus (AIN) (✗ EDC, ✗ FCR, ✗ FDP ring/small, ✗ FCU)

"True about Gantzer muscle (TO)?"accessory head of FPL · AIN-innervated (✗ popliteus, ✗ biceps femoris, ✗ rectus femoris)

"Book test (FNB, TO)?"answer key not provided · module = FPL

Anti-inflammatory Medications

Basic Science · 5 / 108

1Basics

NSAID mechanism (COX-1, COX-2)

NSAID effectsanti-inflammatory · antipyretic · analgesic · antiplatelet

Arachidonic aciddirectly metabolised by ✓ cyclooxygenase → prostaglandins, prostacyclin, thromboxane

5-lipoxygenasearachidonic acid → leukotrienes (✗ "1-lipoxygenase")

HMG-CoA reductasemevalonate / cholesterol pathway (statins) · ✗ not arachidonic acid

Steroidsinhibit ✓ phospholipase A2 → no arachidonic acid from membrane phospholipids · earliest step of cascade

Enzyme Role
COX-1 Constitutive · gastric mucosal protection (PGE2) · renal blood flow · platelet TXA2
COX-2 Inducible · inflammation, pain, fever · no platelet effect · needed for endochondral ossification

Drug classes

Drug Binding Note
Aspirin Irreversible · acetylates serine 530 of COX Blocks TXA2 → antiplatelet · effect >1 wk (platelet life)
Ibuprofen Reversible, competitive · non-selective COX-1 + COX-2 GI ulcer, ↓ TX + ↓ PG
Indomethacin, naproxen, diclofenac, meloxicam Reversible COX-1 Indomethacin = HO prophylaxis · highest GI toxicity
Ketorolac (Toradol) COX-1 selective (OB) —
Celecoxib, rofecoxib COX-2 selective Spare platelets + gastric mucosa · cardiac toxicity

Aspirin — dose-dependent action

Dose Effect
Low 75–81 mg/day Irreversibly acetylates COX-1 → ↓ platelet TXA2 → antithrombotic
Intermediate 650 mg–4 g/day Inhibits COX-1 + COX-2 → ↓ PG → analgesic, antipyretic

5Management

Indicationspain · heterotopic ossification prophylaxis

Contraindicationssevere renal disease · gastric ulcers

COX-2 selectiveanti-inflammatory without COX-1 loss (gastric mucosa, renal blood flow, platelets) · usable perioperatively (no platelet effect) · no more effective for OA than non-selective

Perioperative NSAID (OB)dose- and time-dependent effect on bone healing → lowest effective dose, ≤7 days postop

Corticosteroids

Systemic GC bone lossadverse skeletal effects within 3 months · at doses as low as 2.5–5.0 mg/day prednisone-equivalent · dose-dependent

High fracture risk on long-term oral GC✓ give bisphosphonate (antiresorptive) + calcium + vitamin D

After stopping GCfracture risk normalises unless previously high-dose

Epidural steroid injection✓ ↓ lumbar BMD (esp. postmenopausal women) · ✗ knee/other large-joint injections do not ↓ lumbar BMD

Other GC effectstransient hyperglycaemia (caution in diabetics) · interfere with RANKL pathway

6Complications

NSAID side effects & fracture healing

Most strongly associated✓ renal impairment (all NSAIDs, incl. COX-2) · ✗ hepatic · ✗ QTc · ✗ seizures · ✗ haematuria

GIdyspepsia · ulcer perforation/bleeding/obstruction 2–4% of chronic users · elderly 4–5× risk of death from GI haemorrhage

GI mechanismnon-specific COX-1 + COX-2 inhibition → ↓ thromboxane + ↓ prostaglandin → uncoupled mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation, ↑ permeability, disruption of intestinal phospholipid bilayer

GI risk factorsconcurrent anticoagulant (most important) · age >60 · prior GI disorder

Other↑ bleeding time · ↑ creatinine · tinnitus/sensorineural hearing loss (reversible) · cardiac toxicity · ✗ NOT ↑ IL-6 (NSAIDs ↓ IL-6)

Indomethacin for HOmost likely side effect = ✓ GI ulceration

Fracture healing

Mechanism↓ COX-2 → ↓ PGE2 → ↓ mesenchymal stem cell → osteoblast/chondrocyte differentiation (PGE2 acts via RANKL/OPG balance)

Effect✓ ↓ endochondral ossification → ↓ callus → delayed union/nonunion

Which healingsecondary (callus) > primary

Risk ↑prolonged use · large doses · adults · ✗ no effect shown in children

COX-2thought necessary for normal endochondral ossification (animal data) · ✗ "definitively shown in human studies"

Intra-articular corticosteroid injection complications

Describedlocal flare · fat atrophy · skin pigmentation (depigmentation) · facial flushing · injection-site pain, bleeding

Not described✗ apoptosis of myocytes

X vs Y

Steroids NSAIDs
Enzyme inhibited Phospholipase A2 COX-1/COX-2
Level in cascade Earliest (no arachidonic acid) Arachidonic acid → prostanoids
Leukotrienes ↓ Unaffected (5-LOX intact)
COX-1 COX-2
Expression Constitutive Inducible (inflammation)
Function Gastric protection, platelets, renal flow Pain, fever, inflammation, endochondral bone
Selective drug Ketorolac (OB) Celecoxib
Inhibiting it causes GI ulcer, bleeding ↓ fracture healing, cardiac events

How it's asked

"Arachidonic acid is directly metabolised by?"cyclooxygenase (✗ 1-lipoxygenase, ✗ HMG-CoA reductase, ✗ carbonic anhydrase, ✗ thromboxane synthetase)

"All bind COX-1 reversibly EXCEPT?"aspirin (irreversible) (✗ meloxicam, diclofenac, indomethacin, naproxen)

"Mechanism of aspirin?"binding of COX-1 and COX-2 (✗ vitamin K carboxylation, ✗ Xa via antithrombin, ✗ direct thrombin inhibition)

"Chronic ibuprofen → GI ulcer mechanism?"non-specific COX-1 + COX-2 inhibition → ↓ thromboxane + ↓ PG → phospholipid bilayer disruption

"Indomethacin for HO prophylaxis — likely side effect?"GI ulceration

"Side effect most strongly associated with NSAIDs?"renal impairment

"NOT a side effect of prolonged NSAIDs?"↑ circulating IL-6 (✗ ulcer, ✗ ↑ creatinine, ✗ ↑ bleeding time, ✗ tinnitus)

"True about COX-2?"necessary for normal endochondral ossification (✗ constitutive form, ✗ more gastric irritation, ✗ proven in humans)

"True about NSAIDs in femoral nailing?"prolonged use ↓ endochondral ossification (✗ inhibits PLA2, ✗ COX-1 selectivity ↓ GI effects, ✗ adults lower risk than children, ✗ primary > secondary healing)

"How do NSAIDs impair bone healing?"↓ PGE2 and COX-mediated osteoblastic MSC differentiation (✗ ↑ osteoclastogenesis, ✗ ↑ COX-2)

"Corticosteroids inhibit?"phospholipase A2 (✗ lipoxygenase, ✗ COX-1, ✗ TXA2, ✗ prostacyclin)

"NOT a complication of corticosteroid injection?"apoptosis of myocytes

"Most accurate about corticosteroid complications?"epidural steroid injection ↓ lumbar BMD (✗ no effect <1 yr, ✗ bisphosphonate contraindicated, ✗ 2.5–5 mg safe, ✗ knee injection ↓ lumbar BMD)

Antibiotic Classification & Mechanism

Basic Science · 6 / 108

1Basics

Mechanism of action by class

Five basic mechanismscell-wall synthesis · membrane permeability · ribosomal (protein) inhibition · DNA metabolism · antimetabolite (folate)

Target Drugs Cidal / static
Cell wall (transpeptidase / PBP) Penicillins, cephalosporins, carbapenems (imipenem), aztreonam, β-lactamase inhibitors Bactericidal
Cell wall — D-Ala-D-Ala Vancomycin (binds D-Ala-D-Ala terminal → blocks transpeptidation, peptidoglycan cross-linking) Bactericidal
Membrane permeability Bacitracin · polymyxin —
Membrane depolarisation Daptomycin Bactericidal
Fungal membrane sterols Amphotericin, nystatin — bind sterols → disrupt membrane —
30S ribosome Aminoglycosides (gentamicin, tobramycin — irreversible) · tetracyclines (doxycycline — block tRNA binding) AG cidal · tetracycline static
50S ribosome Macrolides (erythromycin — reversible, blocks aminoacyl translocation) · clindamycin · chloramphenicol (peptidyl transferase) · streptogramins Static
50S 23S rRNA — initiation complex Linezolid (oxazolidinone) — prevents 30S–50S (70S) initiation complex Static vs staph/enterococci · cidal vs strep
DNA gyrase (type II topoisomerase) Fluoroquinolones (cipro, levo, moxi) Bactericidal
DNA (metabolic by-products) Metronidazole Bactericidal
RNA polymerase (DNA-dependent) Rifampin Bactericidal
Mycolic acid Isoniazid —
Folic acid (PABA mimic) TMP/SMX, sulfonamides Static

Classification & indications

Class Drugs Drug of choice / coverage Toxicity
Natural penicillin Penicillin G, V Strep pyogenes (A), Strep agalactiae (B), C. perfringens Hypersensitivity, haemolytic anaemia
Aminopenicillins Ampicillin, amoxicillin + E. coli, E. faecalis As above
Penicillinase-resistant Methicillin, nafcillin, oxacillin, cloxacillin + PCNase-producing S. aureus + Interstitial nephritis
Antipseudomonal penicillins Piperacillin, ticarcillin + Pseudomonas —
1st-gen cephalosporin Cefazolin, cephalexin S. aureus, S. epidermidis, some GN (E. coli, Klebsiella) Allergy · Coombs + anaemia (3%)
2nd-gen Cefoxitin, cefaclor, cefuroxime + GN Disulfiram reaction
3rd-gen (4th = cefepime) Ceftriaxone, ceftazidime + GN, Pseudomonas Disulfiram reaction
Vancomycin — MRSA, PCN/ceph allergy, S. epidermidis Red man syndrome, nephro-, ototoxicity
Carbapenems Imipenem (+ cilastatin), meropenem Broadest spectrum (✗ MRSA, Mycoplasma) —
Aztreonam — GN rods, aerobes, hospital-acquired —
Class (non-β-lactam) Drugs Drug of choice / coverage Toxicity
Aminoglycosides Gentamicin, tobramycin, amikacin Aerobic GN, Pseudomonas · with cephalosporin in open fractures Nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity
Tetracyclines Doxycycline, minocycline Rickettsia, Mycoplasma, Lyme Tooth/bone staining · avoid <12 yr
Macrolides Erythromycin, azithro Strep, H. influenzae, Mycoplasma Warfarin interaction (P450)
Chloramphenicol — Meningitis, brain abscess Aplastic anaemia, grey baby
Clindamycin — B. fragilis, S. aureus · excellent bone penetration Pseudomembranous colitis
Linezolid — Resistant G+ (MRSA, VRE) Serotonin syndrome (MAOI)
Streptogramins Quinupristin/dalfopristin VRE —
Fluoroquinolones Cipro (+ Pseudomonas), levo, moxi (+ anaerobes) Equivalent IV/PO · osteomyelitis, PJI Phototoxicity · Achilles rupture · ↓ fracture healing
Metronidazole — Anaerobes Seizures, cerebellar, disulfiram
Rifampin — Staph (intracellular), TB Orange body fluids · hepatotoxic (with INH)
TMP/SMX — UTI, Proteus Thrombocytopenia · avoid 3rd trimester

Splenectomy

Requiredpneumococcal · Hib · meningococcal C · influenza vaccines · lifelong oral penicillin V or erythromycin

2Mechanism

Resistance (mecA, biofilm, efflux, D-zone test)

Three basic mechanisms (OB)avoidance · decreased susceptibility · inactivation

Mechanism Confers resistance to Example
β-lactamase (hydrolysis) Penicillin E. coli, S. epidermidis, Pseudomonas, Klebsiella → add clavulanate (Augmentin)
Genetic mutation — mecA (on SCCmec) → PBP2a (low β-lactam affinity) Methicillin, oxacillin, nafcillin, cephalosporins MRSA · SCCmec type IV = CA-MRSA (less multidrug-resistant)
Altered cell-wall permeability Tetracyclines, quinolones, trimethoprim, β-lactams —
Biofilm (glycocalyx) = avoidance Adherence to implants, resists phagocytosis S. epidermidis, Salmonella
Active efflux pumps Erythromycin, tetracycline msrA gene (Staph)
Altered peptidoglycan (D-Ala-D-Ala) Vancomycin VRE
Ribosome alteration — erm gene methylates 23S rRNA Inducible MLS (macrolide–lincosamide–streptogramin) D-zone test
Altered DNA gyrase Quinolones —
Altered RNA polymerase Rifampin —
Altered membrane-binding protein β-lactams —

Tetracycline resistance (3)increased drug efflux · ribosome protection · tetracycline modification

Biofilm biologyforms when bacterial mass sufficient → quorum sensing → stationary/reduced growth phase · high-shear environments stimulate it

MRSA options (don't bind PBPs) — vancomycin, TMP/SMX, clindamycin, linezolid

4Diagnosis & Classification

D-zone test

Indicationerythromycin-resistant MRSA (or β-haemolytic strep) before starting clindamycin

Basisplasmid alters 50S binding site for both clindamycin + erythromycin → inducible clindamycin resistance

Methoderythromycin disc next to clindamycin disc → D-shaped zone of inhibition = positive → ✗ don't use clindamycin

Test Use
D-zone ✓ Inducible clindamycin resistance
Etest MIC
CAMP Group B strep
Ziehl–Neelsen Acid-fast (mycobacteria)
Oxidase Cytochrome c oxidase +/−
Phenylalanine deaminase Proteus
Citrate / decarboxylase Enterobacteriaceae
β-lactamase Penicillin resistance

5Management

Clinical use (rifampin, antibiotic cement, dental prophylaxis)

Rifampinlipophilic → high cell penetration → effective vs intracellular phagocytised S. aureus (in macrophages) · always combined (synergy)

Vancomycin indicationsserious β-lactam allergy · MRSA/MRSE infection · prosthesis prophylaxis where MRSA/MRSE rate is high

Vancomycin coverageG+ only · ✗ GN bacilli (too large) · time-dependent killing

Antibiotic cement (PMMA)max 5% of cement weight without ↓ mechanical strength → 2 g per 40 g · commercial = 1 g / 40 g · add as powder, never liquid

Antibiotic nail for septic tibial nonunionPMMA + vancomycin (G+) + tobramycin (GN)

Dental prophylaxis after TJAonly high-risk: immunocompromised (transplant, AIDS, inflammatory arthritis, chemo) · prior PJI · uncontrolled diabetes

Regimencephalexin / amoxicillin / cephradine 2 g PO within 1 h · ✗ routine IV

6Complications

Adverse effects & contraindications

Quinolonesinhibit early fracture healing (toxic to chondrocytes → immature callus, more cartilage) · Achilles tendinitis/rupture (RR 3.7)

Tetracycline✓ contraindicated in children (stains teeth + bone) · ✗ penicillin, cephalexin, erythromycin, ampicillin

Linezolidreversible non-selective MAOI → serotonin syndrome (esp. with SSRIs): headache, diaphoresis, tachycardia, hypertension, tremor, clonus, unreactive pupils → stop drug + IV benzodiazepines · severe → serotonin antagonist

Vancomycinflushing = red man syndrome

X vs Y

Aminoglycoside (gentamicin/tobramycin) Tetracycline (doxycycline) Macrolide (erythromycin) Clindamycin Linezolid
Subunit 30S 30S 50S 50S 50S (23S rRNA)
Binding Irreversible Blocks tRNA Reversible Translocation / peptidyl transferase Blocks initiation complex
Action Cidal Static Static Static (cidal high dose) Variable
Vancomycin Tobramycin
Target Cell wall (D-Ala-D-Ala) 30S ribosome (irreversible)
Spectrum Gram-positive Gram-negative

How it's asked

"Mechanism of vancomycin?"inhibition of cell-wall synthesis (✗ permeability, ✗ ribosome, ✗ DNA, ✗ antimetabolite)

"All inhibit ribosomes EXCEPT?"vancomycin (✗ gentamicin, tobramycin, erythromycin, linezolid)

"All interfere with cell-wall synthesis EXCEPT?"gentamicin (✗ cefazolin, penicillin G, vancomycin, imipenem)

"Family that inhibits DNA gyrase?"quinolones (✗ penicillins, aminoglycosides, macrolides, sulfonamides)

"Binds the 30S subunit?"aminoglycosides (✗ macrolides — 50S, ✗ rifampin — RNA polymerase)

"Linezolid (oxazolidinone) targets?"ribosomal activity (✗ DNA-dependent RNA polymerase — rifampin, ✗ membrane — daptomycin)

"Cephalosporins block transpeptidase → effect?"inhibit cell-wall synthesis

"Fluoroquinolones inhibit?"DNA gyrase (✗ RNA gyrase, ✗ DNA polymerase, ✗ telomerase)

"Amphotericin acts by?"binding sterols, disrupting the cell membrane

"Tobramycin in an antibiotic nail — mechanism?"irreversibly binds 30S (✗ reversible, ✗ 50S, ✗ D-Ala-D-Ala = vancomycin)

"Correct class–mechanism pairing?"fluoroquinolones – DNA gyrase

"Prevents 30S–50S initiation complex?"linezolid (✗ gentamicin, ✗ doxycycline, ✗ chloramphenicol, ✗ erythromycin)

"Binds the 50S subunit?"clindamycin (✗ cefazolin, cipro, doxycycline, vancomycin)

"Binds D-Ala-D-Ala to block transpeptidation?"vancomycin (✗ daptomycin — membrane depolarisation)

"Species producing mecA?"MRSA

"How does S. epidermidis adhere and resist phagocytosis?"biofilm

"MRSA resistance to penicillinase-stable antibiotics via?"genetic mutation (mecA → PBP2a) (✗ efflux, ✗ biofilm, ✗ permeability, ✗ peptidoglycan)

"Tetracycline resistance: ribosome protection, modification and?"increased drug efflux

"Biofilm resistance is an example of?"avoidance

"Erythromycin-resistant MRSA, want clindamycin — test?"D-zone test (✗ oxidase, CAMP, Ziehl–Neelsen, Etest)

"MSSA abscess → recurrence now methicillin-resistant — mechanism?"mecA gene mutation

"Antibiotic toxic to chondrocytes, inhibits early fracture healing?"quinolones

"Contraindicated in children?"tetracycline

"Linezolid → clonus, tachycardia, hypertension — next step?"IV benzodiazepines (serotonin syndrome)

"Why rifampin kills intracellular staph?"high cell penetration (lipophilic)

"Max vancomycin in 40 g PMMA?"2 g (5% by weight)

"TKA 8 mo ago, A1c 9.0, gingival dental procedure — prophylaxis?"cephalexin 2 g PO within 1 h (✗ IV daptomycin, ✗ none, ✗ delay, ✗ repeat A1c)

Anticoagulation Medications

Basic Science · 7 / 108

1Basics

Coagulation & Virchow's triad

Cascadeseries of reactions → fibrin → platelet activation → clot · imbalance → thromboembolism / DVT

Virchow's triadvenous stasis · endothelial damage · hypercoagulable state

Factor Ia= fibrin · broken down by plasmin (from plasminogen)

Overview of anticoagulants

Agent Mechanism Metabolism Reversal Advantage / risk
Compression stocking Mechanical — — No bleeding · compliance
Aspirin Irreversible COX acetylation in platelets → ↓ TXA2, PG Renal None (acts at platelet) Convenient
Unfractionated heparin Activates ATIII → inhibits IIa, III, Xa Hepatic Protamine Reversible · highest bleeding + HIT
LMWH (enoxaparin, dalteparin) ATIII → principally Xa (also IIa) Renal Protamine (partial — anti-Xa not normalised) Fixed dose, no monitoring · t½ 4–5 h
Fondaparinux (Arixtra) Indirect Xa via ATIII · synthetic pentasaccharide · ✗ no IIa effect Renal — No monitoring · ↓ DVT vs enoxaparin (hip fx, TKA) · highest bleeding · ✗ with epidurals · t½ 17–21 h
Warfarin Vitamin K 2,3-epoxide reductase (VKORC1) → ↓ γ-carboxylation of II, VII, IX, X, C, S Hepatic Vit K (≤3 days) · FFP (immediate) Most effective · hard to reverse/dose
Dextran Dilutional · coats RBC/platelets/endothelium (↑ electronegativity) · ↓ vWF/VIII-Ag — — Fluid overload
Rivaroxaban, apixaban, edoxaban Direct Xa ("-xa-ban") Liver (major) + kidney, gut Andexanet alfa (see below) Oral · peak 2–4 h
Dabigatran (Pradaxa) Direct thrombin (IIa), reversible Renal Idarucizumab (FDA 2015) GI upset, bleeding
Hirudin (leech), bivalirudin Direct thrombin — — Hirudin = most potent thrombin inhibitor
Clopidogrel ADP receptor on platelets — — —

5Management

Warfarin mechanism & pharmacology

Mechanism✓ inhibits vitamin K-dependent clotting factors · blocks reduction of vit K epoxide → active vit K (hydroquinone) · inhibits carboxylation

FactorsII (prothrombin), VII (first affected), IX, X + proteins C and S · ✗ not ATIII, ✗ VIII, ✗ Xa directly

Alcoholic / liver diseasecaution due to factor VII (narrow margin)

Pharmacokineticsoral, rapid GI absorption · affected by liver function + diet · plasma t½ 36–42 h · therapeutic in 72–96 h · INR not affected for 2–3 days

Target INR2–3 for orthopaedic patients · ✗ 2.5–3.5

Elective surgerystop 3–5 days before

Drug interactionsrifampin · phenobarbital · diuretics · cholestyramine

Heparin, LMWH & fondaparinux

Activates ATIII✓ heparin (✗ warfarin, aspirin, rivaroxaban, dabigatran)

Heparin inhibits IIa (thrombin)✓ heparin (✗ fondaparinux — Xa only · ✗ rivaroxaban — Xa only · ✗ TXA)

Enoxaparinprimarily Xa (antithrombin complex irreversibly inactivates Xa) · protamine less effective than for UFH

Indirect Xa inhibitor✓ fondaparinux (✗ rivaroxaban, apixaban, edoxaban — direct · ✗ dabigatran — direct IIa)

Protamine reverses✓ LMWH (partially), UFH · ✗ aspirin, clopidogrel (no medical reversal) · ✗ warfarin (vit K)

Highest bleeding + thrombocytopenia✓ unfractionated heparin (HIT — antibodies activate platelets → intravascular clot) · rare with LMWH · ✗ coumadin

Factor Xa inhibitors

Direct Xa✓ rivaroxaban (Xarelto), apixaban, edoxaban · antithrombin-independent · ✗ fondaparinux (indirect)

Class factsoral · max effect 2–4 h · predictable → no routine monitoring · low food/drug interaction · excretion liver (major) + kidneys + intestine

Half-liferivaroxaban 8 h · apixaban 12 h · urgent surgery delayed until a half-life has elapsed

ReversalOrthobullets: no current antidote, andexanet alfa under investigation · OB: FDA-approved coagulation factor Xa (recombinant) inactivated-zhzo for life-threatening bleeding — read the options

Tranexamic acid

Mechanismsynthetic lysine analogue · competitively inhibits plasminogen activation (binds lysine-binding site) · non-competitive plasmin inhibitor at high dose · ↓ active plasmin → ↓ fibrinolysis

Potency8–10× ε-aminocaproic acid

Route Fact
IV 10–20 mg/kg bolus ± repeat every 3 h ×1–4 or infusion 1–10 mg/kg/h
Topical As effective as IV · sprayed on wound at closure · no detectable serum level
Oral As effective as IV · 1.95 g 2 h preop · cheaper

Kinetics<5% metabolised · joint-fluid t½ 3 h · in tissues up to 17 h

Safetyno ↑ thromboembolic events (no route difference in DVT) · avoid with cardiac stents, prior VTE/ischaemic stroke

Jehovah's Witness THATXA ↓ active plasmin (✗ epoetin alfa — ↑ Hb preop · ✗ dexamethasone — pain · ✗ celecoxib)

Antiplatelet effects & supplements

Aspirin's target cell✓ platelets (irreversibly blocks TXA2)

Supplement Effect
Ginkgo (ginkgolide B displaces PAF) ↑ bleeding — platelets
Ginseng (panaxynol) ↑ bleeding — platelets · hypoglycaemia
Garlic (aged extract — ↑ cyclic nucleotides, ↓ fibrinogen binding) ↑ bleeding — platelets
Omega-3 fish oil ↑ INR (platelets + vit K factors)
St John's wort ↓ INR (↑ warfarin catabolism) · CNS reuptake inhibition · ✗ not a platelet effect
Coenzyme Q10 · green tea ↓ INR (green tea = direct warfarin antagonist)
Ephedra Cardiovascular instability
Kava, valerian ↑ sedative effect of anaesthetics

VTE prophylaxis guidelines

CRISTAL (9,711 THA/TKA)symptomatic VTE aspirin 3.45% vs LMWH 1.8% → aspirin failed non-inferiority (1% margin) · no difference in mortality, joint readmission, major bleeding

ACCPmechanical + pharmacological · minimum 10–14 days, ideally up to 35 days · LMWH preferred, aspirin acceptable

Aspirin dose81 mg BID = efficacy of 325 mg BID with less GI · <4 weeks → ↑ mortality + bleeding

No prophylaxisDVT >50% after TKA → ✗ mechanical alone

Weight-based LMWH= therapeutic dose → ✗ ↑ bleeding/wound drainage

AAOS VTED guideline (elective THA/TKA)
Postop duplex screening ✗ Not recommended
Only risk factor clearly supported for VTE Previous VTE
Only bleeding risk factors clearly supported Known bleeding disorder, active liver disease
Antiplatelet agents Stop preop
All patients Pharmacological and/or mechanical
Previous VTE Pharmacological AND mechanical
Bleeding disorder / liver disease Mechanical
Anaesthesia Neuraxial (spinal/epidural)
Mobilisation Early
IVC filter Not clearly supported
Aspirin ✓ Included as a prevention option

Operative criteria — when chemoprophylaxis is not mandatory

Below-knee injury, no risk factorse.g. healthy 37-yr-old, isolated rotational ankle ORIF → not absolutely indicated (DVT rate = placebo)

Elective forefoot/midfoot surgery, low risk, immediate WB✓ no routine chemical prophylaxis (ICM-VTE Foot & Ankle) · NWB = independent risk, mitigated by >50% load-bearing

Risk factors that mandate itolder age · obesity · prior DVT · malignancy · OCP · smoking · non-ambulatory · SCI / axial injury · hip fracture · THA

Patient needing VTE prophylaxis decision
  • THA / TKA→MECHANICAL + PHARMACOLOGICAL
    • Prior VTE→PHARMACOLOGICAL AND MECHANICAL
  • Known bleeding disorder / liver disease→MECHANICAL ONLY
  • Below-knee / forefoot, low risk, early WB→NO ROUTINE CHEMOPROPHYLAXIS

X vs Y

Direct Indirect (via ATIII)
Xa Rivaroxaban, apixaban, edoxaban Fondaparinux (Xa only), LMWH (mainly Xa)
IIa Dabigatran, bivalirudin, hirudin UFH (IIa + Xa)
Protamine Vitamin K / FFP Idarucizumab Andexanet alfa
Reverses UFH, LMWH (partial) Warfarin Dabigatran Xa inhibitors

How it's asked

"Mechanism of warfarin?"inhibits vitamin K-dependent clotting factors (✗ platelets, ✗ COX, ✗ ↓ ATIII)

"Inhibits carboxylation of clotting factors?"warfarin (✗ enoxaparin, dalteparin, heparin, hirudin)

"Prevents γ-carboxylation of X, IX and prothrombin?"warfarin (✗ clopidogrel — ADP receptor)

"Alcoholic, venogram DVT — warfarin caution due to factor?"VII

"Specific target of warfarin?"vitamin K 2,3-epoxide reductase (✗ ATIII, ✗ protein C, ✗ VIII, ✗ Xa)

"Plasma half-life of warfarin?"36–42 h

"DVT agent affected by liver function and diet?"warfarin (✗ LMWH, fondaparinux — renal)

"Protamine reverses?"LMWH

"Highest risk of bleeding and thrombocytopenia?"unfractionated heparin (HIT)

"Activates antithrombin III?"heparin

"Inhibits the circled factor IIa?"heparin (✗ fondaparinux, ✗ rivaroxaban — Xa only)

"Where does enoxaparin act?"factor Xa

"Indirect inhibitor of factor X?"fondaparinux

"Direct factor Xa inhibitor?"rivaroxaban (✗ fondaparinux — indirect, ✗ dextran, ✗ coumadin)

"True of Xarelto class?"maximal effect 2–4 h (✗ food interactions, ✗ IV/SC only, ✗ monitoring, ✗ mainly renal)

"Competitive inhibition of plasminogen activation?"tranexamic acid

"Inhibits activation of the enzyme that breaks down factor Ia?"TXA

"TXA in TKA — true?"similar blood-loss reduction IV vs oral (✗ IV better, ✗ topical better, ✗ ↑ DVT)

"Jehovah's Witness THA — drug ↓ active plasmin?"TXA

"Supplement affecting clotting via platelets?"ginkgo (✗ vitamin D, ephedra, St John's wort, selenium)

"↑ bleeding by direct platelet effect EXCEPT?"St John's wort

"Aspirin's primary cellular target (image)?"platelets

"LMWH vs aspirin after THA/TKA — true?"no difference in mortality (✗ aspirin non-inferior, ✗ aspirin fewer DVT, ✗ LMWH more bleeding/readmission)

"AAOS VTED recommendations EXCEPT?""aspirin not approved in low-risk patients" (false — aspirin is included)

"Uncomplicated TKA, BMI 39 — optimal prophylaxis?"aspirin 81 mg BID for 6 weeks (✗ 325 mg × 2 wk, ✗ weight-based LMWH, ✗ INR 2.5–3.5, ✗ SCDs only)

"DVT prophylaxis NOT absolutely indicated?"healthy 37-yr-old, isolated ankle ORIF

"Healthy 49-yr-old, elective bunion surgery, immediate heel WB — VTE strategy?"no routine chemical prophylaxis

Articular Cartilage

Basic Science · 8 / 108

1Basics

Forms of cartilage

Form Example
Hyaline / articular Joint surfaces
Fibroelastic Meniscus
Fibrocartilage Tendon/ligament insertion into bone
Elastic Trachea
Physeal Growth plate

Composition & function (aggrecan, water, type II collagen)

Function↓ friction · distributes load · stress-shielding of the solid matrix (high water, incompressible water, PG–collagen organisation) · anisotropic (✗ isotropic)

% by weightwater > collagen > proteoglycan > non-collagenous protein > cells

Component Amount Role
Water 65–80% of wet weight · 80% superficial, 65% deep Load-dependent deformation · ↓ with age · ↑ in OA
Collagen 10–20% · type II = 90–95% (minor V, VI, IX, X, XI) Tensile strength · framework · resists swelling from aggrecan osmotic pressure
Proteoglycans 10–15% Compressive strength · attract water · fluid/electrolyte balance
Chondrocytes ~2% of volume (OB) · "<10%" / "up to 5%" (TO) Make collagen, PG, enzymes · only cell in matrix

Type I collagen gives tensile strength✗ false (TO) · it is type II

Type X collagencalcified cartilage (mineralisation) · ↑ in early OA at subchondral interface (strain >2.5% induces Col X mRNA)

Type IV collagenbasal lamina (✗ calcified cartilage)

Proteoglycans

Aggrecanmost responsible for hydrophilic behaviour · GAGs (chondroitin sulfate, keratan sulfate) on a core protein → aggrecan → link protein binds it to hyaluronic acid → proteoglycan aggregate

Negative chargesulfated GAG chains (✗ positive) → osmotic swelling pressure · holds 50× its weight in water · swelling restrained ~20% by type II collagen · MW ~1,000,000

Molecule Role
Aggrecan ✓ Hydrophilicity, swelling pressure
Decorin, biglycan, fibromodulin Small PGs · bind type II collagen · matrix stabilisation (decorin organises collagen)
Versican Cell-surface PG
Matrilin-1 Cartilage tissue organisation
Type IX collagen Stability · links matrix molecules to type II
COMP Regulates chondrocyte proliferation
Osteocalcin Most prevalent non-collagenous protein of bone

Structure & zones

Zone Collagen Chondrocytes Content
Lamina splendens Cell-free, random flat fibril bundles None Surface layer
Superficial (tangential, zone 1) Parallel to surface (resists shear) Flattened, parallel to tidemark Highest collagen, lowest PG, high water · thinnest · only zone with progenitor cells
Transitional / intermediate (zone 2) Oblique / random, larger fibres Round, active Thickest · more PG
Deep / radial / basal (zone 3) Perpendicular, crosses tidemark, largest fibrils Round, in columns Highest PG, lowest water (lowest collagen)
Tidemark Basophilic line — Boundary uncalcified / calcified cartilage
Calcified (zone 4) Type X Small, metabolically inactive Anchors to subchondral bone

Tidemark (TO)lies between radial (deep) and calcified zone · part of zone 4 · ✗ calcified/subchondral · ✗ superficial/transitional

Tidemark (Orthobullets)separates true articular cartilage from remnant of cartilage anlage · divides nutritional sources · found only in joints, most prominent in adults

Water vs depth (TO)as PG ↑ from superficial → deep, water ↓

Nourishment, growth factors

Avascularfed by synovial fluid (surface) + subchondral bone (base) · ATP by glycolysis

Immature cartilagehas stem cells · mature does not

Growth factor Effect
TGF-β1 Most investigated · ↑ PG + ECM synthesis · ↓ IL-1 + MMP catabolism · synovial proliferation + fibrosis · osteophytes
b-FGF (FGF-2) ↑ chondrocyte DNA synthesis, PG · but ↑ inflammation, osteophytes · no defect healing
IGF-1 ↑ DNA + matrix synthesis (adult) · ↓ synovial inflammation · additive with TGF-β · ↓ in OA
PDGF Laceration healing (from PRP data) · no adverse or positive effect in vivo
IL-1 Catabolic · ↑ MMP, COX-2, nitric oxide synthase
Glucosamine Anabolic · prevents IL-1β-induced ↓ PG synthesis

2Mechanism

Response to loading & mechanotransduction

Stimulates matrix synthesis Suppresses synthesis, ↑ chondrolysis
Cyclic stress 1–5 MPa Excess >5 MPa
Moderate frequency 0.1–1 Hz Static <0.01 Hz
Low rate <1000 MPa/s High rate >1000 MPa/s

Mechanosensorsprimary cilia (chondrocytes + osteoblasts) · transduction via integrins

Marathon runner, cartilage 2× thick✓ chondrocyte modulation via mechanotransduction

Moderate running↑ thickness · ✓ ↑ GAG production (T1 gadolinium MRI) · lateral femoral GAG > medial

Strenuous loadingthinning + PG loss

Immobilisation / NWBthinning, softening, ↓ PG · only in the off-loaded limb (✗ contralateral hypertrophy)

Lubrication (lubricin, boundary, fluid film)

Type Mechanism
Elastohydrodynamic Main during dynamic function · elastic deformation + thin fluid film
Boundary Non-deformable surfaces, lubricant partially separates · lubricin (superficial zone protein) + HA
Boosted Fluid trapped in pools between contact regions
Hydrodynamic Fluid separates sliding surfaces
Weeping Fluid exuded under load → hydrostatic separation
Squeeze film Fluid slowly squeezed out from between surfaces

Lubricinmucinous glycoprotein · binds hyaluronic acid → boundary layer · ✗ vitronectin (doesn't bind HA)

Fluid-film in MoM THA↑ by larger head, slight radial clearance (most important), low roughness · ✗ complete congruence prevents fluid film

Wear mechanismsadhesion · abrasion · transfer · fatigue · third body

4Diagnosis & Classification

Aging vs osteoarthritis changes

Aging Osteoarthritis
Water ↓ ↑ (→ ↑ permeability, ↓ strength)
Modulus / stiffness ↑ (less elastic, brittle) ↓ (more elastic) · ↓ tensile + shear + compressive modulus
Chondrocytes Fewer but larger (hypocellular) Clusters / clones (stage 2)
GAG ratio ↑ keratan sulfate : chondroitin (KS ↑ until ~30 yr, CS ↓; C4S → C6S) ↑ chondroitin 4-sulfate : keratan sulfate
Proteoglycans ↓ synthesis, ↓ size · ↑ decorin ↑ degradation · unbound from HA · aggrecan ↑ early, ↓ late
Collagen ↑ cross-linking (passive glycation) Disorganised (↑ collagenase)
AGEs ↑ (→ ↑ stiffness, brittleness, fatigue failure) AGE accumulation thought to lead to knee/ankle OA

Common to both✓ ↓ proteoglycan content

OA collagen↓ collagen content (TO, FNB 2016) · "relative ↑ collagen concentration from PG loss" (TO second key) — read the options

AGEsnon-enzymatic glycation (glucose, fructose, ribose + lysine/arginine) · pentosidine · cross-link type II collagen · ↓ chondrocyte response to TGF-β, IGF-1, BMP-7

OA stages

Stage Event
1 (early) Matrix disruption → ↑ water (hallmark), ↓ PG aggregation, ↓ aggrecan → ↓ stiffness
2 Chondrocytes detect damage → mediators → ↑ proliferation (clusters/clones) + ↑ matrix synthesis and ↑ catabolism
3 ↓ proliferative + anabolic response → visible cartilage loss → clinical OA

Initiatorsinflammation · overload · ↓ matrix production

Microscopyfibrillation of superficial + transitional zones → tidemark breached by vessels → subchondral remodelling

Late / terminalwater ↓ with degradation

MolecularWnt-induced signal protein 1 ↑ proteases · ↓ response to TGF-α, IGF-1 · ROS, senescence

5Management

Healing

Deep laceration (through tidemark into subchondral bone)haematoma → marrow MSCs → fibrocartilage (type I collagen) · poor wear, ↓ stiffness

Superficial laceration (above tidemark)chondrocyte proliferation but no healing (avascular)

6Complications

Post-traumatic OA & inflammatory mediators

Initiating event✓ chondrocyte apoptosis along superficial matrix cracks

Direct mediator of chondrocyte death✓ caspases (mainly caspase-3) · aspartate-specific cysteine proteases

Cytokines after intra-articular fractureIL-1β, TNF-α, nitric oxide, MMPs, aggrecans, DAMPs

Neutrophil recruitment✓ IL-8 (with IL-6) · IL-6 → MMPs, hypertrophy (STAT3/ERK) · ✗ IL-10 anti-inflammatory · ✗ RANKL osteoclasts

Intervertebral disc degeneration

DDD✓ ↑ MMP-1, ↓ aggrecan/PG, ↓ water → ↓ osmotic pressure → disc height loss · ↓ T2 signal ("dark disc")

Bioabsorbable Materials

1Basics

Why bioabsorbable

Problems solvedmigration · growth disturbance · rigidity · radio-opacity · infection · need for removal

Indicationspaediatrics (transphyseal SR PLGA 80/20 screws → only temporary growth arrest in rabbits) · osteomyelitis (antibiotic-eluting PLA) · carriers for rhBMP-2/-7 · iliac-crest harvest site

Types

Material Properties
PGA Hydrophilic, crystalline · Tg 36 °C · early degradation + strength loss · must be heated/cooled intraop
PLA More hydrophobic than PGA
PLLA (L-isomer) Hydrophobic, crystalline · Tg 57 °C · degrades over years → late reactions
D-isomer PLA Amorphous, less stable · for copolymers
P(L/D)LA Adding D → less tightly packed → less crystalline, faster than PLLA · e.g. 70/30 (maxillofacial)
PLGA (PLA + PGA) Low crystallinity
Self-reinforced (SR) Oriented fibres in matrix (composite) · ↑ rigidity + strength · malleable at room temp · withstands 4× bending · minimal memory · gamma sterilisation OK (✗ non-reinforced — ↓ molecular weight)

Material Time

PLLA >5 yr

PGA 1–2 months

2Mechanism

Biodegradation & excretion

Main mechanismrandom hydrolysis of ester bonds → ↓ molecular weight → loss of strength → CO₂ + H₂O + Krebs (TCA) cycle products

PLA excretionlactic acid → Krebs cycle → ✓ CO₂ via lungs (respiratory) · ✗ hepatic, ✗ renal, ✗ GI

KidneyPGA and PDS (poly-p-dioxanone) products may be renally excreted

Enzymesinvolved in PLA + PGA degradation

↓ pHbreakdown products lower pH → accelerate breakdown

Crystallinityamorphous / hydrophilic → faster · crystalline / hydrophobic → slower

Other variablescomposition, MW · fibre orientation (SR vs simple) · monomer concentration · stereoisomerism · porosity, surface area/volume · sterilisation · enzymatic vs hydrolytic

4Diagnosis & Classification

Histopathology

Granulomatous inflammationT lymphocytes (CD4 > CD8) · plasma cells · endothelial cells · birefringent polymer debris · thin macrophage layer · multinucleated giant cells

Capsuleinner 2–3-cell layer (type III collagen) · outer fibrous layer (type I collagen)

Sequenceneutrophils → CD4 T cells → macrophages last

6Complications

Adverse tissue reactions

Incidence3% children · up to 60% adults

Causedegradation outpaces debris clearance → sterile fluid accumulation · fluctuant papules · synovitis · sinus

Timinglate with slow materials (PLLA ~5 yr)

Labscultures sterile unless secondary infection

X-rayosteolysis in up to 60%

Bioabsorbable implant reaction
  • Mild, sterile→OBSERVE
  • Fluid / sinus→ASPIRATE ± DEBRIDE
    • Sterile implant failure or 2° infection→REMOVE IMPLANT
  • Severe OA→ARTHRODESIS

X vs Y

PGA PLLA
Water affinity Hydrophilic Hydrophobic
Tg 36 °C 57 °C
Absorption 1–2 months >5 yr
Problem Early strength loss Late reactions
Excretion Kidney possible Lungs (CO₂)

How it's asked

"PLA implants excreted after absorption by?"respiratory system (CO₂) (✗ hepatic, ✗ renal — PGA/PDS, ✗ GI)

"Bioabsorbable that degrades fastest?"PGA (1–2 months) · slowest SR PLLA

"Degrade faster — amorphous or crystalline?"amorphous / hydrophilic

Bisphosphonates

Basic Science · 13 / 108

1Basics

Overview

Classstable pyrophosphate analogues · strong affinity for hydroxyapatite (calcium) → concentrate in bone → taken up by osteoclasts during resorption

Net effect✓ inhibit osteoclast bone resorption · ↓ recruitment + activity, ↑ apoptosis · loss of ruffled border (cytoskeleton/microtubules) · ↓ bone turnover · bone formed on therapy is histologically normal

Excretion✓ renal → avoid in severe renal disease

Mechanism (nitrogen vs non-nitrogen containing)

Nitrogen-containing Non-nitrogen (simple)
Drugs Alendronate, risedronate, pamidronate, zoledronate (IV yearly) Etidronate, clodronate, tiludronate
Target Farnesyl pyrophosphate (diphosphate) synthase — mevalonate (cholesterol) pathway Metabolised to non-functional / toxic ATP analogue (replaces terminal pyrophosphate of ATP)
Result ↓ protein prenylation (geranylgeranylation) of small GTPases → osteoclast inactivation/apoptosis Competes with ATP → targets mitochondria → osteoclast apoptosis
Potency Much more potent Earlier, weaker

Traps✗ HMG-CoA reductase (statins) · ✗ caspase · ✗ MMP · ✗ reverse transcriptase · ✗ ↑ osteoblast activity · ✗ ↑ gut Ca · ✗ ↓ PTH

5Management

Clinical uses (OI, fibrous dysplasia, osteolysis, fracture prevention)

Indicationsosteoporosis · metastatic bone disease · multiple myeloma · Paget's · polyostotic fibrous dysplasia · osteolysis after TJA · early AVN · osteogenesis imperfecta · metastatic hypercalcaemia

Use Outcome
Osteoporotic VCF (T-score ≤ −2.5) 1 yr therapy → vertebral fracture risk ↓ 50–60%
Hip fracture risk (N-BP, e.g. alendronate) ↓ ~50% (45–55%)
Non-vertebral fragility fracture ↓ risk of multiple fractures
After hip fracture fixation Resume at 1 week → no effect on outcome (✗ nonunion, cutout, AVN, faster union)
OI — cyclical IV pamidronate ↓ bone pain, ↓ fractures, ✓ ↑ bone density, ↑ ambulation
McCune-Albright / polyostotic FD pain ✓ Bisphosphonates (✗ calcitonin, PTH, vit D, MTX)
Myeloma / metastases (pamidronate, zoledronate) ↓ skeletal events, pain, radiotherapy need · ✗ no survival benefit · monthly IV most effective
TJA osteolysis ✓ ↓ periprosthetic bone loss (alendronate, 6 yr DXA) · may ↑ BMP-2
Precollapse AVN Investigational · RCT collapse at 2 yr 6.9% vs 76% placebo

Contraindications

Severe renal diseaserenal excretion

After lumbar fusionanimal models: ↑ fusion-mass size but ↓ fusion rate

Invasive dental workcomplete before starting bisphosphonates

6Complications

Osteonecrosis of the jaw & dental work

ONJ✓ chronic alendronate / bisphosphonates (✗ warfarin, LMWH, diclofenac, ketorolac)

Risk factorsIV therapy · duration >36 months (length of exposure most important) · elderly myeloma · recent dental extraction · incidence 4–17%

Teriparatideused to treat bisphosphonate ONJ

Atypical femur fractures

Site✓ subtrochanteric / proximal diaphyseal femur · lateral cortical thickening ("beaking") → transverse fracture

ASBMR featuresno/minimal trauma · transverse lateral, oblique medial · periosteal reaction · behaves as stress fracture

Numbers17–29% of subtroch fractures atypical · 28% bilateral → ✓ contralateral films · risk 3.2–50 / 100,000 person-yr → up to 100 with prolonged use

Prodromethigh pain (often misdiagnosed as quadriceps strain)

Incomplete AFF with "dreaded black line"stop bisphosphonate + prophylactic IM fixation

Atypical femur fracture — drug management after nailing (TO)

Denosumab AFF✓ stop denosumab → start teriparatide (anabolic window) → mandatory short-acting bisphosphonate after the anabolic course (TO)

Whydenosumab doesn't embed in matrix → withdrawal = rebound osteoclastogenesis, rapid bone loss, multiple vertebral fractures → bisphosphonate "locks in" bone (TO)

Traps (TO)✗ drug holiday alone (rebound) · ✗ continue denosumab ± teriparatide (suppressed turnover) · ✗ switch to IV zoledronate (keeps turnover paralysed)

AFF nailing technique (TO)

Step Rule (TO)
Implant Full-length cephalomedullary nail (protects neck) · nail with smaller radius of curvature (1.0–1.5 m) vs standard 2.0–3.0 m
Plates ✗ Contraindicated — fatigue failure before union
Entry Lateralised (± slightly posterior) trochanteric start
Reaming ✓ Over-ream ≥1.5–2.0 mm above nail diameter · ✗ line-to-line (incarceration) · sharp reamers, slow, flexible shafts (avoid anterior blowout, thermal necrosis)
Nail size Largest feasible (fatigue life) · ✗ never deliberately under-ream/downsize as routine
Extreme bow Downsize slightly or closing-wedge osteotomy at apex
Biology Pack reamer dust into fracture · no distraction (→ nonunion) — back-slap or compress

Paediatric effects

Osteopetrosis-like radiographic changes in children✓ bisphosphonates (osteoclast is the common pathway) · ✗ RANKL antibody, TNF inhibitor, BMP, fluoroquinolone

X vs Y

Drug Mechanism
Bisphosphonate Inhibits osteoclasts
Denosumab Anti-RANKL antibody · no matrix binding → rebound on withdrawal
Teriparatide PTH analogue → activates osteoblasts (anabolic)
SERM (raloxifene) Oestrogen action on bone → ↓ vertebral fractures
Corticosteroid Cause of osteonecrosis

"Osteonecrosis hip, drug inhibits FPPS in figure?"nitrogen-containing BP

"Medication ↓ osteolysis after TJA?"bisphosphonates

"Restart BP 1 week after hip fracture fixation — effect?"no effect on outcomes

"Which hip fracture needs contralateral films?"atypical (subtrochanteric) femur fracture

"Primary excretion of BPs?"renal

Osteoblasts (Runx2, osteocalcin, RANKL, PTH)

Structure↑ ER, Golgi, mitochondria · cuboidal, line immature osteoid · most active at bone surface

Productsnon-mineralised matrix: type I collagen, alkaline phosphatase, osteonectin, osteocalcin (↑ by 1,25-(OH)₂D)

Most specific marker of mature osteoblast✓ osteocalcin (not in immature, proliferating cells) · ✗ TGF-β, ✗ COL2A1 (chondrocyte), ✗ c-Fos (osteoclast), ✗ IL-1

Differentiation signalsBMP (MSC → osteoprogenitor) · Runx2 · stable β-catenin (→ intramembranous bone) · PDGF · IGF

Signal on osteoblast Effect
PTH receptor ↑ ALP, type I collagen · → RANKL + M-CSF · intermittent rhPTH (1-34) → anabolic · chronic ↑ PTH → resorption
1,25-(OH)₂D receptor ↑ matrix, ALP, osteocalcin
Oestrogen ↓ resorption, ↑ formation (inhibits adenylyl cyclase)
Glucocorticoids ↓ collagen + matrix
Prostaglandins ↑ resorption (activate adenylyl cyclase)

Couplingosteoblast RANKL → RANK on osteoclast precursor → resorption · osteoblast OPG = decoy for RANKL → ↓ osteoclasts

Jagged1–Notch✓ PTH induces Jagged1 on osteoblasts → Notch on HSCs → HSC proliferation (immune/haematopoietic regulation)

Osteoclasts (origin, cathepsin K, integrins, RGD)

Appearancemultinucleated giant cell, monocyte lineage, ruffled border · sits in Howship's lacunae

DifferentiationM-CSF + RANKL (progenitors express RANK) · also PU.1, CSF-1

ActivatorsRANKL (osteoblasts, tumour cells) · IL-1 (next to loose TJA implants)

Inhibitorscalcitonin · IL-10 · OPG

Acid phosphatase✓ osteoclast (TRAP) (TO) · ✗ osteoblast · ✗ osteocyte

Step Molecule
Attachment (sealing zone) Integrins — αVβ3 = vitronectin receptor · bind RGD (Arg-Gly-Asp) sequence on vitronectin, fibronectin, osteopontin · reversible binding · anti-αVβ3/RGD block resorption
Polarisation Ruffled border · functional secretory domain · basolateral membrane
Mineral dissolution HCl into resorption lacuna · ATP proton pumps · H⁺ from carbonic anhydrase II · Cl⁻ channels · CA deficiency → no resorption
Organic matrix Cathepsin K (major) · MMP-9 · lysosomal cysteine proteinases
Removal Transcytosis to FSD · TRAP in vesicles → reactive O₂ species destroy collagen
End Apoptosis or return to non-resorbing state

Cathepsin Kfunction = ✓ bone resorption · mutation → pycnodysostosis · inhibitors studied for osteoporosis

Bisphosphonatesblock ruffled border + acid hydrolase production

Disuse↓ integrin expression → disuse osteoporosis

Osteocytes

Originformer osteoblasts trapped in their own matrix · 90% of cells in mature skeleton

Structurehigh nucleus:cytoplasm · long processes through canaliculi → gap junctions (connexins; cAMP, IP3 pass) · radial around Haversian canal

Functionmaintain bone matrix · Ca/P regulation · ✗ no ALP

Signalsstimulated by calcitonin, inhibited by PTH

Structure Role
Canaliculi ✓ Osteocyte–osteocyte communication
Haversian canal Centre of osteon · vessels + nerves
Volkmann canal Perpendicular · links Haversian canals + periosteum
Cement line Boundary of osteon

Bone Circulation

Basic Science · 15 / 108

1Basics

Overview

Share of cardiac output5–10% · intraosseous flow 5–20 mL/min/100 g

Tenuous supplyscaphoid · talus · femoral head · odontoid

Three sources (long bone)nutrient artery · metaphyseal–epiphyseal · periosteal

Blood supply to physis & diaphysis

System Pressure Supplies
Nutrient artery High Enters via nutrient foramen → medullary canal → ascending/descending → inner ⅔ of mature cortex (Haversian)
Metaphyseal–epiphyseal — From periarticular plexus (e.g. geniculates)
Periosteal arterioles Low Outer ⅓ of diaphyseal cortex · linked by Volkmann (perpendicular) + Haversian (parallel)
Perichondrial artery (of LaCroix) — ✓ Major source of nutrition of growth plate
Epiphyseal artery — Proliferative zone of physis

Emissary systemvenous only · ✗ no "emissary artery"

Direction of flow

Arterial Venous
Mature bone Centrifugal (inside → out; nutrient high-pressure dominates) Centripetal (cortical capillaries → sinusoids → emissary veins)
Immature bone Centripetal (periosteal dominates) —

↑ Bone blood flowhypoxia · hypercapnia · sympathectomy

2Mechanism

After fracture

Phase Flow
Immediate ↓ flow · becomes centripetal (nutrient system disrupted, periosteal dominates)
Hours–days ↑ flow (regional acceleratory phenomenon) · peaks 2 wk · normal by 3–5 months

Intramedullary nails

Unreamed nailpreserves endosteal supply

Reamingdevascularises inner 50–80% of cortex · delays endosteal revascularisation

Loose-fitting nailspares cortical perfusion, faster reperfusion · tight-fitting → compromised, slow

How it's asked

"Disruption interrupts major nutrient supply to growth plate?"perichondrial artery (✗ diaphyseal, metaphyseal, synovial)

"Supplies outer third of diaphysis?"periosteal arterioles (✗ nutrient — inner ⅔, ✗ emissary artery — doesn't exist, ✗ perichondral — physis)

"Direction of arterial flow in mature bone?"centrifugal · immature → centripetal

"Immediately after fracture, flow becomes?"↓ and centripetal

Bone Grafting

Basic Science · 16 / 108

1Basics

Overview

Volume~1 million grafting procedures/yr in the US · growth ~12–13%/yr

Indicationsfractures, delayed union, nonunion · arthrodesis, spinal fusion · defects after trauma/tumour

Graft properties (osteogenic, osteoinductive, osteoconductive)

Property Definition Example
Osteoconductive Scaffold — permits ingrowth of capillaries, perivascular tissue, osteoprogenitors → creeping substitution Allograft, ceramics, DBM
Osteoinductive Factors that recruit + differentiate MSCs into osteoblasts (bind transmembrane receptors) BMP (TGF-β superfamily), DBM
Osteogenic Living bone-forming cells — MSCs, osteoblasts, osteocytes Fresh autograft, bone marrow aspirate only
Osseointegration Direct bone–implant contact (Brånemark) · ✗ not a graft property (TO) Ca-phosphate-coated stems

Most common / most important property (TO)✓ osteoconduction

Live mesenchymal precursor cells✓ only autograft / bone marrow aspirate (✗ fresh-frozen allograft, ✗ rhBMP, ✗ DBM, ✗ Ca phosphate)

Cancellous > cortical for new bone — larger surface area

Material Conductive Inductive Osteogenic
Autograft (ICBG, RIA, local cancellous/cortical) ✓ ✓ ✓
Bone marrow aspirate ✗ (no structure) ✓ (OB) ✓ (TO: osteogenic potential)
DBM ✓ ✓ ✗
BMP (rhBMP-2, -7) ✗ ✓ only ✗
PRP ✗ "solely osteoinductive" (Orthobullets/OB) · TO: mitogenic + angiogenic only, not true osteoinduction ✗
Allograft (freeze-dried, cortical, cancellous, fibular strut) ✓ only ✗ (fresh/fresh-frozen retain some BMP) ✗
Ceramics (Ca phosphate, sulfate, carbonate, TCP, HA), polymers ✓ only ✗ ✗

Most osteoinductive (of listed)cancellous autograft

Diamond concept & graft biology in nonunion (TO)

Giannoudis Diamond (4 pillars)osteoconductive scaffold · osteoinductive mediators · osteogenic cells · mechanical stability (TO)

HA/β-TCP + rhBMP-2 + LR-PRP + locking platedeficient in ✓ osteogenesis (no viable MSCs) → add autograft or BMAC (TO)

rhBMP-2serine/threonine kinase receptors → SMAD → MSC → osteoblast (TO)

Antigenicity

Antigensclass I + II MHC on graft cells → host T cells · marrow cells most immunogenic · endothelium · type I collagen (humoral + cell-mediated) · non-collagenous matrix

Hydroxyapatiteno immune response

Rejectioncell-mediated, MHC incompatibility · greater mismatch → less new bone

Ligament allograft antigenicityrelated to bone > soft tissue → BPTB for ACL = highest (also meniscal bone plugs)

2Mechanism

Graft healing stages

Stage Event
1 Inflammation Necrotic debris → chemotaxis
2 Osteoblast differentiation From mesenchymal precursors
3 Osteoinduction Osteoblast + osteoclast stimulation
4 Osteoconduction Bone forms on scaffold
5 Remodelling Years

Types of autograft

Type Facts
Cancellous Little structure · ↑ osteoconduction · rapid incorporation by creeping substitution
Cortical Slow (remodel existing Haversian canals) · structural · interstitial lamellae preserved
Vascularised Faster union, cells preserved · free fibula (peroneal a.), free iliac crest (deep circumflex iliac a.), distal radius 1,2-ICSRA for scaphoid

Structural allograft biology & outcomes

Incorporationparticulate > structural · massive cortical graft largely never remodels, stays devascularised

Insufficiency fracture15–30% (~25%) of massive structural allografts

Retrieved bulk allograft (10 yr)junction heals by bridging external callus + persistent callus perpendicular to junction · little penetration (✗ osteonal/Haversian remodelling, ✗ complete incorporation)

Osteoarticular allograft cartilage at 5 yrcompletely acellular (no donor or host chondrocytes)

Meniscal allograftsuccess depends most on accurate sizing (within 5%) · ✗ donor cell viability (repopulated by host) · ✗ immunosuppression · ✗ cryopreservation

5Management

Types of allograft & processing

Allograft Immunogenicity BMP Disease transmission Shelf life
Fresh Highest Preserved Highest Hours–days
Fresh-frozen Less Preserved (inductive) Intermediate · stronger — better structural −20 °C: 1–2 yr · −70 °C: up to 5 yr
Freeze-dried ("croutons") Least Depleted (purely conductive) Lowest (theoretical HIV 1 : 2.6 billion) Indefinite (5 yr–∞)

Shelf life orderfreeze-dried > fresh-frozen −70 °C > fresh-frozen −20 °C > fresh

Processingdebride soft tissue → ethanol wash (removes cells) → gamma irradiation (dose-dependent: kills pathogens but ↓ mechanical properties)

Method Process
Cryopreservation ✓ Chemicals (DMSO/glycerol) displace cellular water + controlled-rate freezing → no ice crystals · keeps some chondrocytes (osteoarticular)
Freeze-drying Water → alcohol to 5% moisture → vacuum (sublimation) · alters growth-factor proteins
Fresh-frozen Frozen twice, packaged dry at −80 °C
Fresh Not frozen · max viability, short shelf life

DBM vs freeze-dried allograftDBM additionally osteoinductive (factors bind transmembrane receptors on MSCs) · neither is osteogenic

DBMacid extraction of allograft → removes mineral, keeps collagen + non-collagenous proteins (BMPs, TGF-β, IGF, FGF, residual Ca) · no structural support · inter-lot variability

Bone graft substitutes (calcium sulfate, calcium phosphate, HA)

All ceramicsosteoconductive void fillers · scaffold for progenitor cells · low shear, tensile, torsional strength · biocompatible with steel + titanium · used in spinal fusion

Resorption (fastest → slowest)calcium sulfate > tricalcium phosphate > hydroxyapatite

Substitute Resorption Strength Notes
Calcium sulfate (plaster of Paris, OsteoSet) 4–12 wk (5–7 wk; gone by 6 wk) · passive dissolution · faster than bone grows ≈ cancellous Sterile serous wound drainage (osmotic load) (TO) · fails alone in nonunion
β-TCP 6–18 months · cell (osteoclast)-mediated (TO) · dissolution + fragmentation ≤ cancellous · brittle Less bone formed than resorbed → used as adjunct
Calcium phosphate cement ~1 yr (1–10 yr) Highest compressive — 4–10× cancellous Hardens to dahllite · early WB without subsidence (plateau, calcaneus) · osseointegration + osteoconduction (stem coatings) · reduce + fix before injecting
Coralline HA (Interpore) Very slow (decades) Slightly > cancellous Coral CaCO₃ → HA by hydrothermal exchange · neovascularisation + appositional bone (✗ creeping substitution, ✗ dissolution)
Calcium carbonate (Biocoral) — — Unaltered coral · conductive only
Collagen matrix + HA coating Collagen fast, HA slow < cancellous —
Aluminium oxide — — Bonds to bone under stress/strain

Level I evidenceCa-phosphate fills defects, allows early rehab, prevents articular subsidence (distal radius, tibial plateau)

Ca-phosphate cure (TO)isothermal / mildly exothermic · ✗ PMMA-type thermal necrosis

HOcomplication of BMP, ✗ not calcium sulfate (TO)

Bone morphogenetic proteins

rhBMP-2, rhBMP-7FDA-approved, long bones + spine · solely osteoinductive · perivascular MSC → osteoblast via serine-threonine kinase receptors

Complicationsunder/over-production of bone · inflammation · early bone resorption · near-zero disease transmission (synthetic)

Autograft harvest (iliac crest, RIA)

Autograftgold standard · all three properties · least immunogenic

Iliac crestcommonest site · cortical + cancellous · complications 2–36%

Anterior vs posterioranterior = more complications (pain, gait) · posterior = more stem cells (viability same), less pain

Structure at risk Where
Superior cluneal nerves — commonest posterior complication (buttock numbness) Cross crest >8 cm lateral to PSIS → ✓ stay within 8 cm (TO)
Superior gluteal artery Retractor/elevator slipping caudally into sciatic notch · brisk bleeding (✗ vein, ✗ inferior gluteal)
SI joint posterior ligaments Full-thickness posterior graft → SI instability/dislocation
LFCN, ilioinguinal nerve Anterior crest
Deep circumflex iliac artery Anterior crest
Corona mortis Anterior intrapelvic approaches
External iliac artery ✓ Not at risk (TO)

Other complicationshaematoma · hernia · infection · fracture · cosmetic defect · chronic pain

Vascularised autograft sourcesfibula, ribs

Reamer–irrigator–aspirator (RIA)

RIA vs ICBG
Volume Higher (~50–90 mL per femur)
Union Comparable (~90%)
Operative time Shorter · technically easier
Immediate complications Fewer
Blood loss Higher → transfusion (up to 44%)
Structural support Less (fragments ≤2 mm)
Equipment More expensive
Biology All three properties · >95% cell viability · ≥ ICBG growth factors/MSCs · similar RNA transcription profile
Specific risks Femoral shaft fracture (eccentric reaming) · insertion-site (abductor) pain

RIA false statement (TO)✗ "heat injury from reaming abolishes osteoinductive properties" · RIA is a viable alternative to cancellous autograft, product of long-bone nailing, osteogenic (TO)

Overview of growth factors

Factor Source Receptor Function
TGF-β Platelets, bone ECM, cartilage Serine-threonine kinase Pleiotropic · undifferentiated MSC proliferation · type II collagen + PG by MSCs · osteoblast collagen synthesis · in fracture haematoma · coats porous implants
BMP Osteoprogenitors, osteoblasts, bone ECM Serine-threonine kinase → SMADs ✓ MSC → chondrocytes + osteoblasts · skeletal patterning
FGF (FGF-1, -2 most abundant) Macrophages, MSCs, chondrocytes, osteoblasts Tyrosine kinase Mitogenic · angiogenesis · early fracture healing
IGF-1 (somatomedin C) Bone matrix, osteoblasts, chondrocytes Tyrosine kinase Most abundant GF in bone · GH–IGF-1 → physeal proliferation without maturation (linear growth) · intramembranous defects
IGF-2 — Tyrosine kinase More potent than IGF-1 · type I collagen, cartilage matrix, proliferation, bone
PDGF Platelets, osteoblasts Tyrosine kinase Mitogen · macrophage/inflammatory-cell chemotaxis to fracture
PPAR-γ — Nuclear receptor (binds DNA) Adipogenic differentiation
Wnt — LRP5/6 → β-catenin to nucleus Osteoblast differentiation

Thyroid hormonenuclear receptor → physeal proliferation and maturation

Morphogens vs mitogens (disc)BMP-2, BMP-7, TGF-β change phenotype (↑ chondrogenic phenotype + matrix) without proliferation · IGF-1, FGF ↑ proliferation

BMP signalling (SMADs, serine-threonine kinase)

FamilyTGF-β superfamily (with GDFs, AMH, activin, Nodal) · ~30 members

Targetundifferentiated perivascular MSCs

CascadeBMP → heterodimer of type I + II serine-threonine kinase receptors → phosphorylate SMAD 1, 5, 8 → complex with SMAD 4 → nucleus → osteoblast genes · 8 SMADs known

BMP Fact
BMP-2, 4, 6, 7 Osteoinductive (BMP-2 potent osteogenic + osteoinductive)
BMP-3 ✗ Not osteoinductive · antagonises BMP-2 · most abundant BMP in DBM · knockout → 2× trabecular bone · ↑ chondrogenic markers (type II collagen, aggrecan)
BMP-1 Metalloprotease (protease for collagen I, II, III) · ✗ not bone-inducing
BMP-4 Altered signalling → fibrodysplasia ossificans progressiva

Lineage factors

Factor Drives

Bone Remodeling

Basic Science · 19 / 108

1Basics

Remodelling laws & cortical vs cancellous turnover

Law Statement
Wolff's law Bone remodels/adapts to the loads (mechanical stress) placed on it · ↑ load → stronger · ↓ load → osteopenia · osteocytes sense load
Davis's law Soft tissues heal according to how they are mechanically stressed (TO)
Hueter–Volkmann Compression on physis slows growth, tension/distraction accelerates (immature bone)
Delpech's law Tension on physis speeds growth (TO)
Piezoelectric Compression side electronegative → osteoblasts (formation) · tension side electropositive → osteoclasts
Maxwell's law ✗ Not a physeal law (TO)

Law governing remodelling (TO)✓ both Wolff's and Davis's (bone and soft tissue)

Remodelling mechanism

Basic multicellular unit (BMU, Frost)osteoclasts + osteoblasts + osteocytes in the remodelling cavity

Cortical bone Cancellous bone
Mechanism Osteoclastic tunnelling — cutting cone (head = osteoclasts → capillaries → osteoblasts fill = closing cone → cement line) Osteoclastic resorption on trabecular surface + osteoblastic lamellae
Cycle (TO) Shorter — median ~120 days ~200 days

Turnover extremes (TO)~100 days in thyrotoxicosis, 1° hyperparathyroidism · >1,000 days in myxoedema and after bisphosphonates (slower, ✗ "faster")

Sclerostin & Wnt pathway

Sclerostinglycoprotein from osteocytes · ✓ inhibits osteoblastogenesis → ↓ bone formation

Sclerostin + Dkk-1block Wnt binding to LRP5/6 → ✓ inhibit Wnt/β-catenin pathway

Wnt onβ-catenin not phosphorylated → accumulates → nucleus → osteoblast genes · also ↑ OPG

Knockout / inhibition of sclerostin or Dkk-1↑ bone mass

Age-related changes

Ageing cortex↓ cortical thickness/area, ↑ medullary canal volume (bone volume constant) → ↑ fracture risk

2Mechanism

Calcium, vitamin D & calcitonin

SunlightUV converts 7-dehydrocholesterol → cholecalciferol (D3) in skin → liver 25-OH → kidney 1,25-(OH)₂D

↓ Sun exposure✓ impaired GI calcium absorption (no 1,25-D) · liver/kidney hydroxylation intact · PTH ↑ (✗ ↓)

Phosphate ↓ urinary Ca✓ complexes with Ca in the intestine (less absorbed) · also ↑ PTH, complexes in bone/soft tissue (↓ filtered load)

Calcitonin✓ interferes with osteoclast maturation (receptor on osteoclasts) · ↓ renal Ca + phosphate reabsorption · chondroprotective (MMP inhibition) · ✗ no osteoblast receptor · ✗ no major gut Ca effect · used in Paget's when bisphosphonate-intolerant

Anabolic therapy — intermittent PTH

Direct anabolic✓ intermittent PTH (✗ bisphosphonate, calcitonin — antiresorptive · ✗ corticosteroid · ✗ TNF-α inhibitor)

Continuous ↑ PTHbone resorption

TNF-α↓ osteoblasts, ↑ osteoclastic resorption

Excess cortisol↓ gut Ca absorption, ↑ renal Ca loss, ↓ matrix formation, secondary hyperparathyroidism

Bone healing

Rigid compression plating (absolute stability)✓ primary healing by Haversian remodelling (osteonal cutting cones cross fracture) · no visible callus

Relative stability (IM nail)secondary healing, callus, endochondral ossification

5Management

Hueter–Volkmann principle

Clinical usestension-band (8-) plate guided growth · scoliosis progression (concave side compressed → slower growth) · Scheuermann extension brace (anterior bodies in tension → growth corrects wedging)

X vs Y

Wolff Hueter–Volkmann
Governs Remodelling (mature + immature) Longitudinal growth (immature physis)
Stimulus Intermittent ↑ stress → apposition Sustained compression → growth suppression
Primary healing Secondary healing
Stability Absolute (compression plate) Relative (nail, cast)
Mechanism Haversian / cutting cones Callus, endochondral

How it's asked

"Direct anabolic effect on bone?"intermittent PTH (✗ bisphosphonate, calcitonin, corticosteroid, TNF inhibitor)

"Inhibits osteoblastogenesis to ↓ bone formation?"sclerostin (✗ RANKL, IL-1, Wnt, OPG)

"Sclerostin + Dkk-1 inhibit?"Wnt/β-catenin

"Physeal growth modulation by compression/tension (TO)?"Hueter–Volkmann

"Law NOT pertaining to growth plate (TO, FNB 2023)?"Maxwell's law

"Scheuermann extension brace corrects wedging by?"Hueter–Volkmann principle (✗ Hooke, Kirchhoff, Wolff)

"Tension-band plate relies on?"Hueter–Volkmann (✗ cutting cones, ✗ Wolff, ✗ piezoelectric)

"Phosphate ↓ urinary Ca by?"intestinal Ca–phosphate complex

"↓ Sun exposure harms bone by?"↓ GI calcium absorption

"Calcitonin mechanism (Paget's)?"interferes with osteoclast maturation

"Rigid compression plating — healing type?"primary, Haversian remodelling

"25-yr-old vs 85-yr-old femur?"thicker cortex, smaller canal

"Law governing remodelling (TO)?"both Wolff's and Davis's

"True about remodelling (TO)?"cortical cycle shorter than cancellous (✗ cutting cones in cancellous, ✗ bisphosphonates speed remodelling)

Bone Signaling & RANKL

Basic Science · 20 / 108

1Basics

RANK–RANKL–OPG axis

Central pathwaycouples osteoblast and osteoclast activity · resorption stimulated by RANKL (normal) · PTHrP (pathological — metastases)

RANKLmajor source = ✓ osteoblasts (+ marrow stromal cells, activated T cells, osteocytes) · binds RANK on osteoclast precursors + mature osteoclasts · RANKL + M-CSF → fusion into multinucleated osteoclasts · secreted in response to inflammatory stimuli · TNF superfamily

OPG (osteoprotegerin)decoy receptor from osteoblasts + stromal cells · ✓ binds and sequesters RANKL → ↓ osteoclast differentiation, fusion, activation

Primary stimulus for osteoclastic resorption✓ RANK–RANKL interaction (✗ PTH–osteoclast direct · ✗ OPG–RANKL)

Osteoclast transcription factorsPU.1, NF-κB, AP-1, NFATc1, Mitf, Myc, Src · DC-STAMP required for fusion

Osteoclast activators vs inhibitors

Stimulate resorption Mechanism
RANKL Binds RANK
PTH Receptor on osteoblasts → adenylyl cyclase/cAMP/PKA → ↑ RANKL + M-CSF, ↓ OPG
IL-1 ↑ osteoclast differentiation
1,25-(OH)₂D ↑ RANKL expression · terminal osteoclast differentiation
PGE2 Activates adenylyl cyclase
IL-6, MIP-1α Myeloma
TNF-α ↑ resorption (✗ not an inhibitor)
Thyroid hormone, GH Systemic stimulators
Inhibit resorption Mechanism
OPG Sequesters RANKL
Calcitonin ✓ Binds directly to receptor on osteoclast → loss of ruffled border, retraction, ↓ number + activity · from thyroid parafollicular cells · ↑ by hypercalcaemia, gastrin, pentagastrin · on osteocytes: ↓ apoptosis, ↑ sclerostin
Oestrogen ↓ RANKL · inhibits adenylyl cyclase · also anabolic
TGF-β ↑ OPG
IL-10 Suppresses osteoclasts
Androgens Systemic
Bisphosphonates Osteoclast apoptosis
Denosumab Anti-RANKL IgG2 (OPG-like)

Dexamethasone / corticosteroids↓ OPG → ↑ osteoclast formation + longevity → osteoporosis

Wnt/β-catenin signalling (TO)

Wntbinds frizzled + LRP5/6 → β-catenin accumulates → binds lymphoid enhancer factor (LEF) → bone-formation genes + OPG (TO)

Wnt antagonistsDkk-1, soluble frizzled-related proteins (sFRP), sclerostin (SOST) (TO)

Favour formation (TO) Favour resorption (TO) Coupling factors (TO)
OPG, BMPs, Wnt, β-catenin–LEF complex RANK, RANKL, M-CSF, DC-STAMP, Dkk-1, sFRP, sclerostin TGF-β (released + activated by acid during resorption) · ephrinB2 (osteoclast) → EphB4 (osteoblast)

Does NOT inhibit bone formation (TO)✓ β-catenin–LEF complex (✗ sclerostin, Dkk-1, RANKL)

BMP-2 cascadereceptor phosphorylates regulatory Smad 1, 5, 8 → complex with ✓ co-Smad 4 (not phosphorylated) → nucleus

Smad 6, 7inhibitory · Smad 2, 3 — TGF-β pathway

Second messengers

Adenylyl cyclase / cAMP✓ PTH (Gαs/PKA in osteocytes) · PGE2

Drug Target

4Diagnosis & Classification

Genes & lab markers

Alkaline phosphatasefrom bone (osteoblasts) + liver · bone ↑: Paget's, metastases, osteomalacia · liver ↑: hepatitis, cirrhosis, liver metastases, biliary obstruction

↑ ALP, negative bone scan, known cancer✓ check ALT/AST (liver) (✗ CEA, ✗ PSA, ✗ CT hip, ✗ MRI brain)

6Complications

Clinical implications

Condition RANKL-pathway link
Osteoporosis Loss of OPG function → constitutive osteoclast activation · OPG knockout mice → osteoporosis
Osteopetrosis Absent osteoclastic resorption · RANKL knockout → osteopetrosis-like · ✗ not OPG knockout
Paget's Altered RANK cytoplasmic binding or OPG mutations
Lytic metastases ✓ Cancer cells directly produce RANKL · OPG blockade ↓ metastasis (animals) · denosumab ↓ pathological fractures
TJA osteolysis PE debris → macrophages → cytokines incl. RANKL → osteoclasts
Inflammatory arthropathy (RA pannus, gout tophi) IL-6, IL-8, TNF-α · ↑ RANKL, ↓ OPG

OPG overexpression would help✓ osteoporosis (also Paget's, metastases) · ✗ osteopetrosis, rickets, achondroplasia, scurvy

RANKL OPG RANK
Made by Osteoblasts, T cells, tumour Osteoblasts, stromal cells — (receptor)
Located Ligand Soluble decoy Osteoclast precursors
Effect ↑ resorption ↓ resorption Mediates differentiation
Knockout Osteopetrosis-like Osteoporosis —

How it's asked

"How OPG affects RANKL-mediated resorption?"inhibits by binding RANKL directly (✗ binds RANK)

"Major source of RANKL?"osteoblasts (✗ osteoclasts, ✗ integrins, ✗ macrophages)

"Lytic breast metastasis in humerus — involved molecule?"RANK (✗ OPG, IL-4, TNF-α, Sox9)

"True of OPG?"binds and sequesters RANKL (✗ from osteoclasts, ✗ ↑ resorption, ✗ knockout → osteopetrosis)

"Why metastases are lytic?"cancer cells directly produce RANKL (✗ osteoclasts produce RANKL)

"Cells producing RANKL?"osteoblasts

"Osteoclastogenic factor secreted by osteoblasts after inflammation?"RANKL

"Primary stimulus for osteoclastic resorption?"RANK–RANKL interaction

"Function of OPG?"sequesters RANKL (✗ adenylyl cyclase — oestrogen, ✗ binds osteoclasts — calcitonin, ✗ ↑ RANKL — PTH)

"Drug causing OPG overexpression helps?"osteoporosis

"Mediator reducing bone resorption?"calcitonin (✗ RANKL, IL-6, PTH, dexamethasone)

"Calcitonin mechanism?"directly binds osteoclast receptor → ↓ activity

"Osteoporosis drug binding osteoclasts directly?"calcitonin (✗ denosumab, romosozumab, teriparatide, vitamin D)

"All inhibit osteoclasts EXCEPT?"TNF-α

"Uses adenylyl cyclase in osteocytes?"PTH (✗ SMADs, IGF-II, BMP-2, TGF-β)

"↑ ALP, negative bone scan, metastatic lung cancer — next test?"ALT/AST

"Complexes with Smad 1/5/8?"Smad 4 (✗ Smad 6/7 — inhibitory)

"Does NOT inhibit bone formation (TO)?"β-catenin–LEF complex

Cartilage

Basic Science · 23 / 108

1Basics

Types of cartilage

Type Location Collagen Function
Hyaline (articular) Articular surfaces, ribs, nasal septum Type II ↓ friction, distributes load
Fibrocartilage Tendon/ligament–bone junction, pubic symphysis, annulus fibrosus, menisci Type I (predominantly) Compressive strength · repair tissue after microfracture, drilling, abrasion arthroplasty
Fibroelastic Menisci — Fewer PGs + glycoproteins than hyaline
Elastic Auricle, epiglottis, auditory tube Type II + elastin network Tolerates repetitive deformation
Epiphyseal (physeal) Between epiphysis + metaphysis — Linear growth (endochondral ossification) · remodelling potential

Physeal zonesreserve → proliferative → hypertrophic → primary spongiosa → secondary spongiosa · ECM holds PGs + GFs (BMP-2)

Cartilage biology

Propertiesavascular, aneural, alymphatic · nutrients by diffusion from synovial fluid · pH 7.4 · metabolism regulated mechanically

Composition (hyaline)water (65–80%) > collagen > PG > non-collagenous protein > cells · stress-shielding of solid matrix

Loadingphysiological = chondroprotective · underloading → thinning, softening, ↓ PG → fibrillation, erosion · overloading → damage (in vitro only)

SOX9 & chondrocyte differentiation

SOX9✓ "master switch" transcription factor for chondrocytes · HMG superfamily · binds type II collagen promoter/enhancer · ↑ type II, IX, XI collagen, aggrecan, link protein · ✗ not a tyrosine kinase receptor

Matrix components (aggrecan, type II & IX collagen)

Type II collagen✓ tensile strength + reinforcement of PG gel · restrains swelling · superficial-zone collagen bears ~20% of load · degradation → fibrillation

Aggrecannegative GAG charge (chondroitin, keratan sulfate) → ↑ osmolality → attracts water → compressive strength

↓ Aggrecan expression✓ ↓ water content (and ↓ osmolality, ↓ compressive strength) · no direct AGE/collagen change

Pericellular integrin anchorsfibronectin, type VI collagen

Genes & lab markers

OA linkage in women✓ type IX collagen (400-family study · knockout mice → early OA) · also ADAMTS-5 (aggrecanase)

PTH effect on cartilage

PTHchondroprotective · ✓ ↓ cartilage degradation · via Indian hedgehog (IHH) signalling · synergy with bFGF, IGF

Traps✗ ↑ MMP-13 (degradation) · ✗ ↑ type X collagen (end-stage hypertrophic/OA) · ✗ ↓ PG

X vs Y

Hyaline Fibrocartilage Elastic
Collagen II I II + elastin
Example Joint surface Annulus, symphysis, meniscus, enthesis Ear, epiglottis

How it's asked

"SOX9 drives differentiation of?"chondrocytes (✗ osteoclasts, osteoblasts, fibroblasts)

"SOX9 regulates which tissue (histology)?"cartilage (✗ bone, fat, muscle, nerve)

"Strong genetic linkage with OA in women?"type IX collagen

"Downregulate aggrecan genes → effect?"↓ water content

"Primary biomechanical function of type II collagen?"tensile strength, reinforcing PG matrix (✗ osmotic swelling — PGs)

"PTH effect on cartilage?"↓ degradation (chondroprotective)
