-22 Unusually complicated (difficulty, time, effort)
-24 Unrelated E&M in the postoperative period
-25 Significant, separate E&M on the day of a (minor) procedure
-58 ✓ Staged / planned / more extensive procedure in global period · restarts global · 100% paid (e.g. spacer removal + graft, 2nd-stage PJI)
-59 Distinct procedural service, same encounter (different site, incision, lesion)
-76 Repeat procedure
-78 Unplanned return to OR for related condition/complication in global period
-79 Unrelated procedure in global period · overlapping globals · 100% paid

5Management

New vs established patient

New patientno professional services from the physician or anyone in the same group and same specialty within 3 years

Establishedseen by physician or same-group same-specialty partner within 3 years

Exceptionhand + sports surgeons with a CAQ may bill patients seen by other group orthopaedists as new

Consultationrequested by another physician for advice (✗ transfer of care) · request documented · level documented · written response to requester · no 3-yr rule · Medicare no longer recognises consult codes

Coding error rate32.4% of Medicare claims (Shalowitz) · consult errors common

E&M coding & global period

Component-based levelhistory + examination + MDM · billed at the lowest of the three

Time-basedvisit mostly counselling/coordination · time documented

Global period0, 10 or 90 days · single payment

In the 90-day global Not in the global
Preop visits/work, marking, positioning, prep Diagnostic tests (e.g. POD1 CBC)
Skin-to-skin work Immobilisation applied outside OR
Immobilisation/bracing applied in OR Visits for separate problems
Inpatient + postop visits (physician or extender) Evaluation by a different group
Closed fracture "restorative treatment" follow-up (code 99024, $0) Reoperation for complications (infection) · staged procedures

Fraudservices not provided · not provided by billing physician · not supported by documentation · intentional or not · responsibility = billing physician

How it's asked

"Which scenario is a new patient (2009 guidelines)?"arthroscopy by partner 4 years ago (✗ seen <3 yr by you or partner)

"Time to revert to new patient?"3 years

"Referred with consult request, partner saw 4 yr ago, history L3/exam L4/MDM L3, letter sent?"level 3 consultation

"Cast removal 4 wk after closed reduction of phalanx fracture?"not billed (90-day global)

"ICD-10 seventh character?"phase of treatment

"Planned second-stage spacer removal + graft at 6 wk — modifier?"58 (✗ 78 — unplanned, ✗ 79 — unrelated, ✗ 59, ✗ 25)

Clinical Trial Design

Basic Science · 25 / 108

1Basics

Observational vs experimental

Clinical study
  • Observational (no allocation)→DESCRIPTIVE or ANALYTIC
    • Descriptive→CASE REPORT · CASE SERIES · CROSS-SECTIONAL
    • Analytic→CASE-CONTROL · COHORT · META-ANALYSIS
  • Experimental (researcher allocates)→RCT
    • Double-blind prospective RCT→GOLD STANDARD

Internal validitymethodological rigour (↓ bias, confounding) · external validity — generalisability · homogeneous, tightly controlled populations ↑ internal, ↓ external

Study types (case-control, cohort, parallel, factorial, RCT)

Design Definition Level Measure
RCT Random assignment to treatment/control, followed prospectively I — · best for causality
Prospective cohort Groups separated non-randomly by exposure, exposure after study start II Relative risk, incidence
Retrospective cohort Exposure before study start III Relative risk
Case-control Groups by presence (cases) / absence (controls) of disease → look back for exposure · always retrospective III (most) Odds ratio
Case series Retrospective, same injury/treatment, no control · generates hypotheses IV —
Cross-sectional Snapshot at one time, no follow-up (e.g. prevalence) — Prevalence
Meta-analysis Systematic review pooling studies · ↑ power of under-powered studies Level of included studies —
Case report / expert opinion — V —

RCT with 70% follow-updowngraded to Level II (<80% follow-up)

RCT designs

Design Allocation
Parallel Each group gets one intervention (commonest RCT) · e.g. bisphosphonate vs placebo
Crossover Each participant gets both therapies sequentially in random order · needs adequate washout
Factorial Groups get A + B, A, B or neither → tests interaction
Cluster Pre-existing groups (schools, villages) randomised

2Mechanism

Randomisation & selection bias

Purpose of computer randomisation✓ prevent selection bias (unbiased placement into groups) · ✗ ↑ power, ✗ ↑ treatment effect, ✗ significance, ✗ prevent crossover

Sealed opaque envelopes / allocation concealmentminimise selection bias (recruiters can't predict next allocation)

Randomisation alsodistributes confounders (known + unknown) evenly → ✓ best reduces confounding · facilitates blinding · allows probability theory

Confoundersexternal factors affecting both predictor and outcome · controlled by randomisation, stratification, multivariable regression

Blinding & other biases

Single-blindpatients unaware, surgeon delivers + assesses (experimenter bias remains)

Double-blindpatient + doctor/assessor unaware

Blinding the outcome assessor✓ ↓ reported treatment effect (unblinded assessors inflate effect — Poolman, 32 RCTs)

Bias Definition
Selection Dissimilar groups / non-random enrolment · prevented by randomisation + concealment
Washout period Crossover — first therapy's effect persists (drug t½ long)
Recall Subjects' recollection differs by outcome or after unblinding · common in retrospective/survey studies
Observer-expectancy Investigator finds what they expect · prevented by masking
Procedure Groups treated differently beyond the intervention (e.g. extra PT)
Attrition Patients don't complete study
Detection Look harder for outcome in one group
Verification Test result influences group assignment
Incorporation Diagnostic test includes features of target outcome
Confirmation Interpretation skewed by researcher's opinion
Sampling Homogeneous population → ↓ generalisability
Publication Unfavourable results unpublished (industry funding)
Hawthorne effect Behaviour changes when observed

Intention-to-treat analysis

Analysis Who is analysed
Intention-to-treat As randomised, regardless of treatment received/compliance · preserves randomisation · ↓ non-responder bias
As-treated By treatment actually received · no longer an RCT · used with high crossover
Per-protocol Excludes non-compliant patients

Bayesian analysisprior probability + new data → posterior probability

Effect size(difference between groups) ÷ SD

5Management

Evidence-based care & conflict of interest

Evidence-based health careEBM principles applied to all health professions incl. purchasing + management

Analysis Effects expressed as
Cost-benefit Money
Cost-effectiveness Health units (cost per event prevented)
Cost-utility Utilities (cost per QALY)
Decision analysis Explicit quantitative choice under uncertainty

Conflict of interest statement✓ discloses all financial interests of each author

X vs Y

Cohort Case-control
Grouped by Exposure Outcome/disease
Direction Forward (or retrospective) Backward
Measure Relative risk Odds ratio
Level II (prospective) / III III

How it's asked

"Purpose of computer random-number generator in Level I trial?"prevent selection bias (✗ power, ✗ significance, ✗ as-treated)

"Intern's RCT, attending recommends computer randomisation — why?"prevent selection bias (✗ blinding, ✗ prevent crossover)

"Randomisation in sealed opaque envelopes minimises?"selection bias (✗ attrition, ✗ NNH)

"Reason for computer randomisation (hemi vs screws)?"unbiased placement into groups (✗ confirmation bias, ✗ heterogeneity)

"Closure-technique QI study — best reduces confounding?"randomisation (✗ Hawthorne, ✗ blinding, ✗ power analysis)

"Crossover PT vs drug (t½ 2 wk), immediate switch — bias?"washout period bias

"Patients unaware, surgeon injects and scores outcomes?"single-blinded randomised

"Effect of blinding the outcome assessor?"↓ reported treatment effect

"Patient discovers their drug before final evaluation — bias?"recall bias (✗ selection, ✗ observer-expectancy, ✗ procedure)

"50 knee ON patients vs 23 without, compare alcohol intake?"case-control

"Two groups randomised to bisphosphonate vs placebo?"parallel design

"Two interventions applied separately or combined?"factorial

"Dislocated vs non-dislocated THAs, look for factors?"case-control

"Definition of cohort study?"groups separated non-randomly by exposure, before or after study start

"Randomised prospective drug vs standard for AVN — true?"best method for causality (✗ OR — case-control, ✗ RR — cohort, ✗ ↑ power — meta-analysis, ✗ hypothesis-generating — case series)

"RCT with 70% follow-up — level?"II

"Athletes choose cleat, followed prospectively through season — level?"II (prospective cohort)

"30 non-completers kept in PT group?"intent-to-treat (✗ per-protocol)

"Evidence use across health professions incl. purchasing?"evidence-based health care

"Purpose of conflict-of-interest statement?"disclose all financial interests

Collagen

Basic Science · 26 / 108

1Basics

Overview

Most abundant protein in humans~25% of body protein · >25 types · made by fibroblasts · synthesis inside and outside the cell

Structuretriple helix: two α1 + one α2 chains · repeats Gly-X-hydroxyproline, Gly-Pro-X (glycine every 3rd residue)

Type I genesCOL1A1 (pro-α1) + COL1A2 (pro-α2)

Assembly

Collagen synthesis
  • Intracellular → α-CHAINS→PROCOLLAGEN (2 α1 + 1 α2, triple helix)
  • Extracellular→TROPOCOLLAGEN (enzymatic processing after Golgi)
    • Lysine/hydroxylysine aldehyde cross-links → FIBRIL→FIBRE

Collagen types by tissue

Type Tissue Disease
I (>90% of body collagen) Bone, tendon, ligament, meniscus, annulus fibrosus, skin, healed cartilage/fibrocartilage, scar, nerve (tensile strength), deep calcified cartilage Osteogenesis imperfecta, Ehlers–Danlos
II (90–95% of cartilage collagen) Articular/hyaline cartilage, nucleus pulposus, elastic cartilage · very long half-life —
III Skin, blood vessels · early tendon/ligament healing (inflammatory + early regenerative) Ehlers–Danlos (vascular, type IV), Dupuytren's
IV Basement membrane Goodpasture, Alport
V, VI, IX Small amounts in articular cartilage · VI only pericellular (lacunae, mechanotransduction) · V, VI in disc —
IX Cartilage Deletion → OA in women, knockout mice · COL9A1–3 → MED
VII, VIII Epithelial basement membrane —
X Deep calcified cartilage · only hypertrophic chondrocytes in endochondral ossification (physis, callus, HO) · ↑ early OA Schmid metaphyseal chondrodysplasia
XI Adhesive holding cartilage collagen lattice together —
XII Annulus only —

Discannulus = type I · nucleus = type II · type VI both

Bone collagen

Bone weightinorganic 60–70% · water 5–8% · organic remainder · collagen = 90% of organic = 20–25% of bone weight · collagen gives flexibility

Multiple epiphyseal dysplasia

GenesCOMP (70%) · COL9A1, COL9A2, COL9A3

Featuresfragmented ossific centres · coxa vara · genu valgum · short stubby metacarpals

2Mechanism

Tendon & ligament healing

Inflammatory + early regenerative phase✓ type III predominates → type I in late regenerative phase

How it's asked

"Type I collagen in peripheral nerve provides?"tensile strength (✗ conduction velocity/strength, ✗ compression)

"Collagen first produced in inflammatory phase of tendon/ligament healing?"type III

"Major cartilage collagen, 90–95%?"type II

"Collagen % of bone weight?"20–25%

"Predominant collagen of nucleus pulposus?"type II (annulus — type I)

"Type II most abundant in all EXCEPT?"blood vessels (type III)

"Collagen of hypertrophic chondrocytes, ↑ in early OA, Schmid?"type X

Complex Regional Pain Syndrome (CRPS)

Basic Science · 27 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

SynonymsRSD (type I) · causalgia (type II)

Incidence5.5 / 100,000 person-yr (US) · 6–26 / 100,000 (OB) · ↑ with age to 70

Sexfemale 4:1 (Orthobullets/OB) · 2–4× (TO)

Siteupper limb 60% (Orthobullets) · 70% (TO) · type I 88% vs type II 12% (TO)

Postoperative rate10–37% (OB)

Risk factors

Listedtrauma with exaggerated response (commonest cause of poor outcome after foot crush) · surgery (multiple procedures) · prolonged immobilisation, tight dressings/casts (early cast complaints) · anxiety/depression · ACE inhibitor at time of trauma · migraine, asthma · smoking · fibromyalgia (↑ up to 2.5×) · RA, carpal tunnel

Not a risk factor✗ male sex

Pathophysiology

Triad✓ aberrant inflammatory response · vasomotor dysfunction · maladaptive neuroplasticity → sustained sympathetic activity in a perpetuated reflex arc

MediatorsIL-1β, IL-6, TNF-α, substance P · ↑ osteoclasts → regional migratory osteoporosis / bone marrow oedema

Genes & lab markers

Genetics↑ risk in siblings of young-onset cases · TNF-α and ACE gene polymorphisms

LabsWBC, ESR, CRP normal (distinguishes from infection)

3Presentation

Cardinal signspain out of proportion · swelling · stiffness · skin discolouration

Examvasomotor disturbance · trophic skin changes · hyperhidrosis · allodynia, hyperalgesia, hyperpathia · cool shiny skin · flamingo gait (knee) · equinovarus (ankle) · ✗ crepitus

Not typical✗ ↓ fingertip perfusion (Raynaud) · ✗ ptosis/anhidrosis (Horner, preganglionic plexus) · ✗ fingertip ulcers (Buerger) · ✗ positive Spurling

Natural course (TO)warm, ↑ hair/nail growth, oedema, erythema → later slow hair growth, brittle nails, weakness, cold limb

Lankford & Evans stages

Stage Onset Exam Imaging
Acute 0–3 mo Burning pain, redness, swelling, warmth, hyperhidrosis X-ray normal · bone scan +
Subacute (dystrophic) 3–12 mo ↑ pain, cyanosis, dry skin, stiffness, skin atrophy Subchondral osteopenia
Chronic (atrophic) >12 mo ↓ pain, glossy skin, fibrosis, contractures, hair/nail loss Extreme osteopenia

4Diagnosis & Classification

IASP classification (type I vs II)

Type I Type II
Nerve damage ✓ None identifiable Identifiable nerve injury
Frequency Most common Less common
Sympathetic block Responds Minimal / no response
Distribution Whole region Single nerve territory

Not distinguishingautonomic dysfunction · trophic changes · functional impairment · positive bone scan (all in both)

Diagnostic criteria — Budapest

Criterion Requirement
1 Continuing pain disproportionate to inciting event
2 Symptoms ≥1 symptom in 3 of 4 categories (clinical) · 4 of 4 (research)
3 Signs ≥1 sign in ≥2 categories at evaluation
4 No other diagnosis explains it
Category Symptoms / signs
Sensory Hyperaesthesia, allodynia, hyperalgesia (pinprick)
Vasomotor Temperature asymmetry, skin colour change/asymmetry
Sudomotor / oedema Oedema, sweating change/asymmetry
Motor / trophic ↓ ROM, weakness, tremor, dystonia, hair/nail/skin changes

Budapest vs IASPIASP sensitivity 1.00, specificity 0.41 · Budapest sensitivity 0.99, specificity 0.68–0.79

Best diagnostic test✓ history + physical examination (clinical) · confirmed by relief with sympathetic block · early diagnosis critical

Key radiology findings

Modality Finding
X-ray Osteopenia (patella — commonest knee finding) · soft-tissue swelling · subperiosteal resorption · joint spaces preserved
Three-phase bone scan ↑ uptake all phases · phase III (2–3 h) most sensitive · high NPV (rules out type I) · most sensitive investigation (TO) · TO note: high specificity, ↓ sensitivity vs Budapest, not confirmatory
MRI Diffuse bone marrow oedema (warm phenotype)
Thermography Limb temperature difference ≥1 °C significant · questionable utility
EMG/NCV Slowing in known nerve (type II, e.g. median) · ✗ not diagnostic for type I · myoclonus only 11–36%
Bone scan phase Shows
I (2 min) Arteriogram
II (5–10 min) Cellulitis, synovitis
III (2–3 h) Bone
IV (24 h) Osteomyelitis vs cellulitis

Differential

Listedsoft-tissue infection · malingering · clenched fist syndrome (psychiatric) · neuropathic/chronic pain · Raynaud (digital vasospasm) · TOS · arterial insufficiency · erythromelalgia

Clenched fist syndromenon-organic somatoform disorder · fixed finger flexion · macerated foul-smelling palm, paradoxical stiffness · psychiatric association, poor prognosis · normal imaging/EMG → therapy + psychiatry

5Management

Vitamin C prophylaxis

Regimen✓ vitamin C 500 mg daily × 50 days · 200 mg if renal impairment

EvidenceLevel I (Zollinger, 2 RCTs) in distal radius fractures · Lancet: CRPS 7% vs 22% placebo · JBJS: 200 mg 4.2%, 500 mg 1.8%, 1500 mg 1.7% → 500 mg recommended · later studies conflicting · also ↓ CRPS type I after foot & ankle surgery (non-diabetics)

Mechanism↓ lipid peroxidation · scavenges hydroxyl radicals · protects capillary endothelium · ↓ vascular permeability

Other preventionavoid tight dressings + prolonged immobilisation

Treatment (physiotherapy, blocks, sympathectomy)

CRPS treatment ladder
  • First line→PT (GENTLE / ACTIVE, desensitisation) + DRUGS
    (✗ casting, ✗ immobilisation, ✗ aggressive PROM)
  • Failed → SYMPATHETIC / NERVE BLOCK→CHEMICAL SYMPATHECTOMY
    • Focal LE CRPS failing blocks→DRG STIMULATION (TO)
  • Refractory→SURGICAL SYMPATHECTOMY
    • Type II with known nerve→DECOMPRESS NERVE (e.g. CTR)

Early CRPS✓ immediate PT: desensitisation + active ROM + multidisciplinary follow-up · ✗ restrict PT · ✗ LSB first · ✗ bone scan before treating

Modalitiesgentle physiotherapy · contrast baths · tactile discrimination training · graded motor imagery · mirror therapy · splinting, elevation, massage · TENS — positive only in type II (OB)

Graded motor imagery sequence (TO)✓ laterality restoration → explicit motor imagery → mirror therapy

DrugsNSAIDs · α-blockers (phenoxybenzamine, prazosin) · β-blocker (propranolol) · antidepressants · anticonvulsants · Ca-channel blockers · gabapentin · bisphosphonates · antiarrhythmics · corticosteroids · calcitonin · vitamin C

Also used (TO)ketamine (NMDA antagonist, topical/IV) · botulinum toxin A · mirror therapy · naltrexone · plasma exchange · cannabis inhaler · ✗ pramipexole — not used

Warm phenotype + bone marrow oedema on MRI (<6 mo, TO)✓ IV bisphosphonates (neridronate) first-line pharmacological

Post-TKA CRPS✓ α-blockers + PT + tactile discrimination + graded motor imagery (✗ explore knee, ✗ I&D/poly exchange, ✗ L-spine MRI)

Nerve stimulationTENS · peripheral nerve · spinal cord stimulator · indicated when symptoms mainly in one peripheral nerve

Neuromodulation — focal LE CRPS after failed blocks (TO)✓ dorsal root ganglion stimulation (ACCURATE trial: up to 80% pain ↓, fewer postural changes vs tonic SCS) · diffuse whole-limb → SCS

Block / sympathectomy Target
Stellate ganglion Upper limb
Lumbar sympathetic Lower limb
Agents (block) Lidocaine, bupivacaine, bretylium, guanethidine
Agents (chemical sympathectomy) Phenol, alcohol

Second line after failed early PT modalities (OB)✓ surgical sympathectomy (✗ cast, ✗ boot NWB, ✗ exchange nail, ✗ SCS) · best candidates = responded to sympathetic block

Best successfind + treat an associated nerve problem (type II)

6Complications

Prognosispoor response to treatment · better: upper limb, warm CRPS, children · late CRPS refractory → permanent disability

X vs Y

CRPS Raynaud Horner (preganglionic plexus) Clenched fist
Hallmark Pain out of proportion, allodynia, sweating Digital vasospasm in cold Ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis Fixed clenched fist, psychiatric
Mechanism Inflammatory + vasomotor Exaggerated arterial vasoconstriction Sympathetic chain injury Somatoform

How it's asked

"Most sensitive investigation for CRPS (TO)?"bone scan (✗ CT, MRI, US)

"Clinical features of LE CRPS EXCEPT?"crepitus

"Commonest X-ray finding in knee CRPS?"patellar osteopenia

"Healed DRF, now hyperalgesia, allodynia, hyperhidrosis — pathophysiology?"aberrant inflammatory and vasomotor response

"Post-Kidner, allodynia, hyperalgesia, oedema of medial foot?"RSD

"All common in CRPS EXCEPT?"↓ perfusion to fingertips

"Fibromyalgia patient with DRF — at ↑ risk of?"hyperalgesia, allodynia, oedema (CRPS)

"CRPS type I vs II differs by?"no identifiable peripheral nerve involvement

"Whole-leg pain, shiny cool cyanotic skin after tibia ORIF — diagnosis + best test?"CRPS type I; history and examination (✗ type II bone scan, ✗ EMG)

"Wrist sprain, allodynia, sweating, relief with sympathetic block?"CRPS type I (type II doesn't respond)

"Vitamin C after DRF ORIF prevents?"pain out of proportion + ↑ sweating (CRPS)

"Adjuvant improving outcome after DRF ORIF?"oral vitamin C

"Level I evidence for vitamin C reducing CRPS in?"distal radius fractures

"Vitamin C after F&A surgery ↓?"CRPS type I

"Protocol shown to prevent CRPS?"vitamin C 500 mg × 50 days

"NOT used in CRPS treatment (TO)?"pramipexole

"Graded motor imagery sequence (TO)?"laterality → explicit imagery → mirror therapy

"Focal knee CRPS after ACL, failed blocks — neuromodulation (TO)?"DRG stimulation

"Post-TKA burning pain, oedema, ↑ hair, marrow oedema — first-line drug (TO)?"IV bisphosphonate (neridronate)

"Positive effect in early CRPS?"gentle physiotherapy (✗ casting, plyometrics)

"Post-TKA burning, sweating, purple leg, aspirate negative?"α-blockers + PT + tactile discrimination + GMI

"Failed splinting, contrast baths, TENS — second line?"surgical sympathectomy

"Early CRPS after ankle sprain — initial step?"immediate PT, desensitisation + AROM, multidisciplinary follow-up

"Crush foot CRPS — risk factors EXCEPT?"male gender

"Painful clenched hand, depression, normal studies?"clenched fist syndrome

Deep Vein Thrombosis

Basic Science · 28 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence80 / 100,000 / yr · LE 1 / 1,000 / yr · 2.5–5% of population · usually >40 yr · rare in children

High-risk proceduresspine fracture with paralysis · TKA (2–3× DVT rate of THA) · THA · hip fracture · polytrauma

DVT rate ≠ PE or deathAAOS review: DVT rate does not correlate with PE/death after THA/TKA

Risk factorsimmobilisation · mechanical compression · trauma · prior DVT · surgery · polycythaemia vera · thrombocytosis · dehydration · malignancy · CHF · obstructive airway disease

No ↑ riskfactor V Leiden in THA (no ↑ DVT or PE vs controls) · rheumatoid arthritis in TKA (age/comorbidity-matched)

Pathophysiology

Virchow's triadhypercoagulability · venous stasis · endothelial injury

Factor V Leidenfactor V resistant to protein C inactivation → hypercoagulable (factor V = cofactor for Xa → thrombin)

Anatomy

Deep veins
Proximal LE Popliteal and above (above trifurcation)
Distal LE — axial Peroneal, anterior + posterior tibial
Distal LE — muscular Soleal, gastrocnemius
UE Radial, ulnar, brachial, axillary, subclavian, IJ, brachiocephalic

2Mechanism

Timing & intraoperative embolisation

Peak marrow embolisation in cemented THA✓ femoral stem insertion (also acetabular prep, reduction)

Symptomatic VTE after TJA✓ 1–6 weeks (2nd–6th week) · events on day of surgery = fat embolism or cardiac · ~10% readmitted within 3 months

3Presentation

Symptomsswelling (70%) · calf pain (50%) · redness · warmth

Examunilateral/bilateral swelling · erythema · dilated veins · tenderness · Homans' sign not specific (or sensitive)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Test Sens / spec Role
Complete duplex US 96% / 97% ✓ First line · non-compressible vein, loss of phasic flow with Valsalva, ↑ superficial flow · operator-dependent · ✗ routine screening not recommended
Contrast venography — Historical gold standard · invasive · if US unavailable/inconclusive
CT venography 90% / 95% Suspected PE or inconclusive US
MR direct thrombus imaging 90% / 90% Not widely used
Plethysmography (proximal) 75% / 90% —

D-dimer

High sensitivity, low specificityexcludes VTE in low clinical probability · >500 ng/mL suggests PE · ✗ useless after injury/surgery (false positives)

Differential

Mimic Clue
Cellulitis Infective labs
Post-thrombotic syndrome Prior DVT, chronic swelling, stasis ulcers
Ruptured Baker cyst US: no venous thrombus
Superficial thrombophlebitis Cord-like superficial vein
CHF, nephrotic, cirrhosis Chronic bilateral oedema

5Management

Anticoagulation

Agent Role
DOACs (dabigatran, rivaroxaban, apixaban, edoxaban) ✓ First line for DVT + PE (ACCP 2016, ESC)
LMWH (enoxaparin) Early postoperative period · high bleeding risk
Warfarin Bridge with LMWH until INR 2–3 · preferred with mechanical heart valve

Indicationpostoperative above-knee DVT · below-knee treatment controversial

Scenario Duration
Isolated provoked DVT 3 months
With PE >3 months (longer if large clot, persistent risk, recurrence)
>3 months Unprovoked · recurrent · active malignancy · paralysis/immobilisation · thrombophilia

Procedures

Intervention Indication
IVC filter DVT identified preop in high-risk pelvic/LE trauma · contraindication to anticoagulation
Thrombolysis (systemic) Life/limb-threatening PE or acute DVT · high morbidity
Catheter-directed thrombolysis Initial treatment only for threatened limb loss
Thrombectomy Anticoagulation ineffective, high-risk thrombus

Risk factors & individualised prophylaxis

Foot & ankle (ICM-VTE)✓ patient-specific risk assessment (age, DM, prior DVT, NWB, obesity) · NWB = independent risk factor but no prophylaxis rule based on NWB alone · ✗ routine chemoprophylaxis for all NWB · ✗ blanket "none for below-knee" · ✗ surveillance duplex · ✗ postop D-dimer

6Complications

Post-thrombotic syndromechronic venous insufficiency · venous HTN · swelling, pain, pigmentation, ulceration, induration · recurrent DVT 4–8×

PE1 in 1,000 / yr · anticoagulation ± intervention

Bleedinganticoagulants, thrombolysis

How it's asked

"Peak marrow embolisation in cemented THA?"femoral stem insertion

"When do most symptomatic VTE occur after TJA?"1–6 weeks

"Factor V Leiden and THA?"no increased risk of DVT or PE

"RA (RF, anti-CCP, MTX) vs OA patient for TKA — VTE prophylaxis?"equal risk, standard prophylaxis for both

"Hindfoot fusion, NWB 6 wk, BMI 31, DM, prior DVT — strategy?"patient-specific risk assessment, consider chemoprophylaxis (✗ none, ✗ routine for all, ✗ screening duplex, ✗ D-dimer)

"First-line imaging for DVT?"complete duplex ultrasound · routine screening ✗

"First-line DVT treatment?"DOAC · warfarin if mechanical valve

Limb bud signalling (AER, ZPA, timing)

Limb budlateral plate + somatic mesoderm under ectoderm · Shh from notochord regulates formation · growth = AER ↔ progress-zone mesoderm interaction (FGF-driven)

All UL bones endochondralexcept distal tufts of distal phalanges (membranous)

Shh gradient

Little fingerhighest Shh (posterior/ulnar) · thumb — lowest Shh (anterior/radial)

↑ Shh posteriorlyulnar (postaxial) polydactyly (dose-dependent) · ↓ Shh posteriorly → loss of ulnar digits

Sclerotome ✓ Axial skeleton — vertebral bodies + annulus fibrosus
Myotome Muscle (dorsal epimere → dorsal muscles)
Dermatome Dermis/skin

Vertebra precursor (TO)✓ somites · each vertebra = caudal half of one sclerotome + cranial half of the next (two somite levels) · sclerotome cells engulf notochord in week 4

Metameric shiftspinal nerves, originally mid-sclerotome, exit between vertebral bodies

Hox (homeobox) genes✓ regulate segmentation/identity of axial skeleton (rostral → caudal) · with Shh, AP patterning

Structure Origin

Endochondral Bone Formation

Basic Science · 30 / 108

1Basics

Where it occurs

Three settingslongitudinal physeal growth · embryonic long-bone formation · non-rigid (secondary) fracture healing (cast, brace, IM nail)

Principlecartilage model → chondrocytes hypertrophy/calcify → osteoclasts resorb → osteoblasts lay bone on the cartilage framework (bone replaces cartilage, ✗ cartilage converted) · deposition on metaphyseal side · type X collagen

Molecular regulation

Sox-9master regulator of chondrogenesis · collagen II, IV, XI, aggrecan

Low β-catenin✓ mesenchyme → cartilage (↑ SOX9) → collagen II, IX, XI · anlage grows interstitially + appositionally (TO)

High β-catenin (Wnt)chondrocytes stop growing, hypertrophy → express RUNX2 + VEGF-A → vessels, osteoblasts, osteoclasts → primary ossification centre (TO)

Ihh–PTHrP loopprehypertrophic chondrocytes secrete Indian hedgehog → ↑ PTHrP → delays hypertrophic differentiation

Mutation Effect on hypertrophic maturation
Deactivating PTHrP receptor ✓ Accelerated
Activating Ihh Delayed (↑ PTHrP)
Activating TGF-β / SMAD-3 Delayed (TGF-β inhibits hypertrophy, type X, ALP)
PGE2 No physeal effect shown

Physeal blood supply

Perichondrial arterymajor nutrition of the physis

2Mechanism

Physeal zones & pathology

Zone Features Diseases
Reserve / resting / germinal Stores lipids, glycogen, PG aggregates · max PG storage (TO) · low O₂ · supplies cells, organises plate Gaucher's, diastrophic dysplasia, Kniest, pseudoachondroplasia
Proliferative Chondrocyte division + columns (stacking) · ↑ O₂ inhibits calcification · highest ECM production (TO) Achondroplasia, gigantism, MHE
Hypertrophic — maturation, degeneration (cells ↑ 5×), provisional calcification Chondrocyte hypertrophy → death → Ca release → matrix calcifies · type X collagen + ALP · Ihh/PTHrP · weakest zone (TO) SCFE (non-renal), rickets (provisional calcification), enchondromas, MPS, Schmid · physeal fractures (SH I/II) through zone of provisional calcification
Primary spongiosa (metaphysis) Vascular invasion, transverse septa resorbed, osteoblasts on cartilage bars → woven bone Metaphyseal corner fracture (abuse), scurvy
Secondary spongiosa Internal remodelling (woven → lamellar), funnelisation Renal SCFE

Type X collagenproduced by terminal hypertrophic chondrocytes · ✓ abrupt appearance heralds ossification (TO) · type II still most abundant in hypertrophic zone (TO)

Longitudinal growthchondrocyte hypertrophy contributes most (cell volume/height) (OB8254) · proliferative zone "responsible for longitudinal growth" (OB3234) — read the options

Physis periphery

Structure Function Disease
Groove (zone) of Ranvier Wedge of germinal cells · supplies chondrocytes to periphery · ✓ appositional (circumferential) growth · ↑ mechanical strength Osteochondroma · SH VI injury (lawnmower, burns)
Perichondrial ring of LaCroix Dense fibrous ring · peripheral stability · links Ranvier zone to metaphyseal periosteum —
Perichondrial artery Physeal nutrition —

Growth regulation

Hueter–Volkmannsustained compression → decelerated longitudinal growth · tension accelerates

Embryonic long-bone formation

Mesenchymal anlage (6th week in utero)
  • Cartilage model→CHONDROBLASTS (hyaline precursor)
  • Vascular buds invade→PRIMARY OSSIFICATION CENTRE (~8 wk) + MARROW
  • Ends keep growing→SECONDARY OSSIFICATION CENTRES (epiphyses, physis)

Non-rigid fracture healing

Soft callus = cartilage intermediate → proliferation → hypertrophy → calcification → bone · IM nail → both endochondral and intramembranous

How it's asked

"Physeal zone with maximum PG storage (TO)?"resting zone

"Collagen whose abrupt appearance heralds ossification (TO)?"type X

"SH I fractures occur through?"zone of provisional calcification

"SH II distal radius — zone?"zone of provisional calcification

"Gigantism affects?"proliferative zone (C)

"Type X collagen prominent in?"zone of hypertrophy

"Accelerated hypertrophic maturation?"deactivating PTHrP-receptor mutation (✗ activating TGF-β, SMAD-3, Ihh)

"Found during chondrocyte differentiation / anlage formation (TO)?"low β-catenin (✗ RUNX2, VEGF-A — hypertrophy/ossification, ✗ RANKL)

"Sustained compression on physis?"decelerated longitudinal growth

"Groove of Ranvier responsible for?"appositional growth

"Process contributing most to longitudinal growth?"chondrocyte hypertrophy

Ethics in Orthopaedic Practice

Basic Science · 31 / 108

1Basics

Ethical principles

Pillar Definition
Beneficence Duty to do good
Non-maleficence Duty not to harm
Respect for autonomy Patient self-determination
Justice Fair, equitable, appropriate treatment (✗ "everyone gets the same treatment")
Confidentiality Patient's right + doctor's duty to protect information

5Management

Industry relationships & conflicts of interest

AAOS Standards of Professionalism
Gifts Market value <$100 (except textbooks, patient-education material) · dinners ≤$100/person with useful education
Industry non-CME course ✓ Tuition, travel, modest hospitality acceptable (✗ "must decline non-CME courses")
Faculty at industry meeting ✓ Travel + hotel paid (contribution must match expenses)
CME event Corporate subsidy to the sponsor OK · ✗ direct industry reimbursement to the surgeon · ✗ spouse's travel
On-site training (company HQ workshop, new technique/implant) ✓ Expenses reimbursable
Disclosure All industry relationships → patients, colleagues, institution · even without financial compensation
Royalties Disclose full relationship incl. royalties to patient before surgery · may still use own device
Kick-backs ✗ No payment for using a product ($500/knee, "consultant" after 25 implants)
Stark II ✗ No referral to centres in which physician has a financial interest
Consulting Defined duties + work product · fair pay for time · ✗ thinly veiled payment
Residency grants Unrestricted for CME ✓ · ✗ restricted to industry course
Personal deals (e.g. rep buys 90% of a boat) ✗ Reject — unacceptable conflict (equal share doesn't fix it)

Implant selection✓ surgeon responsible for knowing the implant's clinical track record · ✗ rely on sales rep · publish negative results too

Research conduct

Industry-funded RCT✓ paid fair market value, disclose, register (ClinicalTrials.gov — FDA law for implanted devices), regulatory binder, publish · ✗ company statistician analysing data (bias → use independent blinded statistician)

Sunshine Actall industry payments to physicians reported to CMS

Publicationgovernment-funded studies must publish regardless of outcome · private studies need only report serious adverse events to FDA (publication bias)

Advertising

Who regulatesAMA/AAOS cannot stop physicians advertising (FTC Act) · FTC, AAOS, state medical boards, consumer protection agencies sanction false advertising

✗ Avoid ✓ Acceptable
"Cure" (if no true cure) "Relatively little pain"
"Painless / pain-free", "bloodless" "Less invasive / minimally invasive" (no outcome claims)
"Board certified in joint replacement" (doesn't exist) "Relatively safe"
"World renowned" · "safe surgery for all" —
"Return to all sports after TJA" —

Interpreters & consent

Preferred✓ certified (professional) interpreter, on-site or telephonic (medico-legal)

Family members✗ confidentiality, unless patient offers/agrees · ✗ minors except emergencies

Interpreter summarising (short phrases)consent invalid → ✓ repeat consent with qualified interpreter (✗ another nurse, ✗ proceed)

Interpretation styleconduit, literal, first person, no omissions

Patient transfer — EMTALA

Requirementspatient screened (+ stabilised) at original ED · benefit of transfer > risk · accepting facility knows and formally accepts · ✓ exiting facility lacks resources to treat (OB key)

Not required✗ insurance discussion · ✗ full lab work-up · ✗ cost of transport

Team physician obligations

Team-employed physician✓ agent of the team → must disclose findings to employer · record = employment record, not HIPAA-protected

Private physicianHIPAA applies · ✗ no obligation to disclose to prospective teams without athlete consent

6Complications

Patient complaints, communication & litigation

Leading cause of litigation✓ poor communication · ↓ litigation with effective communication, standard of care, thorough informed consent, documentation

Orthopaedic surgeons ratedhighest for technical skills · lowest for empathy, listening, communication ("high tech, low touch") · doctors interrupt within 23 s

Most common patient complaint✓ lack of empathy

Formal written complaint about carewritten response letter to patient

Formal medical-board complaint✓ written response to the board with all relevant records · governed by state boards (✗ federal) · ✗ automatic licence suspension · ✗ contact patient's attorney · attorney not required

Physician burnout & suicide

Burnoutloss of enthusiasm, cynicism (depersonalisation), ↓ personal accomplishment · ✓ 45–55% of US physicians · rising · 1.5–2× general population · ortho among higher-risk specialties · Maslach Burnout Inventory

Suicide✓ orthopaedic surgeons = highest number among surgical subspecialties (28%) · surgeons 6% more likely than general population

Disparities & diversity

Cultural competency goal✓ better patient–physician relationships, access, quality of care (✗ quotas, ✗ stereotypes, ✗ foreign languages, ✗ travel abroad)

Implicit biasunconscious prejudice influencing care · ✓ determinant of healthcare disparity

Paediatric accessdetermined by type of insurance

How it's asked

"Standards of professionalism EXCEPT?""decline industry-sponsored non-CME courses"

"You receive royalties on a TKA that suits your patient?"disclose before surgery

"True of ethical implant management?"surgeon must know the implant's track record

"Most appropriate industry relationship?"travel/hotel for faculty at non-CME industry meeting (✗ spouse travel, ✗ $200 dinner, ✗ restricted resident grant)

"Rep offers 90% share of a $200,000 boat?"reject — conflict of interest

"Acceptable way to learn the Genuflex knee?"company pays expenses for HQ workshop (✗ company pays CME tuition, ✗ $500 per knee)

"Royalty-receiving designer uses product — obligation?"disclose full relationship incl. royalties

"Violates standards of professionalism?"not reporting a conflict because no compensation received

"Inappropriate in company-funded TKA RCT?"company statistician analysing data

"Acceptable newspaper advert?""relatively little pain" (✗ bloodless, ✗ board certified in joint replacement, ✗ cure)

"Acceptable advert phrase?""less invasive hip and knee replacements"

"Non-English-speaking mother-in-law of colleague — interpreter?"certified translator

"Nurse interpreter gives short phrases, patient offers to sign?"repeat consent with qualified interpreter

"True about burnout?"45–55%

"True about physician suicide?"ortho = highest among surgical subspecialties (28%)

"Formal complaint after infected TKA?"written response to medical board with records

"Ortho surgeons rated highest for?"technical skills

"Commonest patient complaint about ortho surgeons?"lack of empathy

"Associated with ↓ litigation?"effective communication

"Benefit of diversity/cultural competency training?"better relationships, access, quality of care

"Prisoner with hip OA — important to?"understand implicit bias

"EMTALA requires?"exiting facility lacks resources to treat (✗ insurance discussion, ✗ complete labs)

"Incorrectly paired ethical term?"justice = everyone gets the same treatment

"Free-agent athlete's private surgeon — disclosure?"team physician must disclose to own team · private physician not obligated

Fat Embolism Syndrome

Basic Science · 34 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence3–4% isolated long-bone fracture · 10–15% polytrauma · ~90% of long-bone fractures have some fat emboli (subclinical ↓ PaO₂ in almost all)

Mortalityup to 15%

Causeslong-bone fractures (femur, tibia, pelvis) · IM nailing · hip & knee arthroplasty · rare: crush, burns, marrow biopsy/transplant, liposuction, CPR, haemoglobinopathy, steroids, pancreatitis, fatty liver (TO)

Risk factors (TO)young age · polytrauma, multiple fractures · closed fractures · conservative treatment of long-bone fractures · IM nailing: over-zealous nailing, reaming, ↑ reaming speed, ↑ nail–cortex gap

Pathophysiology

Theory Mechanism
Mechanical Marrow fat droplets enter venous system (shear of intraosseous vessels, reaming) → obstruct pulmonary vessels
Biochemical ✓ Lipoprotein lipase → free fatty acids + glycerol → endothelial toxicity → cytokines → ARDS-like picture · ↓ surfactant

Arthroplasty embolifat + marrow (obstructive, act like PE) vs free fat of fractures (chemical injury)

2Mechanism

Intraoperative embolisation in arthroplasty

Peak intramedullary pressurecement pressurisation + femoral component insertion (whole canal pressurised) > reaming/broaching

Uncemented hemiarthroplasty stem insertion → hypotension, hypoxia✓ intramedullary fat + marrow embolisation

TKAIM alignment rods → emboli · ✓ extramedullary guides ↓ embolisation risk · overdrilling the femoral canal entry ↓ risk

3Presentation

Onsetwithin 24 h (Orthobullets) · 24–72 h (OB) · 12–72 h (TO)

Classic triadrespiratory distress (first) · cerebral signs (confusion) · petechial rash (last)

Petechiaeaxillae, upper chest, shoulders, conjunctivae, oral mucosa · appear within 36 h, gone within a week · RBC extravasation from distended capillaries · pathognomonic (TO)

Othertachycardia, tachypnoea, fever (non-specific but near-universal) · jaundice, oliguria (TO) · respiratory recovery in ~3 days if no further embolism (TO)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria

Major (≥1) Minor (≥4)
Hypoxaemia (PaO₂ <60 mmHg) + respiratory signs, radiographic changes Tachycardia >110
CNS depression (unrelated to head injury) Pyrexia >38.5 °C
✓ Petechial rash Retinal fat/petechiae
Pulmonary oedema (Orthobullets) Fat in urine/sputum · renal changes · jaundice
— Thrombocytopenia · ↓ Hct/Hb · ↑ ESR

Gurd & Wilson (TO)1 major + 4 minor + fat macroglobulinaemia

AdditionalPCO₂ >55 · pH <7.3 · RR >35 · dyspnoea · anxiety

Key radiology findings

ABG (TO)early hypoxia + hypocarbia, respiratory alkalosis → later respiratory acidosis · ↑ A–a shunt within 24–48 h without ARDS

CXR (TO)initially normal → diffuse fluffy bilateral infiltrates ("snowstorm"), bases + periphery

CT angiogramnegative for PE (distinguishes)

5Management

Prevention

Most important✓ early fracture stabilisation (<24 h) of long bones (✗ delay fixation, TO)

Techniquesnarrower-shaft reamers · overdrill femoral entry in TKA · external fixation in medically unstable patients

Treatment

Supportive (ICU)O₂ → CPAP (↑ PaO₂ without ↑ FiO₂) → mechanical ventilation with high PEEP if FiO₂ >60% + CPAP >10 cm needed (TO)

Fluidsalbumin preferred (restores volume, lipophilic) (TO) · CVP monitoring (pulmonary HTN, RV failure)

Not recommended (TO)✗ heparin (bleeding in polytrauma) · ✗ high-dose steroids (insufficient evidence)

6Complications

Bone cement implantation syndrome (TO)

Definitionhypoxia, hypotension ± unexpected loss of consciousness, arrhythmia, ↑ PVR, cardiac arrest around cementation, prosthesis insertion, reduction or tourniquet deflation in cemented arthroplasty (TO)

Not a feature✗ hypertension (TO)

Timing (5 stages)femoral reaming · acetabular/femoral cement · prosthesis insertion · joint reduction · (tourniquet deflation in TKA) (TO)

Mechanismexothermic cement (up to 96 °C) expands, traps air + marrow under high intramedullary pressure → embolisation (TO)

BCIS grade (TO) Criteria
1 SpO₂ <94% or SBP fall >20%
2 SpO₂ <88% or SBP fall >40% or unexpected LOC
3 Cardiovascular collapse → CPR

Risk factors (TO)old age · poor reserve · cardiopulmonary disease · pulmonary hypertension · osteoporosis · bony metastases · hip fracture (pathological/intertrochanteric) · previously uninstrumented canal · long-stem component

Treatment (TO)IV fluids, inotropes, vasopressors → ECMO if no recovery

X vs Y

FES PE (DVT) BCIS
Timing 12–72 h after fracture/nail 1–6 wk postop Intraop — cement, stem, reduction, tourniquet release
Rash Axillary petechiae No No
CNS Confusion Rare LOC
Test Clinical · CTA negative CTA positive Clinical

How it's asked

"Hypotension + hypoxia during uncemented hemi stem insertion?"intramedullary fat and marrow embolisation (✗ DVT-PE, ✗ MI, ✗ fluid deficit)

"Benefit of extramedullary guides in TKA?"↓ embolisation risk

"Cemented bipolar, death after hypoxia; marrow emboli in lungs — cause?"femoral component insertion after cement pressurisation (✗ reaming, ✗ undersized broaches)

"Emboli during TKA composed of?"fat and marrow

"Major criterion for fat embolism (TO)?"petechial rash (✗ tachycardia, pyrexia, retinal changes — minor)

"True regarding FES (TO)?"CPAP and PEEP for hypoxia (✗ heparin, ✗ steroids, ✗ delay fixation)

"Bilateral femur + humerus fractures, tachycardia, hypoxia, confusion, axillary petechiae, CTA negative?"fat embolism syndrome

"Day after reamed tibial nail: ↑ O₂, delirium, chest rash — mechanism?"lipoprotein lipase activation → free fatty acids (✗ tissue factor/collagen — PE, ✗ IgE, ✗ plasmin inhibition — TXA, ✗ ↑ surfactant)

"TKR — hypotension + dyspnoea after tourniquet deflation (TO)?"bone cement implantation syndrome (✗ fat embolism — IM rod insertion)

"NOT a component of BCIS (TO)?"hypertension

Fracture Healing

Basic Science · 38 / 108

1Basics

Stem-cell sources

Two major sourcesperiosteum + endosteum · periosteal response ↑ by motion, ↓ by rigid fixation

Primary vs secondary healing & strain theory

Strain (definition)✓ change in length ÷ original length (ΔL/L) · stress = force/area

Perren's interfragmentary strain theorygap tissue differentiation depends on strain · comminuted fractures tolerate more motion (motion shared across many gaps) · simple transverse fractures in a nail → higher strain

Strain Outcome
<2% Primary healing (intramembranous, Haversian remodelling / cutting cones)
2–10% Secondary healing (endochondral, callus)
10–100% Fibrous tissue (or hypertrophic nonunion)
>100% Nonunion

Tissue tolerance (TO)cortical bone tolerates only 2% · lamellar bone up to 10% (TO wording) · granulation tissue tolerates the greatest strain

Osteoblastsstimulated at 0.3–2.8% strain · optimal interfragmentary motion 0.2–1 mm

Construct Healing
Compression plate, lag screw + neutralisation plate, tension band, medial malleolar screws Primary — Haversian remodelling (absolute stability, anatomic reduction, <2%)
IM nail (incl. CMN), external fixator, bridge plate, cast/brace/splint Secondary — endochondral (relative stability, 2–10%)
Locked compression plate (bridge mode) ✓ Secondary (TO) — "internal fixator"

Primary types (TO)contact healing (ends apposed → osteoclast cutting cones cross, osteoblasts follow) · gap healing (small gap → transverse woven bone → lamellar) · also unicortical/greenstick fractures

Primary healing preferredperiarticular · both-bone forearm · simple ankle fractures with syndesmotic disruption (callus would impair rotation/joint)

Biomechanics of callus

Peripheral callus✓ ↑ moment of inertia → ↓ strain · bending stiffness ∝ diameter⁴ (2× diameter → 16× stiffness) · ↑ torsional strength, peak torque

2Mechanism

Stages of secondary healing

Stage Events
Inflammation (haematoma) Haematopoietic cells, macrophages, neutrophils, platelets → PDGF, TNF-α, TGF-β, IL-1, 6, 10, 12 (from 24 h) · BMPs ↑, MSC migration (SDF-1, G-CSF) · granulation tissue · COX-2 → Runx2/osterix (NSAIDs act here)
Repair Primary callus within 2 wk · stable → osteoblasts, unstable → chondrocytes · type II collagen early → type I · soft → hard callus (woven bone) · callus amount ∝ 1/immobilisation
Remodelling Starts mid-repair, continues years · Ihh, PTHrP, FGF, BMP · type X collagen (hypertrophic chondrocytes, calcification) · chondrocyte apoptosis → VEGF → vessels · woven → lamellar (Wolff's law, piezoelectric)

Biologic stages & mediators

Mediator Role
TNF-α Pro-healing inflammatory cytokine · deficiency (HIV) delays endochondral + intramembranous ossification
BMP-2 ↑ in inflammatory phase · recruits MSCs · differentiation
SDF-1 + G-CSF ✓ MSC migration to fracture (absence → nonunion)
VEGF Neovascularisation
RANKL Osteoclasts
IL-10 Anti-inflammatory
COX-2 MSC → osteoblasts · knockout mice heal more slowly (✗ faster) · converts arachidonic acid → PGH2 · ↑ in cancers
MMP-13 ✓ From terminally differentiated chondrocytes → degrades cartilaginous ECM (soft → woven bone)
Collagen by phase (OB) Type
Inflammation/clot I, II
Fibroblasts / fibrous tissue III / V
Soft (chondroid) callus II + IX
Callus calcification ✓ X (hypertrophic chondrocytes)
Bone deposition I

4Diagnosis & Classification

Variables that influence healing

Blood supply — most important↓ immediately → ↑ peaks 2 wk → normal 3–5 months · unreamed nails preserve endosteal supply · reaming devascularises inner 50–80% · loose nails reperfuse faster

Head injurymay ↑ osteogenic response

Mechanicalsoft-tissue attachments · stability/strain · location · bone loss · segmental/butterfly → ↑ nonunion (intercalary blood supply)

Patient factor Effect
Vitamin D / Ca deficiency 84% of nonunions metabolic · >66% vitamin D deficient
Protein malnutrition ↓ callus strength · amino acids ↑ albumin, body mass, muscle protein, callus mineralisation (✗ not IGF-1 mRNA)
Roux-en-Y gastric bypass Duodenum bypassed → ↓ Ca/vit D → 2° hyperparathyroidism → nonunion · ✗ sleeve, band, colectomy, nephrectomy
Diabetes ↓ callus cellularity, delayed endochondral ossification, weak callus · 1.6× longer
Nicotine ↓ angiogenesis, weak callus · spine pseudarthrosis +500% · open tibia ~70% longer
HIV Fragility fractures, delay — ARVs, poor circulation, TNF-α deficiency, nutrition
Bisphosphonates Longer healing (wrist ORIF) · atypical femur fractures
Corticosteroids 6.5% higher IT nonunion · ↓ IGF-1, TGF-β
NSAIDs COX inhibition
Quinolones Chondrocyte toxicity
High-dose radiation ↓ cellularity, long-term remodelling change

Detrimental (TO)age · malnutrition (albumin <3.5, lymphocytes <1,500) · smoking · alcohol · DM · cancer, osteomalacia, Cushing's, obesity · anaemia (iron for proline hydroxylation, mineralisation) · PVD · NSAIDs, chemo, steroids, BPs, quinolones · radiation · infection · open, intra-articular, segmental, comminuted, denervated fractures · intact fellow bone · soft-tissue interposition · inadequate reduction/fixation (too short, too stiff) · ✗ nephrotic syndrome — not listed (TO)

Vicarious blood supply (TO)femoral neck · scaphoid waist/proximal pole · talar body · paediatric lateral condyle · 5th MT base · distal ⅓ tibia · distal ⅓ ulna

5Management

Nonunion (stability, metabolic causes)

Nonunion
  • Hypertrophic (abundant callus)→↑ MECHANICAL STABILITY
    (cause: inadequate stability, biology intact)
  • Atrophic / oligotrophic (poor biology)→TAKEDOWN + BONE GRAFT + STABILISE
  • No infection signs → METABOLIC WORK-UP (25-OH vit D)→VITAMIN D + OBSERVE

Oligotrophic distal tibia nonunion, no infection✓ observe + vitamin D (keep weight-bearing) · ✗ NWB · ✗ suppressive antibiotics

Lab of choice for vit D25-hydroxyvitamin D

Bone stimulation (LIPUS, capacitive coupling)

Modality Mechanism
LIPUS (30 mW/cm² pulsed) ✓ Nanomotion (mechanical strain across cell wall) · ↑ vascularity, protein expression · ↑ callus torque/stiffness · delayed/nonunion healing ~80%
Capacitive coupling (AC) ✓ Ca²⁺ translocation via voltage-gated channels → calmodulin → ↑ BMP, cAMP, TGF-β1 · collagen, cartilage calcification
Direct current ↓ O₂, ↑ pH → ↓ osteoclasts, ↑ osteoblasts · inflammatory-like response
PEMF Calcification of fibrocartilage (✗ fibrous tissue)

LIPUS Level I evidence✓ radius shaft, distal radius, casted tibia, scaphoid · ✗ tibia treated with reamed IM nail

Dynamisation

Term Definition
Dynamisation Gradually destabilise fixation to load bone
Reverse dynamisation ✓ Early micromotion → callus, then convert to rigid fixation to accelerate union
Accordion method Alternate distraction/compression of regenerate

How it's asked

"Healing with locked compression plating (TO)?"secondary (bridge mode)

"True regarding fracture healing (TO)?"rigid compression fixation → healing by cutting cones (✗ cortical bone tolerates 10%, ✗ contact healing in LCP)

"Femoral nail — healing type?"secondary (✗ Haversian, ✗ cutting cones, ✗ extramembranous)

"Definition of strain?"ΔL / original length (✗ force/area — stress)

"Direct (primary) healing with?"lag screw + neutralisation plate (✗ nails, ex-fix, bridge plate)

"Peripheral callus biomechanics?"↑ moment of inertia, ↓ strain

"Bridge plating of distal tibia relies on?"endochondral ossification via chondrocyte proliferation + hypertrophy

"Construct healing by enchondral ossification?"bridge plate

"Tibial nail healing — true?"soft callus replaced by woven bone (✗ <2% strain, ✗ cutting cones)

"Comminuted femur IMN — healing?"secondary, strain 2–10%

"Radial shaft compression plate — true?"Haversian remodelling

"Why comminuted vs transverse nailed femurs tolerate different gap deformation?"Perren's strain theory (✗ moment of inertia, ✗ Young's modulus)

"Medial malleolar screw fixation — healing?"Haversian remodelling

"Relies on strain <2%?"transverse radial shaft + compression plating

"NOT detrimental to healing (TO)?"nephrotic syndrome (✗ PVD, DM, anaemia)

"Hypertrophic humeral nonunion heals with?"↑ mechanical stability

"Commonest cause of hypertrophic nonunion?"inadequate mechanical stability

"Oligotrophic tibial nonunion, no infection — labs support?"observation + vitamin D

"Surgery contributing to tibial nonunion?"Roux-en-Y gastric bypass

"FALSE about COX-2?""knockout mice heal faster"

"Type X collagen characteristic of?"cartilage callus formation + calcification

"Amino acid supplementation in malnourished rats ↑ all EXCEPT?"IGF-1 mRNA expression

"Role of MMP-13 in early callus?"from terminally differentiated chondrocytes, degrades cartilaginous ECM

"True about healing stages (clavicle)?"BMP ↑ in inflammatory phase (✗ callus in inflammation, ✗ NSAIDs act in remodelling, ✗ TNF-α excess slows, ✗ woven bone final)

"No SDF-1 or G-CSF → affects?"MSC migration to fracture

"LIPUS mechanism?"nanomotion at fracture site

"Capacitive coupling mechanism?"Ca²⁺ translocation via voltage-gated channels (✗ ↓ O₂/↑ pH — direct current)

"Level I LIPUS benefit in all EXCEPT?"tibia shaft with reamed IM nail

"Reverse dynamisation?"early micromotion then rigid fixation

"Ex-fix of comminuted MC — healing like all EXCEPT?"Weber B ankle ORIF (primary)

1Basics

Components of anaesthesia

Five A'samnesia · anxiolysis · analgesia · akinesia · attenuation of autonomic responses

General anaesthesia

Inhalationalisoflurane, sevoflurane, desflurane · nitrous oxide → gaseous abdominal (bowel) distension (diffuses in, can't leave) → ✗ fluoroscopy in pelvic/spine surgery

IV non-opioidspropofol, etomidate, benzodiazepines, dexmedetomidine, ketamine · opioids — fentanyl family, morphine, hydromorphone

Neuromuscular blocker Agents Reversal
Depolarising (depolarises + blocks ACh-R) Succinylcholine (short only) —
Non-depolarising Intermediate: rocuronium, vecuronium, atracurium, cisatracurium · long: pancuronium Neostigmine · sugammadex (rocuronium)

5Management

Upper-limb blocks

Block Site Use Key complication
Interscalene Brachial plexus between anterior + middle scalenes, cricoid level Shoulder, upper arm, elbow Phrenic block → hemidiaphragm paresis (very common side effect) · sensory neuropathy / temporary paraesthesia ≤6 mo (2.3%) = commonest complication · Horner's · RLN · pneumothorax · vertebral artery · epidural/spinal spread
Supraclavicular Above clavicle Lower humerus → hand Hemidiaphragmatic paralysis > infraclavicular · pneumothorax · subclavian artery
Infraclavicular Below clavicle Lower humerus → hand Less phrenic involvement
Axillary Axilla Elbow, forearm, wrist, hand —
Bier block IV regional (lidocaine distal IV, tourniquet) Short (<60 min) hand/forearm (CTR) Deflate ≥30 min → avoid LAST

Interscalene contraindications✓ moderate–severe lung disease (FEV1 45%) (diaphragm paresis ↓ lung volumes ~30%) · contralateral phrenic injury · LA allergy · infection · uncontrolled seizures · coagulopathy · pre-existing nerve injury · ✗ age, aspirin, obesity alone, 1st-degree heart block

Hemidiaphragm paresis, SpO₂ 97% on 2 L✓ observe + monitor (✗ ABG, CTPA, chest tube, intubation)

Postop dyspnoea after supraclavicular block, normal CXR✓ hemidiaphragmatic paralysis (sniff test) · phrenic nerve (C3–5) on anterior scalene

Interscalene catheter → ptosis, miosis (TO)✓ Horner's syndrome → remove catheter (reversible) · stellate ganglion paralysis from high LA concentration (TO)

Neuraxial

Technique Notes
Spinal Single shot, 24–27 G needle, morphine + bupivacaine · THA/TKA, hip fracture (= GA for delirium, 60-day mortality, ambulation) · spinal headache (↓ small needle), haematoma, opioid effects
Epidural Indwelling catheter · hypotension, motor block
Combined spinal-epidural Both

Lower-limb blocks

Block Target / landmark Use Issue
Lumbar plexus (psoas compartment) L1–4 (obturator, LFCN, femoral) · 3–5 cm lateral to L4 spinous process Hip, thigh, knee Retroperitoneal haematoma, epidural/intrathecal spread, falls
Posterior quadratus lumborum Behind QL → thoracolumbar fascia Hip Deep, technically hard
Fascia iliaca Femoral, obturator, LFCN · 1 cm caudal to lateral ⅓ / medial ⅔ of pubic tubercle–ASIS line Hip fracture, thigh, knee ✓ ↓ postoperative delirium in geriatric hip fracture
Femoral Lateral to femoral artery (L2–4) Knee Prolonged quadriceps weakness, falls
Adductor canal Saphenous + nerve to VMO (distal to quad motor branches) TKA ✓ = pain control, better mobility than femoral
iPACK Between popliteal artery + posterior capsule TKA Motor-sparing · US-guided · ✓ iPACK + ACB > ACB alone
Sciatic GT–PSIS / GT–sacral hiatus lines Leg, ankle, foot Heel ulcers, delayed nerve-injury diagnosis
Obturator 2 cm inferior + 2 cm lateral to pubic tubercle Adductor spasm, hip OA pain, knee adjunct Anterior branch → hip/inner thigh · posterior → knee
Popliteal Sciatic ~10 cm above popliteal crease Foot/ankle (+ saphenous) Foot drop, pressure sores
Saphenous Behind sartorius Medial knee/leg/ankle —
Periarticular injection — TKA/THA Better TKA pain control than iPACK (Orthobullets)

Geniculate blockschronic OA pain before TKA, not perioperative

Regional techniques — haematoma block & tourniquet (TO)

Haematoma blockLA injected directly into the fracture site · distal radius, ankle fracture-dislocation · ✗ "around nerves at target site" (false) (TO)

Tourniquet facts (TO)
Cuff Widest possible (arm 10 cm, thigh ≥15 cm) — effective at lower pressure
Pressure Arm SBP + 100 · thigh SBP + 100–150 mmHg · best: limb occlusion pressure (LOP) + margin (LOP <130 → +40 · 131–190 → +60 · >190 → +80)
Time 2 h safe limit · if longer, deflate 10 min
Commonest complication Nerve injury · UL > LL · radial nerve most susceptible
Nerve injury cause Cuff pressure > duration (✗ "duration more than pressure" — false) · direct pressure > ischaemia · too low pressure (venous congestion) also
Post-tourniquet syndrome Ischaemia · oedema, redness, pain, dysaesthesia, stiffness
Relative contraindications PVD, DVT, severe infection, poor cardiac status · avoid exsanguination in infection/malignancy
Prep solutions Prevent seepage under cuff (chemical burns)

Blood management

Hypotensive epidural anaesthesia (HEA)✓ best ↓ blood loss + transfusion in bilateral THA · block to T2 (cardio-accelerator fibres) → MAP ~50 mmHg · bradycardia → low-dose epinephrine · normal HR, CVP, SV, CO · usable in HTN, poor cardiac function, CKD · beats spinal, normotensive epidural, hypotensive TIVA, GA

Otherpreop EPO · autologous donation · acute normovolaemic haemodilution · cell saver

6Complications

Malignant hyperthermia & local anaesthetic toxicity

Malignant hyperthermia LAST
Cause AD, RYR1 · triggered by volatile agents + succinylcholine Intravascular bupivacaine (Na-channel block)
Features Rigidity, masseter spasm, ↑ EtCO₂, ↑ temp, tachycardia, dark urine (myoglobin) CNS: perioral numbness, metallic taste, seizures, coma · CVS: arrhythmia, asystole/VF
Treatment Stop triggers, hyperventilate O₂, cool + ✓ dantrolene ✓ IV 20% lipid emulsion + ACLS
Incidence 1 : 15,000–50,000 —
Antidote For
Naloxone Opioids
Flumazenil Benzodiazepines
Protamine Heparin
Neostigmine / sugammadex Non-depolarising NMB (rocuronium)
Atropine, isoproterenol β-blocker bradycardia

Bone cement implantation syndromehypotension + hypoxaemia → IV fluids, vasopressors, 100% O₂

Epidural morphine + steroid paste after laminectomy✓ surgical site infection

How it's asked

"Interscalene catheter, facial discomfort, ptosis/miosis (TO)?"Horner's — remove catheter (✗ facial palsy)

"Most likely complication of interscalene block?"sensory neuropathy (✗ motor neuropathy, CRPS, pneumothorax)

"Relative contraindication to interscalene block?"FEV1 45% (✗ age, aspirin, obesity, 1st-degree block)

"Most common complication after interscalene block?"temporary paraesthesia up to 6 months

"Post-interscalene high-riding diaphragm, SpO₂ 97%?"observe and monitor

"Post-supraclavicular block dyspnoea, normal CXR?"hemidiaphragmatic paralysis (✗ pneumothorax, ✗ LAST)

"Best describes iPACK?"ACB + iPACK > ACB alone (✗ femoral branches — ACB, ✗ motor-sacrificing)

"Block C (adductor canal) vs A (femoral)?"equivalent pain control, better mobility

"Cardiac arrest during TKA, resuscitated with 20% lipid?"bupivacaine

"Family history, rigidity, ↑ EtCO₂ after succinylcholine — antidote?"dantrolene

"Nitrous oxide effect?"gaseous abdominal distension

"Bilateral THA — best ↓ transfusion?"hypotensive epidural anaesthesia

"Epidural morphine + steroid paste after laminectomy — complication?"surgical site infection

"False about haematoma block (TO)?""LA injected around nerves at target site"

"False about tourniquet palsy (TO)?""related more to duration than pressure"

Tumour suppressors & translocations (RB, p53, Li-Fraumeni)

Tumour suppressornormally inhibits proliferation · loss → unregulated growth

Gene Type Facts
RB-1 ✓ Tumour suppressor · recessive (both alleles) Abnormal = phosphorylated (can't bind DNA) · 100% retinoblastoma · 35% osteosarcoma
p53 Tumour suppressor · dominant (one allele) Blocks entry to S-phase if DNA damaged · ~50% of all tumours · osteosarcoma 20–65% · chondrosarcoma · Li-Fraumeni (germline, AD)

Li-Fraumenibreast (25%) > osteosarcoma (20%) · soft-tissue sarcoma, leukaemia, brain, colon · homozygous p53 → 100% malignancy

OncogenesFAK (focal adhesion kinase) · Erb-2 (EGF receptor variant)

Sarcoma Translocation Fusion
Ewing's t(11;22) EWS-FLI1
Alveolar rhabdomyosarcoma t(2;13) (PAX3 70%) / t(1;13) (PAX7) PAX3-FKHR
Myxoid liposarcoma t(12;16) TLS-CHOP
Synovial sarcoma t(X;18) (>90%) SYT-SSX1/2/4
Clear cell sarcoma t(12;22) EWS-ATF1
Myxoid chondrosarcoma t(9;22) EWS-CHN
Osteosarcoma ✓ None (Rb/p53 mutations instead) —

Translocationsin up to 95% of sarcomas · allow expression of normally silent oncogenes

Gout

Basic Science · 42 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Demographicsrecurrent attacks in men 40–60 yr

Sitelower limb · podagra (1st MTP) · knee, ankle · hand/wrist possible · tophi: ear helix, eyelid, olecranon, Achilles

Risk factorschemotherapy · alcohol

Pathophysiology

Primary goutidiopathic disorder of nucleic acid (purine) metabolism → hyperuricaemia → MSU crystal deposition

Secondary gouthigh cell turnover: psoriasis, haemolytic anaemia, leukaemia, chemotherapy

Inflammationcrystals → proteases, prostaglandins, leukotriene B4, free radicals · tophi/pannus release IL-6, IL-8, TNF-α

Associationsrenal stones, urate nephropathy · septic arthritis can coexist (~1.5%) — crystals don't exclude infection

3Presentation

Symptomsacute severe joint pain, swelling, erythema · mimics septic arthritis · remits in 1–2 wk, recurs

Tophus aspiratewhite, toothpaste-like · tophi often confused with infection

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria

Definitive✓ joint aspiration + polarised microscopy (or tophaceous deposits)

Gout (MSU) Pseudogout (CPPD)
Needle-shaped, thin, tapered, intracellular Rhomboid
Strongly negatively birefringent Positively birefringent
Yellow parallel to red compensator · blue perpendicular Blue parallel
1st MTP Knee (large joints)

Serum uric acid✗ not diagnostic · 80% with hyperuricaemia never get gout · may be normal during an attack

Key radiology findings

X-raypunched-out periarticular erosions with sclerotic overhanging edges · soft-tissue tophi · joint space preserved until late

Foot X-ray Diagnosis
Periarticular erosions + tophi, 1st MTP Gout
Pencil-in-cup, bilateral Psoriatic arthritis
Flattened 2nd MT head Freiberg's
Hindfoot fragmentation Charcot
MTP dislocations, aspherical 1st MT head RA

Biopsyacute + chronic inflammation, prominent clefts (crystals dissolved), no atypia

5Management

Treatment & distinguishing from septic arthritis

Phase Treatment
Acute NSAID — indomethacin 50 mg tid (inhibits phagocytosis) · colchicine (if peptic ulcer history; can be IV) · steroids (oral, IA, IV) if NSAID/colchicine contraindicated (IA only if no sepsis)
Chronic ✓ Allopurinol (xanthine oxidase inhibitor) — ✗ not in acute attack
Prophylaxis after recurrent attacks Colchicine (up to 85% effective)
Hot swollen joint, aspirate
  • MSU crystals, synovial WBC <50k, well→NSAIDs (± ALLOPURINOL LATER)
  • Crystals + WBC >50k / fever / infection risk → EMPIRIC IV ANTIBIOTICS→I&D
    (crystals do not exclude sepsis)
  • CPPD crystals→NSAIDs ± IA STEROID (allopurinol ✗)

Fever 38.3, tachycardia, recent abdominal abscess, crystals on aspirate✓ empiric IV antibiotics (cultures taken) → likely I&D

WBC 20k, normal serum markers, MSU crystals✓ oral NSAIDs (✗ antibiotics, ✗ allopurinol, ✗ I&D, ✗ uric acid level)

Isolated gout flare suitable for discharge✓ SFA 35k WBC + MSU (✗ 75k + MSU — superimposed sepsis · ✗ CPPD · ✗ 200k RBC — haemarthrosis)

Hand lytic lesions, uric acid 10.4, clefts on biopsy✓ colchicine + indomethacin (✗ radiation, chemo, amputation, antibiotics)

X vs Y

Aspirate image Diagnosis
Negatively birefringent needles Gout
Positively birefringent rhomboids Pseudogout
Haemosiderin-laden giant cells PVNS
Gram-positive cocci Septic arthritis
Histiocytes, inflammatory cells RA

How it's asked

"60-yr-old, 2 yr intermittent great-toe pain, erosions + soft-tissue densities?"MSU crystal deposition (✗ CPPD, renal osteodystrophy, TB, sarcoid)

"Foot radiograph most consistent with gout?"periarticular erosions + tophi (B)

"Clinical + radiographic tophaceous lesion caused by?"uric acid deposition

"Swollen hot 1st MTP — definitive diagnosis?"aspiration of 1st MTP (✗ serum uric acid/CRP/ESR, ✗ MRI, ✗ RF)

"Gout diagnosed by tophi or?"MSU crystals in synovial fluid (✗ ↑ serum uric acid)

"Hand lesion, negatively birefringent intracellular rods?"gout (✗ CPPD, infection, GCT)

"Alcoholic, recurrent knee swelling, colchicine — aspirate image?"negatively birefringent needles

"Crystal analysis consistent with gout?"negatively birefringent needle-shaped

"Line cook, tophi on fingers, acute wrist pain — SFA?"negatively birefringent needles

"Recurrent medial forefoot pain responsive to NSAIDs, normal WBC?"needle-shaped negatively birefringent crystals

"Febrile, recent diverticular abscess, crystals in knee aspirate — next?"empiric IV antibiotics

"Elderly woman, finger lytic lesions, uric acid 10.4 — treatment?"colchicine + indomethacin

"Knee aspirate WBC 20k, MSU, normal labs — next?"oral NSAIDs

"Isolated gout flare suitable for discharge?"35k WBC + MSU

Hemophilia

Basic Science · 43 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology & types

Haemophilia A Haemophilia B (Christmas)
Factor VIII IX
Incidence 1 : 5,000 males 1 : 30,000 males
Inheritance X-linked recessive X-linked recessive

Acquired haemophiliarare, autoimmune, not inherited

Family historyaffected maternal uncles

PathophysiologyVIII + IX needed for thrombin generation in the intrinsic pathway → unstable clot

Genes & lab markers

Coagulation✓ aPTT prolonged, PT normal · LFTs normal · CBC (Hb)

Factor assaysplasma factor VIII/IX activity + inhibitor assay (diagnosis)

Bethesda assayquantifies inhibitor antibody if inhibitor positive · none / <5 BU low / >5 BU high

Severity Factor level
Mild 5–25%
Moderate 1–5%
Severe 0–1%
Bleeding disorder Inheritance Feature
Factor VIII / IX deficiency ✓ X-linked recessive Spontaneous haemarthroses
Factor XI (haemophilia C) Autosomal recessive Surgical bleeding, no spontaneous haemarthrosis
von Willebrand (commonest bleeding disorder) Autosomal dominant (chr 12) Mucosal bleeding · ↓ platelet adhesion
Protein C / S deficiency, factor V Leiden AD Thrombosis (not bleeding)
Vitamin K deficiency Acquired Diet, malabsorption, liver disease

DIC (fulminant)

Labs✓ aPTT ↑ and PT ↑ · ↓ fibrinogen, ↓ platelets · ↑ FDPs, ↑ D-dimer · heparin not proven · causes: massive transfusion, sepsis, burns, crush, liver disease, malignancy, obstetric

3Presentation

Bleedingjoints (knee most common haemarthrosis; elbow, ankle, shoulder, spine) · muscles (quadriceps, hamstrings, iliopsoas, biceps, triceps) · mucocutaneous

Orthopaedic manifestations

Haemophilic arthropathysynovitis → cartilage destruction → deformity

Intramuscular haematoma (pseudotumour)✓ iliacus haematoma → femoral nerve compression → paraesthesia in L4 / saphenous distribution (medial distal tibia) · groin pain after sport (forceful hip flexion)

OtherLLD (epiphyseal overgrowth) · fractures (osteopenia, heal in normal time) · compartment syndrome

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Modality Finding / use
X-ray (AP, lateral, oblique, skyline) Epiphyseal overgrowth · osteopenia · squared patella + condyles (Jordan's sign) · ballooned distal femur · wide intercondylar notch · long thin patella
CT Pseudotumour location (bone vs soft tissue), fracture pattern
MRI Best soft tissue · haemarthrosis, synovitis, haemosiderin · cartilage
US Effusion vs pseudotumour · follow intramuscular haematomas

5Management

Management (factor replacement, synovectomy, haematoma)

Situation Target factor level
Vigorous physiotherapy 20%
Acute haematoma 30%
Acute haemarthrosis, soft-tissue surgery 40–50% (OB: "within 60%")
Skeletal surgery 100% for week 1 → >50% for week 2

High-titre factor VIII inhibitor✓ factor VIIa (bypasses VIII) for acute bleed, compartment syndrome, surgery

Femoral fracture + compartment syndrome + high inhibitor titre✓ factor VIIa immediately → fasciotomy + IMN (✗ VIII replacement — won't work · ✗ operate first · ✗ plate)

Non-life-threatening bleedanalgesia, physio · ✗ routine immobilisation or paracentesis

Synovectomy✓ recurrent haemarthrosis despite optimal medical management · ↓ bleeding but ✗ doesn't restore ROM or reverse cartilage damage · ✗ not for radiographic narrowing, stiffness, prophylaxis

Radioactive synoviorthesis↓ recurrence more than surgical synovectomy

End-stagearthroplasty

VTE prophylaxis

Known bleeding disorder (factor VIII deficiency)✓ pneumatic compression only, no pharmacoprophylaxis (TO) · also active liver disease (✗ protein C/S deficiency, factor V Leiden — thrombophilias)

6Complications

Inhibitors (IgG)4–20% · any time · relative contraindication to surgery · screen preop

Blood-borne infectionHIV ~10–15% (this note) · up to 90% (arthropathy note, older cohorts) · recombinant factor VIII (1992) eliminated risk

Allergy to blood products

How it's asked

"Avoid pharmacoprophylaxis in favour of pneumatic compression (TO)?"factor VIII deficiency (✗ protein C, S, factor V Leiden)

"Haemophilic boy, iliacus haematoma, medial distal tibia paraesthesia — nerve?"femoral (✗ obturator, genitofemoral)

"Femur fracture + compartment syndrome + high VIII inhibitor?"factor VIIa now, then fasciotomy + IMN

"Primary indication for synovectomy?"recurrent bleeding despite optimal medical management

"Fulminant DIC finding?"↑ aPTT (✗ ↑ fibrinogen, ✗ ↓ D-dimer, ✗ normal PT, ✗ heparin curative)

"Factor VIII deficiency inheritance?"X-linked recessive

Hemophilic Arthropathy

Basic Science · 44 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence✓ ↓ significantly due to home factor treatment (recombinant factor VIII since 1992 — also removed HIV/hepatitis risk)

Demographicsyoung males, 3–15 yr

Siteknee (most common) · elbow, ankle, shoulder, spine

Prevention, factor replacement & inheritance

Cause Inheritance Factor
Haemophilia A X-linked recessive ↓ VIII
Haemophilia B (Christmas) X-linked recessive ↓ IX
von Willebrand Autosomal dominant Abnormal VIII + platelet dysfunction · mucosal bleeding, rare joint bleeds

X-linked recessive bleeding disorders✓ both factor VIII and IX deficiency (✗ factor XI — AR, ✗ vWD — AD)

Pathoanatomyrepeated minor trauma → haemarthrosis → synovitis (hypertrophic, hyperplastic) → enzymatic cartilage destruction → deformity

Associationsiliacus haematoma → femoral palsy (L4 paraesthesia) · LLD (epiphyseal overgrowth) · osteopenic fractures (normal healing) · HIV up to 90% (older cohorts)

3Presentation

Haemarthrosis Features
Acute Painful, tense effusion
Subacute After 2 prior bleeds
Chronic Contractures, arthritis

4Diagnosis & Classification

Arnold–Hilgartner staging

Stage Radiographic finding
1 Soft-tissue swelling
2 Osteoporosis (± epiphyseal overgrowth)
3 Subchondral cysts, joint grossly intact
4 Cartilage loss — joint narrowing
5 Severe arthritis (end-stage, fibrous ankylosis)

Key radiology findings

Kneesquared patella + condyles (Jordan's sign) · ballooned distal femur · widened intercondylar notch · narrowing · long thin patella

MRIearly degenerative change, haemosiderin · US — follow muscle haematomas

Labs

Factor VIII inhibitor (IgG) screen✓ before surgery (negates factor therapy) · found in 5–25% · relative contraindication to surgery

Exclude septic arthritisexam + aspiration

5Management

Indication Factor VIII target
Vigorous PT 20%
Acute haematoma 30%
Acute haemarthrosis, soft-tissue surgery, synovectomy 40–50%
Skeletal surgery, TJA, arthrodesis 100% wk 1 → >50% wk 2

Before TKA (X-linked bleeding disorder)✓ factor VIII replacement (✗ protein C/S, ✗ vit K, ✗ vWF)

Desmopressinmild–moderate haemophilia A

Non-operativecompressive dressing, analgesia, short immobilisation → rehab, steroids, splints, PT

Synovectomyrecurrent haemarthroses despite medical management · ↓ recurrence, pain, swelling

Synoviorthesisintra-articular radioactive colloidal P-32 chromic phosphate

Arthroplastyend-stage · arthrodesis — ankle

How it's asked

"Main reason for ↓ haemophilic arthropathy over 10–20 yr?"home factor treatment (✗ PT, steroid injections, debridement, splints)

"X-linked clotting disorder before TKR — replace?"factor VIII

"Bleeding disorders that are X-linked recessive?"factor VIII and IX deficiency

"Arnold–Hilgartner stage with subchondral cysts, joint intact?"stage 3

Hyperparathyroidism

Basic Science · 45 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence0.1% · women > men · with malignancy = most cases of hypercalcaemia

Cause (primary)single adenoma 80–90% · hyperplasia 10%

Pathophysiology

PTH acts indirectlybinds osteoblast receptor → RANKL + M-CSF → osteoclasts → excess resorption · cortical > cancellous

PTH effects (OB)↑ renal tubular Ca reabsorption · ↑ osteoclast activity · ↑ active vitamin D → ↑ gut Ca

Classification

Type Cause Ca Phos PTH
Primary Adenoma / hyperplasia ↑ ↓ ↑
Secondary Compensation for ↓ Ca / ↑ phosphate — CKD (renal osteodystrophy), malabsorption (coeliac), vit D deficiency ↓ / N ↑ (renal) / ↓ (malabsorption) ↑
Tertiary Autonomous glands after long secondary HPT / renal transplant ↑ ↑ ↑
Malignancy (PTHrP) Ectopic ↑ ↓ ↓

Genes & lab markers

Serum↑ ALP · normal anion-gap metabolic acidosis (↓ HCO₃ reclamation)

Urine (primary)hypercalciuria (stones) · ↑ cAMP

ECGshort QT

3Presentation

"Stones, bones, groans"renal stones · bone pain · constipation · weakness · often asymptomatic · rare 2° HTN · fragility fracture with abnormal labs

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Finding Notes
Subperiosteal resorption Radial side of middle phalanges, phalangeal tufts, distal clavicle, proximal tibia/humerus/femur
"Salt and pepper" skull Calvarial trabecular resorption
Rugger-jersey spine Sclerotic endplate bands (excess unmineralised osteoid) — distinctive for HPT (TO)
Brown tumours Lytic, expansile → pathological fracture · osteitis fibrosa cystica (jaw)
Chondrocalcinosis Menisci, articular cartilage
Looser zones Renal osteodystrophy (osteomalacia)

Brown tumour histology

Featuresbland spindled fibrous stroma + small multinucleated giant cells · haemorrhage + haemosiderin · variable lytic/dense multiple lesions

vs GCTGCT: larger giant cells, round stromal nuclei identical to giant-cell nuclei

vs myeloma/metastasesserum markers (Ca, PTH) distinguish

5Management

Acute hypercalcaemiaIV fluids + loop diuretics

Symptomaticparathyroidectomy → watch postop hypocalcaemia (numbness, tingling, cramps) → IV calcium gluconate

6Complications

Peptic ulcer (↑ gastrin) · pancreatitis (↑ lipase) · CNS (anxiety, confusion, coma) · osteoporosis/fragility fractures

X vs Y

Lab panel Diagnosis
↑ PTH, ↑ ALP, ↑ Ca, ↓ PO₄, normal renal function Primary HPT
↑ PTH, ↑ ALP, ↓ Ca, ↑ PO₄ Secondary (renal)
↑ PTH, ↑ Ca, ↑ PO₄ Tertiary
Normal PTH, ↓ ALP, ↑ Ca, ↑ PO₄, ↑ urinary phosphoethanolamine Hypophosphatasia
↓ PTH, normal ALP, ↓ Ca, ↑ PO₄ Hypoparathyroidism

How it's asked

"Rugger-jersey spine X-ray characteristic of (TO)?"hyperparathyroidism

"Dialysis patient, Looser zone, rugger jersey, salt-and-pepper skull — labs EXCEPT?"hypophosphataemia (phosphate ↑ in renal osteodystrophy)

"25-yr-old, brown tumours, normal GFR — labs?"↑ PTH, ↑ ALP, ↑ Ca, ↓ PO₄ (primary)

"Coeliac sprue, brown tumour fracture — expected?"↑ PTH (✗ ↑ Ca, ↑ 1,25-D, ↑ PO₄, ↓ ALP)

"66-yr-old, multiple variable tibial lesions, spindle stroma + small giant cells + haemosiderin?"hyperparathyroidism (brown tumours) (✗ myeloma, mets, GCT)

"Fragility fracture, ↑ Ca, ↑ ALP, ↓ PO₄, ↑ PTH — aetiology?"PTH adenoma (✗ age-related osteoporosis — normal labs)

Hypoparathyroidism

Basic Science · 46 / 108

1Basics

Pathophysiology

Cause↓ PTH from chief cells · thyroidectomy (incl. parathyroids) = most common

Effects of ↓ PTH↓ renal phosphate excretion → ↑ serum phosphate · ↓ 1α-hydroxylation → ↓ 1,25-(OH)₂D → ↓ calcium

Genes & lab markers

↓ ↑ Normal
PTH, calcium, 1,25-D, urinary Ca Serum phosphate ALP

Alkalosis↑ albumin binding of ionised Ca → hypocalcaemia

ECGprolonged QT

PTH & vitamin D lab patterns

Condition Ca PO₄ PTH Cause
Hyperparathyroidism ↑ ↓ ↑ Adenoma
Hypoparathyroidism ↓ ↑ ↓ Thyroidectomy
Ectopic PTH(rP) ↑ ↓ ↓ Malignancy
Vitamin D malabsorption ↓ ↓ ↑ Coeliac, GI disease
↓ Vit D + no renal PO₄ excretion ↓ ↑ ↑ Renal failure, pseudohypoparathyroidism

3Presentation

Hypocalcaemianeuromuscular irritability: Chvostek's sign, tetany, seizures · cataracts · nail fungal infections · hair loss · vitiligo, blotchy skin
