Skull X-raybasal ganglia calcification

5Management

Calcium + vitamin D supplementation · monitor labs · no hormone replacement available (Orthobullets)

Renal disease & PTH

Chronic renal disease✓ ↑ PTH production (Ca wasted, PO₄ retained, ↓ 1,25-D) · ✗ TSH unaffected

Renal osteodystrophyhigh-turnover (↑ PTH, gland hyperplasia → osteitis fibrosa cystica, persists after correction) vs low-turnover (more common on dialysis) · metaphyseal enlargement, frontal bossing, bowing, genu varum

How it's asked

"Chronic renal disease — endocrine abnormality?"↑ PTH (✗ ↓ PTH, ✗ TSH change)

"Post-thyroidectomy tetany, ↑ PO₄, ↓ Ca, ↓ PTH?"hypoparathyroidism

Hypophosphatasia

Basic Science · 47 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Severe (perinatal/infantile)1 : 100,000–300,000 · mild (adult) — 1 : 500–3,000 · diagnostic delay common

Genes & lab markers

Gene✓ loss-of-function TNSALP (tissue-nonspecific alkaline phosphatase) in osteoblasts

Inheritancesevere forms autosomal recessive · mild/adult often autosomal dominant

Pathophysiology↓ ALP → extracellular ↑ inorganic pyrophosphate (PPi), pyridoxal-5'-phosphate, phosphoethanolamine · PPi inhibits hydroxyapatite formation → hypertrophic-zone osteoid fails to mineralise → no zone of provisional calcification

Lab Finding
Serum ALP ✓ ↓ (low)
Urine phosphoethanolamine ✓ ↑ — diagnostic
Urine PPi ↑
Ca, PO₄ Normal or ↑ (hypercalcaemia)
PTH Normal

Low ALP in metabolic disease✓ hypophosphatasia (✗ FHH, X-linked hypophosphataemia, secondary/tertiary HPT — all ↑ ALP)

3Presentation

Rickets-likegenu varum, short stature, ↑ fractures

Form Features
Perinatal Hypomineralisation, long-bone (pretibial) bowing, osteochondral spurs, caput membranaceum, respiratory failure / lung hypoplasia
Infantile Craniosynostosis, rachitic ribs, flared metaphyses
Childhood Premature loss of deciduous teeth, short stature, bone pain, fractures
Adult Femoral pseudofractures, metatarsal stress fractures, chondrocalcinosis, poor healing, osteopenia

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray"deossification" adjacent to physis (tongues of lucency) · physeal widening · flared metaphyses

5Management

Therapysummary lists enzyme replacement, anti-inflammatories, multidisciplinary care · treatment section: "no approved therapies, phosphate under investigation" (Orthobullets) — read the options

Hypophosphataemic rickets — contrast

Child with marked genu varum + wide physes✓ first get serum phosphate, calcium, ALP (✗ osteotomy, orthosis, scanogram, reassurance)

X-linked hypophosphataemic (vit D-resistant) ricketsX-linked dominant · presents 18–24 mo · renal phosphate wasting · ↓ PO₄, ↑ ALP, normal Ca, normal vit D · short (<10th centile)

Treatmentphosphate + calcitriol together · calcitriol alone → ✓ no effect on alignment or height · phosphate alone → ↓ Ca → 2° HPT → more phosphaturia

X vs Y

Hypophosphatasia Hypophosphataemic rickets
ALP ↓ ↑
PO₄ N / ↑ ↓
Urine Phosphoethanolamine Phosphate wasting
Inheritance AR (severe) X-linked dominant

How it's asked

"Hypophosphatasia lab finding?"↓ serum ALP (✗ ↓ urinary phosphoethanolamine, ✗ ↓ urinary PPi)

"Metabolic disorder with ↓ ALP?"hypophosphatasia

1Basics

Innate & adaptive immunity

Innate Acquired (adaptive)
Specificity Non-specific (structures common to microbes) Pathogen/antigen-specific
Memory ✗ ✓
Components Skin barrier, inflammation, complement Antigen processing + presentation
Cells NK cells, mast cells, eosinophils, basophils, macrophages, neutrophils, dendritic cells CD8+ T cells, T helpers, γδ T cells, B cells, plasma cells

First step of acquired response✓ antigen processing + presentation to T cells by APCs (B cells, dendritic cells) → peptides on MHC → TCR recognises MHC/antigen (✗ T cells phagocytose antigen · disulfide cleavage occurs in APC)

MoM bearing reactivityinnate (cell-mediated) per OB · metal implants → type IV (Orthobullets)

Type Mediator Examples
I — immediate/anaphylactic IgE → mast cells, basophils Allergy, urticaria (rapid hives)
II — cytotoxic IgG, IgM Transfusion reactions
III — immune complex IgG, IgM complexes deposit —
IV — delayed (cell-mediated) T cells, monocytes, macrophages · 2–3 days (7–10 for dermatitis) TB skin test · metal implants (plate + screws) · topical antibiotic (bacitracin) contact dermatitis

Bacitracin contact dermatitis5–10% · erythema, oedema, blistering, ✗ no pus/fluctuance → stop agent ± steroid cream

Ig Key fact
IgG Most abundant
IgM ✓ First to appear after antigen exposure
IgA Secretions (gut, bronchial mucus, saliva, tears)
IgE Parasites, allergy

Most common neutropenia worldwide25–50% of people of African descent, some Middle Eastern groups · lifelong low ANC without ↑ infection risk

Informed Consent & Time Out

Basic Science · 50 / 108

1Basics

Elective consent

Optimal✓ by the surgeon, in the office, a few days (within 7 days) before surgery (✗ nurse/surgeon in pre-op holding, ✗ risk manager, ✗ scheduler)

Disclosewho will operate · risks, benefits, indications, alternatives · surgeon's HIV/HBV/HCV status for exposure-prone procedures (at scheduling) · origin of implants (bovine — Hindu · porcine — Jewish, Muslim)

AdolescentsAAP: >14 yr who dissent treated as having capacity, even though parental legal consent needed

Emergency consent

Life-threatening, no legal consent availableproceed; confirm + document necessity with a colleague

Non-life-threateningconsent required first (language barrier, child)

Next of kin orderspouse → children → parents → siblings → grandchildren

Research & privacy

HIPAAno patient consent needed to share PHI with other treating providers

IRBmandates clinical-trial consent process · levels: exempt (still needs chair certification), expedited, full board

No IRB review needed✓ internal quality-improvement studies (OR efficiency, preop delays) even with identifiers · becomes research (IRB) if published/presented nationally or applied beyond the institution

IRB requiredany study using PHI (medical record number is PHI) · vulnerable populations: prisoners, pregnant women, fetuses, neonates, cognitively impaired, children, students, employees

Surgical safety checklist

WHO checklist (2009)✓ ↓ surgical mortality, ↓ in-hospital complications, ↑ adherence in OR crises (haemorrhage, arrest)

How it's asked

"Optimal setting for elective written consent?"surgeon in office within 7 days prior

"Which studies need no IRB review?"internal QI studies (2 and 4) (✗ MRN retrospective study, ✗ children with CP)

"Next of kin first in line?"spouse

Inheritance Patterns of Orthopaedic Syndromes

Basic Science · 51 / 108

1Basics

Mendelian patterns & pedigrees

Mendelian (monogenic)AD · AR · X-linked dominant · X-linked recessive · ~1% of humans

Rule of thumb✓ AD → structural protein defects (e.g. FGFR3 in achondroplasia) · AR → enzymatic/biochemical defects

Pedigree keycircle = female · square = male · filled = affected · horizontal line = mating · vertical = offspring

Pattern Pedigree clue
AD Every generation, both sexes, ~50% of offspring (no skipping if complete penetrance)
AR Both sexes equally, skips generations, affected children of unaffected (carrier) parents, ¼ of offspring
X-linked recessive Males; affected mother → all sons affected
X-linked dominant Affected father → all daughters
Autosomal dominant Autosomal recessive X-linked recessive X-linked dominant
Syndactyly, polydactyly Diastrophic dysplasia Duchenne, Becker MD Hypophosphataemic rickets (PHEX → ↑ FGF23)
Marfan (fibrillin) Friedreich ataxia Hunter syndrome Léri-Weill (bilateral Madelung)
Cleidocranial dysplasia (RUNX2) Gaucher (β-glucocerebrosidase) Haemophilia A, B —
MHE (EXT1 ~10% sarcoma, EXT2/3 ~1%) SMA SED tarda —
Achondroplasia Sickle cell Lesch-Nyhan —
MED · Schmid + Jansen chondrodysplasia · Kniest Hypophosphatasia — —
Malignant hyperthermia Osteopetrosis — infantile malignant — —
Ehlers-Danlos · OI I–IV — — —
Osteopetrosis tarda (Albers-Schönberg) · osteopoikilosis · NF1 — — —

Multiple patternsCMT (AD, AR, X) · osteopetrosis (AD, AR) · OI (AD, AR) · NF (AD, AR) · SED (AD, X) · hypophosphataemic rickets (XD commonest, AD, AR)

No inheritancefibular hemimelia · McCune-Albright / fibrous dysplasia (GNAS — sporadic mosaicism, non-Mendelian)

Non-Mendelian inheritance (imprinting, anticipation, mosaicism)

Phenomenon Definition Example
Genomic imprinting Expression depends on parent of origin Prader-Willi (paternal defect — hypotonia, obesity, hypogonadism; chr 15) · Angelman (maternal — epilepsy, tremor, smiling)
Anticipation Earlier onset + more severe in each generation · trinucleotide repeats Huntington's (CAG, chr 4, AD), myotonic dystrophy, Friedreich ataxia, fragile X
Mosaicism Post-zygotic mutation → mixed cell lines Fibrous dysplasia, McCune-Albright
Variable penetrance/expressivity Same gene, varying degree —
Genetic drift Chance change in genotype frequency —
Trisomy Extra chromosome Down (21)

Lesch-Nyhan syndrome

DefectX-linked recessive · deficient HGPRT (purine metabolism) → ✓ ↑ serum uric acid

Featurescognitive impairment · choreoathetosis · self-mutilation (biting fingertips), aggression · fractures, hip dysplasia, progressive scoliosis · surgery outcomes ≈ CP · ↑ HO, femur fixation failure

Lab Condition
↑ Serum uric acid Lesch-Nyhan
↑ Urine keratan sulfate Morquio
↑ Ca, ↓ PO₄ Hyperparathyroidism
↑ Glucocerebrosides Gaucher
Chromosome 15 deletion Prader-Willi

How it's asked

"Prader-Willi / Angelman — parent-of-origin expression?"genomic imprinting

"Symptoms earlier each generation?"anticipation

"Genetic defect NOT following Mendelian inheritance?"Gs-α defect (McCune-Albright) (✗ FGFR3, sulfate transporter, fibrillin, glucocerebrosidase)

"Chorea at 46, father at 55?"autosomal dominant with anticipation (Huntington's)

"Example of anticipation?"more severe and earlier in subsequent generations

"AR pedigree — which condition?"diastrophic dysplasia (✗ DMD, Hunter, haemophilia B, SED tarda — XR)

"X-linked recessive disorder?"haemophilia A (✗ Gaucher, diastrophic — AR, ✗ Prader-Willi — imprinting, ✗ hypophosphataemic rickets — XD)

"AD genes typically cause which defect?"structural

"Pedigree for MHE?"autosomal dominant

"Spotted juxta-articular sclerotic lesions in pregnant woman — inheritance?"AD (osteopoikilosis)

"Radiograph suggesting X-linked dominant disease?"hypophosphataemic rickets

"Lesch-Nyhan — lab finding?"↑ serum uric acid

"Self-mutilating, cognitively impaired, X-linked recessive boy?"Lesch-Nyhan (✗ Léri-Weill — XD, ✗ Apert — AD)

"Bit off his fingertip, purine metabolism disorder?"Lesch-Nyhan

Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis

Basic Science · 53 / 108

1Basics

Definition & epidemiology

JIAchronic autoimmune arthritis >6 weeks in a child <16 yr · diagnosis of exclusion (rule out infection, malignancy, rheumatic fever, IBD, SLE, Lyme)

Sexfemale > male

Jointsknee > hand/wrist > ankle > hip > C-spine

Genes & lab markers

HLA Subtype
DR4 Polyarticular
DR8, DR5, DR2.1 Pauciarticular

RF✓ seronegative common (RF+ <15% · 97% normal RF) · RF+ → chronic, destructive, progresses to adult RA

ANA"positive is diagnostic" (Orthobullets) · "so often false-positive that alone it has no diagnostic value" (OB) · ANA+ → ↑ uveitis risk

HLA-B27positive in only ~8%

ESR, CRPfrequently elevated (OB) · Orthobullets: basic serology often normal, not diagnostic · anaemia, ↓ albumin, leukocytosis

Serology does NOT confirm JIA✗ "definitive diagnosis by serology" (false)

3Presentation

Symptomsmorning stiffness, limp that improves during the day, joint swelling, fever, rash, visual change

Diagnostic features (one required)rash · RF · iridocyclitis · C-spine involvement · pericarditis · tenosynovitis · intermittent fever · morning stiffness

Classification

Type % Joints Features
Pauciarticular (oligoarticular) 50% — most common <5, large (knee, ankle), asymmetric Girls 1–4 yr (peak 2–4) · M:F 1:4 · affected limb LONGER · highest uveitis/blindness risk (TO) · best prognosis
Polyarticular 30% >5, hand/wrist Wrist ulnar deviation + flexion, MCP stiff in extension, radially deviated fingers · 60% remission
Systemic (Still's) 20% Multiple Fever, rash, splenomegaly, anaemia, ↑ WBC, ESR, CRP, platelets · M = F · 5–10 yr · lowest uveitis risk · worst prognosis

Onsetearly (pre-teen) vs late (teens)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Uveitis & ophthalmology screening

Iridocyclitis (anterior uveitis)often asymptomatic/indolent · rapid irreversible vision loss if missed · up to 30% lose vision

Risk by type (TO)✓ pauciarticular > polyarticular > systemic · pauciarticular + ANA+ highest (~75%, OB2240 · ~20% in OB1331 — read the options)

Screening✓ urgent slit-lamp exam · every 4 months if ANA+, twice yearly if ANA− · pupil asymmetry → immediate

Eye finding Condition
Anterior uveitis JIA (pauci, ANA+) — urgent, preventable
Superior lens dislocation Marfan
Inferior lens dislocation Homocystinuria
Corneal clouding Hurler (not preventable), Hunter (mild)
Ptosis Pompe

Key radiology findings

X-rayoften normal early · late juxta-articular osteopenia, destruction

C-spinekyphosis, facet ankylosis, atlantoaxial subluxation → ✓ flexion–extension C-spine films before intubation/surgery

5Management

First lineDMARDs (etanercept — TNF inhibitor · rituximab — anti-CD20 · azathioprine — purine synthesis inhibitor · IA methotrexate/etanercept) · intra-articular steroids · NSAIDs/high-dose aspirin (less now) · gold occasionally · frequent ophthalmology exams

Painless elbow loss of motion + multiple joint pains (13-yr-old pitcher)✓ DMARDs (JIA, not OCD — ✗ microfracture, drilling, immobilisation)

Operativesynovectomy (failed medical) · epiphysiodesis for LLD (affected leg longer) · osteotomy deferred to maturity · arthrodesis/arthroplasty for severe disease

6Complications

Prognosis50% resolve · 25% mild disability · 25% crippling arthritis or blindness

Lead toxicity (related)

Leadinhibits ✓ PTHrP → growth inhibition · ↓ BMD (animal studies)

X vs Y

Pauciarticular JIA Lyme arthritis
Joints Knee, oligo Knee, oligo
Rash No Erythema migrans
Antibiotic response ✗ ✓
Association Uveitis Bilateral facial palsy, carditis

How it's asked

"JRA type with highest iritis/blindness risk (TO)?"pauciarticular (✗ systemic — lowest)

"4-yr-old girl, knee effusion, limp better later in day, ANA+ low titre — next workup?"ophthalmology evaluation (✗ skeletal survey, MRI, bone scan, factor levels)

"Highest risk of anterior uveitis needing ophthalmology?"pauciarticular JRA with +ANA (✗ Marfan — lens, ✗ Hunter — cornea)

"Urgent ophthalmology to prevent irreversible complication?"6-yr-old girl, 2 mo knee swelling, fever, ↑ ESR (JIA)

"5-yr-old, oligoarthritis better at end of day, antibiotics failed, RF− — association?"uveitis (✗ facial palsy — Lyme)

"FALSE about JIA?""definitive diagnosis by serology"

"4-yr-old with presumed JIA — likely serum result?"normal RF (✗ normal ESR/CRP, ✗ ASLO, ✗ HLA-B27)

"Peak onset of pauciarticular JRA?"2–4 years

"14-yr-old with JIA before rectal surgery — required work-up?"cervical (flexion–extension) radiographs

"Painless elbow stiffness + polyarthralgia in young pitcher — first line?"DMARDs

"Lead toxicity inhibits?"PTHrP

Legal Considerations in Orthopaedic Practice

Basic Science · 55 / 108

1Basics

Medical negligence — four elements

Element Definition
Duty Obligation to meet the professional standard of care (same specialty)
Breach of duty ✓ Failure to exercise the diligence and care a reasonable, prudent person would under similar conditions — deviation from standard of care
Causation Breach directly caused the injury
Damages Money awarded for injury

Standard of care (legal)✓ reasonable treatment showing knowledge, skill, diligence and care (what a reasonable physician would do) · ✗ majority norm, ✗ minimum, ✗ average, ✗ "what you'd want for family"

Corrected error, good outcome, lawsuit✓ defend: error occurred but was corrected, little/no damages (✗ settle, ✗ claim no error, ✗ contact patient directly)

Litigation statistics

Trendortho suits ↑ 13% 2003–2008 (ageing population) · ortho 7th most sued specialty · ~33% of claims pay out · defence ~$47,000

Causes"improper performance" 45% · procedures: joints (non-fusion), open/closed reductions · diagnosis: OA (21%), joint disorder, femur fracture

Subspecialty✓ arthroplasty most sued (29.9%) > spine (22%) > trauma (17.7%) · most cases won by surgeon · highest plaintiff success: fasciotomy (48%), infection (43%)

Highest total indemnity by fracture✓ tibia (> femur despite fewer cases)

Wrong-site claims84% pay indemnity vs 30% of other ortho claims

Law Content

IRB

OriginNuremberg Code (Nazi physicians) · dignity, autonomy, informed consent, fair selection, equal burden/benefit · mandates trial consent process

Vulnerable populationsfetuses, pregnant women, children, prisoners (✗ "prisoners are not"), cognitively impaired, students, employees

No IRB needed✓ internal QI study (even with name, DOB, MRN) · ✗ MRN-based research, phase III device trial, CP children, RCT

5Management

Informed consent (emergencies, minors, interpreters, competency)

Five elements (research + procedures)voluntariness · provision of information (risks, benefits, alternatives, no-treatment consequences, lay terms) · competency · understanding · decision · ✗ jurisdiction

Late-stage dementia excluded from trial✓ competency

Situation Action
Unconscious, life/limb-threatening, no family ✓ Confirm + document necessity with a colleague of similar expertise, then operate
GCS 10, fiancé present, IIIC tibia Two surgeons of similar expertise (fiancé has no legal standing)
Intoxicated, compartment syndrome, refusing, no translator ✓ Fasciotomy after documenting necessity (lacks capacity; friend can't consent)
Non-emergent (closed femur), patient lacks capacity ✓ Delay; obtain next-of-kin consent (daughter before mother)
13-yr-old, urgent tibia fracture, parents abroad (German) ✓ Call parents with a German interpreter (✗ cousin, ✗ child translating)
Limited English ✓ Certified live interpreter → certified telephone (✗ family, ✗ minors, ✗ own limited Spanish)
Jehovah's Witness ✓ Crystalloid, starch (Hetastarch), colloid · ✗ whole blood, RBC, platelets, plasma, autologous/donor-directed · cell saver = individual choice
Consent says resident only retracts, attending leaves before cementing ✓ Ethically unsound — patient misled ("ghost surgery" → battery)

Next of kinlegal guardian → spouse → children → parents → siblings → grandchildren

Implant originbovine → Hindu · porcine → Jewish, Muslim

Surgeon seropositivedisclose HIV, HBV, HCV at scheduling of exposure-prone procedure · needlestick transmission: HIV ~0.3% · HCV ~1.8% · HBV 6–30% (30% if HBeAg+)

Wrong-site surgery & Universal Protocol

Joint Commission Universal Protocol (2004)pre-procedure verification (documents, labelled imaging displayed, blood/implants/equipment available) · site marking · time-out

Site markingsurgeon's initials (or "YES"), permanent marker, with the patient, in pre-op holding, visible in the surgical field after draping · ✗ "X" · ✗ "NO" on other limb · ✗ plantar foot for TKA · may be delegated to resident/PA/APRN (LIP accountable)

Correct sequence✓ read consent with patient → mark site → regional block → time-out → incise

JCAHO time-outcorrect patient, side, site, procedure · ✗ antibiotic not mandated · all team members speak (✗ only surgeon) · any member can lead · attending present in room

Two teams (ortho + plastics) both presentsingle time-out sufficient · otherwise repeat

Late-arriving residentgreet the team · no mandate to repeat time-out

Wrong side about to be incised (resident)✓ ask surgeon to revisit safety checklist

Trendreported wrong-site surgeries ✓ increased since 1997/2003 protocols (↑ reporting) · 12.7/million · ortho + podiatry 41%

WHO checklist (2009)sign-in (before induction: ID, site, consent, airway, blood-loss risk) · time-out (team introductions, antibiotics within 60 min, imaging displayed) · sign-out · ✗ "implants + rep present" not a component · ↓ mortality, complications, ↑ crisis adherence · surgeon most effective member

Errors & disclosure

Error Response
Wrong-side incision noticed ✓ Immediately discuss with patient + family (then risk management) · never alter record
Wrong knee incised in TKA ✓ Close wound, complete correct TKA, tell family after surgery
Wrong spinal level (T7 not T6) ✓ Complete correct level, document, apologise, inform after · ✗ abort
Intraop MCL laceration ✓ Tell the patient the MCL was injured and plan adjusted
Documentation error ✓ Addendum only — altering record is illegal, no one can authorise it

Full disclosure + apology + responsibility + prevention plan✓ ↑ patient satisfaction (not ↑ litigation, not guaranteed prevention) · accept responsibility ≠ blame

Second opinionethical (not legal) duty to disclose error/standard-of-care breach to patient · ✗ legal duty to testify (but subject to subpoena) · ✗ duty to tell original surgeon · only patient can transfer care

Impaired colleague (drinking attending)✓ express concern to the attending → escalate to chief if needed · ✗ operate unsupervised

Communication & patient safety

Communication breakdown✓ most common cause of errors harming patients · leading cause of wrong-side surgery, medication errors, diagnostic delays, loss to follow-up (✗ SSI) · 70% of sentinel events · ↑ costs + complications

Communication affectspatient satisfaction, adherence, physician satisfaction, malpractice · ✗ Stark II litigation

Best ↓ sentinel events✓ culture where all team members feel empowered to speak (flattened hierarchy)

Retained sponge after shift change✓ OR-wide crew resource management training (✗ blame nurses, ✗ single policy)

CPOE✓ ↓ medication errors (incl. allergy errors)

Physicians interrupt23 seconds

Child lost to follow-up after casting (Spanish-speaking)✓ instructions not communicated · discharge transitions need clear follow-up

Summons received✓ notify liability carrier first (assigns attorney) · ✗ call patient · ✗ discard notes

Level of Evidence

Basic Science · 56 / 108

1Basics

Steps of evidence-based medicine

Step Action
1 Formulate an answerable question
2 Gather the evidence
3 ✓ Appraise the evidence (levels of evidence used here)
4 Implement
5 Evaluate the process

Assigning level by study design & follow-up

Level Therapeutic study (JBJS/Oxford)
I High-quality RCT (randomised, blinded, >80% follow-up, narrow CI) · systematic review/meta-analysis of homogeneous Level I RCTs · all-or-none
II Lesser-quality RCT (<80% follow-up, no blinding, improper randomisation, crossover) · prospective cohort / prospective comparative · SR of Level II or heterogeneous Level I
III Retrospective cohort · case-control · retrospective comparative · SR/meta-analysis of Level III
IV Case series (no or historical control)
V Expert opinion, case report, personal observation, bench/animal work

Meta-analysis level= lowest level of included studies (OB, Orthobullets) · one RCT with 70% follow-up in an SR → Level II · meta-analysis of retrospective cohorts → Level III · TO table: meta-analysis of cohort studies = Level 2 (TO) — read the options

RCT with >20% loss to follow-up (TO)✓ downgraded to Level II (attrition bias) even with flawless randomisation, concealment, ITT, blinded assessors (TO)

Reporting guidelinesQUORUM / PRISMA (meta-analyses of RCTs) · MOOSE (observational meta-analyses) · CONSORT (RCTs)

Study typestherapeutic (results of treatment) · prognostic (patient characteristic → outcome) · diagnostic (test efficacy) · economic/decision

Most common study type in ortho literature✓ Level IV case series (58–64%)

AAOS guideline recommendation strength

Strength Evidence
Strong ≥2 high-quality studies
Moderate 1 high or 2 moderate
Weak 1 moderate or ≥1 low
Consensus Expert opinion — only for interventions preventing loss of life or limb

GRADE framework (TO)

Baseline (TO)RCTs start High · observational studies start Low · separates quality of evidence (high → very low) from strength of recommendation (strong / weak)

Downgrade (5) (TO) Upgrade (3) (TO)
Risk of bias (no blinding, no concealment, attrition, early stopping) Large magnitude of effect — RR >2 or <0.5 (+1) · RR >5 or <0.2 (+2)
Inconsistency (heterogeneity, high I²) Dose–response gradient
Indirectness (PICO mismatch, surrogate outcome e.g. BMD vs fracture) ✓ All plausible confounders would reduce the observed effect
Imprecision (small n, few events, wide CI) —
Publication bias (funnel plot) —

Not upgrade criteria (TO)✗ explained heterogeneity · ✗ PROSPERO registration / PRISMA adherence · ✗ precise CI around small RR 1.2

How it's asked

"Level of meta-analysis of cohort studies (TO)?"2

"RCT, blinded, ITT, flawless randomisation, 22% loss to follow-up (TO)?"Level II — attrition >20%

"Level II study?"prospective cohort (✗ case-control — III, ✗ high-quality RCT — I)

"Level III study?"retrospective case-control

"Moseley sham-surgery RCT, >90% follow-up?"therapeutic Level I

"SR of 15 homogeneous high-quality RCTs?"Level I

"Meta-analysis of retrospective cohorts (QUORUM)?"Level III

"Prospective non-randomised matched PRP cohort, >80% follow-up?"Level II

"Prospective surgeon-preference IT fracture study, 85% follow-up, wide CI?"Level II

"RCT, 20% crossover, unblinded surgeon assessor, 65% follow-up?"Level II

"10 patients, new procedure, no control?"case series

"Surgeon's 25-yr technique article?"Level V

"SR of 6 RCTs, one with 70% follow-up?"Level II

"Most common study type in ortho literature?"Level IV case series

"Third step of EBM?"appraise the evidence

"Upgrade Low → Moderate in GRADE (TO)?"all plausible confounders would reduce the effect

Ligaments

Basic Science · 57 / 108

1Basics

Function & composition

Functionrestrict motion · stabilise joint · proprioception (mechanoreceptors, free nerve endings)

Compositionwater · type I collagen (70% dry weight) · elastin (more than tendon) · lipids · proteoglycans · epiligament (some) · fibroblasts · low vascularity + cellularity

Ligament vs tendonligament: less collagen, more PG + water, less organised fibres, rounder fibroblasts

Blood supplyenters at insertion (unlike tendon) · uniform microvascularity · ACL/PCL: middle geniculate artery

Insertion anatomy (direct, indirect, Sharpey fibres)

Indirect (fibrous) Direct (fibrocartilaginous)
Frequency Most common —
Mechanism Superficial fibres → periosteum · deep fibres → bone via Sharpey fibres (type I collagen) 4 transition zones of ↑ stiffness (force dissipation)
Example MCL into proximal tibia Supraspinatus, native ACL
Direct zone Content
1 Ligament/tendon Aligned type I collagen, decorin
2 Fibrocartilage (unmineralised) Types II + III, small I, IX, X · aggrecan, decorin
3 Mineralised fibrocartilage Type II + significant type X, aggrecan
4 Bone Type I, high mineral

2Mechanism

Viscoelasticity & mechanical behaviour

Viscoelastic✓ behaviour depends on loading rate (faster → stiffer + stronger) → control load rate when testing prosthetic ligaments

Property Definition
Creep ↑ deformation over time under constant load
Stress relaxation ↓ stress over time under constant deformation
Hysteresis Unloading curve ≠ loading curve · area = energy dissipated

Load–elongation curvetoe region (crimped fibres straighten, non-linear) → linear region (slope = stiffness / Young's modulus) → yield (elastic → plastic) → failure

Ligament vs tendon curveligament recruits fibres gradually → longer toe region · tendon → shorter toe, higher loads

Anisotropicstrong in tension, weak in compression · Young's modulus < cancellous bone, > cartilage

Failure

Mechanismsequential collagen fibre rupture · no plastic deformation

Siteadults: midsubstance · children: bony avulsion at junction of mineralised + unmineralised fibrocartilage

Grade Injury
I Mild sprain
II Moderate / partial tear
III Complete tear

5Management

Healing & graft incorporation

Phase Time Events
Inflammatory 1–7 d Neutrophils, macrophages · type III collagen · TGF-β1, IGF, PDGF, BMP-12/13, bFGF
Proliferative 7–21 d → type I collagen · weakest day 5–21
Remodelling >14 d —
Maturation ≤18 months —

Healed MCL at 1 year vs normal✓ ↑ number of fibres · type III proportion back to normal · cross-links only ~45% · ↓ fibre mass + diameter · scar → ↓ ultimate strength, adhesions

Impair healingintra-articular (ACL < MCL) · age · immobilisation · smoking · NSAIDs · DM · alcohol · ↓ bFGF, NGF, IGF-1 · ↓ procollagen I, COMP, tenascin-C, tenomodulin, scleraxis

Improve (experimental)extra-articular · CD44 knockout · IL-10 · IL-1 receptor antagonist · macrophage depletion · MSCs (tunnel healing) · PDGF-BB (↓ adhesions, no strength gain) · G-CSF (tunnel) · BMP-2/-12 (cuff) · collagen–PRP hydrogel for primary ACL repair · CGRP (MCL)

Allograft vs autograft ACLallograft: ✓ prolonged inflammation, slower incorporation, more large-diameter fibrils · tendon grafts heal to tunnel by fibrovascular scar → Sharpey-like fibres (✗ native 4-zone insertion) · intra-articular part: necrosis → host synovial repopulation → revascularisation → ligamentisation

How it's asked

"Order of direct insertion zones?"ligament → fibrocartilage → mineralised fibrocartilage → bone

"MCL tear — structure and insertion?"ligament; Sharpey fibres (indirect)

"Why control loading rate testing a prosthetic ligament?"ligaments are viscoelastic

"MCL sprain — mechanical property?"non-linear stress–strain on initial loading (✗ isotropic, ✗ strongest in compression)

"Grade III MCL at 1 yr vs normal — ↑?"gross number of fibres (✗ type III, ✗ cross-links, ✗ diameter)

"Allograft vs autograft ACL inflammatory phase?"prolonged

Material Properties

Basic Science · 60 / 108

1Basics

Definitions

Term Definition Units
Load Force on a body N
Stress Internal force intensity = force / area Pa (N/m²)
Strain Relative deformation = ΔL / L None
Elastic deformation Reversible — returns to shape when unloaded —
Plastic deformation ✓ Irreversible — doesn't return to original length —
Toughness ✓ Energy absorbed per volume before fracture = area under stress–strain curve J/m³
Creep ✓ ↑ deformation (strain) over time under constant load —
Load (stress) relaxation ✓ ↓ stress (peak load) over time at constant strain/elongation —
Hysteresis Loading ≠ unloading curve (viscoelastic) · area = energy dissipated —
Ductility Ability to deform plastically under tension before rupture —
Strain rate Strain ÷ time —
Finite element analysis Complex shape split into triangles/quadrilaterals, forces balanced —

Creep in polyethylene linersplastic deformation under load without wear debris · TO note: creep "at high temperature, below elastic limit"

Creep (TO, FNB)answer key not provided by source · options: ↑ length under constant load, ↓ length under constant load, stiffness change from viscoelasticity, ↑ stiffness with time · Orthobullets definition = increased deformation under constant load

Stress–strain curve (yield, elastic, plastic, toughness, fatigue)

Axes (TO)✓ strain on X-axis, stress on Y-axis

Feature Meaning
Elastic zone Linear — returns to shape (A → B "elastic behaviour") · Hooke's law: stress ∝ strain
Young's modulus Slope in elastic zone = stiffness (resistance to deformation)
Yield point / yield strength ✓ Elastic → plastic transition · stress producing a set permanent deformation
Plastic zone Permanent deformation
Ultimate (tensile) strength Highest point — maximum stress / load to failure · then necking (ductile)
Breaking (fracture) point Failure
Toe region Ligament/tendon — crimped fibrils straighten

Implant absorbing most energy before failure✓ high toughness (✗ modulus, ✗ yield stress alone, ✗ brittle)

Two plates load–displacementlarger area = tougher · higher peak = stronger · earlier break = more brittle

Hip screw returns to shape after bendingelastic deformation

Metal characteristics

Fatigue✓ failure below ultimate tensile strength after repetitive cycles · depends on stress magnitude + cycles · ✓ mode of failure of a nailed femur that fails to unite (cyclic fatigue) · ✗ static/catastrophic (single supraphysiological load)

Endurance (fatigue) limitmax cyclic stress never causing failure · arthroplasty implants designed above physiological loads · fracture fixation only until union (TO)

Bone vs implants (TO)bone ultimate strength ~4× lower than steel → fails first in single load (periprosthetic fracture) · bone resists fatigue better (remodelling repairs microcracks)

2Mechanism

Material descriptions

Type Definition Examples
Brittle Linear to failure, little/no plastic deformation PMMA, ceramics, hydroxyapatite
Ductile Large plastic deformation before failure Metals
Viscoelastic Stress–strain depends on rate + duration of load (internal friction) Ligaments, bone, cartilage
Isotropic Same properties in all directions Metals (steel, titanium), golf ball
Anisotropic Properties depend on load direction Ligaments, bone

Viscoelasticity (creep, stress relaxation, anisotropy, strain rate)

Ligamentsviscoelastic → strength depends on rate of load · creep, stress relaxation, non-linear elasticity · ✗ isotropic · load–elongation region B = linear elastic after fibrils straightened (A = toe, C = ultimate load, D = energy, E = ultimate elongation)

Cortical bone — ↑ strain rate✓ ↑ elastic modulus AND ↑ ultimate strength · physiological rates (<0.1/s) elastic + ductile · high-impact rates brittle · modulus up to 2× in falls (TO)

Bone true statement (TO)✓ time-dependent viscoelastic properties · ✗ isotropic · ✗ less fatigue-resistant than steel · ✗ repetitive tension → oblique fracture (→ transverse)

Anisotropy of cortical bone (TO)compressive strength 50% > tensile · longitudinal modulus 17 GPa vs transverse 12 GPa · longitudinal strength 193 MPa vs 133 MPa

Articular cartilagebiphasic viscoelastic (flow-dependent fluid drag + flow-independent matrix) · modulus changes with duration + rate of load

Allograft ACLmay lengthen by creep

Bone failure (fracture patterns)

Bone strength✓ strongest in compression, weakest in shear (TO), intermediate tension

Load Fracture
Tension Transverse (muscle pull)
Compression Crush · short oblique (fails at 45° plane of max shear) (TO)
Bending Butterfly fragment (convex side fails in tension)
Torsion Spiral · longer bone → ↑ outer-cortex stress
Bending + compression / bending + torsion Short oblique (TO)

Bone compositioncollagen (low modulus, tensile strength) + hydroxyapatite (stiff, brittle, compressive) · remodels to ↑ moment of inertia

Ligaments/tendonstolerate 5–10% strain (bone 1–4%)

Increasing modulus (TO)✓ polymers < bone cement < cortical bone < titanium < stainless steel < cobalt-chromium < ceramics

PMMA modulusbetween cortical and cancellous (OB) · 10× lower than cortical bone (Webb & Spencer quote) — read the options

Ligament modulus< cancellous bone, > cartilage

Strength rankings Order
Ultimate (tensile) strength Co-Cr > titanium > stainless steel > cortical bone
Fatigue limit + tensile strength (TO) Stainless steel < titanium < Co-Cr
Yield strength Cast Co-Cr > titanium > wrought Co-Cr, stainless steel

Titanium vs cold-forged Co-Cr✓ ↓ tensile strength · ↓ fatigue + yield strength · ↑ ductility

Specific materials

Material Composition Pros Cons
Titanium (Ti-6Al-4V) — Most biocompatible · self-passivation (adherent oxide) → corrosion resistant · low modulus (≈ bone) Notch sensitivity, poor wear → ✗ femoral head · more metal debris
316L stainless steel Iron + carbon (L = low, 0.03%) + chromium (~17–18%) + nickel (~12%) + molybdenum (~2.5–3%) · ✗ no cobalt (TO) Stiff, fracture resistant, cheap, ductile Pitting + crevice corrosion · stress shielding
Cobalt alloy Co + Cr + Mo Very strong · better corrosion resistance than steel · TKA femoral components Very stiff → stress shielding (✓ for cemented stems — protects mantle)
UHMWPE — Tough, ductile, resilient, wear-resistant, high fracture toughness Abrasion (third body), thermoplastic, weak in tension · gamma cross-linking ↑ wear resistance, ↓ fatigue + fracture resistance
PMMA Powder: polymer, benzoyl peroxide (initiator), barium sulfate (radio-opacifier), colour · Liquid: monomer, DMPT (accelerator), hydroquinone (stabiliser) Interlocks with bone (grout, not glue) · strongest in compression · ultimate strength at 24 h Brittle, notch-sensitive · poor tension + shear · exothermic polymerisation · hypotension on insertion · fails by microfracture
Silicones — Non-WB joint replacement Poor strength/wear → synovitis
Ceramics Alumina, zirconia Best wear vs PE, high compressive strength, inert Brittle, low fracture toughness, high modulus, low tensile strength, poor crack resistance

316L components (TO)chromium → oxide layer (anti-rust, passivation) · nickel → manufacturing (austenite) · molybdenum → protects oxide layer from pitting

5Management

Corrosion (galvanic, fretting, crevice, passivation)

Type Mechanism Key facts
Galvanic ✓ Electrochemical potential between two dissimilar metals in conductive medium (anode corrodes) 316L + Co-Cr = highest risk · steel very susceptible · Co-Cr "most susceptible" of listed (✗ Ti, ceramics, PE) · cerclage wire SS on Ti stem · mixed-metal neck/stem
Crevice O₂ tension difference in cracks/crevices 316L most prone · titanium least · plate countersinks, cementless cups
Fretting ✓ Micromotion at contact sites under load → passive layer scraped off → pits, grooves, oxide debris Head–neck taper (trunnionosis), ↑ serum Co/Cr, synovitis, ALTR · commonest cause of modular mid-stem junction failure · ↑ with more interfaces · worsens over time · ceramic heads ↓ it
Fretting fatigue Cyclic load + small oscillation → cracks ✓ Initiates mid-stem modular junction fracture (completed by bending) · risk: high BMI, poor proximal bone support
Pitting Local depassivation → holes Stainless steel
Intergranular Grain-boundary attack —
Stress corrosion High tensile stress + corrosive environment (manufacturing) —
Degradation / oxidative Polymers / free radical —

Self-passivation✓ reason titanium is corrosion resistant (oxide layer forms spontaneously)

Ceramic properties & fracture

Ceramic disadvantage✓ low fracture toughness (poor crack resistance)

Most serious CoC complication✓ fracture (fragments embed → third-body wear) · squeaking (annoying, may herald failure)

CoC THA: new squeak + stiffness + LLD, fractured ceramic, normal CRP/ESR✓ thorough debridement + one-stage head + liner revision (✗ two-stage — no infection)

Structural mechanics (moment of inertia, bending rigidity, tension band)

Area moment of inertia✓ ∝ distance² of material from the centre of mass (tube > solid rod of same area) · ✗ density, ✗ modulus

Implant Bending rigidity ∝
Solid pin / rod radius⁴ (diameter⁴)
Hollow (cannulated) nail ~radius³
Plate thickness³ (2× thickness → 8× stiffness)
Ex-fix pin diameter⁴ · pin stiffness ∝ 1/(bone–rod distance)³ · fragment contact most important

Plate on tension side✓ minimises bending stresses in the plate (fracture closes under load, bone shares load)

Strain sharing5 mm displacement across one 5 mm gap = 100% strain · shared across multiple gaps (each 5 → 6 mm) = 20% → 80% ↓ strain (Perren)

Stress risers around plates/prostheses (TO)✓ overlap plate with stem, gap <8 cm risky · interprosthetic distance <110 mm risky in weak bone · end-screw fracture 1–3% (non-locking), 2.6% (locking) · ✓ oblique end screw · ✓ non-locking end screw in all-locked construct · ✗ unicortical end screw — NOT recommended (weaker) · insert non-locking before locking screws

Screw mechanics (TO)

Term Fact (TO)
Core (root/minor) diameter = drill size · determines strength, fatigue resistance, bending strength, tensile strength
Major (thread) diameter Thread depth (major − minor) → pull-out strength, stripping torque
Runout Shaft–thread junction · weakest part of partially threaded screw
Pitch Distance between threads · lead = advance per turn
Mechanical advantage = circumference / pitch (✗ pitch/circumference)
Pull-out strength ↑ outer diameter, ↑ cortical thickness · ✗ thread density (if 5–6 threads in cortex) · ✓ ↓ cortex thickness ↓ pull-out
AO 3.5 mm cortical screw drill ✓ 2.7 mm (TO key)

Screw types (TO)non-tapping (tap needed) · self-tapping (cutting flutes) · self-drilling · cancellous (deep threads; partially threaded = lag) · locking (threaded head) · Herbert headless (variable pitch → compression) · interference (no compression, presses graft)

Plating principles (TO)

MIPO ideal (TO)✓ comminuted metaphyseal fractures of long bones (✗ abraded skin, ✗ compound, ✗ diabetic)

DHS barrel (TO)standard (long) 38 mm · short 25 mm · lag-screw thread 22 mm

Bridge plating (TO)plate spans comminution, screws away from fracture

Working length (TO)✓ distance between the screws/pins closest to the fracture on either side (✗ "max distance between proximal-most and distal-most screws" = bridge span confusion) · ↑ working length → more flexible, ↓ stress risers · unicortical screw has shorter working length than bicortical · ex-fix: pin/cluster spacing · >3 diaphyseal screws add little

Bioabsorbable polymers (TO)

Polymer (TO) Modulus Strength retention Full resorption
PGA ~7 GPa · highest initial strength · hydrophilic 2–4 wk 6–12 mo (TO) · 1–2 mo (Orthobullets)
PLLA ~3 GPa · hydrophobic methyl group 3–6 mo 2–5 yr
PLGA 50:50 ~2 GPa · most amorphous 1–3 wk ✓ 1–2 mo — fastest of all
PCL ~0.4 GPa · rubbery — 2–4 yr
Magnesium ~45 GPa (≈ bone) · H₂ gas 3–6 mo 6–12 mo
β-TCP Brittle — 6–18 mo (matches bone formation)
Calcium sulfate Very low — 1–2 mo

PGA vs PLLA (TO)✓ PGA higher initial strength but degrades much faster

X vs Y

Creep Stress relaxation Hysteresis
Held constant Load Strain/deformation —
Changes ↑ Strain ↓ Stress Energy lost

1Basics

Branches in the hand (TO)

Carpal tunnelmedian nerve enters hand deep to flexor retinaculum → divides into lateral and medial divisions (TO)

Division (TO) Supplies
Lateral (stouter) Thenar 2½: APB, opponens pollicis, superficial head FPB · 1st lumbrical · sensation thumb + radial index
Medial (mainly sensory) Rest of lateral 3½ digits · 2nd lumbrical

Recurrent motor branchinnervates thenar muscles → ✓ at risk excising a thenar mass (e.g. lipoma) (✗ deep ulnar, ✗ AIN, ✗ palmar cutaneous ulnar · no "recurrent ulnar branch")

AINFPL, pronator quadratus, FDP index (± middle) · terminal branch → wrist capsule only

Median nerve palsy — thenar function & opposition (TO)

Loss of opposition (TO)✓ median nerve (✗ ulnar, ✗ radial, ✗ medial division)

Thumb abductors (TO)✓ median + radial nerve both (APB — median · APL — radial/PIN)

3Presentation

High median palsy vs AIN

Lesion Attempted fist
High median (arm) ✓ Thumb + index can't flex (FPL, FDP + FDS index lost) — "benediction" attitude · middle partly flexes
AIN syndrome Loss of DIP flexion thumb, index, middle (FPL, FDP) — no sensory loss
PIN syndrome Finger drop at MCPs, wrist extends in radial deviation
Radial nerve (arm) Wrist + finger drop
Median at wrist (CTS) Thenar weakness + sensory loss, long flexors intact

How it's asked

"Excising thenar lipoma — nerve at risk?"recurrent motor branch of median

"Can't flex thumb + index on making a fist?"median nerve lesion in the arm (✗ AIN, ✗ PIN, ✗ CTS)

"Nerve supply to thumb abductors (TO)?"median and radial both

"Nerve injury causing loss of opposition (TO)?"median

Strands Double Usually single (double in some viruses/interactions)
2' position Hydrogen (deoxyribose) Hydroxyl (ribose) → less stable
Helix B-form A-form
Location Nucleus Nucleus + cytoplasm
RNA Role

rRNA Ribosome
tRNA Brings amino acids to mRNA
Term Definition
Gene DNA segment encoding a protein
Exon Coding — expressed
Intron Non-coding
Codon 3 nucleotides → 1 of 20 amino acids
Promoter Controls transcription initiation
Enhancer Transcription-factor binding site, regulates transcription
Transcription ✓ RNA polymerase reads DNA → complementary mRNA
Translation mRNA codons → polypeptide
Replication DNA synthesis in S phase
Recombination Meiotic haploid product with new allele combination
Translocation Exchange between non-homologous chromosomes (Ewing, rhabdo, synovial sarcoma)
Cell-cycle phase Event Ploidy
G0 Stable, not cycling 2N
G1 Growth 2N
S ✓ DNA replication → 4N
G2 Gap 4N
M Mitosis → 2N daughters

No "R phase"

Apoptosisprogrammed cell death via intracellular signalling · ✗ lysis (contents spilled) · loss = hallmark of cancer · TNF receptor 1 → ✓ apoptosis

Technique Use
Agarose gel electrophoresis Separates DNA by size (negative charge, small runs faster)

Multiple Sclerosis

Basic Science · 63 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology & pathophysiology

Incidence5 / 100,000 (US) · 20–40 yr · women > men · northern latitudes

Risk factorsstress · smoking · ↓ sunlight / low vitamin D · ✗ not considered hereditary

MechanismCNS demyelination + axonal injury · T-cell–mediated autoimmune · genetic + environmental + infectious factors

Orthopaedic associations↑ fracture risk (falls + ↓ BMD) · osteoporosis (inactivity, ↓ vit D, immunomodulators) · gait abnormality (foot drop) · spasticity, contractures

3Presentation

Clinical definition≥2 episodes of neurological dysfunction separated in time and space (brain, cord, optic nerve)

Symptomsoptic neuritis, diplopia, nystagmus · fatigue, depression · dysarthria, dysphagia · weakness, ataxia, falls · paraesthesia, peculiar sensations (wetness) · bowel/bladder dysfunction · relapsing–remitting transient limb numbness (non-dermatomal)

Examwide-based gait, limb ataxia, slapping foot · spasticity, hyperreflexia, Babinski, Hoffmann · internuclear ophthalmoplegia (MLF) · Lhermitte's sign

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

MRI brain + spinal cord with gadolinium✓ multiple focal demyelinating (T2) lesions · asymmetric periventricular plaques · cord lesions commonest in cervical spine

Genes & lab markers

CSF↑ IgG (80%) · oligoclonal bands · mononuclear pleocytosis (25%) · routine bloods normal (exclude mimics)

Differentialcervical myelopathy · CNS mass · B12 deficiency · sarcoidosis · CNS infection

5Management

Young woman, transient arm numbness, UMN signs, multifocal T2 cord lesions✓ neurology referral (✗ laminoplasty, ✗ plasmapheresis/IVIG — GBS, ✗ B12 — subacute combined degeneration, ✗ lumbar MRI)

Acute relapsecorticosteroids · prophylaxis — interferon-β (↓ relapses, slows RRMS)

Spasticitybaclofen, gabapentin, clonazepam, botox · PT: gentle stretching, progressive resistance · osteoporosis treatment

6Complications

Course✓ relapsing–remitting (commonest) · primary progressive · secondary progressive · progressive relapsing

Life expectancy5–10 yr shorter

How it's asked

"29-yr-old woman, relapsing arm numbness, brisk reflexes, bilateral Hoffmann, multifocal cord lesions — next?"neurology referral

Muscle Biology & Physiology

Basic Science · 64 / 108

1Basics

Gross anatomy

Sheath Surrounds
Epimysium Whole muscle (bundles)
Perimysium Fascicles
Endomysium Individual fibres

Myotendinous junctioninfolded membrane ↑ attachment area · ✓ weak link — commonest site of strains (eccentric)

Sarcomere

Unitsfibre (cell, from myoblasts) → myofibril (chain of sarcomeres) · thick = myosin, thin = actin

Band Content In contraction
A band Actin + myosin Unchanged
I band Actin only Shortens
H zone Myosin only Shortens
Z line Actin attachment, sarcomere border Move closer

Action potential & excitation–contraction coupling

Nerve action potential✓ Na⁺ through voltage-gated channels (positive-feedback depolarisation)

Motor end plateACh released → binds ACh receptor · myasthenia gravis = ↓ ACh receptors · botox = blocks ACh release

Couplingdepolarisation → sarcoplasmic reticulum releases Ca²⁺ → Ca²⁺ binds troponin → tropomyosin shifts → myosin sites on actin exposed → cross-bridges + ATP hydrolysis → sliding · low Ca²⁺: tropomyosin blocks binding sites

2Mechanism

Contraction types (concentric, eccentric, isotonic)

Type Definition Example
Isometric Constant length Push against wall
Isotonic Constant tension — ✓ concentric and eccentric Biceps curl up AND down
Concentric Shortens while contracting ✓ Upward biceps curl
Eccentric Lengthens while contracting · highest forces Lowering phase · ✓ distal biceps rupture (eccentric phase of isotonic exercise)
Isokinetic Constant speed (Cybex machine) —
Plyometric Rapid lengthening → contraction Box jumps

Force generation & fibre types (cross-sectional area, recruitment)

Force / maximal tension✓ most dependent on cross-sectional area (number of contracting myofibrils) · ✗ fibre type, ✗ length (Blix curve), ✗ morphology (pennation angle)

Speed + duration of contraction✓ fibre type

Progressive overload (strength training)✓ muscle fibre hypertrophy (↑ fibre size), ↑ coordination

Type I (slow twitch) — "slow red ox" Type II (fast twitch)
Metabolism Aerobic, oxidative Anaerobic, glycolytic (IIa oxidative-glycolytic, IIb glycolytic)
Energy Krebs / oxidative phosphorylation ATP-CP
Use Endurance · low strength, low speed Sprinting · high strength + speed
Features ↑ mitochondria, vascular ↑ ATPase, low triglyceride · fatigue rapidly (IIb)
Atrophy First to atrophy with deconditioning —

Recruitment order (size principle)✓ Type I (slow, fatigue-resistant) → Type IIa (fast, fatigue-resistant) → Type IIb (fast, fatigable) · no "slow fatigable" units

Metabolic systems

System Duration Example Notes
ATP-CP (phosphagen) <20 s 100 m sprint Anaerobic, no lactate · creatine supplement (cramps)
Lactic anaerobic 20–120 s 400 m Glucose → lactic acid
Aerobic Long, low intensity Distance running Krebs cycle, glucose + fatty acids

6Complications

Muscle injury & healing

Soreness (DOMS)oedema + inflammation of connective tissue · worse with unaccustomed eccentric exercise · peaks 24–48 h · ↑ CK

First cells after injury (contusion)✓ neutrophils (free radicals may ↑ damage)

Strain✓ myotendinous junction (eccentric) · inflammation → fibrosis · contraction bands seal membrane defects · basal lamina scaffolds preserved

Healing phasesnecrosis → inflammation → repair → fibrosis · regeneration needs macrophage clearance (NSAIDs may impair)

Fibrosis✓ TGF-β1 · regeneration trophic factors IGF-1, bFGF · angiotensin-II receptor blockade ↓ fibrosis

Atrophydisuse/denervation → fatty infiltration, ↑ fatigability · single-joint muscles atrophy faster · ↓ CSA → ↓ force

How it's asked

"Upward biceps curl — contraction?"concentric

"Distal biceps rupture during dumbbell curl — phase?"eccentric phase of isotonic exercise

"Isotonic contraction?"both upward and downward biceps curl

"True of skeletal muscle?"duration + speed depend on fibre type (✗ force depends on length/fibre type)

"Progressive overload in adults →?"fibre hypertrophy

"Cross-sectional area determines?"maximal tension

"Force most dependent on?"cross-sectional area

"Motor-unit recruitment order?"slow fatigue-resistant → fast fatigue-resistant → fast fatigable

"First cells after thigh contusion?"neutrophils

"Promotes fibrosis after muscle injury?"TGF-β1 (✗ IGF-1, bFGF — regeneration)

"Calf strain — structure affected?"musculotendinous junction (C)

"Na⁺ through voltage-gated channel →?"nerve action potential

Nonunion and Bone Defects

Basic Science · 67 / 108

1Basics

Definitions

Nonunionarrest of fracture repair, no further capacity to heal without intervention

FDA definition (TO)✓ ≥9 months since injury + no visible progressive healing for 3 months

Delayed unionnot healed in expected time · failure to unite by 6 months (Orthobullets) · 3–6 months (TO) · varies with site and soft-tissue injury

Large segmental defectfunctional nonunion

Causes

Commonestinadequate stabilisation + poor blood supply · infection · smoking (biggest modifiable risk) · location (scaphoid, distal tibia, 5th MT base) · segmental/butterfly (intercalary blood supply)

Smokingnicotine vasoconstriction + CO binding Hb · prior smokers also at risk · > NSAIDs, BMI >40, delay >24 h · reamed nailing ↓ nonunion (✗ risk)

Spine fusion in smoker✓ maintain smoking cessation best ↓ pseudarthrosis

4Diagnosis & Classification

Classification & biology

Type Biology Mechanics Appearance Treatment
Hypertrophic Adequate vascularity Inadequate stability (too much motion) Abundant callus, no bridging ("elephant foot") ✓ ↑ mechanical stability only (e.g. exchange nail, compression plate) — rarely needs graft
Oligotrophic Usually adequate Malreduction/distraction (displacement) · or overly stiff construct Little/no callus Improved fixation ± biological stimulation
Atrophic Inadequate blood supply Inadequate immobilisation (Orthobullets) · excessively stiff fixation (OB) No callus, sclerotic/rounded ends ✓ Revision fixation + bone graft (autologous ICBG gold standard · BMP · DBM, cancellous chips) + healthy soft tissue
Septic Infected — Variable callus Staged: debride, beads/VAC, flaps, transport · eradicate infection
Synovial pseudarthrosis Sealed canals + fluid-filled pseudomembrane — False joint · bone scan "cold cleft" Resect non-viable ends, stable fixation

Classification axes (TO)epiphyseal / metaphyseal / diaphyseal · septic / aseptic · hypertrophic / oligotrophic / atrophic · pseudarthrosis

Biopsy of aseptic nonunion after ex-fix✓ bland cartilage callus (failed endochondral healing) · ✗ PMNs (infection) · ✗ Haversian/lamellar bone (healed)

Locked plate distal femur — short working length, high screw density✓ high stiffness → low strain → oligotrophic nonunion → fatigue failure · revised with long working length, healed without graft

Work-up

Historymechanism, treatments, metabolic/nutritional/immune status, NSAIDs, nicotine · pain on axial loading

Examneurovascular, soft tissue, nonunion mobility, deformity

Labs✓ CBC, ESR, CRP (infection) · total protein, albumin · vitamin D (deficient in 60–70%), TSH, PTH

Most accurate test for septic nonunion✓ CRP (rises in 6 h, peaks 2–3 days, normalises 5–21 days with treatment) · ✗ IL-1, IL-6, WBC, ESR

First step when nonunion + risk of infection (open fracture, erythema)✓ labs: vitamin D, TSH, inflammatory markers before revision

Key radiology findings

X-raycornerstone · 4 views · full-length standing films if LLD

CTif union uncertain (hardware artefact)

5Management

Nonunion (rule out infection: CRP, ESR)
  • Hypertrophic→↑ STABILITY (exchange nail / compression plate)
  • Oligotrophic→IMPROVE FIXATION ± BIOLOGY
  • Atrophic→REVISION FIXATION + AUTOGRAFT + SOFT-TISSUE ENVELOPE
  • Septic→STAGED DEBRIDEMENT, BEADS, FLAP, TRANSPORT

Atrophic ulna nonunion 6 mo after BBFA ORIF, normal CRP/ESR✓ revision ORIF with autograft (✗ observe, cast, IM nail, revision without graft)

Atrophic radius nonunion with gap✓ supportive soft-tissue envelope essential · plating + cancellous graft treats defects up to 6 cm · 3.5 mm plates adequate · poor envelope → vascularised graft + soft tissue

Atrophic tibial nonunion 9 mo, normal labscause ✓ lack of biology → exchange nail + graft

Non-operativebrace · bone stimulators (✗ synovial pseudarthrosis, ✗ mobile nonunion, ✗ gap >1 cm)

Electrical stimulation

Induction coupling↑ BMP-7, BMP-2, TGF-β1, ↑ osteoblast proliferation · ✗ not ↓ osteoclast differentiation

Mode Effect
Direct current ↓ O₂, ↑ pH → ↓ osteoclasts, ↑ osteoblasts
Capacitive coupling (AC) cAMP, collagen, cartilage calcification
PEMF Fibrocartilage calcification
Combined magnetic —

How it's asked

"Ulna fracture 12 mo, pain, abundant callus on X-ray (TO)?"hypertrophic nonunion

"Nonunion after ex-fix, cultures negative — histology?"bland cartilage callus

"Least likely to need added biology?"abundant callus without bridging (hypertrophic)

"Tibial nail 9 mo, no bone formation, normal labs — cause?"lack of biology (atrophic)

"Humerus ORIF, little callus, broken plate, normal labs?"atrophic

"Distal femur locked plate nonunion healed with revision, no graft — initial problem?"high stiffness

"Smoker having spine fusion — ↓ pseudarthrosis by?"smoking cessation

"Obese smoker, NSAIDs, delayed nailing — highest nonunion risk?"smoking

"Open subtroch femur, 9-mo pain, erythema, nonunion — next?"vitamin D, TSH, inflammatory labs

"Septic nonunion — most accurate lab?"CRP

"Atrophic ulna nonunion, normal CRP/ESR?"revision ORIF with autograft

"Atrophic radius nonunion with gap — required besides revision + graft?"supportive soft-tissue envelope

"Induction coupling effects EXCEPT?"↓ osteoclast differentiation

Normal Bone Metabolism

1Basics

Regulators

Peak bone mass & oestrogen

Peak bone masship 16–25 yr · greater in men, African-Americans · ✓ timing varies by anatomic site (appendicular before axial · women earlier at hip, later at spine; spine PBM may be 3rd–4th decade) · strong genetic + environmental influence · correlates poorly with postmenopausal BMD

Bone loss0.3–0.5%/yr after maturity · 2–3%/yr in untreated women years 6–10 after menopause

Most important sex steroid for peak bone mass (men and women)✓ oestrogen

Osteoporosis risk factorsage · female · early menopause · fair skin · family history of hip fracture · low weight · smoking · glucocorticoids · alcohol · low protein · anticonvulsants, antidepressants

Calcium Phosphate
In bone 99% (hydroxyapatite) 86%
Intracellular 1% 14%
ECF 0.1% 0.08%
Serum forms 45% protein-bound · 45% ionised · 10% anion-bound Mostly inorganic H₂PO₄⁻, unbound
Regulated by Gut absorption, bone resorption, renal reabsorption Renal reabsorption/excretion · ↑ PO₄ → ↑ PTH
Daily need See below 1000–1500 mg
Group Calcium (mg/day)
Lactating 2000
Pregnant, postmenopausal, healing fracture 1500
Adolescents / young adults 1300
Adults 750
Children 600

Hormones

Hormone Source Net effect Key mechanisms
PTH (84 aa) Chief cells of 4 parathyroids ↑ Ca, ↓ PO₄ Bone: osteoblasts → IL-1, IL-6, RANKL, M-CSF → osteoclasts (osteoclasts lack PTH receptor) · kidney: ↑ 1α-hydroxylase (25-OH → 1,25-(OH)₂D), ↑ Ca reabsorption, ↑ PO₄ excretion · gut: indirect via 1,25-D · intermittent = anabolic
PTHrP Tumours ↑ Ca Same receptor (osteoblast, kidney) · metaphyseal dysplasia link
Calcitonin (32 aa) Thyroid parafollicular C cells Limited role · ↓ osteoclast number + activity Osteoclasts have calcitonin receptor · ↑ in medullary thyroid Ca / MEN II · used in Paget's, osteoporosis, malignant hypercalcaemia
Vitamin D (secosteroid) Skin UVB + diet ↑ Ca (gut absorption, osteoclast activation) · ✓ mineralisation of osteoid Liver → 25-OH-D · kidney → 1,25-(OH)₂D (↑ by ↑ PTH, ↓ Ca, ↓ PO₄) · or inactive 24,25-(OH)₂D (↓ PTH, ↑ Ca, ↑ PO₄) · terminal osteoclast differentiation
Oestrogen Ovaries Prevents bone loss (↓ resorption + remodelling frequency) ↑ femoral-neck BMD, ↓ hip fracture
Growth hormone Pituitary ↑ Ca (gut absorption, ↓ urinary loss) Excess → gigantism (proliferative zone)
Thyroid hormone Thyroid Physeal growth (chondrocytes, type X collagen, ALP) · ↑ resorption Excess thyroxine → osteoporosis
Glucocorticoids — ↑ bone loss ↓ gut Ca absorption · ↓ formation (cancellous > cortical) — ↓ collagen, ↓ osteoblasts

Phenytoinimpairs vitamin D metabolism

Vitamin D deficiencyosteomalacia, rickets

Therapeutic oestrogen

Benefit↓ bone loss if started within 5–10 yr of menopause · BMD gain 2–4%/yr for first 2 yr

Risks/effects↑ heart disease, breast cancer · ↓ hip fracture, ↓ endometrial cancer (with cyclic progestin) · ↓ urinary pyridinoline, ↓ serum ALP

Anabolic vs antiresorptive

Anabolic✓ intermittent PTH / teriparatide (receptor on osteoblasts, preosteoblasts) · sustained PTH → resorption

Antiresorptivebisphosphonates · oestrogen · SERMs · denosumab (RANKL inhibitor) · calcitonin

4Diagnosis & Classification

Formation (TO) Resorption (TO)
Bone-specific ALP Urine + serum N-telopeptides
Osteocalcin Urine + serum C-telopeptides
P1CP (carboxy-terminal procollagen-1 propeptide) Urine hydroxyproline

— Urine hydroxylysine glycosides
— Serum TRAP, bone sialoprotein

How it's asked

"Peak bone mass most dependent on which sex steroid?"oestrogen

"True of peak bone mass?"timing varies by anatomic site (✗ always end of 2nd decade, ✗ independent of environment, ✗ correlates with postmenopausal BMD)

"Hormone over-secretion affecting proliferative zone (picture)?"gigantism

"True of anti-osteoporosis drugs?"intermittent PTH is anabolic (✗ calcitonin anabolic, ✗ denosumab ↑ osteoclasts, ✗ SERMs anabolic)

"NOT a bone formation marker (TO)?"urine deoxypyridinoline

1Basics

Radiation safety (fluoroscopy technique, lead, pregnancy, cataract dose)

↑ Radiation exposure ↓ Radiation exposure
Standing close to emitter · on the emitter (tube) side Distance (inverse square law) · stay >2 m (6 ft)
Larger body part / large patient (↑ kVp, mA, scatter) Smaller part · mini C-arm
Limb close to X-ray source Limb/patient close to image intensifier (detector)
Large C-arm, continuous fluoro, cine Short beam-on time, pulsed shots, surgeon controls pedal
No collimation ✓ Collimation (↓ scatter, better image)
Geometric magnification (moving intensifier away / tube toward patient) Electronic magnification only when needed
Intraop CT (O-arm) — patient cancer risk ~2× (2.3/10,000) Inverted beam (tube beneath patient, intensifier above)
— Lead apron (≥0.5 mm), thyroid shield (2.5×), lead glasses ≥0.15 mm, sleeves + axillary supplements

Highest scatter with a lateral shot✓ 3 ft from the emitter side (patient attenuates beam toward collector)

False statement (TO)✗ "image magnified by moving X-ray tube away from patient" · (geometric magnification = intensifier away → ↑ dose) · large patients need more exposure, mini C-arm less, collimation ↓ scatter (TO)

Lead aprons (TO)↓ exposure 4× (lateral), 16× (PA)

Distance (TO)at 18 inches from beam only 0.1% of direct dose · scatter <1 mSv outside 2 m · in-beam 1,200–4,000 mrem/min

Dose limits

Site Annual limit
Whole body (TO) 20 mSv
Thyroid / eyes (TO, ICRP) 150 mSv
Hands (TO) 500 mSv
Lens — cataract threshold (NCRP) ✓ 50 mGy cumulative/yr (1 mSv = 1 mGy in diagnostic imaging)

Surgeon cancer risk↑ breast cancer (upper outer quadrant — commonest site, poorly covered by vests → ✓ sleeves + axillary supplements) · lateral images expose breast more · breast as radiosensitive as marrow, colon, lung · cataracts (lead glasses)

Bloodborne pathogen transmission

Exposure Risk
HIV needlestick ✓ ~0.3% (↑ with blood volume, viral titre) · PEP with NRTIs protective

HIV blood transfusion 1 / 500,000 units (window period)
Hepatitis B needlestick 37–62% seroconvert · 22–31% clinical hepatitis
Hepatitis C needlestick 0.5–1.8%

Resident duty hours (ACGME)

Limits≤80 h/week (4-week average) · +10% if educationally justified · 1 day off in 7 · in-house call ≤ every 3rd night · 10 h off between shifts · includes clinical, academic, admin, call

Concerncontinuity of care (handoffs) · patient safety

Handoff failuresomitted current condition, no anticipatory guidance, no rationale, no tasks assigned · ✗ signing out to physician extenders is not cited

"↑ patient exposure on C-arm?"imaging a larger body part (✗ limb near intensifier, ✗ collimation, ✗ mini C-arm)

Ochronosis

1Basics

Alkaptonuria — homogentisic acid oxidase

Defect✓ deficiency of homogentisic acid oxidase (rare inborn error, tyrosine/phenylalanine pathway) → homogentisic acid accumulates in fibrillar collagen

Ochronosisbluish-black discoloration of cartilage + connective tissue · acid polymerises in joints → early degenerative arthritis

Tendonsdeposition in Achilles, patellar tendons → spontaneous rupture

3Presentation

Black urine (homogentisic acid polymerises on standing) · early large-joint OA · back pain

Ochronotic spondylitis4th decade · progressive degeneration

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Spinedisc calcification + disc-space narrowing (multilevel)

Kneechondrocalcinosis, early OA

Genes & lab markers

Diagnosishomogentisic acid in urine

5Management

No specific medical treatment (Orthobullets) · treat as OA: observation, NSAIDs, tramadol, steroids · early arthroplasty for severe disease

Finding Disease

Urate crystals Gout
Lead Plumbism

How it's asked

Operative Blood Loss & Transfusion

Basic Science · 72 / 108

1Basics

Risk factors for transfusion

Strongest predictor✓ preoperative haemoglobin

Othersfemale · older age · larger patient · longer surgery · lateral release in TKA

5Management

Preoperative optimisation

Measure Facts
Labs CBC + MCV (microcytic → iron; macrocytic → folate/B12), folate, B12
Iron / vitamin regimen Iron 256 mg + vit C 1 g + folate 5 mg daily × 30–45 d → ↓ transfusion (only if deficient) · iron s/e: constipation, reflux, pain
Erythropoietin Renal pericapillary cells · > autologous donation efficacy · cost ≈ 4 units blood · high-risk patients · regimens: 600 IU/kg × 4 (days −21, −14, −7, 0) · 300 IU/kg × 15 · 150 IU/kg × 9
Preop autologous donation 1–2 units ≥3 weeks before · Hb >11, weight >50 kg (best if Hb >14) · ✗ not cost-effective, many units wasted
Preop embolisation Vascular tumours (renal cell) within 24 h of surgery

Intraoperative

Method Facts
Acute normovolaemic haemodilution Blood drawn at surgery, replaced with colloid, reinfused · less planning · rarely used (OR time)
Tourniquet SBP + 100–150 mmHg · ≤2 h · Esmarch (✗ infection, tumour) · caution PVD: arterial insufficiency, absent pedal pulses, popliteal aneurysm, arterial calcification → vascular consult
TXA Lysine analogue · blocks plasmin(ogen) binding sites on fibrin → ↓ fibrinolysis, stabilises clot · ↓ blood loss + transfusion (TKA <3%, THA <10%) · IV = oral = topical · no ↑ DVT/PE · PT/PTT unchanged · s/e: seizures (glycine/GABA)
Aminocaproic acid Lysine analogue, plasmin inhibitor · cheaper · no seizure-threshold effect
Collagen (microfibrillar) Activates intrinsic pathway · dry powder, dry field + dry instruments
Fibrin sealant Fibrin + factor XIII + thrombin + Ca²⁺
PRP / platelet-poor plasma Haemostasis, healing / autologous fibrin sealant with thrombin + Ca
Gelfoam Animal-skin gelatin · mechanical for low-pressure bleeders · ± thrombin · remove excess (impairs bone healing)
Gelatin–thrombin matrix (Floseal, Surgiflo) Porcine/bovine gelatin + human thrombin · spine epidural bleeding · end of cascade
Cell saver Filter, wash, reinfuse within 6–8 h · complications: coagulopathy (FDPs, TNF-α, ILs — esp. unwashed), wound drainage, contamination, cost

Topical haemostats✗ not cost-effective in TJA · useful in spine (near neural tissue)

TXA dosing & contraindications (TO)

TXA in TKR (TO)✓ oral and IV equal efficacy · ✗ "topical for 2 min better than IV" · ✗ ↑ DVT · ✗ "IV best"

Doses (TO)IV 10–15 mg/kg or 1 g (20 min before tourniquet inflation ± 15 min before deflation) · topical 1.5–3 g in 100 mL for 5 min before tourniquet release · oral 2 g 2 h preop + 2 doses postop (cheapest)

Absolute contraindications (FDA)✓ current subarachnoid haemorrhage, active intravascular clotting, severe hypersensitivity only · ✗ embolic stroke, DVT, AF, factor V Leiden are not absolute

Relative (OB/TO)pregnancy, OCP, seizures, coronary stent <6 mo, prior VTE/CVA/MI (TO list) · renal dosing

Topical TXAalternative route if MI, CAD, CVA history

Postoperative transfusion

1 unit PRBC↑ Hb ~1 g/dL

Thresholdconsider at Hb <8 g/dL · ✗ routine transfusion >8 (no ↑ mortality, even cardiac patients) · transfuse if symptomatic (orthostasis, fluid-resistant tachycardia)

Allogeneic bloodimmunomodulation → ↓ cell-mediated immunity, ↑ infection

Platelet count Action
<5,000 Transfuse (spontaneous bleeding)
<50,000 Transfuse before surgery
<100,000 Consider before surgery
>100,000 Not needed

Platelets✓ blood product most likely contaminated (stored at room temperature) · 10/million units · gram-positive commonest

Mild postop anaemiaoral iron

Jehovah's Witness

Allogeneic bloodforbidden (some individuals consent)

Acceptable✓ continuous closed-loop cell salvage (✗ batch-processed — breaks continuity) · colloids, coagulation factors (personal choice) · bovine-derived haemostats generally OK · human-plasma products individual decision

Profound intraop haemorrhage✓ continuous closed-loop cell saver (✗ TXA infusion, ✗ EPO — too slow)

How it's asked

"True about TXA in TKR (TO)?"oral and IV equal efficacy

"TXA true EXCEPT?""increases fibrinolysis"

"Absolute contraindication to TXA in THA?"none of these (embolic stroke, DVT, AF, FVL)

"Jehovah's Witness, profound intraop bleeding?"continuous closed-loop cell salvage

"Blood product most likely contaminated?"platelets

"Strongest predictor of postop transfusion?"preoperative haemoglobin

Opiates & Analgesic Medications

Basic Science · 73 / 108

1Basics

Pain physiology

Definitionsacute (tissue damage) · chronic (3–6 months, no damage needed) · pathological (neuropathic)

Pathwaynociceptors (substance P — depletion ↑ pain threshold; capsaicin depletes it) → A and C fibres → dorsal column + spinothalamic tract → thalamus · brainstem = endogenous opioid modulation

Analgesic mechanisms (bupivacaine, gabapentin, acetaminophen)

Drug Mechanism
Acetaminophen Central · ✓ inhibits PGE2 production via IL-1β · minimal anti-inflammatory · = aspirin for pain · hepatotoxic (avoid in liver disease)
NSAIDs ↓ pain transduction (COX)
Local anaesthetics (lidocaine, bupivacaine) ✓ Block intracellular portion of voltage-gated Na⁺ channel → no depolarisation
Gabapentin / pregabalin ✓ Bind α2δ subunit of presynaptic voltage-gated Ca²⁺ channels → ↓ excitatory neurotransmitter release, ↑ GABA · effective: postherpetic neuralgia, diabetic neuropathy, fibromyalgia · gabapentin = pregabalin, cheaper · taper to stop
Opioids (fentanyl) μ (μ1 supraspinal analgesia, μ2 respiratory depression/dependence) + κ (sedation, spinal analgesia)
Tramadol μ-opioid agonist
Midazolam ↑ GABA-A Cl⁻ influx → hyperpolarisation
Rocuronium Competitive ACh-receptor block
Phentolamine α-adrenergic blocker
Cyclobenzaprine Central · ↑ noradrenaline, serotonin receptors · helps first 2 weeks · ✗ neuromuscular spasticity · interacts with MAOIs, alcohol
Local anaesthetic Duration (± adrenaline) Max dose (± adrenaline)
Lidocaine (amide) 2 h (4 h) 4.5 mg/kg (7 mg/kg)
Bupivacaine (amide) 4 h (8 h) 2.5 mg/kg (3 mg/kg)

Amideslidocaine, bupivacaine (liver metabolism, renal excretion) · esters — procaine, cocaine, benzocaine

Opioid metabolism

CYP2D6 activatesoxycodone, hydrocodone, tramadol, codeine · SSRIs/SNRIs block CYP2D6 → analgesic failure

Morphine milligram equivalentshydrocodone 1 · oxycodone 1.5 · hydromorphone 4 (3–7× morphine) → ✓ hydromorphone > 2× oxycodone

Methadonesynthetic, 3× morphine bioavailability, cheap · CYP450 · distribution 2–3 h, elimination 15–60 h → accumulates · respiratory depression, torsades → pain specialist

5Management

Special populations

Morbidly obese✓ IV PCA dosed on ideal body weight (✗ actual weight, ✗ IM/SC — poor fat vascularity)

Red hair (MC1R mutation)✓ need more anaesthetic (MAC) + higher postoperative opioid doses (esp. women)

Elderly↑ opioid sensitivity (↑ fat %, ↓ hepatic/renal function)

ChildrenAAP dosing guidelines

Opioid prescribing & consumption

Bony > soft-tissue proceduresORIF distal radius uses more than cubital tunnel release

Upper limb consumption75% need ≤15 pills · 45% <5 · reasonable: 15 pills + 1 refill (3–4% use it) · ✓ >50% of prescribed pills unused

Carpal tunnel release✓ ibuprofen 600 mg ≈ oxycodone 5 mg pain scores · most use <10 tablets for ≤2 days · opioids → more adverse events · open = endoscopic · standardised protocol ↓ prescribing

Hand prescribing guide Tablets
Trigger finger, lumps/bumps 0
Mucous cyst, CTR, de Quervain, Dupuytren 10
Wrist ganglion, hand ORIF, LRTI, tendon transfer 20
Large trauma, wrist fusion, DRUJ reconstruction 40

Chronic opioidspain specialist, written contract, in-person refills · for OA → ↑ surgical complications

Weaning opioids 50% before THA✓ better outcome than not weaning, similar to opioid-naïve

6Complications

Pain-pump chondrolysis

Continuous intra-articular local anaesthetic infusionFDA warning · glenohumeral chondrolysis (onset ≥2 months, mean diagnosis 8.5 months) · pain, stiffness, ↓ ROM after shoulder scope → ✓ shoulder radiographs to assess chondrolysis

How it's asked

"Morbidly obese postop analgesia?"IV PCA by ideal body weight

"Distal radius ORIF vs cubital tunnel release — opioid use?"ORIF patient uses more

"Opioid user weans 50% before THA?"better than not weaning, similar to opioid-naïve

"True about CTR pain management?"over half of prescribed pills unused

"CTR analgesia — most accurate?"ibuprofen 600 mg ≈ oxycodone 5 mg

"MME comparison?"hydromorphone > 2× oxycodone

"Binds marked site on voltage-gated Na channel?"bupivacaine

"Binds presynaptic Ca channels?"gabapentin

"Inhibits PGE2 via IL-1β?"acetaminophen

"Red-haired patient — postoperative implication?"higher analgesic doses

Orthopaedic Implants

Basic Science · 74 / 108

1Basics

Determinants

Implant behaviourmaterial properties (modulus, strength) + structural properties (shape, size, moment of inertia)

Screws

Term Definition
Pitch Distance between threads
Lead Distance advanced per revolution
Working length (distance) of a screw ✓ Length of bone traversed by the screw
Root / core diameter Narrowest diameter
Bending strength ∝ inner (minor) diameter³
Pull-out strength ∝ outer (major) diameter² · ↑ with large major–minor difference, fine pitch

Pull-out in osteoporotic bone ↑ bycortical purchase · placement parallel to trabeculae · locking (fixed-angle) construct · PMMA augmentation · ✗ tapping (↓ pull-out)

Pedicle screw pull-outmost affected by bone quality · pedicle screws need the largest force to disrupt bone–implant interface (vs sublaminar cables, laminar/pedicle hooks)

Plate biomechanics (compression, bridging, working length, stress shielding)

Load-bearing devicebest on tension side · bending rigidity ∝ thickness³ · titanium modulus closest to cortical bone

Plate working distance✓ distance between the two screws closest to the fracture on either side · ↓ working distance → ↑ stiffness

Plate function Mechanism
Compression (DCP) ✓ Cortical screw placed eccentrically (away from fracture) in oval hole → head slides to centre → compression · transverse fractures
Concave pre-bend Compresses near and far cortices (transverse fracture)
Neutralisation / protection With lag screws — resists shear, rotation (absolute stability)
Buttress / antiglide Resists shear
Tension band Converts tension to compression
Bridging Spans comminution, screws far from fracture · relative stability, length + alignment · preserves blood supply · flexible but axially stable (long working length) · indirect reduction (distraction) · locking not required
Stability Healing Requirement
Absolute Primary (Haversian/intramembranous) Lag screw ± neutralisation / compression plate · low strain (<2%), high stiffness · no micromotion
Relative Secondary (endochondral) Strain <10% (else fibrous union) · nail, bridge plate, ex-fix, cast

Strainresidual 2 mm gap after lag screws (simple oblique) → greater interfragmentary strain than bridged comminuted fracture (strain shared)

Open vs submuscular platingopen plating ✓ compromises periosteal and medullary blood flow more

LC-DCP vs DCP✓ less contact-induced osteopenia (less plate–bone contact)

Stress shielding✓ ↓ physiological stress in bone because a stiffer structure shares load → resorption (Wolff)

Locked bridge platebiomechanically ≈ external fixator without compression ("internal fixator")

Locking plates (indications, hybrid, unicortical, stiffness)

Indicationsindirect reduction · osteoporotic diaphyseal/metaphyseal fractures · bridging severe comminution · short periarticular segments · can't plate tension side · periprosthetic · proximal humerus, distal radius, distal femur, proximal tibia

Not indicated✗ compression plating of transverse fractures · ✗ simple transverse both-bone forearm · ✗ oligotrophic diaphyseal nonunion · lateral malleolus (least evidence)

Advantagesless angulation in comminuted metaphyseal fractures · axial load borne by the plate (no plate–bone friction needed) · preserves periosteum · ↓ screw toggle

↑ Locked construct stiffness ↓ Stiffness
Bicortical locking screws (vs unicortical — biggest difference in torsion) Unicortical screws
More screws Far cortical locking screws (flexible shaft, unthreaded near cortex)
Longer plate Overdrilling near cortex
Screw divergence from hole <5° Angular cross-threading
Screws closer to fracture (short working length) Screws far from fracture
Stainless steel > titanium · supplemental medial plate —

Unicortical locked vs bicortical non-locked✓ less torsional, greater axial strength

Locked vs non-locked rigid construct✓ ↓ interfragmentary strain (stiffer)

Hybrid platingnon-locking screws first (lag plate to bone = reduction aid) → then locking screws (fixed angle, resist varus collapse; osteoporotic diaphysis)

Closed MIPO locked articular fracturerisk ✓ non-anatomic articular reduction (articular surface must be reduced under vision) · percutaneous → less stripping, ↑ malunion

2Mechanism

Intramedullary nails (rigidity, curvature, dynamisation)

Load-sharing device

Property Proportional to
Torsional rigidity radius⁴ (shear modulus, polar moment) · ↑ by reaming (larger nail) · ↓ by slotting
Bending rigidity — solid nail radius⁴
Bending rigidity — hollow nail ~radius³

6 mm vs 12 mm solid nail✓ 1/16 torsional rigidity (½⁴)

Radius of curvature✓ nail ROC > femur ROC (straighter) → anterior distal cortical perforation risk · closed-section stiffer than slotted · start point affects varus/valgus · healing mostly endochondral

Lockingdynamic (axially + rotationally stable) · static (unstable) · secondary dynamisation: remove proximal screw or move it into the dynamic (long) slot — hole A

External fixator stiffness

↑ Stability (unilateral) Notes
Fracture-end contact Most important overall
✓ Larger pin diameter Greatest single factor — stiffness ∝ r⁴ (2× diameter → 16×)
More pins —
✓ ↓ Bone–rod distance Quick, safe fix (✗ oversizing small-bone pins — fracture risk)
Pins in different planes · multiplanar —
Larger / stacked rods · rods in different planes —
↑ Spacing between pins in each fragment · near pin close to fracture —
Bicortical pins · HA-coated —
Circular (Ilizarov) — ↑ stability
Larger wires · olive wires · extra wires —
Smaller ring diameter · more rings · 2 central rings near fracture —
Wires crossing at 90° · ↑ tension ✓ Tensioned wires → more axial compression, less transverse shear than half pins

5Management

Total hip implants

Stem rigiditylength + radius

Femoral componentneutral or slight valgus (↓ moment arm, ↓ cement stress)

↑ Femoral offset✓ ↑ abductor moment arm, ↓ joint reaction force, ↓ abductor force needed · ✗ ↑ implant strain + medial cement-mantle strain

Additive manufacturing (TO)

Technology (TO) Material Use
FDM PLA, ABS, PEEK filament Anatomical models, splints · PLA Tg ~60 °C → warps in autoclave
SLA Photopolymer resin (UV) Highest resolution · models, autoclavable PSI (HDT >135 °C resins)
SLS Polyamide (nylon), metal powders ✓ Autoclavable PSI cutting guides (HDT >135 °C)
SLM / DMLS Ti-6Al-4V, Co-Cr (full melt) Custom implants, cages, tumour prostheses · ✓ gradient porosity → ↓ effective modulus → ↓ stress shielding
EBM Titanium · vacuum, >700 °C ✓ Porous acetabular cups / cones (pores 400–600 μm) · in-situ annealing ↓ residual stress
Bioprinting Cells in bioink Experimental

AM limits (TO)✗ doesn't eliminate fatigue (micro-voids → need HIP/polishing) · introduces build-axis anisotropy · no intrinsic anti-biofilm property

Solid titanium modulus (TO)~110 GPa vs cortical 10–20, cancellous <3 GPa
