Osteopenia & Osteoporosis

Basic Science · 75 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Prevalence10 million Americans · 200 million worldwide · 34 million US osteopenia

Fragility fractures / yr1.5 million → 700,000 vertebral · 300,000 hip · 200,000 wrist

Commonest fragility fracture✓ vertebral body > peritrochanteric (hip) > distal radius · ✗ "distal radius more common than VCF"

Peak agewrist 50–60 · vertebral 60–70 · hip 70–80

M:F1:4 · men → secondary osteoporosis in 60% (hypogonadism, glucocorticoid excess, alcoholism)

Bone masspeak bone mass attained before 30 yr · failure to attain it = major cause of osteoporosis

Ageing✓ trabecular (cancellous) bone loses strength more than cortical · cancellous ↓ 8.5%/decade vs cortical 5%/decade · volume fraction (apparent density) explains most of the loss

Pathophysiology

Osteoporosis✓ quantitative defect · ↓ bone quantity, normal bone quality (mineralisation) · ✗ qualitative (= osteomalacia)

Causesfailure to build peak mass · bone loss later in life · ↓ loading (non-weight-bearing)

Histologythinned trabeculae · ↓ osteon size · enlarged Haversian + marrow spaces

Osteoclast ruffled border Agents
↑ (activated) PTH · 1,25(OH)₂ vit D₃ · prostaglandin E
Flattened Bisphosphonates · calcitonin

Disuse osteopenia — Wolff's law

Wolff's law✓ bone densifies under repetitive load, ↓ density with ↓ load → disuse osteopenia after non-weight-bearing (e.g. bilateral calcaneus fractures) · dense calcar = load path

✗ Distractor What it actually describes
Hydraulic stiffening Tissue fluid stiffens skeleton → ↑ strength
Hooke's law Stress–strain of a solid
Hueter-Volkmann law Compression inhibits physeal growth
Delpech's law Growth across physis

Genes & lab markers

Gene polymorphismscalcitonin receptor · estrogen receptor-1 · vitamin D receptor · COL1A1 (+ >45 others) · ✗ COMP (= multiple epiphyseal dysplasia) · no single cause

Labsgenerally normal in osteoporosis · ↓ 25-OH vitamin D = most likely abnormal lab after low-energy fracture (✗ hydroxyproline, PTHrP, Sox-9, LRP5) · other labs exclude secondary causes

Vitamin DD₃ → 25-OH D (liver) → 1,25-(OH)₂ D (active) or 24,25-(OH)₂ D (inactive) in kidney · deficient <20 ng/mL · not diagnostic of osteoporosis

Vitamin D replacement (TO)✓ ↑ 25-OH D and ↑ 1,25-(OH)₂ D, ↓ sclerostin (TO) · osteocytes have 1,25-D receptors → ↓ sclerostin synthesis → ↑ bone formation (TO)

Bone remodelling (TO)peak mass at 30 yr · resorption ~10 days, formation ~3 months · up to 20% of skeleton remodelled/yr (TO)

Bone turnover markers (TO) Examples
Formation (osteoblast) P1NP / P1CP (type 1 collagen propeptides) · ALP (total, bone-specific) · osteocalcin
Resorption (osteoclast) CTX-1, NTX-1 (telopeptides) · hydroxyproline · pyridinoline / deoxypyridinoline · bone sialoprotein · TRAP · cathepsin K
Osteocyte activity RANKL · OPG · Dickkopf-1 · sclerostin
Reference markers (IOF) ✓ serum CTX-1 (resorption) + serum P1NP (formation) (TO)

BTM uses (TO)fracture risk (↑ BTM ∝ risk, independent of BMD) · monitor compliance/response — resorption markers fall >40% within 3 mo of bisphosphonate, formation markers fall over 6–12 mo · denosumab → very rapid fall in resorption markers · guide restart after drug holiday · circadian variation — time assays (TO)

Response thresholds (TO)>30–40% ↓ CTX (antiresorptive) or >30% ↑ P1NP (anabolic) at 3–6 mo = response (TO)

3Presentation

Risk factors for osteoporosisincreasing age · postmenopausal · long-term ↓ calcium · steroids · alcohol · smoking · low BMI

Drugs ↑ osteoporosis risk ✗ Not associated
Oral glucocorticoids · androgen deprivation · aromatase inhibitors · protease inhibitors · SSRIs · prolactin-raising antiepileptics (phenytoin) · cytotoxics ✓ NSAIDs · cephalexin · simvastatin · glipizide · allopurinol

Phenytoin mechanismhepatic CYP450 induction → ↑ vit D catabolism · direct osteoblast inhibition · ↓ Ca absorption · ↑ homocysteine · PTH resistance · ↓ sex hormones · ↓ vit K

Disease associationshyperparathyroidism · thyrotoxicosis · type I DM · hypogonadism · malnutrition / malabsorption · immobilisation · RA · alcoholism · vit D deficiency · multiple myeloma

Pelvic ring insufficiency fracturerisk: osteoporosis, corticosteroids, RA, THA with constrained liner (redistributes force to pelvis) · ✗ ceramic bearings · groin pain in elderly · treat bed rest + analgesia

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-raythinned cortices · loss of trabecular bone · kyphosis · codfish vertebra · views: lateral spine, AP pelvis/hip

X-ray sensitivity✓ loss not apparent until 30–40% (Orthobullets/OB5227) · 40% (OB8542) — read the options · normal X-ray ✗ excludes osteopenia

DEXA✓ most accurate, least radiation · lumbar L2–L4 + hip (neck, trochanter, intertrochanter) · measures axial AND appendicular skeleton (✗ "axial only")

Other densitometrySPA reliable in appendicular only (spine unreliable) · QCT ~10× DEXA radiation · conventional MRI ≈ QCT accuracy without radiation · QCT also used to monitor therapy (cost/radiation limit use) (TO)

After low-energy fracture✓ DEXA (✗ MRI pelvis, urine electrophoresis, CT, bone scan) · old multilevel VCFs on MRI, no acute pain → ✓ DEXA then medical Rx (✗ brace, cement, fusion)

Diagnostic criteria (WHO 1994)

Term Definition
T-score BMD vs young normal controls (peak bone mass, 25–30-yr-old same sex/ethnicity)
Z-score BMD vs age-, sex-, race-matched controls
Normal T ≥ −1.0 (e.g. T −0.7 = normal)
Osteopenia T −1.0 to −2.5
Osteoporosis T ≤ −2.5 (e.g. −2.6, −3.5 = osteoporosis regardless of other factors)

Which scoreT-score for postmenopausal women (lowest of spine, femoral neck, trochanter, total hip; ✗ peripheral sites) · Z-score for premenopausal women, young men, children → metabolic bone disease in young · ✗ osteopenia/osteoporosis "by Z-score"

WHO limitsno data to define criteria for men or other ethnic groups · T-score tells nothing about metastases

Vit D levellow but ✗ diagnostic · DEXA remains the diagnostic test

Biopsyafter tetracycline labelling · rules out osteomalacia

Type I vs Type II

Type I (postmenopausal) Type II (senile)
Age 50–70 >70
Bone Almost exclusively trabecular Trabecular > cortical
Fractures Distal radius, vertebral Hip, pelvis
Calcium Net negative (↓ gut absorption, ↑ urinary loss) Poor absorption
Vit D ↓ total (not free) 1,25-D —

5Management

Risk factors & fracture prediction

Strongest predictor of future fragility fracture✓ prior fragility fracture (✗ T < −2.5, low vit D, family history, prednisone)

Future vertebral fracture✓ prior vertebral fracture (~4×; risk ↑ with number) > prior hip/wrist · 2 previous VCFs = strongest predictor in postmenopausal women (✗ T −2.8, hip fracture in 6 mo, age >75, bisphosphonate)

After 1 VCF5× 2nd vertebral · 5× hip · 15% ↑ 5-yr mortality · morbidity: back pain, height loss, balance, restrictive lung disease, pneumonia

After hip fracture20% ↑ mortality · men > women · only 1/3 regain prior function · up to 10× 2nd hip fracture

Baseline risk65-yr-old white woman, no risk factors → 10% 10-yr fragility fracture risk

FRAX✓ Fracture Risk Assessment tool · WHO 2008 (Kanis, Sheffield) · 10-yr probability of hip and major osteoporotic fracture (proximal humerus, wrist, hip, clinical vertebral) · with/without femoral neck BMD (TO)

FRAX inputsage · sex · race · height · weight · BMI · prior fragility fracture · parental hip fracture · oral glucocorticoids · RA · secondary osteoporosis · current smoking · alcohol ≥3 units/day (TO)

FRAX ✗ includesfalls risk · bone turnover markers · dietary calcium · vitamin D deficiency (TO) · developed because ~50% of fragility fractures occur with BMD above osteoporosis threshold (TO)

Monitoring response to treatment (TO)✓ DEXA (guideline tool) · QCT (reliable, costly) · BTMs · ✗ FRAX — least useful for assessing adequacy of therapy (TO)

DXA monitoring (TO)every 2 yr (1 yr on anabolics) · BMD ↓ >5% spine or >4% hip (beyond 1–2% precision error) = failure → look for secondary causes · new fragility fracture on treatment = failure regardless of BMD (TO)

Screening (USPSTF / OB)women ≥65 + younger women with risk ≥ 65-yr-old white woman · BMD testing also postmenopausal women with ≥1 risk factor · men ≥70 (OB922) · evidence insufficient for men (USPSTF)

Operative criteria — when to start pharmacological treatment (NOF 2008)

Postmenopausal women / men ≥50 with ANY of Treat?
Hip or vertebral fracture (even without DEXA) ✓
T ≤ −2.5 femoral neck / spine ✓
T −1.0 to −2.5 AND 10-yr hip ≥3% ✓
T −1.0 to −2.5 AND 10-yr major ≥20% ✓
✗ Major ≥10% ✗ not a criterion (TO)
✗ FRAX hip ≥3% without documented osteopenia ✗
T −2.0, hip 1.5%, major 8.9% ✗ no antiresorptive
T −1.5, hip 4.8%, major 8% (smoker on phenytoin) ✓ Ca 1200 + vit D 800 + bisphosphonate + neuro review of phenytoin + repeat DEXA 1 yr
Fragility fracture / low BMD
  • Hip or vertebral fragility fracture→TREAT
  • T ≤ −2.5→TREAT
  • T −1.0 to −2.5
    • FRAX hip ≥3% or major ≥20%→TREAT
    • below both→CALCIUM + VIT D, NO ANTIRESORPTIVE
  • T ≥ −1.0→NORMAL

Calcium & vitamin D

Age Daily calcium
1–3 yr 500 mg
4–8 yr 800 mg
9–18 yr (e.g. 13-yr-old) 1000–1500 mg
Adults >50 / osteoporosis 1200–1500 mg

Vitamin D800–1000 IU/day (NOF, >50 yr) · prevention dose 400–800 IU (OB418/OB6265) · on 1200 mg Ca alone after DRF → ✓ add 800–1000 IU vit D (✗ 1600–2000 IU, ✗ ↑ Ca to 2000, ✗ calcitonin)

Healthy 52–55-yr-old woman✓ 1000–1500 mg Ca + 400–800 IU vit D · ✓ 1500 mg + 1000 IU (✗ 2200 mg, ✗ 5000–10,000 IU) · ✗ semiannual BMD · ✗ estrogen (HRT ✗ approved for treatment — OB6265)

Premenopausal, T −2.2, DRF✓ 1200 mg calcium (prevention dose, ✗ enough for T < −2.5)

EffectsCa ↓ fracture risk 34% · vit D ↓ hip 10%, non-vertebral 7% · high-dose (~800 IU) ↓ hip 24%, non-vertebral 30% · best for type II (senile)

Osteoporosis drugs

Drug Mechanism Key facts
Non-N bisphosphonates (etidronate, clodronate, tiludronate) Toxic ATP analogue → osteoclast apoptosis Esophagitis, ulcers, ONJ, atypical fractures
N-bisphosphonates (pamidronate, alendronate, risedronate, zoledronate, ibandronate) Inhibit farnesyl pyrophosphate synthase (mevalonate) 1st line · renal excretion, unmetabolised · bind hydroxyapatite → detectable years later
HRT (estrogen-progestin) — Type I within 6 yr of menopause · ↓ hip fracture but ↑ breast ca, CAD/MI, stroke, PE/DVT, Alzheimer's
Estrogen only ER on osteoblasts + osteoclasts Prior hysterectomy only · unopposed → endometrial hyperplasia / uterine ca
Testosterone — Hypogonadal men · ✗ FDA-approved
Salmon calcitonin Binds osteoclast receptors → ↓ resorption (from thyroid parafollicular cells) Women >5 yr postmenopause · ✓ ↓ pain of acute VCF (neurotransmitter) · nasal/injection (gastric acid destroys) · rhinitis; nausea, flushing
Raloxifene (SERM) ER agonist bone, antagonist breast (competitive partial agonist) Vertebral fractures only · ✗ anabolic · hot flashes, leg cramps · CI: VTE
Teriparatide PTH 1–34, intermittent → osteoblasts Anabolic · daily SC · transient ↑ Ca
Denosumab Anti-RANKL IgG2 (like OPG) 68/40/20% · CI severe hypocalcaemia
Romosozumab Anti-sclerostin → Wnt ↑ ↓ vertebral 73% at 12 mo, 75% at 24 mo · CV events
Strontium ranelate Dual action (↑ formation, ↓ resorption) ✗ FDA-approved · ↑ MI

FDA-approved for active treatment (OB418)alendronate, risedronate, raloxifene, estrogen, calcitonin → ↓ fragility fractures up to 50%

Bisphosphonates (indications, atypical fractures, ONJ, drug holiday)

Mechanismincorporate at remodelling sites → released in acid resorption pit → taken up by osteoclasts → flattened ruffled border, ↓ function + survival (✗ formation) of osteoclasts

Agent Fracture reduction
Alendronate Hip, spine, wrist ~50% · vertebral 48%, non-vertebral 47% · 70 mg weekly or 10 mg daily
Risedronate Vertebral 65% / non-vertebral 39% (table) · 40% each over 3 yr (text)
Ibandronate Vertebral 77%, hip 41%, non-vertebral 15% (Orthobullets) · ✓ failed to significantly ↓ hip / non-vertebral fracture in BONE trial (TO) — read the options
Zoledronic acid (IV annual) Spine 70%, hip 40% over 3 yr · ✓ only bisphosphonate ↓ all-cause mortality — HORIZON-RFT, infusion within 90 days of hip fracture repair (TO)

Fragility VCF / hip fracture✓ start antiosteoporotic medication regardless of DEXA → ✓ inhibit farnesyl pyrophosphate synthase (✗ calcitonin receptor inhibition, ✗ RANK activation, ✗ ER inhibition, ✗ continuous PTH)

Atypical femoral fracture (AFF)long-term BP → extremely low turnover (↓ urinary NTx) → microdamage unrepaired in lateral tension cortex

AFF radiographic features
Focal lateral cortical thickening (beaking)
Transverse orientation
Medial spike
Simple, non-comminuted pattern
Subtrochanteric / diaphyseal (✗ distal femur)

ONJIV bisphosphonates (✗ oral) · also denosumab · rare · stop inciting drug · long-term IV zoledronate for Paget → ✓ osteonecrosis of mandible (✗ sarcoma — teriparatide risk)

Long-term alendronate✓ diaphyseal femoral insufficiency fractures · ONJ only jaw (✗ femoral head AVN, ✗ retinal detachment, ✗ uterine ca, ✗ scurvy)

Drug holiday✗ in high risk / declining BMD / severe osteoporosis / chronic steroids in first 10 yr → continue (e.g. 8 yr BP, steroids, T −2.6) · after 10 yr → holiday 1–2 yr or until BMD ↓ / fracture (OB)

Drug holiday (TO)alendronate skeletal half-life ~10 yr · reassess after 3–5 yr oral or 3 yr IV ZA · moderate risk → holiday 2–3 yr (oral) / 3 yr (ZA) with DXA + BTM · high risk → continue or switch · common practice holiday after 3–5 yr (TO)

No drug holiday (TO)denosumab (rebound) · anabolics (BMD lost) · SERMs (no residual effect) (TO)

Anabolic agents (teriparatide, abaloparatide, romosozumab)

Teriparatiderecombinant N-terminal PTH 1–34 (of 84 aa; chief-cell hormone) · activates adenylate cyclase · 20 µg/day SC (abdomen/thigh) · max 2 yr · ✓ stimulates osteoblast function · only US anabolic (OB, older)

Dosing patternintermittent (daily) → anabolic · continuous infusion → net resorption + hypercalcaemia (e.g. 2-wk infusion)

Intermittent PTH effects↑ osteoblast number · activates existing osteoblasts · lining cells → osteoblasts · ↓ osteoblast apoptosis · osteoblasts secrete RANKL + IL-6 (coupling)

Anabolic mediators (TO)↑ IGF-1 · ↑ FGF-2 · ↓ sclerostin (Wnt/β-catenin ↑) · ↑ Runx2 · ✗ RANKL (= catabolic pathway, with M-CSF) (TO)

Signalling (TO)PTH1R (GPCR) on osteoblasts, osteocytes, renal tubule → Gs-cAMP-PKA (main) + Gq-PKC · both anabolic and catabolic effects (TO)

FDA indications (TO)postmenopausal high-risk · men with primary or hypogonadal osteoporosis · glucocorticoid-induced (TO)

Teriparatide contraindications (TO)
Paget disease / unexplained ↑ ALP
Skeletal malignancy / bone metastases
Prior skeletal radiation
Open epiphyses (children)
Hypercalcaemia
Hypercalciuria / urolithiasis

Osteosarcomarats, dose/duration dependent (3–60× human exposure) · ✗ shown in humans · black-box (since removed per TO; 24-mo course still standard — plateau) (TO)

Adverse effectstransient hypercalcaemia · hypercalciuria · nausea, headache, dizziness, orthostatic hypotension · ✗ hyperphosphataemia · ↑ ALP (formation marker, ✗ ↓)

Monitoring (TO)serum + urine Ca before · serum Ca at 1 mo · hypercalcaemia → ↓ calcium supplement first, then alternate-day dosing (TO)

Fracture effect↓ vertebral + non-vertebral (if anything, preferential vertebral) · Neer: non-vertebral ↓ 35% (20 µg) / 40% (40 µg)

With bisphosphonates✗ concomitant use beneficial (NOT true) — BPs blunt anabolic effect (TO) · IV ZA concomitant ✗ blunts; denosumab + teriparatide → enhanced BMD (TO) · prior antiresorptive blunts (✗ abolishes) response (TO) · OB213138: may be given alone or as adjunct; long-term BP users → stop BP

Refractory osteoporosis (5 yr BP, prior cement augmentation, new VCFs) — ✓ recombinant N-terminal PTH

Abaloparatide (TO)synthetic PTHrP 1–34 (41% homology) · ✓ binds more selectively to RG state (transient cAMP → formation) · 8.5× lower R0 affinity · → less resorption, less cortical porosity, better radius/femoral neck BMD, less hypercalcaemia · ✗ PTHR2 agonist (TO)

PTH1R conformation (TO) Signal Effect
R0 Prolonged Osteoclast → resorption
RG Transient Osteoblast → formation

SclerostinSOST gene, osteocytes · binds LRP5/6, blocks Wnt → β-catenin degraded → ↓ osteoblast differentiation · loss of SOST → ↑ bone mass (van Buchem, sclerosteosis) · ✗ hereditary osteoporosis · ✗ antagonises BMP · ✓ anti-sclerostin antibodies ↑ BMD

Romosozumab✓ inhibits an antagonist of Wnt/β-catenin · humanised IgG2 · dual action (↑ formation, ↓ resorption via ↓ RANKL, ↑ OPG) · ✓ most rapid + greatest ↑ lumbar BMD in 12 mo (13.3% spine, 6.9% hip) (TO)

Romosozumab dose210 mg SC monthly (2 × 105 mg) × 12 mo (TO) · 120 mg monthly × 12 mo (TO17130219) — read the options

Romosozumab indicationFDA Apr 2019 · treatment of postmenopausal women at high risk · ✗ prevention (TO) · very high risk = T < −2.5 + vertebral/hip fracture in 24 mo or >2 vertebral fractures, or FRAX major >30% / hip >4.5% (TO)

Romosozumab trials (TO)FRAME ↓ vertebral 73% at 12 mo · ARCH vs alendronate: 48% fewer vertebral, 38% fewer hip · CV events 2.5% vs 1.9% → black box: ✗ MI/stroke within 1 yr (TO) · must follow with antiresorptive (BMD → baseline 1 yr after stopping) (TO)

Blosozumabinvestigational anti-sclerostin

Denosumab

Mechanismfully human IgG2 anti-RANKL · blocks RANKL–RANK (like OPG) · ✓ inhibits bone resorption · ↓ osteoclast formation, function, survival (BPs ✗ formation) (TO) · ✗ RANK, ✗ OPG, ✗ TNF

Pharmacology✗ binds hydroxyapatite · cleared within 6 mo, undetectable at 1 yr · fully reversible · usable with bone metastases · also GCT of bone

Dose60 mg SC every 6 mo (100% compliance) (TO)

FREEDOM (TO)↓ vertebral 68%, hip 40%, non-vertebral 20% over 3 yr · 10-yr extension: spine BMD +21.7%, no plateau (BPs plateau 3–5 yr) (TO)

Indications (TO)BP alternative (eGFR <30–35, oesophageal disease, intolerance) · second line (TO)

Adversearthralgia · nasopharyngitis · back pain · ONJ (✓ antibody-based therapy linked to ONJ; ~0.04%) · cellulitis · hypocalcaemia (renal impairment, vit D deficiency) · CI severe hypocalcaemia

Abrupt discontinuation✓ rebound turnover → multiple vertebral fractures (✗ hypercalcaemia, ✗ ONJ, ✗ AFF) (TO)

Sequential therapy & treatment transitions (TO)

Anabolic first✓ anabolic → antiresorptive (DATA / DATA-Switch) · antiresorptive first blunts later anabolic response (TO)

Sequence (decreasing BMD gain) (TO)
Romosozumab 12 mo → denosumab (greatest)
Teriparatide/abaloparatide 24 mo → denosumab
Romosozumab 12 mo → bisphosphonate
Denosumab alone
Bisphosphonate 3–5 yr then holiday (moderate risk)
Situation (TO) ✓ Correct ✗ Trap
After 24 mo teriparatide ✓ Immediately (1–3 mo) → denosumab (continued ↑ spine + hip BMD) or BP (ZA 1–2 yr / alendronate 3–5 yr) ✗ drug holiday (lose almost all gain within 12 mo) · ✗ "effects long-lasting, no antiresorptive"
Stopping denosumab ✓ Bisphosphonate — ZA exactly 6 mo after last dose (≤8 mo if CTX-guided) · >2.5 yr use → repeated ZA / 1–2 yr alendronate, monitor CTX at 3 + 6 mo ✗ ZA at 12 mo · ✗ teriparatide after denosumab (transient hip/radius bone loss, no rebound protection)
Stress reaction on ZA (AFF precursor) ✓ Stop ZA, consider teriparatide · protected WB · contralateral femur imaging (bilateral 20–50%) · correct vit D >30 ng/mL ✗ switch to denosumab (absolutely avoid) · ✗ oral alendronate · ✗ continue ZA
Incomplete AFF (TO) Management
Prodromal thigh/groin pain OR lucent lateral cortical line ("dreaded black line") ✓ Prophylactic full-length cephalomedullary nail
Asymptomatic, beaking only, no lucent line Protected WB 2–3 mo + serial X-rays

Thigh pain + lateral cortical thickening on alendronate✓ cephalomedullary nailing (protects femoral neck) · ✗ standard antegrade nail · ✗ CT staging / oncology referral · ✗ observation · nail pearl: exaggerated anterior bow → avoid anterior cortical perforation (TO)

Fracture liaison & post-fracture management

Fragility fracture work-uphip + spine densitometry · labs for secondary causes · Ca 1500 mg + vit D 400–800 IU · protein-enriched diet (✗ low protein diet)

Inpatient hip ORIF with new osteoporosis✓ metabolic work-up as inpatient + osteoporosis clinic appointment (✗ PCP follow-up, ✗ PT, ✗ nutritionist, ✗ orthopaedist starting BP and sending to PCP) · treatment rates after hip fracture surprisingly low

Fracture liaison service✓ more likely to receive DEXA + referral to metabolic bone clinic · ✗ fewer labs / PCP / endocrine visits · ✗ changes fracture care

Vertebral compression fracture & vertebroplasty

Osteoporotic VCFcommonest fragility fracture · T likely < −2.5 · ✗ CT if neuro intact with adequate films · ✗ neoplasm in typical demographic

Vertebroplasty✓ no benefit in RCTs · kyphoplasty for pain persisting >6 wk nonop (limited AAOS strength) · ✗ kyphoplasty in first week for narcotic-level pain

6Complications

Bisphosphonatesesophagitis · dysphagia · gastric ulcer · ONJ (IV) · atypical subtrochanteric fracture

Teriparatidetransient hypercalcaemia · rat osteosarcoma · Paget sarcoma risk (contraindicated)

DenosumabONJ · rebound vertebral fractures on stopping · hypocalcaemia

RomosozumabCV events · hyperostosis · OA · cancer · ONJ · AFF

RaloxifeneVTE · hot flashes · leg cramps

HRTbreast ca · CAD/MI · stroke · PE/DVT · Alzheimer's

Osteoporosis vs Osteomalacia

Osteoporosis Osteomalacia
Defect ↓ mass, normal mineralisation (quantitative) Variable mass, ↓ mineralisation (qualitative)
Age Postmenopausal / elderly Any
Cause Endocrine, age, inactivity, alcohol, ↓ Ca Vit D deficiency/pathway, ↓ PO₄, hypophosphatasia, RTA
Symptoms Pain at fracture site Generalised bone pain
X-ray Axial fractures Appendicular, symmetric, Looser zones
Serum Ca / PO₄ Normal Low or normal
ALP Normal ↑ (except hypophosphatasia)
Urinary Ca High or normal Normal or low
Tetracycline biopsy Normal Abnormal

Antiresorptive vs Anabolic

Bisphosphonate Denosumab Teriparatide Romosozumab
Target FPP synthase / ATP analogue RANKL PTH1R Sclerostin
Action Antiresorptive Antiresorptive Anabolic Anabolic + antiresorptive
Dosing Oral weekly / IV yearly 60 mg SC 6-monthly 20 µg SC daily, 2 yr SC monthly, 12 mo
Persistence Years in bone Gone by 6–12 mo BMD lost without relay BMD lost without relay
Drug holiday ✓ (moderate risk) ✗ rebound ✗ ✗
Signature risk ONJ, AFF Rebound VCFs, ONJ Osteosarcoma (rats), ↑ Ca CV events

How it's asked

"T-score −3.5 / −2.6?"osteoporosis (✗ needs more information, ✗ normal for elderly)

"Osteoporosis T-score?"−2.5 or below (✗ −3.0, ✗ +2.5)

"Postmenopausal, T −0.7, Z −0.4?"normal (✗ osteopenia by Z-score)

"Osteopenia definition?"T −1 to −2.5 (✗ Z-score definitions, ✗ T vs age-matched)

"X-ray shows loss after what %?"40% (✗ 5%, 20%, 90%)

"BMD assessment EXCEPT?"DEXA measures axial skeleton only (false)

"Next investigation after fixation of low-energy hip / old VCFs on MRI?"DEXA scan (✗ bone scan, ✗ cement augmentation)

"Strongest predictor of future low-energy fracture?"prior fragility fracture (✗ T < −2.5)

"Greatest predictor of future vertebral fracture?"2 previous VCFs (✗ T −2.8, ✗ recent hip fracture)

"Which elderly woman at greatest VCF risk?"prior T6 compression fracture (✗ prior hip, ✗ T −3.0)

"Bone that loses more strength with age?"trabecular more than cortical

"Drug NOT associated with osteoporosis?"NSAIDs (✗ SSRI, steroids, phenytoin, protease inhibitors)

"Medication ↑ non-traumatic hip fracture?"phenytoin (✗ simvastatin, glipizide, allopurinol)

"Pelvic insufficiency fracture risk EXCEPT?"THA with ceramic bearings (✗ constrained liner)

"Gene polymorphism NOT linked to osteoporosis?"COMP (✗ calcitonin receptor, ER-1, VDR, COL1A1)

"Lab most likely abnormal after low-energy DRF?"25-OH vitamin D

"Vit D replacement — expected change?"↑ 25-OH D and 1,25-D, ↓ sclerostin (TO)

"Calcium for a 13-yr-old?"1000–1500 mg

"Calcium for 52-yr-old with T −3.0?"1000–1500 mg

"On 1200 mg Ca alone after DRF — adjust?"add 800–1000 IU vitamin D

"Premenopausal DRF, T −2.2 — treatment?"1200 mg calcium

"Healthy 52-yr-old, no risk factors?"1000–1500 mg Ca + 400–800 IU vit D (✗ estrogen, ✗ semiannual DEXA)

"FRAX stands for?"Fracture risk assessment tool (TO)

"Least useful tool to assess response to bisphosphonate?"FRAX (✗ BTMs, DEXA, QCT) (TO)

"NOT an indication to treat?"10-yr major fracture risk ≥10% (TO)

"T −2.0, hip 1.5%, major 8.9%?"do not start antiresorptive

"T −1.5, hip 4.8%, on phenytoin?"Ca + vit D + bisphosphonate + replace phenytoin + DEXA in 1 yr

"NOF 2008 bisphosphonate indication?"T −1.0 to −2.5 AND hip risk >3% (✗ major >10%)

"Teriparatide — NOT true?"concomitant bisphosphonate beneficial (TO)

"Teriparatide anabolic effect NOT mediated by?"RANKL (TO)

"Intermittent PTH effect?"anabolic

"Recombinant PTH that builds bone?"teriparatide (Forteo) — hormone of chief cells (✗ SOST protein, ✗ P-C-P backbone, ✗ parafollicular hormone)

"Net bone resorption?"teriparatide continuous infusion (✗ daily injection)

"Osteoporosis drug with osteosarcoma risk?"recombinant PTH 1–34

"Teriparatide — true?"stimulates osteoblast function (✗ ↓ ALP, ✗ hyperphosphataemia, ✗ inhibits osteoclastogenesis)

"Refractory osteoporosis after 5 yr BP?"recombinant N-terminal PTH

"Abaloparatide vs teriparatide?"binds more selectively to RG state (TO)

"Fastest, greatest ↑ lumbar BMD in 12 mo?"romosozumab (TO)

"Romosozumab — NOT true?"approved for prevention (TO)

"Anti-sclerostin mechanism?"inhibits an antagonist of Wnt/β-catenin (✗ RANKL, ✗ FPP synthase)

"Sclerostin — accurate?"anti-sclerostin antibodies ↑ bone density (✗ SOST → hereditary osteoporosis)

"Direct RANKL inhibitor?"denosumab (✗ romosozumab, blosozumab)

"Antiresorptive, no hydroxyapatite binding, undetectable at 1 yr, OK with metastases?"denosumab

"Denosumab interferes with RANKL → ?"inhibits bone resorption (TO)

"Antibody therapy with ONJ?"denosumab (✗ pamidronate — not antibody)

"Abrupt denosumab cessation risk?"multiple vertebral fractures (TO)

"True about anti-osteoporotic treatment?"denosumab effect short-lived — follow with bisphosphonate (TO)

"Very high risk — best sequence?"anabolic then antiresorptive (TO)

"After 24 mo teriparatide?"switch immediately to denosumab (TO)

"Femoral stress reaction on ZA?"stop ZA, consider teriparatide (TO)

"Bisphosphonate failing to ↓ hip fracture?"ibandronate (TO)

"Therapy ↓ all-cause mortality after hip fracture?"zoledronic acid (TO)

"Fragility VCF — mechanism of indicated drug?"inhibits farnesyl pyrophosphate synthase

"Fracture of long-term bisphosphonate?"transverse subtrochanteric, lateral beak, medial spike

"Long-term alendronate associated with?"diaphyseal femoral insufficiency fracture

"Long-term IV zoledronate for Paget — complication?"osteonecrosis of mandible (✗ sarcoma)

"Thigh pain, lateral cortical thickening, on alendronate?"cephalomedullary nailing

"8 yr BP + chronic steroids, T −2.6?"continue, no drug holiday

"Fragility fracture management NOT included?"low protein diet

"Best osteoporosis care after hip ORIF?"inpatient metabolic work-up + osteoporosis clinic

"Benefit of fracture liaison service?"more likely to get DEXA

"L1 osteoporotic VCF — true?"↓ bone quantity, normal quality (✗ kyphoplasty in week 1, ✗ T −2.1 diagnostic)

"Vertebroplasty for VCF?"no benefit in RCTs

"Disuse osteopenia principle?"Wolff's law (✗ Hueter-Volkmann, ✗ Hooke)

"Minimum BMD for proximal femoral implant fixation?"250 mg/cm³ (TO)

Outcome Measure Tools

Basic Science · 76 / 108

1Basics

SF-36 & patient-reported outcomes

SF-36generic short-form health survey, 36 questions · most widely applied general health instrument · self-administered (office, mail, phone) · validated with normative population data + multilingual translations

SF-36 structure8 scaled scores (each 0–100, equal item weight) · physical functioning · role physical · bodily pain · general health · vitality · social functioning · role emotional · mental health · measures functional ability, well-being, overall health

SF-36 limitation✓ ceiling and floor effects · bias lower > upper extremity · ✗ "no normative data" · ✗ ignores patient perspective · ✗ must be on-site

SF-36 usecompare disease burden · polytrauma with foot injury → lower SF-36 than polytrauma without foot injury (≥12 mo)

SF-1212-item short version · physical + mental health · pre-injury pain-related disability predicts pain at 6 mo

VR-12Veterans Rand 12-item, derived from SF-36 · physical + mental summary scores · HRQOL, disease burden

EQ-5Dgeneric QOL · 5 dimensions (mobility, self-care, usual activities, pain/discomfort, anxiety/depression) + VAS · 243 health states · index 0 = death, 1 = perfect health · 170+ languages

PROMs vs physician-reported✓ better assessment of patient satisfaction (✗ cost-effective care, ✗ ambulation, ✗ disposition, ✗ analgesia needs)

PROMIS

PROMISNIH (2004 Roadmap) · general + disease-specific · adult + paediatric · domains physical, mental, social

Methodcomputer adaptive testing (CAT) + item response theory (IRT) → next item chosen from previous answers → fewer questions, high precision

ScoringT-scores, population mean 50, SD 10

Advantage✓ reduced floor and ceiling effects · quickest, broadest population, highly reliable · high correlation with ODI + SF-36 · ✗ more items · ✗ low reliability/validity

Validated in✓ spine · foot & ankle · upper extremity

Foot & anklePROMIS ✓ superior to FAOS / OMAS at detecting clinically significant change at 6 and 12 mo after ankle fracture · ✗ needs 24 mo follow-up

Legacy measuresSF-36, EQ-5D, HHS, KOOS, DASH, QuickDASH, FAAM, ODI · ✗ IRT-based · validated only in specific populations

4Diagnosis & Classification

Joint-specific scores

Region Scores
Knee KSS · IKDC · KOOS · WOMAC · Lysholm · Tegner · Oxford knee (TO)
Hip Merle d'Aubigné–Postel · Harris Hip Score · modified HHS · HOOS · Oxford hip · Lequesne · WOMAC (TO)
Shoulder DASH · Constant · Oxford shoulder · WOSI / WORC / WOOS · Simple shoulder test · UCLA · SPADI · ASES (TO)

"Not a knee score"✓ DASH (upper limb) (TO)

WOMAChip + knee OA · 24 items: pain 5 · stiffness 2 · function 17 · sum 0–20 / 0–8 / 0–68 · higher = worse · TO key: knee (OA commoner in knee than hip) (TO) · also back pain, RA, JRA, SLE, fibromyalgia

KOOSyoung/middle-aged, post-traumatic OA (also TKA) · 5 domains (pain, symptoms, ADL, sport/recreation, QOL) · 42 items 0–4 → 0–100 (100 = no problems)

Harris Hip Score (THA) Points
Pain (none) 44
Function (gait: no limp, no aid, >6 blocks) 33
Functional activities 14
Physical exam (ROM) 9
Rating 90–100 excellent · 80–90 good · 70–79 fair · 60–69 poor · <60 failed

FAOSlateral ankle instability, Achilles tendinosis, plantar fasciitis · 5 subscales (pain, symptoms, ADL, sport, QOL) · last week · 0–4 → normalised 100 = no symptoms

Constant shoulder score (0–100) Max
Pain (subjective) 15
Function / ADL (subjective) 20
Range of motion (objective) 40
Strength (objective) 25

UCLA shoulder5 sections: pain · function · active forward flexion · strength of forward flexion · patient satisfaction · max 35 · >27 = good/excellent

DASH30-item self-report upper limb · disability/symptom (30 items, 1–5) + optional sport/music/work (4 items) · score = [(sum/n) − 1] × 25 · ✗ calculable if >3 missing · optional module ✗ if any missing

QuickDASH11 items, 5-point Likert · ✗ if >1 missing · 0–100, higher = more disability

Oxford Shoulder Instability Score12 questions · 12 (best) to 60 (worst) · uni- + multidirectional instability

Spine scores

ODIgold standard low back functional outcome · 10 sections × 5 · % = scored / possible × 100 (16/50 = 32%; one section missed → 16/45 = 35.5%)

NDImodified ODI · 10 items · commonest neck pain measure (chronic neck pain, whiplash, radiculopathy) · sum / 50 × 100 · 0% none → 100% complete disability

ASIAspinal cord injury

Cervical myelopathy Based on
mJOA (17 points) UE motor · LE motor · sensory · bladder
Nurick Gait · employment · cord symptoms

5Management

MCID — statistical vs clinical significance

p-valuechance of the observed difference if no true difference · p < 0.05 = statistically significant · ✗ says nothing about clinical relevance

MCID✓ must be identified to judge clinical significance (e.g. KOOS difference, p = 0.04) · ✗ "significant → better outcomes" · ✗ larger sample needed

ODI 38.4 vs 34.1, p = 0.002✓ statistically significant but likely below MCID (4.3 points) · ✗ NNT (applies to risk) · 80% power / β 0.2 = standard · non-randomised → confounding (✗ Hawthorne = behaviour change when observed)

Predictors of pain & patient satisfaction

Predicts moderate/severe pain at 6 mo after MSK injury ✗ Not a predictor
Failure to complete high school · pre-injury pain-related disability · disability compensation · moderate/severe pain at discharge ✓ Age, gender (male)

↑ Satisfaction AND quality✓ interpreter for limited English proficiency · ✗ requested hydrocodone · ✗ early MRI · ✗ waiting-room amenities · ✗ early pain referral

HCAHPSinpatient satisfaction survey · concern it may not reflect quality

Generic vs Specific

Generic Disease/joint-specific
Examples SF-36, SF-12, VR-12, EQ-5D, PROMIS WOMAC, KOOS, HHS, DASH, Constant, ODI, NDI
Strength Compare burden across diseases Sensitive to joint change
Weakness Ceiling/floor effects (SF-36) Population-limited

How it's asked

"Instrument with general + disease-specific measures, validated in spine, foot & ankle, upper extremity?"PROMIS (✗ SRS-22, ODI, SF-12, PHQ-9)

"PROMIS vs other tools in spine?"reduced floor and ceiling effect (✗ more items, ✗ poor correlation)

"IRT-based, quickest, broadest, reliable PROM?"PROMIS (✗ DASH, QuickDASH, FAAM, SF-36)

"PROMIS vs legacy in foot & ankle?"superior at detecting change at 6 and 12 mo

"ODI difference 4.3, p = 0.002?"statistically significant, likely below MCID

"KOOS improvement, p = 0.04 — next?"identify MCID to judge clinical significance

"Polytrauma with Lisfranc injury vs without at 12 mo?"lower SF-36

"Limitation of SF-36?"ceiling and floor effects

"Benefit of PROMs (WOMAC) over physician-reported?"better assessment of patient satisfaction

"NOT a predictor of pain 6 mo after MSK injury?"male gender

"↑ Both satisfaction and quality?"interpreter

"NOT a knee functional score?"DASH (✗ WOMAC, KSS, KOOS) (TO)

"WOMAC is used for which joint?"knee (TO key; hip + knee OA) (TO)

Platelet-Rich Plasma

Basic Science · 80 / 108

1Basics

Definition & biology

PRPplasma from own blood enriched with autologous platelets · controversial use to stimulate bone + soft-tissue healing

Optimal concentration3–5× whole blood · >5× inhibits healing · ✗ 10–15× (OB) · therapeutic working definition ≥1 million platelets/mL (TO)

Yield (TO)30 mL venous blood → 3–5 mL PRP (TO)

Proposed actions↑ ECM deposition · ↓ pro-apoptotic signals · ↓ joint inflammation · acts via growth factors (✗ aggrecan / structural polypeptide production)

Knee OA mechanism✓ ↓ COX-2 and NF-κB + ↑ hyaluronic acid by synoviocytes · ↓ cartilage degradation · ↑ chondrocyte proliferation (✗ NSAIDs — no HA ↑; ✗ steroid — nuclear receptor; ✗ HA injection — rooster comb/bacterial lubricant)

Genes & lab markers

Growth factors releasedPDGF · TGF-β · VEGF · IGF-1 · EGF · CTGF · FGF-2 · >30 bioactive proteins; 7 fundamental GFs (TO)

Cell adhesion molecules (TO)fibrin, fibronectin, vitronectin (TO)

Platelet granule (TO) Contents
α-granules (unique to platelets, most abundant, 50–80/platelet) ✓ Growth factors (VEGF, FGF) · αIIbβ3, P-selectin · PF4, fibrinogen · vWF, factor V · chemokines (CXCL1, IL-8) · >300 proteins
Dense (δ) granules (2nd) Serotonin, histamine · adenine nucleotides · polyphosphates · calcium

Degranulation (TO)secretory proteins activated (histones, carbohydrate side chains) → bind receptors on MSCs, osteoblasts, fibroblasts, endothelial + epidermal cells (TO)

pH dependence (TO)✓ GF effect IS pH dependent · PDGF highest at pH 5.1 · TGF-β lower at pH 5.0 than 7.1/7.6 (Liu) (TO)

Release kinetics (TO)~70% of GFs in first 10 min after activation · ~100% within 1 h · small amounts over platelet lifespan (8–10 days) (TO)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Types of PRP (TO)

Type (TO) Platelets / leukocytes Notes
Pure PRP / leukocyte-poor (LP-PRP) High / minimal Tendon, cartilage — less inflammation · neutrophils ~100 cells/mL
Leukocyte-rich (LR-PRP / L-PRP) High / high Acute inflammatory response · ↑ IL-1β, TNF-α, MMPs antagonise anabolic effect · neutrophils ~5000 cells/mL · most commercial products
Platelet-rich fibrin (PRF) — No anticoagulant → fibrin scaffold → slow sustained GF release
Autologous conditioned plasma (ACP) ✓ High platelet, low leukocyte Leukocyte-poor form · tendon, cartilage, OA
PRGF Balanced GFs, minimal leukocytes Controlled healing

Preparation (TO)

Anticoagulant✓ citrate-based: acid citrate dextrose (ACD) A/B · CTAD · sodium citrate → prevent platelet activation, preserve function · ✗ EDTA damages platelet membrane (EDTA still listed as used by one TO source) · ✗ heparin, ✗ hirudin (TO)

Differential centrifugationsediments constituents by specific gravity · soft spin 1500 rpm × 5 min → hard spin 6000 rpm × 15 min (TO)

Method (TO) Steps PRP location
PRP method ACD tubes · don't chill · soft spin → transfer supernatant → hard spin Lower 1/3 = PRP, upper 2/3 = PPP · platelet pellet at bottom → resuspend in 2–4 mL plasma
Buffy coat method Store 20–24 °C · high-speed spin first · remove supernatant · low spin / leucocyte filter to remove WBC Bottom RBC · ✓ middle = platelets + WBC (buffy coat) · top PPP

Activation✓ calcium chloride (Orthobullets/OB3629 key) · thrombin + calcium chloride both initiate activation (OB, TO) · ✗ citrate dextrose (= anticoagulant) · ✗ TGF-β, IL-8, PDGF (= released products)

Unanswered TO item (TO24050608)"agent commonly used to activate platelets?" · options TGF-α / IL-2 / calcium chloride / thrombin · answer key not provided by source · sources support both CaCl₂ and thrombin — read the options

Point of carein-office / in-OR devices, minutes (✗ 24-h lab processing)

Dosing (TO)up to 3 injections, 3–6 wk apart, 5–10 mL (TO)

Regulation

FDAPRP + preparation devices "cleared" (510(k)), ✗ "approved" · not a drug · ✗ FDA-approved for OA (TO) · blood products exempt from HCT/P pathway (21 CFR 1271) → ✗ regulated like other cellular/tissue products (OB) · one TO table labels PRP "FDA approved" vs ACS not — read the options

5Management

Clinical evidence

Indication Evidence
Lateral epicondylosis (tennis elbow) ✓ Potential benefit — PROs + pain
BPTB donor sites (patellar + tibial plug) ✓ Improved PROs of knee function, ↓ patellar tendon gap
ACL graft ligamentisation / maturation ✗ Not supported
Rotator cuff repair augmentation ✗ No added benefit (possibly detrimental) · safe
Meniscal repair ✗ No clear evidence
Achilles tendinopathy (midsubstance/insertional) ✗ Not supported
Acute ligament/tendon/muscle injury, chronic tendinopathy No consensus
Osteoarthritis No consensus, lack long-term follow-up
Fracture healing / fusion Limited evidence, some detrimental

TO indication listknee OA · partial tears (RC, hamstring, quadriceps, common extensor/flexor origin, triceps) · Achilles + chronic tendinosis · meniscus grade 1–2 ± parameniscal cyst · non-union / delayed union · partial ACL / ATFL tears (TO)

Orthokine — autologous conditioned serum (ACS) (TO)

Orthokineblood incubated with pyrogen-free glass spheres at 37 °C × 24 h → leukocytes stimulated → ✓ ↑ IL-1 receptor antagonist (IL-1Ra) + IGF-1, PDGF, TGF-β1 → cell-free serum injected · ✗ platelet concentration · ✗ stem cells · ✗ steroid (TO)

Process (TO)10–60 mL blood · spin 3000–3500 rpm · serum stored −20 °C · injected once–twice weekly · hypoxia-like (HIF-1α) response, ✗ EPO (TO)

WADA (TO)✗ banned; may need TUE (M1/M2 blood manipulation categories) (TO)

ACS (Orthokine) vs PRP (TO)

ACS PRP
Source Whole blood + glass spheres, incubated Centrifuged whole blood
Mechanism ↑ IL-1Ra (anti-inflammatory) Platelet GFs (PDGF, TGF-β, VEGF)
Best for OA, lumbar stenosis, disc prolapse, muscle injury Tendon, ligament, cartilage, post-surgical
Processing 24 h incubation 15–30 min
Injections Fewer Multiple over weeks
FDA (per TO table) Not approved "Approved" (✗ per other TO key)

How it's asked

"Ideal anticoagulant for PRP?"acid citrate dextrose (✗ EDTA, heparin, hirudin) (TO)

"PRP — all true EXCEPT?"use in OA is FDA approved (✓ α-granules, citrate, 1 million/mL) (TO)

"Buffy coat method — layer with PRP?"middle layer (TO)

"PRP — all true EXCEPT?"GF effect NOT pH dependent (false — it is pH dependent) (TO)

"Autologous conditioned plasma has?"high platelet, low leukocyte (TO)

"Orthokine therapy — true?"↑ IL-1Ra to block inflammation (TO)

"Agent used to activate platelets in prepared PRP?"calcium chloride (✗ citrate dextrose, TGF-β, IL-8, PDGF)

"PRP in acute rotator cuff repair — true?"may give no added benefit (✗ 10–15× concentration, ✗ 24-h processing, ✗ regulated like HCT/Ps)

"Attenuates COX-2 + NF-κB, ↑ HA production in knee OA?"PRP (✗ NSAIDs, steroids, HA)

"PRP potential benefit?"tennis elbow, BPTB donor sites

Prophylactic Antibiotics

Basic Science · 83 / 108

1Basics

Overview

Share of antibiotic use25–50% of all antibiotics used are prophylactic

Indicationsimplanted foreign body (e.g. IM nail — ✗ "no antibiotics needed") · bone grafting · large dissection / dead space / haematoma · significant blood loss

✗ Neededcarpal tunnel release · diagnostic arthroscopy controversial

Commonest pathogensS. aureus · S. epidermidis · aerobic streptococci · anaerobic cocci · gram-positive skin flora

Most effective SSI measure✓ IV antibiotics within 1 h before incision (✗ MRSA screening/decolonisation, ✗ horizontal laminar flow, ✗ body exhaust suits) · lumbar spine: ✗ iodine vs chlorhexidine difference, ✗ >10 min scrub, laminar flow helps TJA but ✗ shown in spine

5Management

Timing before incision

Agent Timing
Cefazolin ✓ Within 1 h before incision · ✗ after incision · before tourniquet inflation
Vancomycin Within 2 h before incision
Redosing 3–4 h after first dose for long cases (OB8335) · 2017 CDC: insufficient evidence on intra-op redosing

WHO Surgical Safety Checklist3 checkpoints: before induction · before skin incision · before leaving OR · ✓ antibiotics given within 60 min before incision · ✗ blood products on standby · ✗ VTE prophylaxis ordered · ✗ implant rep · ✗ credentialing

Agent selection & allergy (TO)

Situation Agent
No β-lactam allergy ✓ Cefazolin (or cefuroxime) — 1st-generation cephalosporin (S. aureus cover, low toxicity, penetration, half-life, cost) (TO)
Dose Double cefazolin if >80 kg
Penicillin / β-lactam allergy ✓ Vancomycin (TO key) or clindamycin (TO)
MRSA history / high-prevalence area Vancomycin + cefazolin · judicious — ↑ VRE risk
β-lactam allergy + MRSA risk Vancomycin ± gentamicin or aztreonam
Trauma IM nail, no allergy, no MRSA ✓ Cefazolin (MRSA colonisation in trauma only 1–4%) · ✗ vancomycin, ✗ cipro, ✗ metronidazole
Antibiotic class Mechanism
Vancomycin (glycopeptide) ✓ Bactericidal, blocks peptidoglycan cross-linkage · red man syndrome
Cephalosporin Bactericidal, peptidoglycan cross-linkage, ✗ red man
Aminoglycoside Bactericidal, irreversible 30S
Macrolide / clindamycin Bacteriostatic, reversible 50S
Fluoroquinolone Bactericidal, DNA gyrase

Other prophylaxisciprofloxacin: GU surgery, brackish/salt/fresh water wounds · metronidazole: GI surgery, soil/faecal contamination

CDC guidelines — duration

AAOS (TJA)prophylaxis should NOT exceed 24 h after surgery

CDC 2017 (Category IA)✓ clean + clean-contaminated: no additional doses after closure in OR, even with a drain → single perioperative dose sufficient · ✗ continue until drains out · ✗ 24 h for drains · ✗ duration by length of surgery

CDC 2002start within 1 h · appropriate agent · stop within 24 h

Dental prophylaxis in TJA patients (AAOS & ADA)

At-risk TJA patients
All patients for first 2 yr after TJA
Immunocompromised — drug- or radiation-induced
Inflammatory arthropathy (RA, SLE)
Previous PJI · type I DM · malnourishment · haemophilia · HIV · malignancy

Evidencelimited / inconclusive → clinical judgment

Dental regimen (1 h before) Drug
No penicillin allergy Amoxicillin or cephalexin 2 g PO · IV/IM: cefazolin 1 g or ampicillin 2 g
Penicillin allergy Clindamycin 600 mg PO (or IV)

Splenectomy prophylaxis

Vaccinespneumococcal (all, incl. functional hyposplenism) · Hib + meningococcal C conjugate (if not immunised) · influenza annually

Antibioticslifelong oral phenoxymethylpenicillin or erythromycin

Sterilisation & disinfection (TO)

Sterilisation vs disinfection✓ endospores: disinfection leaves spores · sterilisation destroys spores + pathogens (TO)

Arthroscopic equipment✓ plasma sterilisation (✗ autoclave — damages delicate scopes · ✗ ETO — 12-h cycle, can't turn around same day · ✗ formaldehyde dip) (TO)

Plasma systems (TO)Sterrad-100: vaporised H₂O₂ (45 min) + radiofrequency (13.56 MHz, 300 W, 0.5 Torr) · Plazlyte: peracetic acid vapour + microwave plasma (O₂/H₂/Ar) (TO)

Spaulding classification (1957)✓ principles of disinfection and sterilisation of reusable devices by risk (✗ skull, pelvic fractures, DDH) (TO)

Spaulding (TO) Definition Examples Reprocessing
Non-critical Intact skin only BP cuffs, stethoscopes, toys, walking aids Clean ± low-level disinfection
Semi-critical Non-intact skin / intact mucosa, no penetration Trans-rectal probes, specula High-level disinfection minimum, sterilisation preferred
Critical Penetrate tissue / bloodstream / sterile area Surgical + dental instruments Sterilisation after every use

Semi-critical touching open lesionstreat as critical (TO)

Methods (TO) Agents
Disinfection — oxidising Sodium hypochlorite · povidone iodine · H₂O₂ · peracetic acid
Disinfection — non-oxidising Chlorhexidine · 70% alcohol · quaternary ammonium · glutaraldehyde, OPA
Sterilisation Autoclave · ETO · H₂O₂ / peracetic acid vapour or plasma

How it's asked

"On WHO Safe Surgery Checklist?"antibiotics within 60 min before incision

"Most reduces infection after lumbar spine surgery?"IV antibiotics before incision (✗ chlorhexidine vs iodine, ✗ laminar flow)

"Optimal antibiotic timing for TJA?"within 1 h before incision (✗ within 2 h, ✗ after incision)

"Most notable impact on SSI?"IV antibiotics within 1 h of incision (✗ MRSA decolonisation, ✗ laminar flow, ✗ exhaust suits)

"Cephalosporin generation used before THR?"1st (TO)

"Penicillin allergy — preferred prophylaxis?"vancomycin (✗ cefazolin) (TO)

"Cefazolin anaphylaxis + prior MRSA, THA — antibiotic?"bactericidal, peptidoglycan cross-link, red man syndrome (vancomycin)

"Femur IMN, no allergy, no MRSA?"cefazolin (✗ none, ✗ vancomycin)

"2017 CDC strong recommendation?"clean-contaminated: single perioperative dose sufficient (✗ continue with drains)

"Most effective sterilisation of arthroscopic equipment?"plasma sterilisation (✗ autoclave, ✗ ETO) (TO)

"Spaulding classification deals with?"disinfection and sterilisation (TO)

Pseudogout (CPPD)

Basic Science · 84 / 108

1Basics

Definitioninflammatory arthritis from intra-articular calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate (CPPD) crystal deposition

Demographicselderly (>60) · rare in young unless associated disease

vs goutolder · more proximal joints · positively birefringent crystals

Associated conditions

Associations
Hyperparathyroidism
Haemochromatosis
Wilson's disease
Gout · RA · SLE
Haemophilia
Long-term haemodialysis (pyrophosphate-like deposition)

Chondrocalcinosispresent in 7% · high association with knee OA (relationship unclear)

3Presentation

Acute attackwarm, erythematous, tender, monoarticular · mimics gout + septic arthritis · ± superficial mineral deposits

Joint frequency
1st ✓ Knee
2nd Wrist
3rd 2nd + 3rd MCP joints

Coursecan cause rapidly progressive joint destruction · inflammatory markers normal / minimally ↑

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Chondrocalcinosis✓ linear calcification of fibrocartilage + articular cartilage · menisci (knee) · TFCC (wrist) · distinguishes CPPD from other secondary OA (gout, haemochromatosis, RA, ochronosis)

Diagnostic criteria — aspiration

Crystals✓ weakly positively birefringent, rhomboid-shaped · WBC moderate (<50,000) · culture negative

5Management

Phase Treatment
Acute NSAIDs · splint (comfort) · intra-articular steroid · rest
Chronic / recurrent Colchicine 0.6 mg PO bid (prophylaxis ↓ recurrence) · intra-articular yttrium-90

6Complications

Complicationspermanent joint damage · renal disease

CPPD vs differentials

Finding Disease
CPPD crystals, chondrocalcinosis ✓ Pseudogout
Monosodium urate, strongly negative needle crystals, punched-out erosions Gout
Destructive pannus, symmetric narrowing, osteopenia RA
Empty osteocyte lacunae Osteonecrosis
Recurrent haemarthroses, wide notch, big condyles Haemophilia
Iron deposition (cirrhosis, pigmentation, DM, cardiac) Haemochromatosis
Homogentisic acid (homogentisic acid oxidase deficiency) Ochronosis

How it's asked

"Knee pain, hyperparathyroidism, meniscal calcification — finding?"CPPD crystals (✗ urate, ✗ pannus, ✗ empty lacunae, ✗ haemarthroses)

"Linear cartilage calcification on knee AP?"chondrocalcinosis (pseudogout) (✗ gout, haemochromatosis, ochronosis)

"TFCC calcification — other joint most involved?"knee (✗ 1st MTP)

Psoriatic Arthritis

Basic Science · 86 / 108

1Basics

Definitionseronegative spondyloarthropathy · DIP-predominant hand arthritis · silvery plaques · uveitis · nail dystrophy

Incidence5–20% of psoriasis patients (Orthobullets, OB210216) · 5–10% (OB3340) · ~20% (OB3581) — read the options · ✗ "most psoriasis patients develop arthritis"

SexM = F

Skin firstplaques precede arthritis by years in 80–85% · arthritis first in ~20% (TO)

Genes & lab markers

HLA-B27positive in ~50% (✗ all patients)

RF and ANAusually negative

Patterns (Wright & Moll) (TO)

Pattern (TO) Features
Symmetrical polyarthritis ✓ Most common · resembles RA but milder · RF-negative (TO)
Asymmetrical oligoarthritis <5 joints, small/medium · 2nd commonest (DIP, PIP, MCP) (TO)
Distal (DIP-predominant) DIP + PIP · acro-osteolysis of tufts + nail changes
Arthritis mutilans Rare · extreme phalangeal + MCP destruction → telescoping · opera-glass hand (doigt / main en lorgnette) · pencil-in-cup
Spondyloarthropathy ~1/3 · SI (usually unilateral) + spine · cervical > thoracolumbar (distinguishes from AS) (TO)

3Presentation

Skinsilvery plaques on extensor surfaces (elbows, knees) · scalp

Handsdactylitis (sausage digit) — up to 50%, toe > finger (TO) · nail pitting (onychodystrophy) · onycholysis (distal nail-plate lifting) · opera-glass hands

Opera-glass handshortened phalanges → excess skin folds transversely, digits retract like telescoping binoculars

Enthesitisup to 40% (TO) · Achilles, posterior tibial tendonitis, plantar fasciitis · heel, foot, hip pain

Otherchronic uveitis · ankylosis of small hand/foot joints more than RA (TO)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Pencil-in-cup✓ DIP: destruction of middle phalanx head + expansion of distal phalanx base · centripetal erosions → joint-space widening (✗ DJD)

Other hand signsacro-osteolysis (distal tuft resorption) · fluffy periostitis (periosteal ossification) · small-joint erosions or fusions (PIP, MCP, wrist) · flail digits

Spinesacroiliitis · syndesmophytes · paravertebral ossification · destructive discovertebral lesions

Diagnostic criteria — CASPAR

Requirementinflammatory articular disease (joint, spine, entheseal) + ≥3 points

CASPAR criterion Points
Current psoriasis 2
Personal or family history of psoriasis 1
Psoriatic nail dystrophy (onycholysis, pitting, hyperkeratosis) 1
Negative RF 1
Dactylitis (current or recorded) 1
Juxta-articular new bone formation on hand/foot X-ray (✗ osteophytes) 1

Unanswered TO item (TO24057301)clinical photo: nail dystrophy, DIP swelling, skin plaques · options psoriatic arthropathy / RA / OA / reactive arthritis · answer key not provided by source

5Management

Medical (mainstay, like RA)NSAIDs · methotrexate · sulfasalazine · cyclosporine · TNF-α inhibitors · rheumatology referral

Treatment — TNF inhibitors

Failed NSAIDs, active disease✓ biologic DMARD — TNF inhibitor (or IL-17, IL-12/23) = ACR first line → ↓ joint destruction + surgery

✗ Option Why not
NSAIDs Symptomatic only, ✗ disease-modifying
Weight loss / bariatric ✗ treats inflammation
Hydroxychloroquine ✗ approved · precipitates / worsens psoriasis
Oral prednisone Weak disease modification · pustular flare on withdrawal
csDMARDs (MTX, leflunomide, SSZ) Limited effect on joint damage + skin

Operativeadvanced disease → digit fusion vs resection arthroplasty · osteotomy, arthroplasty · high infection rates reported

Psoriatic vs other hand arthritis

Psoriatic RA Erosive OA Gout
Joints DIP PIP, MCP (2nd–3rd), ulnar styloid, triquetrum · ✗ DIP DIP, PIP, 1st CMC Any
Erosions Centripetal, pencil-in-cup Marginal, subchondral cysts Central "gull wing", ✗ marginal Punched-out "rat-bite", overhanging edges, sclerotic margins
Other Periostitis, acro-osteolysis, telescoping Ulnar drift, swan-neck, boutonnière Osteopenia, fusiform swelling Tophi
Serology RF− · HLA-B27 ~50% RF+ — —

How it's asked

"Most common form of psoriatic arthritis?"symmetrical polyarthritis (✗ asymmetric oligoarthritis — 2nd) (TO)

"Sausage digits + pencil-in-cup?"psoriatic arthritis (✗ gout, ✗ RA)

"Toe dactylitis, posterior tibial tendonitis, nail pitting?"psoriatic arthritis (✗ scleroderma, ✗ pseudogout)

"Nail pitting + onycholysis — matching radiograph?"telescoping / opera-glass destruction (✗ RA MCP ulnar drift, ✗ erosive OA gull-wing)

"Psoriatic arthritis — true?"DIP erosions often centripetal (✗ most psoriasis patients get arthritis, ✗ all HLA-B27+, ✗ RF/ANA positive, ✗ antifungal)

"Severe PsA after NSAID failure — what would have prevented TKA?"TNF inhibitor (✗ hydroxychloroquine, ✗ prednisone)

PTH & Vit D Physiology

Basic Science · 87 / 108

1Basics

Overview

Organs in Ca homeostasisskin · liver · parathyroid · kidney · bone · small intestine · ↑ PTH + ↑ vit D → ↑ serum Ca

Vitamin D metabolism & serum markers

Step Site Product
7-dehydrocholesterol + UV-B Skin Previtamin D₃ → cholecalciferol (D₃)
Diet / supplements Gut D₂ (ergocalciferol — vegetables, supplements) · D₃ (oily fish, fortified foods) — both inert
Carried on vitamin D-binding protein → 25-hydroxylase Liver 25-(OH) D₃ = calcidiol
1α-hydroxylase (↑ by PTH, ↓ Ca, ↓ PO₄) Kidney ✓ 1,25-(OH)₂ D₃ = calcitriol — active (TO)
24-hydroxylase Kidney 24,25-(OH)₂ D₃ — inactive · 1,24,25-(OH)₃ D₃

Self-regulationhigh 1,25-D → ↑ 24,25-D (inactive) · negative feedback on itself

Skin conversion↓ with age · nearly absent in darkly pigmented individuals

Vitamin D actions↑ intestinal Ca + PO₄ absorption · ↑ bone resorption (Ca + PO₄ release; osteoclastic, TO) → ↑ serum Ca AND ↑ PO₄ · OB219175: vit D (with FGF23) inhibits osteoclast activity → ↑ bone mass — read the options

PTH functions (TO)

Sourcechief cells of parathyroid · trigger hypocalcaemia / hypomagnesaemia

PTH action Effect
Bone ↑ osteoclastic resorption — receptor on osteoblasts → IL-1 → osteoclasts
Kidney ↑ Ca reabsorption (DCT) · ↓ phosphate reabsorption
Kidney ↑ 1α-hydroxylation of 25-OH D → ↑ 1,25-D
Net ↑ serum Ca, ↓ serum PO₄

✗ PTH function✓ "↑ osteoblastic resorption of bone" — no such thing; resorption is osteoclastic (TO)

Genes & lab markers

Best test for deficiency✓ 25-OH vitamin D — long half-life, circulating level ~1000× 1,25-D · ✗ 1,25-D (estimates stores poorly; only a fraction converted)

Half-life 25-OH D 1,25-(OH)₂ D
OB4653 2–3 wk 4–6 h
OB210168 15 days 15 h

Unit conversion1 nmol/L = 0.4 ng/mL · 1 ng/mL = 2.5 nmol/L

25-OH D level Status
<30 nmol/L (<12 ng/mL) At risk of deficiency (NIH, OB217497)
30–50 nmol/L (12–20 ng/mL) Possible inadequacy
≥50 nmol/L (≥20 ng/mL) Sufficient for most
75 nmol/L (30 ng/mL) ✓ Normal (OB217497) · Endocrine Society: >75 nmol/L (>30 ng/mL) needed for max bone/muscle effect
<20 ng/mL Deficient (OB219590)
20–30 ng/mL ✓ Insufficient (OB219590)
30–80 ng/mL Normal range (OB7541)

RDA vitamin D₃400 IU infants <1 yr · 600 IU age 1–70 (incl. pregnancy/lactation) · 800 IU adults >70

5Management

Supplementation & fracture healing

Vitamin D in bone healing✓ regulates inflammatory response at injury site (macrophages, neutrophils, T cells express VDR, convert vit D locally) · ↑ VEGF-mediated vasculogenesis · ↑ MSC commitment to osteoblasts (matrix adhesion, integrins) · ↑ ECM: collagen I, osteocalcin, osteopontin · prevents infection, ↑ muscle/soft tissue

Deficiency↑ fracture + arthrodesis nonunion · ↑ falls in elderly (supplement ↓ falls) · atrophic nonunion work-up → 25-OH D

Insufficiency fracture + 25-OH D 14 ng/mL, normal Ca✓ high-dose vitamin D (✗ observation — at risk elsewhere · ✗ calcium alone · ✗ bisphosphonate before correcting mineral imbalance · ✗ surgery)

Supplement formD₃ preferred · D₂ most available by prescription (US) · hypervitaminosis D rare, high doses tolerated

Vitamin D vs PTH

1,25-(OH)₂ vit D PTH
Serum Ca ↑ ↑
Serum PO₄ ↑ ↓
Gut ↑ Ca + PO₄ absorption Indirect (via 1,25-D)
Kidney — ↑ Ca reabsorption, ↓ PO₄ reabsorption, ↑ 1α-hydroxylase
Disorders Rickets / osteomalacia Hypoparathyroidism · pseudohypoparathyroidism · renal osteodystrophy

How it's asked

"Most active vit D metabolite?"1,25-dihydroxycholecalciferol (✗ 25-OH, ✗ 24,25) (TO)

"Correct regarding vit D?"24,25-dihydroxycholecalciferol is inactive (✗ 1,25-D best lab test, ✗ 1,25-D longer half-life)

"Serum marker after vit D repletion (pathway letter)?"B — 25-hydroxyvitamin D

"Post-hip fracture 25-OH D 75 nmol/L (30 ng/mL)?"normal level

"Atrophic humeral nonunion — value showing insufficiency?"25-OH D 25 ng/mL (✗ 35 / 45 ng/mL, ✗ any 1,25-D value)

"Sacrococcygeal insufficiency fracture, 25-OH D 14 ng/mL?"high-dose vitamin D

"Primary function of vit D in osteoporotic fracture healing?"regulates inflammatory response at injury site (✗ activates RANKL, ✗ ↓ VEGF, ✗ inhibits osteocalcin)

"NOT a function of PTH?"↑ osteoblastic resorption of bone (TO)

Accompanying artery✓ profunda brachii (✗ posterior humeral circumflex — axillary nerve/quadrangular space · ✗ scapular circumflex — triangular space · ✗ brachial — medial with median/ulnar · ✗ lateral thoracic — serratus anterior)

Deficit Level
✗ Wrist, thumb, finger extension (wrist drop) Radial nerve — spiral groove / distal-third humeral shaft
