Rehab & Prosthetics

Basic Science · 90 / 108

1Basics

Principles

Goalrestore function close to original · multidisciplinary team

Upper limblimb salvage ideal (prosthesis lacks sensation) · residual length matters for socket suspension

Lower limb goalscomfortable · easy on/off · light · durable · cosmetic · functional

Most important factor choosing LL prosthesis✓ patient functional status (current + potential) (✗ cost, comorbidities, age, gender)

Metabolic cost of amputation

Amputation ↑ Metabolic cost of walking
Syme's 15%
Traumatic transtibial ✓ 25% — least of the options
Vascular transtibial 40%
Traumatic transfemoral 68%
Vascular transfemoral 100%

Ruletraumatic < vascular (ongoing vascular disease above level) · more proximal → higher cost

Other costswheelchair propulsion +9% vs normal walking · crutch walking > walking with prosthesis

Gait aids & crutch gait (TO)

Antalgic gaitany pain-related gait · shortened stance on affected limb

Gait Pattern
3-point Crutches + injured limb advance together (weight on crutches) → weight onto uninjured limb
4-point One crutch → opposite leg → other crutch → other leg
Swing-to Crutches advanced, legs swung to crutches · slower, ↓ fall risk
Swing-through Crutches advanced, legs swung past them · faster, ↑ energy, ↑ fall risk · ✓ paraplegia / SCI with paraparesis (TO)

Crutch-walking muscleslatissimus dorsi · triceps + biceps · quads · hip extensors · hip abductors

Caneheld contralateral → shifts centre of gravity toward affected side

TTWB / NWB one leg✓ 2 axillary crutches (✗ single forearm crutch, ✗ double cane, ✗ single platform crutch)

5Management

Upper limb prostheses (myoelectric vs body-powered)

Timingfit ASAP, even before full wound healing · better if within 30 days

Myoelectric Body-powered
Best candidate ✓ Midlength transradial (only terminal device to drive) Heavy labour
Mechanism Surface electrodes on residual muscles → motor Harness: shoulder flexion + abduction · ring just off C7 midline toward non-amputated side
Pros Cosmesis · more proximal function/coverage · any position incl. overhead · less gross limb movement · sedentary use Most durable · moderate cost · lighter · more sensory feedback · less maintenance · less training
Cons Heavier, expensive, more maintenance, less sensory feedback · clean environment only Poor cosmesis · more harnessing · more gross movement
Rejection (Silcox) 50% rejected myoelectric 32% rejected conventional (of those with both)

Elbow disarticulation / transhumeraltwo joints → heavy · best = hybrid myoelectric + body-powered + switch components

Proximal transhumeral / shoulder disarticulationno lever arm → universal shoulder joint positioned with contralateral arm + lightweight hybrids · many choose cosmetic only

Component Detail
Passive terminal device More cosmetic, less functional
Active terminal device More functional · hooks / hands with cables or myoelectric
Hand-like device Thumb, index, long finger · gloved · office worker
Non-hand (hook, pincer) Task tools, quick release · physical labour
Voluntary opening Closed at rest, opens with proximal contraction · more common
Voluntary closing Open at rest · heavier, less durable
Quick-disconnect wrist Swap specialised devices
Locking wrist Prevents rotation during lift
Wrist flexion unit Bilateral amputees, on longer limb → midline tasks (shaving, buttoning)
Rigid elbow hinge Short transradial, no pronation/supination, elbow flexion kept
Flexible elbow hinge Wrist disarticulation / long transradial with pronation/supination

Gripsprecision (pincer) · tripod (3-jaw chuck) · lateral (key) · hook power · spherical

Prosthetic foot selection

Foot Features
Single axis Ankle hinge → DF + PF only · poor durability, cosmesis
SACH (solid ankle cushioned heel) Rigid wood keel, foam · low activity / transfers · being phased out · overloads contralateral foot
Dynamic response (energy storing) Keel = spring → DF, ↓ contralateral load, push-off · standard for most
Articulating dynamic response ✓ Inversion, eversion, rotation · absorbs shear · uneven terrain (multi-axis — BK amputee walking uneven ground)
Non-articulating dynamic response Spring but ✗ inversion/eversion
Short keel Less responsive · moderate activity, hiking
Long keel High-demand athletes
Shank Structural support · endo/exoskeletal · lever arm after transmetatarsal amputation

SACH vs dynamic responseSACH: less dorsiflexion · ↑ contralateral GRF · ↓ max gait velocity · less frequent replacement · ✗ longer midstance (dynamic foot has longer midstance)

Hiker on SACH✓ short-keel articulated dynamic-response foot (✗ single axis, ✗ non-articulated, ✗ long keel)

Partial foot / toe amputation, short stance, poor toe-off✓ full-length steel shank + rocker sole (shank restores lever arm, rocker → heel–toe transfer) · ✗ solid AFO, HKAFO, UCBL · rocker may destabilise knee

Prosthetic knees

Knee Indication Features
Polycentric (4-bar) Transfemoral, knee disarticulation, bilateral Variable centre of rotation · controlled flexion · moderately fast pace · supports more weight · shortens limb at step initiation (↓ stumble) · heavier, costly, more maintenance
Stance-phase control (weight-activated) Older patients, proximal amputation, uneven terrain Constant friction in swing · locks with weight-bearing
Fluid (hydraulic / pneumatic) Active patients accepting weight Variable cadence (piston) · ↓ excess flexion · extends earlier
Constant friction (single axis) General · uneven terrain · commonest paediatric · ✗ old/weak Hinge + friction · light, durable, cheap · single speed · stance stability from alignment
Variable friction (cadence control) — Multiple pads · variable speed · not durable
Manual locking — Constant friction + extension lock

Polycentric centre of rotationposterior to weight line → stance control, harder flexion · anterior → easier flexion, less control · ✗ "fixed anterior to weight-bearing line"

Transfemoral sockets & osseointegration (OPRA) (TO)

Socketstump–prosthesis interface · preparatory socket re-adjusted as oedema resolves · PTB socket commonest for BKA · transfemoral/quadrilateral → hard to keep femur adducted (allows 10° adduction, 5° flexion)

Transfemoral socket (TO) Weight bearing Suspension
Quadrilateral Ischial tuberosity on posterior shelf Suction ± belts
Ischial containment Whole stump surface · medial wall contains ischial ramus · more comfort/control Suction

Quadrilateral socket used in✓ transfemoral amputation (✗ Syme's, BKA, transhumeral) (TO)

Suspensionbelts, wedges, straps, suction · silicone liner = airtight seal

Pylontube linking socket and terminal device · exoskeleton (hard shell) / endoskeleton (metal frame, soft cover)

Osseointegration (OPRA)stem in residual bone + percutaneous post → direct skeletal attachment (TO)

OI advantages (TO)✗ socket problems (skin breakdown, fit, pain) · may ↓ disuse osteoporosis + contralateral arthritis · short residual limb, HO, scar, multiple amputations · osseoperception (terrain sensing) · ↑ wear time · easier donning/doffing (✗ "no donning/doffing needed") (TO)

OPRA contraindications (FDA) (TO)
Incomplete skeletal growth · atypical anatomy · <2 mm cortex around implant
Osteoporosis
Age >65 or <22 · weight >220 lb (with prosthesis) · pregnancy
Non-compliance · severe PVD · complicated DM · residual-limb skin disease
Neuropathy, severe phantom pain · active infection / dormant bacteria
✓ NOT a contraindication: malignancy as cause of amputation

Orthoses & splints (TO)

GRAFO✓ all: Newton's third law · accelerates knee extension in midstance (weak quadriceps) · compensates weak gastrocnemius–soleus (TO)

Turnbuckle splintgradual stretch of contracted joint · Volkmann's ischaemic contracture, stiff elbow · ↓ rehab time, good compliance (TO)

Physical modalities — TENS (TO)

TENS frequencies<10 Hz, high intensity → muscle contraction · >50 Hz, low intensity → paraesthesia without contraction (TO)

Indications✓ generalised muscle pain, LBP, spasm, myofascial, arthritic, neurogenic, post-surgical, diabetic neuropathy, dysmenorrhoea · ✗ acute fracture pain (no proven benefit) (TO)

Contraindicationspregnancy, epilepsy, pacemaker (✗ "relative") · pads ✗ over eyes, trans-cerebral, anterior neck, ant + post chest together, broken skin, tumour, spine · ✓ metal implants are NOT a contraindication (insufficient heating) (TO)

6Complications

Choke syndromesocket too tight → venous obstruction · acute: red, indurated, orange-peel skin · chronic: haemosiderin, venous stasis ulcers

Skincontact dermatitis (liner, socks, suspension → remove item) · cysts + sweating = excess shear / poor fit · scar massage

Painful residual limbHO · bony prominence · poor fit · neuroma · thin soft tissue

Transtibial prosthesis alignment & gait problems

Problem Cause / effect
Swing-phase pistoning Ineffective suspension
Stance-phase pistoning Poor socket fit / volume change (change stump sock)
Inset (medialised) foot Varus strain · circumduction · pain medial-proximal + lateral-distal
Outset (lateralised) foot Valgus strain · broad-based gait · pain lateral-proximal + medial-distal (e.g. lateral knee + medial distal stump → ✓ reduce lateral offset)
Anterior foot Stable ↑ knee extension + patellar pain
Posterior foot Unstable ↑ knee flexion · drop-off / buckling
Dorsiflexed foot ↑ patellar pressure · drop-off
Plantar-flexed foot Drop-off + ↑ patellar pressure (per Orthobullets)
Heel too soft ↑ knee extension
Heel too hard ↑ knee flexion + toe external rotation

Drop-off (knee buckling in terminal stance)body weight over MTPs too early · caused by ↓ anterior support: DF foot, posterior foot, soft keel, posterior toe break → ✓ move foot anterior

How it's asked

"Inset BKA foot — EXCEPT?"increased knee extension in stance (✗ varus strain, ✗ circumduction, ✗ medial-proximal pain)

"Drop-off in terminal stance — fix?"move foot more anterior (✗ dorsiflex, ✗ move posterior, ✗ softer keel)

"Toe amputation, short stance, irregular toe-off?"full-length steel shank + rocker sole

"Lateral knee + medial distal stump pain after BKA?"reduce lateral offset of foot

"Hiking BKA amputee struggling on SACH?"short-keel articulated dynamic-response foot

"SACH vs dynamic response — EXCEPT?"longer midstance (✗ less replacement, ✗ less DF, ✗ ↑ contralateral GRF)

"Multi-axis articulated foot recommended for?"BKA walking on uneven ground

"Most important factor choosing LL prosthesis?"functional status

"Polycentric fluid knee vs constant friction — EXCEPT?"lighter weight

"Polycentric hydraulic knee — EXCEPT?"centre of rotation fixed anterior to weight line

"Body-powered vs myoelectric — EXCEPT?"decreased harnessing (✗ heavy labour, lighter, less therapy, less maintenance)

"Myoelectric advantage — EXCEPT?"more sensory feedback

"Least ↑ metabolic demand?"traumatic transtibial

"Swing-through gait used by?"SCI with paraparesis (TO)

"Crutch gait in video (legs swung to crutches)?"swing-to gait

"TTWB after pilon ORIF — device?"double axillary crutches

"GRAFO?"all of the above (Newton's 3rd law, knee extension, weak calf) (TO)

"Identify splint (gradual stretch of contracture)?"turnbuckle splint (TO)

"Quadrilateral socket used in?"transfemoral amputation (TO)

"NOT a contraindication to OPRA?"malignancy as cause of amputation (TO)

"NOT a feature of bone-anchored prosthesis?"no donning/doffing required (TO)

"True about TENS?"used for generalised muscle pain (✗ CI with metal, ✗ fracture pain, ✗ pacemaker relative) (TO)

Renal Osteodystrophy

Basic Science · 91 / 108

1Basics

Definitionmetabolic bone disease of chronic renal insufficiency · deficient mineralisation from electrolyte + endocrine abnormalities → osteomalacia, osteonecrosis, pathological fracture

Labs & pathophysiology (TO)

Key step✓ uraemia-related phosphate retention (✗ PTH receptor sensitivity, ✗ metabolic alkalosis, ✗ dialysis electrolyte shifts)

Chronic renal failure
  • ↓ PO₄ excretion → hyperphosphataemia → Ca-PO₄ precipitates→↓ Ca
  • ↓ 1α-hydroxylase (PO₄ inhibits it) → ↓ 1,25-D → ↓ Ca→OSTEOMALACIA
  • ↓ Ca + ↑ PO₄ → chief cell hyperplasia→SECONDARY HYPERPARATHYROIDISM
    • autonomous gland → hypercalcaemia→TERTIARY HYPERPARATHYROIDISM

Tertiary hyperparathyroidism (TO)prolonged stimulation → gland autonomous, insensitive to Ca/PO₄/vit D → hypercalcaemia (TO)

Classification

High turnover (high PTH) Low turnover (normal PTH)
Cause Chronic ↑ PO₄ → secondary hyperparathyroidism (PO₄ ↓ Ca, ↓ 1α-hydroxylase, directly ↑ PTH) Aluminium deposition in bone
Mechanism Chief cell hyperplasia ↓ osteoblast differentiation/proliferation · ↓ PTH release · disrupted mineralisation
Ca ↓ ↑
PO₄ ↑ Normal
ALP ↑ ↑
PTH ↑ Normal

Genes & lab markers

Newly diagnosed✓ ↑ PTH, ↑ PO₄ (hyperphosphataemia), ↓ Ca · ↓ active vit D · low urinary Ca + PO₄ (TO)

ALP ↑ in (TO)✓ renal osteodystrophy · rickets (all) · osteomalacia · Paget's · primary hyperparathyroidism · CRF with 2° HPT · lytic neoplasms (TO)

ALP normal (TO)osteoporosis · osteopetrosis · fibrous dysplasia · multiple myeloma · hypoparathyroidism (TO)

ALP ↓ (TO)achondroplasia · hypophosphatasia · cretinism (TO)

Lab pattern (Ca / PO₄ / ALP / PTH) Diagnosis
↓ / ↑ / ↑ / ↑ ✓ High-turnover renal osteodystrophy
↑ / N / ↑ / N Low-turnover renal osteodystrophy
↓ / ↓ / ↑ / ↑ Nutritional (vit D deficiency) rickets
↓ / ↑ / N / ↓ Hypoparathyroidism
↑ / N / N–↑ / ↑ Primary hyperparathyroidism

Histologythinned trabeculae · extreme bone turnover · amyloid pink on Congo red

3Presentation

Symptomsweakness · bone pain · pathological fracture (commonest complication) · deformity · growth retardation (children)

Hypocalcaemiaabdominal pain · cramps · dyspnoea · seizures · mental status change · Trousseau (carpopedal spasm with BP cuff) · Chvostek (facial twitch on tapping facial nerve)

Orthopaedic manifestations
Osteomalacia (adults) · growth retardation (children)
AVN
Tendinitis, tendon rupture
Carpal tunnel — β2-microglobulin amyloid
Pathological fracture — brown tumours (HPT) or amyloid
Osteomyelitis, septic arthritis

Deformity in children (TO)✓ genu valgum commonest (TO; ROD occurs later than nutritional rickets' physiological-varus age) · Orthobullets lists femoral varus — read the options · others: genu varum, SCFE, ankle valgus/varus, coxa vara, bowing, scoliosis, osteonecrosis, OCD, brown tumour (TO)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayLooser zones · brown tumour · osteosclerosis (mineralised osteomalacic bone) → rugger jersey spine (alternating sclerotic + lytic bands) · soft-tissue / tumoral calcinosis · chondrocalcinosis · osteopenia · fracture

Childrenwidened physis + zone of provisional calcification · physeal widening, bowing, cupping · varus femurs (e.g. 9-yr-old, <5th centile height, femoral bowing → ✓ renal osteodystrophy; ✗ XLH less likely at that onset, ✗ fibrous dysplasia — no ground glass, ✗ OI — no fractures)

CTosseous resorption · may mimic bone tumour

Osteitis fibrosa cysticamultiple cysts from PTH resorption (TO)

Metaphyseal bands (TO)

Dense metaphyseal bands — causes (TO)
Chronic anaemia — sickle cell, thalassaemia
Chemotherapy — methotrexate · treated leukaemia
Growth arrest/recovery lines — asthma, DM, CF, JIA, malnutrition
Heavy metals — lead (commonest), bismuth, arsenic, phosphorus, fluoride, mercury
Healing renal osteodystrophy · healing rickets · bisphosphonates · neonatal normal variant
✓ Hypervitaminosis D — rarest → "not seen" answer

5Management

Treatmenttreat underlying renal disease / relieve urological obstruction

How it's asked

"Metaphyseal bands NOT seen in?"hypervitaminosis D (✗ lead, methotrexate, sickle cell) (TO)

"Serum ALP increases in?"renal osteodystrophy (✗ osteopetrosis, myeloma, hypophosphatasia) (TO)

"Labs in new renal osteodystrophy?"↑ PTH, hyperphosphataemia, hypocalcaemia

"Key step causing osteomalacia in renal osteodystrophy?"uraemia-related phosphate retention

"High-turnover renal osteodystrophy labs?"↓ Ca, ↑ PO₄, ↑ ALP, ↑ PTH

"Commonest deformity in renal osteodystrophy?"genu valgum (TO)

"Dialysis patient, bilateral hip fractures, ↑ ALP + PTH, thin trabeculae?"renal osteodystrophy (✗ metastasis, myeloma)

"9-yr-old, short, bilateral femoral bowing, cupped wide physes?"renal osteodystrophy (✗ diastrophic dysplasia, OI, fibrous dysplasia)

Rheumatoid Arthritis

Basic Science · 92 / 108

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest inflammatory arthritis3% women, 1% men

GeneticsHLA-DR4, HLA-DW4 · ~15% monozygotic twin concordance · trigger: infection or HLA locus

Pathophysiology & serology (RF, anti-CCP, synovium)

ImmunologyIgM cell-mediated (T cell–MHC class II) response → soft tissue (early) → cartilage, bone (later) · ✗ IgA/IgD/IgE/IgG-mediated

Primary destructive cellmononuclear cells · cytokines IL-1, TNF-α (+ IL-6)

CascadeAg–Ab reactions → microvascular proliferation/obstruction → pannus (prominent intimal hyperplasia) → subluxation, chondrocyte death → tenosynovitis + tendon rupture

RA synovium
✓ Intimal hyperplasia 2 → 10–20 cell layers
✓ ↓ Apoptosis of lining
✓ ↑ Angiogenesis
✓ Abundant lymphocytes Perivascular lymphoid follicles
✗ Basement membrane disruption Synovium has no true basement membrane

Pannusinvasive granulation tissue · fibroblast-like synoviocytes, few inflammatory cells · anchorage-independent growth → directly destroys cartilage

Bone erosion✓ TNF + IL-6 (+ IL-1) blockade slows erosion · TNF mobilises osteoclast precursors · M-CSF + RANKL drive osteoclast differentiation (✗ "synovial fibroblasts resorb") · denosumab slows erosion, ✗ inflammation · TNF ↑ Dkk-1 → blocks Wnt → ↓ repair (Wnt interference ↑ erosion) · erosion ∝ synovitis · steroids, MTX, leflunomide bone-sparing via ↓ synovitis

Genes & lab markers

Rheumatoid factor✓ IgM antibody against Fc of native IgG · immune complexes deposit (kidney) · ✗ IgG vs IgM, ✗ IgA vs IgG

RF positivity75–80% (Orthobullets) · 80–90% (OB6543) — read the options · sensitive, ✗ specific · also non-RA autoimmunity

Anti-CCP✓ most sensitive + specific · sensitivity 50–75%, specificity 90–95% · as sensitive as, more specific than RF · high levels → erosive disease · may precede arthritis · anti-CCP ⊂ ACPA · ACPA bind citrullinated vimentin on osteoclasts → TNF → osteoclastogenesis · independently predicts erosion (✗ "synovitis but not erosion")

Otheranti-MCV · ↑ ESR · ↑ CRP · ANA in 30–40% · joint fluid: ↓ complement, ± RF

Autoantibody Disease
✓ Anti-IgG (RF) RA
Anti-dsDNA SLE
Anti-ACh receptor Myasthenia gravis
Anti-topoisomerase I Scleroderma
Anti-GAD Type 1 DM

3Presentation

Symptomsinsidious morning stiffness · symmetric polyarthropathy of hands + feet · DIP spared · also knees, C-spine, elbows, ankles, shoulders

Rheumatoid nodulescommonest extra-articular manifestation · 20% (Orthobullets presentation) / 25% (finger section) · strongly RF+, aggressive disease · over IP joints, olecranon, ulnar forearm · may form sinus · treat steroid injection / excision (cosmesis, pain, biopsy)

Systemicrheumatoid vasculitis · pericarditis · pulmonary disease

Syndrome Features
Felty RA + splenomegaly + leukopenia
Still's Acute RA + fever, rash, splenomegaly
Sjögren Exocrine glands → ↓ saliva + tears · lymphoid proliferation

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayperiarticular erosions + osteopenia · symmetric joint-space narrowing · central glenoid erosion · protrusio acetabuli

Protrusio acetabulifemoral head medial to teardrop · RA (bilateral + multijoint on bone scan → ✓ RA) · Marfan · Paget · Otto pelvis (arthrokatadysis = primary protrusio, with osteomalacia, ✗ other joints) · ✗ DDH

Diagnostic criteria — 2010 ACR/EULAR (TO)

Target≥1 joint with definite synovitis not better explained · ✓ score ≥6/10 = RA (✗ ">8") · <6 → reassess, may accumulate (TO)

Domain (TO) Points
Joints — 1 large 0
2–10 large 1
1–3 small 2
4–10 small 3
>10 joints (≥1 small) 5 — greatest weight
Serology — RF− and anti-CCP− 0
Low +ve RF or anti-CCP (1–3× ULN) 2
High +ve (≥3× ULN) 3
Acute phase — abnormal CRP or ESR 1
Duration ≥6 wk 1

Excluded joints (TO)hand DIP, 1st CMC, 1st MTP (TO)

1987 ARA criteria≥4 of 7 for 6 wk: morning stiffness ≥1 h · swelling ≥3 joints · nodules · hand X-ray erosions/decalcification · symmetric arthritis · serum RF · hand (MCP, PIP) + wrist arthritis

Post-viral (chikungunya) arthritis (TO)

Phases (TO)acute <3 mo (viraemic 5–10 d, sub-acute 6–21 d) · chronic >3 mo — RA-like, SpA, fibromyalgia patterns · chronic in 14–25% (age >45, viral load >10⁹/mL) · neuropathic pain ~20% (TO)

Tests (TO)RT-PCR days 0–7 · IgM from 5–10 d (peak 3 wk, lasts 2–3 mo) · IgG after 1 wk, lasts years · chronic → check RF, anti-CCP, HLA-B27 (TO)

Phase (TO) Treatment
Acute Rest, fluids, paracetamol · ✗ NSAIDs until dengue excluded · ✗ aspirin · ✗ steroids <3 wk
Sub-acute Low-dose prednisolone (10 mg × 5 d, taper) for synovitis · HCQ for persistent symptoms
✓ Chronic (>3 mo, e.g. 18 mo) ✓ MTX + sulfasalazine + HCQ (combination > HCQ alone) · TNFi if resistant

5Management

DMARDs & biologics (TNF, IL-1, CTLA4, CD20)

Approachaggressive early DMARDs > pyramid · advances → ↓ surgery

Drug Mechanism
Methotrexate Folate analogue — inhibits DHFR · ↓ neovascularisation · adenosine release · ✓ + tetracycline (doxycycline) more effective (anti-collagenase)
Leflunomide Pyrimidine synthesis inhibitor
Sulfasalazine Unknown · ↓ ESR/CRP · ↓ prostaglandins, leukotrienes
Hydroxychloroquine Blocks toll-like receptors → ↓ dendritic cells
Gold Inhibits monocytes
D-penicillamine —
Etanercept TNF receptor (p75/type II)–IgG1 Fc fusion protein binding TNF-α · SC · RA, JRA, PsA, psoriasis, AS
Infliximab Human–mouse chimeric anti-TNF-α mAb · opportunistic infection, malignancy
Adalimumab · golimumab Human anti-TNF-α mAb
Certolizumab Pegylated anti-TNF-α
Anakinra Recombinant IL-1 receptor antagonist (IL-1RI) · refractory / 2nd–4th line
Rituximab Anti-CD20 → B cells · ✗ TNF · with MTX after TNF failure
Abatacept Binds CD80/CD86 (co-stimulation) → T cells
Ustekinumab Anti-IL-12/IL-23
Tocilizumab IL-6 receptor inhibitor · 2nd line after TNF failure
Cyclosporine Calcineurin inhibitor

TNF blockersrevolutionised RA (also JRA, SpA, CRMO, histiocytosis X) · ↓ pannus, cartilage loss, osteoclastic resorption · risks: latent TB reactivation, infection, lymphoma

Perioperative medication management & infection risk

Medication Perioperative rule
NSAIDs Stop 5 half-lives before (aspirin 7–10 d; naproxen 3 d)
Steroids Stress dose by surgical stress
Methotrexate · sulfasalazine · HCQ · penicillamine ✓ Continue
Leflunomide Continue (minor) · stop 1–2 d before major, restart 1–2 wk
Etanercept Hold 1 wk (OB3092 key) · 2 wk before THA (2017 ACR/AAHKS, OB212995; Orthobullets complications) · 4 wk (Keith) · plan at end of dosing interval +1 wk — read the options
Adalimumab Stop 10 days before
Infliximab Lowest level 2 wk before next infusion
Rituximab Stop 7 months before major surgery
Ustekinumab Skip next dose + 1 wk (~13 wk from last) · restart >14 d → ↓ wound complications
Anakinra Continue minor · stop 1–2 d major · restart 10 d

Infection✓ prior SSI = most significant risk factor (✗ age, hardware, sulfasalazine, infliximab) · ✓ etanercept shown to ↑ post-op infection (✗ naproxen, leflunomide, sulfasalazine, aspirin) · immunosuppression effect overall controversial

Cervical spine instability & preoperative screening

C-spineinvolved in 90%, often missed · ✓ pre-op C-spine X-rays with flexion–extension before any arthroplasty (anaesthetic death risk > shoulder MRI) · JRA (polyarticular/systemic) → C-spine assessment most important

Pattern
Atlantoaxial subluxation Commonest
Basilar invagination Cranial migration of dens
Subaxial subluxation Multilevel listhesis (staircase), midcervical kyphosis

PADI (posterior atlanto-dens interval)✓ <14 mm → fusion even if asymptomatic (predicts neuro progression)

Hand & wrist (extensor tendon rupture, tendon transfers, wrist fusion) (TO)

Upper-limb reconstruction order (TO)✓ hand/wrist > shoulder > elbow · lower limb before upper · LL order forefoot > hip > knee > hindfoot > ankle (TO)

Ulnar drift (wind-swept) (TO)✓ ulnar displacement of extensors + radial collateral ligament attenuation · radial sagittal band fails → EDC into ulnar valleys · wrist radial deviation → ulnar bowstring (zigzag) · ECU subluxes volar-ulnar (TO)

Ulnar drift at MCP
  • Early, flexible→SYNOVECTOMY + EXTENSOR CENTRALISATION + INTRINSIC RELEASE (cross-intrinsic)
  • Late / fixed→SILICONE (SWANSON) MCP ARTHROPLASTY
    (correct radially deviated wrist at same time · satisfaction ~70%)
  • Thumb MCP without IP disease→MCP FUSION
Finger deformity Pathoanatomy Treatment
Boutonnière PIP synovitis → central slip attenuation · lateral bands volar · oblique retinacular contracture → DIP hyperextension Splint (flexible) · central slip imbrication / Fowler distal tenotomy (moderate) · PIP fusion / arthroplasty (rigid)
Swan neck Terminal tendon rupture (DIP) · FDS, volar plate, collaterals attenuate · lateral bands dorsal · transverse retinacular attenuation, triangular ligament contracture Splint · FDS tenodesis / proximal Fowler (flexible) · releases (rigid)
Arthritis mutilans RA or PsA · pencil-in-cup, wind-chime fingers Interposition graft + fusion
Nalebuff thumb Deformity Treatment
I — Boutonnière ✓ Most common · MCP flexion + IP (hyper)extension · EPB attenuation, EPL subluxes volar (TO) ✓ Restore MCP extension + stabilise IP (TO) · synovectomy + EPL rerouting (early) · MCP fusion 10–15° (late, most reliable) · IP fusion if fixed
II — Boutonnière + CMC Uncommon / very rare As I and III
III — Swan neck CMC adduction/subluxation + MCP hyperextension + IP flexion Address CMC first — trapeziectomy/LRTI · MCP >30° → volar capsulodesis or fusion · first web release
IV — Gamekeeper Adductor contracture + UCL laxity · P1 radial deviation Flexible: synovectomy, UCL reconstruction, adductor release · fixed: MCP arthroplasty / fusion
V MCP hyperextension — volar plate laxity (swan neck with MCP disease) Volar capsulodesis (MCP in flexion)
VI Skeletal collapse (mutilans) Arthrodeses

Total wrist fusion Pancarpal, limited arc (20°), poor bone · gold standard · often + Darrach
Total wrist arthroplasty Sedentary, good bone, reasonable motion

Elbow (synovectomy, total elbow arthroplasty)

Elbow procedure Indication
Medical + steroid injection First line
Arthroscopic / open synovectomy Pain without instability, ROM preserved (arc >90°) · early (Larsen 3 → ↑ reoperation; 5-yr reoperation-free 77%)
Synovectomy + radial head excision (lateral approach) Radiocapitellar focus · PIN compression from radial head synovitis (wrist/thumb extension weakness)
Interposition arthroplasty (Achilles, fascia, dermis) Young active, not TEA candidates
✓ Semiconstrained TEA End-stage, pain, loss of motion, instability · elderly · 5 lb lifting limit · ✗ unconstrained in inflammatory arthritis
Arthrodesis / resection Salvage only

Synovectomy indicationspreserved joint space · young on DMARD · early disease · heavy work (rules out TEA) · ✗ female gender (not an indication)

Shoulder · hip · knee

Shoulder>90% develop symptoms · rotator cuff tears (large, irreparable → poorer arthroplasty, glenoid loosening) · central glenoid wear, osteopenia, cysts

Hipprotrusio acetabuli

Knee synovectomy↓ pain + swelling · ✗ halts radiographic progression · ✗ prevents TKA · ✗ ↑ ROM · synovium re-forms then becomes rheumatoid

TKA✓ resurface patella in RA · unresurfaced → ↑ anterior knee pain · resurfaced → ↑ extensor rupture, patellar clunk

Forefoot & midfoot reconstruction

Rheumatoid footbilateral, symmetric · forefoot first · HLA-DR4 · ~90% develop forefoot deformity (hallux valgus, hammer toes, lesser MTP dislocation)

Earliest forefoot signMTP synovitis → MTP hyperextension · distal fat-pad migration · plantar callosities · ulcers

Gold standard✓ 1st MTP arthrodesis + lesser metatarsal head resections (+ hammertoe correction — Hoffman-Clayton) · Coughlin & Mann 90% good/excellent

✗ Alternative Why
Keller arthroplasty Unstable platform, recurrence, poorer function
1st MTP replacement Unreliable long-term
Bunionectomy / chevron / realignment Doesn't stabilise ray or address arthritis · osteotomies only if no erosions
Lapidus TMT fusion — ✗ addresses MTP
Weil osteotomies ✗ address erosive lesser MTPs
Lesser MTP fusion Lateral foot needs motion
Lesser MTP arthroplasty Resection preferred

Talonavicular arthritisisolated symmetric TN degeneration, no trauma → ✓ RA (✗ Köhler, OA, OCD) · treat fusion

"RA synovium EXCEPT?"basement membrane disruption (✗ intimal hyperplasia, ↓ apoptosis, angiogenesis, lymphocytes)

"RF targets which immunoglobulin?"IgG

"RF best described as?"IgM antibody against native IgG

"Anti-CCP used for?"rheumatoid arthritis (✗ AS, PsA, SLE, Reiter)

"% adults RF positive?"80–90%

"Etanercept mechanism?"inhibitory binding to TNF-α

"DMARD on IL-1 receptor (figure)?"anakinra

"Binds CD80/CD86?"abatacept

"Blocks TNF–receptor binding (figure)?"infliximab

Rickets

Basic Science · 93 / 108

1Basics

Definitiondefective mineralisation of osteoid/cartilage from inadequate Ca + PO₄ before physeal closure · after closure = osteomalacia

Primary problem✓ low calcium available for mineralisation (Ca/PO₄ homeostasis) · ✗ primarily a physeal (proliferative zone) disorder · ✗ type I collagen (OI) · ✗ EXT-1 (MHE) · ✗ dysplastic fibrous bone (FD)

Sitepoor calcification at zone of provisional calcification → ↑ physeal width + cortical thinning/bowing · fastest-growing bones (tibia, distal femur, distal radius) · rarely hand tubular bones

Causesvit D deficiency · vit D malabsorption · renal osteodystrophy · X-linked hypophosphataemia · ✗ vitamin K deficiency

Activation✓ PTH activates renal 1α-hydroxylase (low Ca) → calcitriol · most potent effect: ↑ intestinal Ca absorption

Classification

Type Inheritance / gene Mechanism Age
Vit D-resistant (familial hypophosphataemic, XLH) ✓ X-linked dominant — PHEX (commonest heritable rickets) Renal tubules can't reabsorb PO₄ · ↑ FGF23 → PO₄ wasting + suppressed 1α-hydroxylase · GFR normal 1–2 yr
ADHR AD — FGF23 (cleavage site) ↓ FGF23 degradation · iron deficiency triggers expression (TO) —
ARHR AR — DMP1 (also ENPP1, FAM20C) Impaired osteocyte maturation, ↑ FGF23 —
Nutritional (vit D-deficient) — ↓ intake → ↓ gut Ca → ↑ PTH → resorption → ↑ ALP 6 mo–3 yr
VDDR I AR — renal 1α-hydroxylase (12q14; CYP27B1) ↓ calcitriol —
VDDR II AR — vit D receptor (inactivating) ↑ calcitriol · end-organ resistance · alopecia (2A) —
HHRH SLC34A3 (NaPi-IIc) FGF23-independent PO₄ wasting (TO) —

XLH inheritanceaffected father → 100% daughters carriers/affected, sons unaffected · more severe in males

Nutritional risk factorspremature infants · ✓ dark-skinned (African American) breastfed >6 mo without vit D · malabsorption (coeliac), TPN · Asian immigrants · vegetarian diets · rare since milk fortified

FGF23-dependent hypophosphataemia (TO) FGF23-independent (TO)
XLH, ADHR, ARHR · tumour-induced osteomalacia · epidermal naevus · osteoglophonic dysplasia · fibrous dysplasia · iron-infusion · α-klotho translocation · Dent's (per TO key) · NPT2a, NHERF1 mutations · post-transplant / hepatic resection ✓ Fanconi syndrome · ✓ HHRH (hereditary hypophosphataemic rickets with hypercalciuria)

FGF23 (TO)high → ↓ PO₄, ↓ 1,25-D, osteomalacia · ✓ low → ↑ PO₄, ↑ 1,25-D, soft-tissue calcification, hyperostosis (TO) · signals via FGFR1 + Klotho; phosphaturia partly PTH-dependent (TO)

Genes & lab markers

Disease (Orthobullets chart) Ca PO₄ ALP PTH 25-D 1,25-D

Nutritional N–↓ ↓ ↑ ↑ ↓ ↓

VDDR II ↓ ↓ ↑ ↑ N ↑↑ (>1000 pg/mL)
Hypophosphatasia ↑ ↑ ↓↓ N N —

Hyperparathyroidism ↑ ↓ ↑ ↑ — —
HHRH (TO) N/↑ ↓ ↑ ↓ N ↑ · ↓ FGF23 · hypercalciuria

Key nutritional vs XLH discriminator✓ PTH ↑ in nutritional, normal in XLH · normal Ca + PTH → ✓ XLH

XLH PTHnormal (OB, Orthobullets) · N/↑ (TO table) — read the options

Urine (TO)XLH/renal tubular: ↑↑ urine PO₄, ↓ urine Ca · nutritional: ↓ urine Ca + PO₄ (TO)

Routine vit D test✓ 25(OH)D · 1,25-D uninformative (normal/↑ with secondary HPT) (TO)

Histologydisordered elongated proliferative zone · poorly defined provisional calcification · widened osteoid seams · "Swiss cheese" trabeculae · collagen perpendicular to Haversian canals

3Presentation

Symptomslistlessness · irritability · weakness

Signstibial bowing (wide proximal tibial physes) · genu varum (commonest) · rachitic rosary (costochondral enlargement) · widened wrists + ankles · skull flattening · kyphosis (cat back) · ligamentous laxity · hypotonia · waddling gait · delayed dentition, enamel defects · pathological fractures · short stature

XLHshort stature · genu varum · windswept deformity · medial tibial torsion · genu valgum in some (e.g. 4-yr-old knock knees) · ✗ myopathy

VDDRlike nutritional but more severe · type I: hypotonia, seizures, fractures in infancy · type II: + bone pain, dental caries/hypoplasia, alopecia

Calcipenic vs phosphopenic (TO) Calcipenic Phosphopenic
Muscle weakness Present Absent (present in TIO)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-ray (AP + lateral)✓ physeal widening · metaphyseal cupping, irregular metaphyses · loss of zone of provisional calcification definition · osteopenia · Looser zones (compression side) · ✓ anterior rib widening (rachitic rosary) · genu varum · codfish vertebrae · dorsal kyphosis

Radiographic mimic Feature
Hyperparathyroidism Punched-out lesions, subperiosteal resorption, erosion around physis edge
Jansen metaphyseal chondrodysplasia Rounded epiphyses, wide epiphysis–metaphysis separation · ligand-independent PTHR1 activation
Renal osteodystrophy Rugger jersey sclerotic vertebral endplates

Extreme physeal widening + osteopenia + deformity✓ pathognomonic for rickets → labs (Ca, PO₄, ALP, vit D) + endocrinology first (✗ osteotomy / guided growth before medical control — high recurrence)

Looser zones (TO)

Looser zonepseudofracture; indentation of soft bone by vascular crossings (TO)

Seen in (TO) ✗ Not seen
Osteomalacia (characteristic) · renal osteodystrophy · fibrous dysplasia · hyperthyroidism · Paget's · XLH · OI ✓ Lead poisoning

Oncogenic (tumour-induced) osteomalacia (TO)

TIOparaneoplastic · adolescents/young adults · mesenchymal tumours secrete FGF23 ("phosphatonin") → phosphaturia (TO)

Tumours (TO)haemangiopericytoma (commonest) · GCT · chondromyxoid fibroma · chondroblastoma · osteosarcoma · osteoblastoma · NOF / metaphyseal fibrous defect · fibrous dysplasia · MFH · fibrosarcoma · haemangioma · ✗ multiple myeloma (TO)

TIO labs (TO)✓ ↓ PO₄ (severe), normal Ca, low/normal 1,25-D, ↑ FGF23 · ↑ ALP · low TmP · PTH less raised than dietary osteomalacia (TO)

TIO work-up + Rx (TO)localise: octreotide scan, FDG-PET/CT, MRI/CT · excise tumour → PO₄ normalises within hours · supplement PO₄ + vit D until normal (TO)

Renal tubular rickets & Fanconi syndrome (TO)

Renal tubular rickets (TO)✓ ↑ urine PO₄, ↓ urine Ca, normal 1,25-D, ↓ serum PO₄, normal PTH · normal Ca · low urinary pH · early infancy, severe deformity, ✗ myopathy, ✗ hypocalcaemia · RTA, Fanconi, XLH (TO)

Fanconi syndrome (TO)proximal tubular defect → wasting of PO₄, amino acids, glucose, bicarbonate (TO)

Fanconi (TO) Finding
Blood Metabolic acidosis · ↓ PO₄ · ↓ 25(OH)D · ↑ ALP · ↑ PTH · ✗ ↑ serum Ca (false statement)
Urine ↑ PO₄ · ↑ K⁺ · calciuria
Bone ↓ BMD, osteoporosis

5Management

Type Treatment
XLH Calcitriol + phosphate (phosphate must be with calcitriol — prevents 2° HPT) · burosumab
Nutritional Vitamin D 5000 IU/day × 6–10 wk
VDDR I Calcitriol (physiological dose) + calcium — excellent response (TO)
VDDR 1B (25-hydroxylase) Calcidiol (TO)
VDDR II Partial resistance → vit D (very high calcitriol) · total → calcium · severe hypocalcaemia → ✓ prolonged high-dose IV calcium (alopecia doesn't respond) (TO)
HHRH Oral phosphate ONLY · ✗ active vit D contraindicated (worsens hypercalciuria, stones) (TO)
ADHR PO₄ + calcitriol · iron repletion may normalise FGF23 (TO)
ARHR PO₄ + calcitriol · risk enthesopathy, vascular calcification (ENPP1) (TO)

Dosescalcitriol 20–30 μg/kg/day (children, as printed) · 0.5–0.75 μg/day adults · phosphate 20–40 mg/kg/day in 3–5 doses (children) · 750–1000 mg/day adults

Burosumab✓ fully human mAb neutralising circulating FGF23 → restores PO₄ reabsorption + 1α-hydroxylase · ✗ PHEX chaperone, ✗ Klotho agonist (TO) · approved ≥1 yr (Orthobullets) · first line >6 months + adults (TO) — read the options

Conventional XLH therapy risks (TO)nephrocalcinosis, tertiary HPT → renal US · nephrocalcinosis → ✓ switch to burosumab (✗ loop diuretic, ✗ more phosphate) (TO)

XLH medical therapyearly calcitriol + phosphate ✓ ↓ need for osteotomies, improves height, ↓ recurrence · ✗ normalises mineralisation completely · continue into adulthood (pain, insufficiency fractures) · ✗ calcium alone · OB2930 (Loeffler): adding phosphate to vit D gave no extra benefit — read the options

Operative criteria

Deformity Surgery
Growth remaining (<11 yr) Hemiepiphysiodesis / guided growth — effective once medically treated
>11 yr / severe tibial bowing Multilevel corrective osteotomy (K-wires, plates, IM nails, ex-fix)
Before metabolic control ✗ — high recurrence

XLH outcomestaged surgery better, but early degenerative joint disease

Rickets types compared

Inheritance — XLD AR AR
PTH ↑ N ↑ ↑
1,25-D ↓ N/↓ ↓↓ ↑↑

Scurvy

1Basics

Definitionsevere vitamin C deficiency

Epidemiologydeficiency in 8% men, 6% women (US) · M:F 4:3 · bimodal: infants 5–10 mo (rare <7 mo if breastfed — breast milk has vit C) + men >60

Risk factorselderly men living alone ("tea and toast") · overcooking · alcoholism · smokers · malabsorption (Whipple's, IBD, chemotherapy) · infants on evaporated/condensed milk

Pathophysiology

Biochemistryhumans lack functional L-gluconolactone oxidase → can't make ascorbic acid · ✓ ↓ chondroitin sulfate + collagen synthesis · impaired hydroxylation of collagen peptides · ↓ carnitine → myalgia

Sitegreatest effect on metaphysis (physeal region) · defective primary + secondary spongiosa · type I collagen defect → ↓ osteoblast function (✗ osteoclast — osteopetrosis) · fragile capillaries → bleeding

Histologyprimary trabeculae replaced by granulation tissue · haemorrhage · ✓ widened zone of provisional calcification

Statistic Definitions

Basic Science · 100 / 108

1Basics

Central tendency & spread

Measure Definition Best for
Mode Most frequent value Nominal (categorical) data
Median Middle value · unaffected by extremes Skewed data · all levels except nominal
Mean Arithmetic average · uses all values · most used Sensitive to extremes
Variance Variability of each data point from the mean · = SD² (average of squared deviations) —
Standard deviation Spread of the group ±1 SD = 68% · ±2 SD = 95% · ±3 SD = 99.7%

Diagnostic test properties (sensitivity, specificity, PPV, ROC)

Disease + Disease −
Test + a = true positive b = false positive
Test − c = false negative d = true negative
Measure Formula Meaning
Sensitivity TP / (TP + FN) = a/(a+c) Positive in those with disease · screening (few false negatives)
Specificity TN / (TN + FP) = d/(b+d) Negative in those without disease · confirmation (avoid treating the unaffected)
False-positive rate b/(b+d) = 1 − specificity —
False-negative rate c/(a+c) —
PPV TP / (TP + FP) = a/(a+b) Positive test truly diseased · depends on prevalence
NPV TN / (TN + FN) = d/(c+d) Negative test truly disease-free · depends on prevalence
LR+ Sensitivity / (1 − specificity) How a positive result changes likelihood
LR− (1 − sensitivity) / specificity How a negative result changes likelihood

Worked examples6 of 10 diseased test + → sensitivity 60% · 5 false + of 90 healthy → specificity 85/90 = 94.4% · sens 90%, spec 95%, prevalence 10% → PPV 9/(9 + 4.5) = 67% · 80/100 flu +, 5/100 healthy + → NPV 95/(20 + 95) = 83% · 86/(86 + 4) = 95.5% → specificity

Trade-offsensitivity and specificity usually traded off · false negative worse than false positive → choose more sensitive test · drug with nasty side effects, must not treat non-diseased → ✓ highest specificity

Precisioncloseness of repeated measurements (random error) · precise ≠ accurate

Post-testpre-test odds = p/(1 − p) · post-test odds = pre-test odds × LR · post-test probability = odds/(odds + 1)

ROC curvex-axis 1 − specificity (FPR) · y-axis sensitivity (TPR) · ✓ measures responsiveness · area under curve (C-statistic): 0.5 = useless, >0.75 adequate, higher = better

Tool Purpose
ROC curve Responsiveness / diagnostic ability
Funnel plot Publication bias in meta-analysis
Forest plot Relative treatment effects across studies (meta-analysis)
Kaplan–Meier Survivorship
Cronbach α Internal consistency of questionnaire items
Log rank Compare survival curves
Multiple regression Predict a variable from ≥2 others · controls confounders
Cohen's kappa Inter-observer reliability
ICC Intra-observer reliability

Incidence, prevalence & risk measures

Incidence✓ new cases in a period per population at risk · long-term population/cohort studies · e.g. 100 new cases among 400 at-risk workers (100 already affected excluded) = 25%

Prevalence✓ total existing cases at a time point · cross-sectional survey (census) · e.g. 200/500 = 40% · MRSA cases on 1 day = prevalence

Measure Formula / source
Relative risk [a/(a+b)] / [c/(c+d)] = incidence exposed / incidence unexposed · cohort studies · RR >1 → more outcome in exposed
Odds ratio (a × d)/(b × c) · case-control (retrospective) + logistic regression · ≈ RR when outcome rare (<10%) · exaggerates risk when common
Absolute risk reduction Risk difference between groups
Relative risk reduction 1 − RR
Attributable risk Risk difference attributable to exposure
NNT 1 / ARR (NNS 1000 → ARR 0.1%)
Hazard ratio Comparison of rates over time (survival curves)

Hep C example75/595 transfused vs 16/712 not → RR = incidence (75/595) ÷ incidence (16/712)

Odds ratio in forest plotOR <1 = ↓ odds in treatment · CI includes 1 → not significant · narrower CI = more precise, less uncertainty · OR size ≠ effect size/clinical importance · adjusted vs crude OR differ (confounding)

Study reportingcohort (e.g. BMI vs THA outcome in 500) → ✓ relative risk · case-control → odds ratio

4Diagnosis & Classification

Study design & meta-analysis

Design Description
Case-control Start with outcome (e.g. tibial nonunion) vs controls without → look back for risk factors → OR
Cohort Start with exposure (e.g. daily vit D) → follow for outcome (hip fracture) → RR · prospective or retrospective
Cross-sectional Single time point → prevalence
RCT Random allocation, prospective
Case series No comparison group
Meta-analysis Combines studies · funnel plot for publication bias

Funnel ploteffect size vs study size/precision · large studies less variable · ✓ symmetric inverted funnel = no publication bias · skewed = bias (negative studies unpublished)

Statistical test selection (t-test, chi-square, ANOVA, non-parametric) (TO)

Parametriccontinuous, normally distributed · non-parametric — skewed continuous, ordinal or categorical

Comparison Parametric Non-parametric
2 groups, paired Paired t-test (means in matched groups) Wilcoxon signed-rank (Orthobullets) · OB1230/OB213156 call it Wilcoxon rank-sum — read the options
2 groups, unpaired Student / independent t-test Mann-Whitney U (= Wilcoxon rank sum)
≥3 groups ANOVA Kruskal-Wallis (Friedman for repeated)
Categorical, 2+ groups Chi-square Chi-square
Categorical, small sample (<5 per cell or total <~50) Fisher exact Fisher exact

ANOVAone-way = one independent variable · two-way = two independent variables · ≥3 groups, normal data (e.g. BMI across pinning/hemi/THA; fastball velocity of 5 pitchers)

Choose a test
  • Categorical / proportions→CHI-SQUARE
    • small cells (<5)→FISHER EXACT
  • Continuous, normal → 2 groups T-TEST (paired if matched) · ≥3 groups→ANOVA
  • Continuous skewed or ordinal → 2 groups MANN-WHITNEY U · ≥3 groups→KRUSKAL-WALLIS

Ordinal grade (Herring) in two unpaired populations✓ Mann-Whitney U (✗ paired t, ✗ Wilcoxon signed-rank, ✗ Kruskal-Wallis) (TO)

Examplesmean age 2 normal groups → t-test · CRPS rate 1% vs 9% → chi-square · single non-normal continuous variable → Mann-Whitney U · OR time robotic vs manual (normal) → t-test · ACL graft return-to-play scores (finite range) → chi-square per OB217862

5Management

Type I & II errors & power

H₀ true H₀ false
Reject H₀ Type I (α) error — false positive Correct — power (1 − β)
Fail to reject H₀ Correct (1 − α) Type II (β) error — false negative

Type I (α)✓ rejecting a true null · α set at 0.05 (1/20) · ↓ by lowering α (0.01) or Bonferroni (but ↑ type II)

Type II (β)✓ accepting a false null · detecting no difference when one exists · commonest in underpowered studies · e.g. small trial negative, later 2000-patient trial positive · crossover with intent-to-treat (40% "no tourniquet" got tourniquet) · 2 groups of 5 with p = 0.079 → may be type II

Power✓ 1 − β · probability of detecting a true effect · usually 80% (β 0.2) · depends on effect size, α, β, n · ✓ ↑ sample size → ↑ power, ↓ type II (✗ prospective vs retrospective, ✗ case-control) · 84 needed per arm, β 0.2 → chance of false-negative = 0.2

Power analysis✓ determines minimum sample size to show a clinically significant difference · ✓ before the study · elements: α, β, effect size (± variance), n · post-hoc power discouraged

Reduces type II ✗ Doesn't
✓ ↑ sample size ↓ α to 0.01 (↑ type II) · blinding (detection bias) · goniometer (measurement error) · randomisation (selection bias) · Bonferroni (↑ type II) · ↑ β

Effect sizemagnitude of difference in means between groups ÷ pooled SD · continuous variables (RRR for dichotomous) · ✗ shown by p-value

Other termsHawthorne effect = behaviour change when observed · confounder = associated with both exposure + outcome (✗ "error") · selection bias · measurement error · sampling error · fragile p-values with small samples

P-values, confidence intervals, correlation & effect size

P-valueprobability, if H₀ true, of a result as or more extreme · p = 0.09 → difference would arise by chance 9 in 100 times · ✓ p-value = probability of type I error (OB217456) · ✗ "groups statistically equal" if p > 0.05 · ✗ gives effect size · ✗ clinical significance

Bonferroni correction✓ post-hoc correction of p-values for multiple simultaneous tests on one data set · α ÷ number of comparisons (10 tests → 0.005)

Confidence level Mean ± SD
85% 1.44
90% 1.65
95% 1.96 (OB218892 uses ±2 SD)
98% 2.32
99% 2.58

Confidence intervalrange likely to contain the true parameter · shows precision + magnitude · ✓ larger sample → narrower CI (smaller margin of error) · 99% CI wider than 95% · overlapping CIs ✗ prove no significance — need formal test · e.g. FNA 0.5 ± 0.2 → 95% range 0.1–0.9 cm³

CorrelationR² = 0.97, p = 0.03 → strongly correlated, unlikely chance, ✗ validates accuracy (both methods could be wrong) · Pearson = 2 normal continuous variables · Bayesian = prior → posterior probability

Reliability, MCID, fragility index & survival

Psychometricsreliability = ✓ reproducibility between repeated tests / observers · validity = measures what it intends · responsiveness = detects change (ROC)

Inter-observerCohen's kappa · intra-observer — ICC

MCIDdifference in outcome that is clinically relevant · few tools have accepted MCID · statistically significant 5-point ASES difference with MCID 10–15 → ✓ may not be clinically meaningful (both groups' 22/27-point gains exceed MCID)

Fragility index✓ number of outcome changes (event ↔ non-event) needed to flip significance · dichotomous outcomes · larger = more robust

Survivorshipjoint replacement success · all assumed operated simultaneously · life table (annual, changes once/yr) vs product limit (recalculated at each failure) · Kaplan–Meier: x = time, y = survival probability · higher curve at a time point = better survival

How it's asked

"Equation for specificity?"TN / (TN + FP) (✗ TP/(TP + FN) — sensitivity)

"Positive predictive value?"TP / (TP + FP)

"86/(86 + 4) = 95.5% (US for meniscus tears)?"specificity

"Sensitivity of an assay?"TP / (TP + FN)

"Tool to determine responsiveness?"ROC curve (✗ funnel plot, Kaplan–Meier, Cronbach α, forest plot)

"Test value to avoid treating people without RA?"specificity (✗ sensitivity, ✗ NPV)

"Probability of finding a true association?"power

"Accepting the null when false?"type II error

"Statistical power defined by?"1 − probability of type II error (✗ 1 − α)

"Ability to detect a true difference between interventions?"power (✗ effect size, ✗ Hawthorne)

"Capsule repair study negative, later large study positive?"type II error

"Rejecting a true null hypothesis?"type I error

"Increase study power by?"increasing sample size

"No difference when one truly exists?"type II error

"Power analysis determines what, when?"sample size, before the study

"84 per arm, β 0.2, α 0.05, p = 0.28 — chance null accepted when false?"0.2 (✗ 0.8 = power, ✗ 0.05)

"Tourniquet RCT, 40% crossover, ITT no difference?"type 2 (β) error

"Purpose and timing of power analysis?"minimum patients for clinically significant difference, before study

"Lateralised RSA study negative, later RCTs positive?"type II error

"Reduce type II error?"increase sample size (✗ α 0.01, ✗ blinding, ✗ β 0.9)

"6300-patient trial positive, 150-patient follow-up negative?"beta error

"Calculate power?"1 − type II error

"Two groups of 5, p = 0.079 — statement?"may have made a type II error (✗ Bonferroni, ✗ underpowered ↑ type I)

"Reduce type 2 error in TKA rehab RCT?"increase sample size (✗ goniometer, ✗ blinding, ✗ Bonferroni)

"Herring grade in rural vs urban groups?"Mann-Whitney U (TO)

"Paired t-test used for?"difference between two means in matched groups

"Chi-square unreliable with small numbers — use?"Fisher exact test

"Mean age, two normal groups?"Student t-test

"CRPS rates 1% vs 9%?"chi-square

"Chi-square most appropriate for?"categorical data between groups (✗ <5 per group — Fisher)

"Normal data, ≥3 independent groups?"ANOVA (✗ Kaplan–Meier, ✗ log rank)

"Single continuous non-normal variable?"Mann-Whitney U

"Ideal ANOVA scenario?"mean BMI across pinning, hemi, THA (parametric)

"Data for chi-squared test?"categorical data of two large groups (✗ two small — Fisher)

"Mean OR time robotic vs manual TKA, normal?"Student t-test

"Best ANOVA application?"fastball velocity of top five pitchers

"One-way vs two-way ANOVA?"one vs two independent variables, ≥3 groups

"Definition of incidence?"new cases in a specific time period

"Relative risk of hep C with transfusion?"(75/595) ÷ (16/712)

"500 workers, 100 symptomatic at start, 100 new in 5 yr — incidence?"25% (✗ 20%, ✗ 40% — prevalence)

"500 THAs followed by BMI — report effect as?"relative risk (✗ odds ratio)

"Total MRSA cases on one day?"prevalence

"Cross-sectional survey establishes?"prevalence

"RR of dehiscence anterior vs posterior (W, X, Y, Z table)?"W/(W + X) ÷ Y/(Y + Z) (✗ (W/Y)/(X/Z) — OR)

"Effect size definition?"estimated magnitude of difference in means

"Range likely to include the unknown parameter?"confidence interval

"Bonferroni correction?"post-hoc p-value correction for multiple tests

"Women vs men steps, p = 0.09 — interpretation?"difference would occur by chance 9 in 100 times if no true difference (✗ statistically equal)

"R² 0.97, p 0.03 between FE and bench model?"strongly correlated, not chance, but doesn't validate the model

"Mean ± 2.58 SD?"99% CI

"Meaning of p = 0.03?"probability of type I error

"1.2 ± 0.3 vs 0.5 ± 0.2 cm³ — conclusion?"FNA yields 0.1–0.9 cm³ 95% of the time (✗ significance from CIs alone)

"Narrower CI in final study — cause?"larger sample size (✗ 99% CI, ✗ more variability)

"Forest plot of aspirin ORs — accurate?"DVT estimate less uncertain than hematoma (narrower CI)

"Funnel plot purpose?"detect publication bias (✗ forest plot, ✗ ROC)

"Nonunion patients vs non-nonunion patients questionnaire?"case-control study

"Daily vit D group vs none followed for hip fracture?"cohort study

"Reliability refers to?"reproducibility between repeated tests or observers

"Kaplan–Meier — accurate statement?"group with highest curve at 1 month more likely alive

"Fragility index?"number of outcome changes needed to change trial significance

"ASES +22 vs +27, p = 0.03, MCID 10–15?"5-point difference may not be clinically meaningful

Bending rigidity (stiffness)

Definitionslope of elastic range on structural stress–strain curve · THA stem stiffness → proximal stress shielding

Shape Bending rigidity ∝
Solid cylinder Radius⁴
Hollow cylinder Radius³
Rectangle (plate) Thickness³

Moments of inertia

Term Meaning
Area moment of inertia (I) Shape property predicting deflection, bending, stress · ∝ length⁴ · function of width, thickness, polar moment · = flexural stiffness
Polar moment of inertia (J) Resistance to torsion
Moment of inertia Resistance to change in rotation

Deflectiondisplacement under load · ∝ (force / elastic modulus) × area moment of inertia (as printed)

Bone anisotropy

Cortical bone✓ anisotropic (collagen fibre orientation) → mechanical properties vary with orientation · loaded along long axis → highest stiffness + tensile strength vs oblique/transverse · ✗ hydroxyapatite/water content differences

How it's asked

"Why orient cortical bone tensile specimens relative to femoral shaft?"mechanical properties vary with orientation (anisotropy) (✗ hydroxyapatite structure, ✗ water content, ✗ easier cutting)

Synovium & Synovial Fluid

1Basics

Synovium

Functionnutrient exchange between blood and joint fluid

Structurevascularised, porous connective tissue · ✓ no basement membrane

Synovial cell Origin / features Function
Type A Macrophage-derived · non-fixed · antigen-presenting · superficial ✓ Phagocytosis
Type B Fibroblast-like · rich RER, dendritic processes · deeper ✓ Produce synovial fluid — hyaluronic acid, fibronectin, collagen
Type C Intermediate · unknown origin/function ? multipotent precursor of A or B

Synovial cells & lubrication

Synovial fluidultrafiltrate of plasma regulated by synovium · ~2 mL in healthy knee · lubricates + nourishes cartilage by diffusion

Component Role
Hyaluronan Synthesis needs uridine diphosphoglucose dehydrogenase
✓ Lubricin Glycoprotein (HA-binding) · ↓ coefficient of friction · made by superficial-zone chondrocytes · deficiency → early arthritis
Urokinase-type plasminogen activator Activates plasmin → prevents intra-articular clot (fibrinolysis)
Collagenases · prostaglandins —
Proteolipids Boundary lubrication
✗ Distractor Actual role
Decorin Commonest tendon proteoglycan · regulates collagen fibril diameter
Scleraxis Tendon/ligament transcription factor · inhibits osteoblastogenesis
Aggrecan Cartilage swelling pressure
Cathepsin Osteoclast protease at ruffled border

Biomechanics✓ non-Newtonian · viscosity not constant, not linear · viscosity ↑ as shear rate ↓

Lipohaemarthrosis (supine)layer 1 fat · layer 2 serum/synovial fluid (✓ uPA → inhibits clot) · layer 3 RBCs (don't form fibrin clot)

Tendons

Basic Science · 105 / 108

1Basics

Functiontransfer muscle force to bone · orient along stress

Paratenon-covered Sheathed
Examples Patellar, Achilles Hand flexors
Blood supply Rich → heal better Avascular areas · nutrition by diffusion
Injury Trauma · fail at musculotendinous or tendon–bone junction Laceration · risk of adhesions

Composition (collagen, decorin, aggrecan, enthesis)

Structurefascicles separated by endotenon, surrounded by epitenon · hierarchical (microfibrils → fibrils → fascicles) · mostly water

Component % dry weight / role
Type I collagen 85% (Orthobullets, OB213905) · 95% (OB8280) — read the options
Type III collagen 0–5% (<5%) · also V, VI small
Proteoglycans 0–5% · attract water
✓ Decorin Most abundant tendon proteoglycan (small leucine-rich PG) · ✓ regulates collagen fibril diameter / size · cross-links fibres, transfers load
✓ Aggrecan Proteoglycan in areas of compression
Tenascin-C Glycoprotein ↑ in tendinopathy
Elastin Tissue recoil after stretch
Fibronectin Cell adhesion, migration, differentiation
Fibrillin Elastic fibre formation

Cellsfibroblasts (tenocytes): spindle-shaped, parallel rows along load · make type I (85%) + type III (5%) collagen · drive healing

Blood supplymusculotendinous junction · osseotendinous junction · surrounding tissue (paratenon, mesotenon, vincula)

Enthesis

Insertion zones (↑ modulus) Contents
Tendon Type I + III collagen, elastin, PGs, fibroblasts
Uncalcified fibrocartilage Aggrecan, types I, II, III, fibrochondrocytes
— Tidemark — Straight basophilic line = soft–hard mechanical boundary
Calcified fibrocartilage Type II, aggrecan, types I + X, fibrochondrocytes · interlocks with bone
Bone Osteocytes, osteoblasts, osteoclasts, type I, apatite
Fibrous (indirect) Fibrocartilaginous (direct)
Site Metaphysis / diaphysis Epiphysis / apophysis
Structure Perforating mineralised (Sharpey) fibres 4 zones
Insertion angle Changes little Changes greatly → overuse injury
Examples Deltoid, adductor magnus–linea aspera, pronator teres Rotator cuff, Achilles

Nerve endings✓ enthesis = free nerve endings (nociception) · myotendinous junction = Golgi organs (steady state), Ruffini (prolonged, sensitive), Pacini (fast) · Meissner = skin light touch

Vascular supply — vincula

Sheathed tendonsvincula = main blood supply to bulk of FDS/FDP · FDS + FDP each have vincula brevia + longa (2 each, Orthobullets) · "5 vincula: 2 short, 3 long" (OB5552)

Vinculum Origin → attachment
VBS (brevia superficialis) PIPJ / adjacent proximal phalanx
VLS (longa superficialis) Proximal P1 / MCPJ → 2 slips
✓ VBP (brevia profundus) DIPJ / distal middle phalanx → FDP at A5
VLP (longa profundus) Distal P1 / PIP volar plate → through VBS → FDP

Jersey finger (Leddy–Packer II, fragment at A2)✓ VBP disrupted · VLP intact · FDS vincula unaffected · ✗ volar plate

2Mechanism

Mechanical behaviour (toe region, elastin)

Propertiesmore collagen, less viscoelastic than ligament · viscoelastic, non-linear · strain-rate dependent · ✓ strong in tension (5–10% strain vs bone 1–4%) · buckle in compression · creep + stress relaxation

Modifiersexercise ↑ · immobilisation ↓ · ↑ to maturity then ↓ · laser/heat → shrinkage · hydration, temperature, pH → test under physiological conditions

Load–elongation region Explanation
✓ Toe (low load, non-linear) Crimped fibres straighten · crimped elastin fibrils (OB5510) · tendon toe smaller than ligament (more parallel)
Linear (elastic) Intermediate load · slope = Young's modulus
Yield point Elastic → plastic
Failure High load

Soft tissue vs metal✓ toe region unique to soft tissue (✗ modulus, plastic region, strain energy density, yield point — shared)

Mechanotransduction✓ deflection of primary cilium (✗ apoptosis, ✗ cell elongation, ✗ pressurisation — older theories)

Training (8 wk half-marathon)✓ net ↑ type I collagen (↑ turnover; ↓ cross-links with endurance) · ✗ known effect on fibril diameter / CSA

5Management

Healing & adaptation

Stage Events Time
Haemostasis Platelets → coagulation · fibrin clot + fibronectin → chemotaxis 5–15 min
Inflammation ✓ Neutrophils + M1 macrophages · chemotaxis of fibroblasts · fibroblasts make type III collagen 1–7 days
Proliferation Disorganised collagen + angiogenesis · GFs: PDGF, IGF, BMP-12/13, TGF-β · M2 shift · ↑ type I + neovascularisation 7–21 days
Remodelling Type III → type I · fibrosis, ↓ cellularity Up to 18 months

Repair strength✓ weakest in inflammatory phase · 7–10 days (Orthobullets) · 5–21 days (OB8271) · most strength by 21–28 days · max at 6 months · ✓ final only ~2/3 normal

Early mobilisationearlier ROM but ↓ repair strength · ✓ flexor tendons: prevents adhesions

Tendon vs Ligament

Tendon Ligament
Collagen More, more parallel Less, less organised
Viscoelasticity Less More
Toe region Smaller Larger

"Region X on load-elongation curve?"toe region with crimped fibres

Thromboembolism (PE & DVT) Prophylaxis

Basic Science · 107 / 108

1Basics

VTEDVT + PE · US incidence: DVT 200,000/yr, PE 50,000/yr

Pathophysiology (Virchow triad, thromboplastin, fibrin, embolisation)

Virchow's triadvenous stasis · hypercoagulability · endothelial (intimal) injury · ✗ thrombocytopenia, ✗ platelet dysfunction

Clot formationstasis → fibrin formation → clot retraction → propagation · DVT starts in venous valve cusps

Surgical trigger✓ thromboplastin (tissue factor, factor III) released during dissection → extrinsic pathway (VII → X → prothrombin → thrombin → fibrinogen → fibrin)

Thrombusfibrin + RBCs, relatively few platelets (platelets key in arterial thrombi) · fibrin = end product of coagulation cascade (thrombin cleaves fibrinogen) · collagen starts intrinsic arm · fibrinolytic system breaks down fibrin

Cement pressurisation of femoral canal✓ greatest V/Q mismatch risk in THA (MMA + fat emboli) · ✗ reaming, modular stem, SCD, posterior approach

Thrombophilias (factor V Leiden, protein C/S)

Inherited Note
MTHFR C677T/TT ✓ Highest risk (✗ "no impact")
Factor V Leiden Gln-for-Arg substitution at protein C cleavage site → factor Va resists inactivation by activated protein C → ↑ thrombin · >50% get DVT with one extra risk (fracture, TJA) · ✗ platelet / endothelial / hepatic disorder · ✗ mandatory Greenfield filter
Antithrombin III deficiency ATIII normally inhibits thrombin + Xa
Protein C / S deficiency Protein S = cofactor to activated protein C; inactivates Va + VIIIa · ✓ deficiency → VTE (✗ fibrinogen, VII, VIII, prothrombin deficiency)
Activated protein C resistance · ↑ factor VIII · hyperhomocysteinaemia · prothrombin G20210A —
✗ Distractor Actually
Familial dysfibrinogenaemia Defective fibrin clot
Plasminogen deficiency Can't lyse clots
Glanzmann thrombasthenia Platelet defect → bleeding
von Willebrand disease Bleeding disorder
Antiphospholipid syndrome Autoimmune, antibodies to protein C/S → clotting

Risk factors (TO)

Acquiredmalignancy (up to 20% of new VTE) · hormones: recombinant EPO (✓ thrombotic event before spinal fusion), HRT (✗ protective), OCP, late pregnancy · antiphospholipid (lupus anticoagulant, anticardiolipin) · prior VTE · obesity · CHF · MI · varicose veins · smoking · ↑ viscosity · thoracic outlet (UE DVT) · type of surgery (TJA) · age · family history · ✗ hypercholesterolaemia

Anaesthesia✓ general > spinal/epidural (conversion spinal → GA ↑ risk) · AAOS: neuraxial anaesthesia ✗ shown to ↓ VTE (↓ blood loss)

Warfarin paradoxrapid INR rise on unopposed warfarin → early protein C fall (t½ 6–8 h) before factor II (48–120 h) → transient hypercoagulability

TXA✓ no ↑ DVT (oral or IV)

Paediatricrising (obesity, OCP, smoking) · ✓ biggest risk = prior VTE (+ thrombophilia, strong family history) · ✗ nephrotic, obesity, OCP, SCI (lesser)

Caprini index✓ DVT risk assessment in trauma · low/moderate/high/highest · major LE fracture = highest · score ≥3 → prophylaxis (TO)

3Presentation

DVTcalf pain · palpable cord · pitting swelling · 50% with classic signs have no DVT · 50% venogram-positive have normal exam · ~20% clinically silent after TJA

PEmost asymptomatic · pleuritic pain · dyspnoea · tachypnoea · saddle embolus → death · post-vertebroplasty PE may be delayed (PMMA, fat, marrow)

Timingmost symptomatic VTE after discharge, 2–6 wk (✗ within 2 wk) · TKA: ↑ DVT but ↓ symptomatic PE vs THA · no prophylaxis TKA → symptomatic PE ~8% · DVT ~60% without chemoprophylaxis

Classificationacute <28 days · chronic >28 days · recurrent

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

DVTduplex compression US ("non-compressible vein", ~95% sens/spec) = gold standard (Orthobullets) · ✓ venography = gold standard (TO) — read the options · US best non-invasive test (97% proximal, ~75% calf) · symptomatic calf pain 2 wk post-TKA → ✓ venous US next (✗ MRI, ✗ CTA, ✗ aspiration) (TO)

Screening tests (TO)D-dimer: sensitive, ✗ specific (inflammation, malignancy, post-op) — rules out · digital photoplethysmography ~100% sensitive (TO)

✗ Routine post-op duplex screening✓ strong AAOS recommendation against in asymptomatic elective arthroplasty, even with prior DVT / estrogen (cost ~$35,000 per extra DVT)

PE✓ CT pulmonary angiography = gold standard · V/Q scan if contrast allergy · CXR usually normal early (oligaemia, prominent hilum) → late wedge/plate atelectasis

Diagnostic criteria — PE

Finding PE
PaO₂ < 80 mmHg ✓
PaCO₂ < 35 mmHg (hypocapnia) ✓ · ✗ PaCO₂ > 35
A–a gradient > 20 mmHg ✓
Tachypnoea with SpO₂ 99% ✓ (pulse oximetry unreliable — hyperventilation)
Tachycardia + new RBBB ✓
ECG Sinus tachycardia (commonest, 8–69%) · RAD · RBBB · S1Q3T3 (McGinn-White) 10–20%
Acid–base Respiratory alkalosis (✗ hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis)

Wells criteriaclinical probability for DVT and PE (TO)

5Management

Prophylaxis guidelines (AAOS, ACCP, mechanical, combined) (TO)

Risk stratification (ACCP 2008)✓ THA, TKA, hip fracture, major trauma, SCI = HIGH risk (✗ "THR moderate risk") (TO)

AAOS approach✓ pre-op assessment of VTE + bleeding risk · weigh bleeding vs PE

AAOS elevated risk of major bleeding AAOS elevated risk of PE
Bleeding disorder (haemophilia, vWD) · recent GI bleed · recent haemorrhagic stroke · active liver disease Hypercoagulable state (e.g. ✓ protein C deficiency) · previous documented PE

Operative criteria — who gets prophylaxis

Procedure Prophylaxis
THA / TKA ✓ All patients — mechanical + pharmacological (moderate strength) · no optimal agent specified
Upper extremity · arthroscopy · isolated fracture knee and below (standard risk) ✗ Not recommended
Foot & ankle ✗ Shown to ↓ VTE (20,043 patients)
Spine No consensus · early mobilisation + IPC · chemo for long surgery, multilevel thoracolumbar, anterior TL · risk epidural haematoma
Shoulder arthroplasty Mobilisation, mechanical, regional · LMWH only if ↑ risk
Trauma ✓ Combined mechanical + chemical → ↓ DVT AND PE · standard: IPC + enoxaparin
Liver failure / bleeding risk (INR 1.36) ✓ Mechanical only (✗ warfarin, ✗ none)
Contraindication to anticoagulation IVC filter considered (✗ routine)

AAOS guideline points✓ mechanical compressive devices post-op · aspirin post-op · ✓ stop antiplatelets pre-op (✗ start aspirin 1 wk pre-op) · ✗ stockings alone sufficient · ✗ routine duplex · ✗ specific INR (2011) · regional anaesthesia suggested · early mobilisation (consensus) · AAOS/ASH 2019: IPC > stockings, ✗ IVC filter, aspirin or anticoagulant, DOAC > LMWH (conditional)

Shown to ↓ VTE after TKAearly mobilisation · aspirin · coumadin · pneumatic boots · ✗ neuraxial anaesthesia

IPC mechanism✓ ↑ venous blood flow/return · ↑ endothelial fibrinolysis · ↓ venous compliance + stasis · ✗ ↓ bleeding time · ✗ endothelial injury

2012 ACCP convergence with AAOS✓ ↑ aspirin monotherapy prescribing · ACCP agents ≥ 10–14 days: ASA, LMWH, fondaparinux, apixaban, dabigatran, rivaroxaban, LDUH, VKA · dual mechanical + chemo inpatient

Compliancealmost all receive prophylaxis but often ✗ ACCP-compliant · LMWH compliant more than warfarin (THA 63 vs 33%) · VTE ↑ costs in- and post-discharge · most acute peri-op pulmonary events = fat embolism / cardiac

Agent (TO) Dose / start Duration Notes / reversal
Aspirin 81 mg BID (favoured) or 160–325 mg · within 12–24 h TKA 10–14 d · THA 28–35 d AAOS 2007 + ACCP 2012 ✓ · SIGN ✗ as sole agent · platelets (no antidote)
LMWH (enoxaparin) 30 mg BID (US) / 40 mg OD · 12–24 h 10–14 d · THA up to 35 d "Gold standard" (TO) · ✗ aPTT monitoring · ↓ HIT, ↓ osteoporosis vs UFH
UFH — — Monitor aPTT · replaced by LMWH
Warfarin (VKA) INR 2–3, target 2.5 (ACCP) · AAOS 2007: INR ≤ 2 Up to 35 d Vit K, PCC, FFP
Fondaparinux 2.5 mg OD — Anti-Xa · ✓ no routine monitoring
Rivaroxaban 10 mg OD · 6–10 h (TO) / 12–24 h (TO EPCAT table) THA 35 d · TKA 14 d Oral Xa · ✗ hip fracture · RECORD 1–4 · andexanet alfa · 20 mg = treatment dose
Apixaban 2.5 mg BID · 12–24 h THA 35 d (32–38) · TKA 12 d (10–14) Oral Xa · ✗ hip fracture · ADVANCE · least bleeding · andexanet alfa (✗ protamine)
Dabigatran 220 mg OD (half dose 1–4 h post-op; 110 mg if >75 or renal) THA 28–35 d · TKA 10 d Direct thrombin (IIa) inhibitor · RE-MODEL, RE-NOVATE · ✓ idarucizumab

Step-down (EPCAT II) (TO)✓ rivaroxaban 10 mg × 5 days → aspirin 81 mg for 30 more days (THA) / 9 days (TKA) · non-inferior, less bleeding (TO)

Switching rules (TO)✗ overlap LMWH + DOAC · give first DOAC dose when next LMWH dose due (24 h after 40 mg OD; 12 h after 30 mg BID) · LMWH → aspirin day 4–6 · ✗ step up aspirin → rivaroxaban 20 mg · CrCl <30 → adjust/avoid · epidural: hold LMWH 12 h before catheter removal, next dose ≥4 h after (TO)

Elective THA / TKA
  • All→MECHANICAL (IPC) + EARLY MOBILISATION
  • Standard risk→ASPIRIN or ANTICOAGULANT (individualised)
  • ↑ PE risk (prior PE, hypercoagulable)→PHARMACOLOGICAL ± MECHANICAL
  • ↑ Bleeding risk (bleeding disorder, liver disease, recent GI bleed / haemorrhagic stroke)→MECHANICAL ONLY
    • anticoagulation contraindicated + high PE risk→CONSIDER IVC FILTER

Treatment of VTE

Isolated calf thrombus <5 cmrarely needs treatment → serial US

Anticoagulation~3 months after DVT · ~12 months after PE

Otherearly mobilisation (dislodgement risk < further clot risk) · graduated compression hose → may prevent post-thrombotic syndrome · thrombolysis / thrombectomy controversial

Suspected intra-op PE in traumadamage control: convert definitive (IMN) to provisional (ex-fix), get helical CT

6Complications

DVTPE · post-thrombotic syndrome 43% at 2 yr (30% mild, 10% moderate, 3% severe) · recurrence 25%

PEsudden death · obstructive shock · PEA · arrhythmias · pulmonary hypertension · cor pulmonale · hypoxaemia · R→L shunt

Mortality6% DVT and 12% PE within 1 month · ~10% of PE die in first hour, 30% later from recurrence

Bleeding on dabigatran✓ idarucizumab (TO)

DOAC comparison (TO)

Rivaroxaban Apixaban Dabigatran
Target Xa Xa IIa (thrombin)
Dose 10 mg OD 2.5 mg BID 220 mg OD
Reversal Andexanet alfa Andexanet alfa Idarucizumab
Hip fracture ✗ ✗ —

How it's asked

"THA step — greatest V/Q mismatch risk?"cement pressurisation of femoral canal

"NOT part of Virchow's triad?"neither thrombocytopenia nor platelet dysfunction

"Molecule released in dissection causing DVT?"thromboplastin (✗ prothrombin, ✗ factor XI)

"What is fibrin?"product of the coagulation cascade (✗ fibrinolytic product, ✗ platelet cytoskeleton)

"Variant cofactor resistant to activated protein C?"factor V Leiden (✗ ATIII, ✗ protein S)

"Variant cofactor can't be inactivated → ↑ thrombin?"factor V Leiden thrombophilia (✗ Glanzmann, ✗ vWD)

"Leiden thrombophilia best described?"abnormal factor V resists aPC inactivation → ↑ clotting

"Deficiency raising VTE risk?"protein S (✗ fibrinogen, VII, VIII, prothrombin)

"Caprini index used for?"DVT risk in trauma (TO)

"Pre-op EPO before scoliosis fusion — risk?"thrombotic event

"THA patient — risk factors (BMI 34, age 70, cholesterol, son FVL)?"type of surgery, age, BMI (✗ hypercholesterolaemia)

"True about VTE risk?"conversion spinal → general ↑ risk (✗ MTHFR no impact, ✗ TXA ↑, ✗ estrogen protective)

"Biggest paediatric VTE risk factor?"history of VTE

"NOT true about VTE prophylaxis?"THR patients are moderate risk (they're high) (TO)

"Step-down protocol after THA (EPCAT II)?"rivaroxaban 10 mg × 5 d → aspirin 81 mg × 30 d (TO)

"GI bleed post-TKA on direct thrombin inhibitor — dose + reversal?"220 mg OD; idarucizumab (TO)

"Twice-daily oral Xa inhibitor after THA — dose, start, reversal?"apixaban 2.5 mg BID, 12–24 h, andexanet alfa (TO)

"Pneumatic compression devices associated with?"↑ venous blood flow (✗ ↑ compliance, ✗ ↓ bleeding time)

"AAOS recommendation after TKA?"mechanical compressive devices post-op (✗ pre-op aspirin, ✗ screening duplex, ✗ INR 1.5–2.5)

"Elevated bleeding risk EXCEPT?"protein C deficiency (PE risk)

"AAOS TJA prophylaxis guideline includes?"pre-op assessment of VTE and bleeding risk (✗ GA, ✗ routine IVC filter, ✗ INR 2–3)

"Liver failure, INR 1.36, TKA — prophylaxis?"mechanical prophylaxis in hospital

"Practice change after 2012 ACCP convergence?"↑ aspirin monotherapy

"True regarding VTE after hip/knee surgery?"almost all get prophylaxis, often not ACCP-compliant (✗ most within 2 wk)

"NOT shown to ↓ VTE after TKA?"neuraxial anaesthesia

"Combined mechanical + chemical in trauma?"lower DVT and PE

"Post-op duplex screening after THA?"strongly recommended against

"Gold standard for DVT post-TKR?"venography (TO)

"Calf pain + low fever 2 wk post-TKA — next?"venous ultrasonography

"Indication for helical chest CT EXCEPT?"PaCO₂ > 35 mmHg

"Dyspnoea, tachycardia 3 days post-TKA — likely finding?"S1Q3T3 on ECG

"Collapse with JVD days after repeat vertebral augmentation?"pulmonary embolism

Ulnar nerve

Basic Science · 108 / 108

1Basics

Anatomy

Coursemedial cord → anteromedial arm → pierces medial intermuscular septum (upper arm) → posteromedial → cubital tunnel → motor branches distal to tunnel (FCU, FDP ring + small) → hand

No elbow function✓ transection above elbow → no elbow limitation (elbow movers = radial, musculocutaneous, median)

Motor innervation (interossei, adductor pollicis, palmaris brevis) (TO)

Region Ulnar supply (TO)
Forearm ("1½ flexors") FCU + medial half of FDP (ring, small)
Hand — thenar ("1½ muscles") ✓ Adductor pollicis + deep (medial) head of FPB
Hand — other ✓ All interossei (palmar + dorsal) · medial 2 lumbricals · hypothenars
Superficial branch ✓ Palmaris brevis (only intrinsic)
Deep branch Hypothenars (FDM brevis, ADM, ODM) · medial lumbricals · adductor pollicis · interossei · FPB deep head
Median supply (TO) Radial supply (TO)
Forearm: pronator teres, PL, FCR, FDS · AIN: FPL, pronator quadratus, lateral FDP Before spiral groove: triceps long + medial · in groove: lateral head, anconeus
Hand: APB, opponens pollicis, superficial FPB, lateral 2 lumbricals Below groove: BR, ECRL (brachialis sensory only) · PIN: supinator + APL, EPB, ECRB, EIP, EDC, EDM, ECU

3Presentation

Cubital tunnel syndromehand clumsiness · intrinsic wasting · ✓ Froment sign · ↓ sensation ulnar small finger · Tinel at medial elbow

Sensory & motor distribution after injury

Martin-Gruber anastomosismedian → ulnar motor connections in forearm · distal ulnar transection with intact finger abduction/adduction · sensation: ✓ dorsal 1st web + index tip intact, small finger tip lost (sensory loss still ulnar)

How it's asked

"Ulnar nerve transected excising medial intermuscular septum — elbow limitation?"none

"Distal ulnar transection, intact abduction/adduction — sensation?"dorsal 1st web + index tip intact, small finger tip lost

"Nerve to adductor pollicis?"ulnar (✗ median, ✗ both) (TO)

"Nerve to palmar interossei?"ulnar (TO)

"Intrinsic supplied by superficial branch of ulnar nerve?"palmaris brevis (✗ adductor pollicis, FDM brevis, ADM) (TO)
